# THE FIRST BOOK
of **Francis Bacon**; on the
proficiency and advancement of
*Learning, divine and human.*

->To the King.<-

<image-desc>A large decorative historiated initial 'T' inside a square border, depicting two winged figures or cherubs amidst floral scrollwork.</image-desc>
Under the Law <note>original: "vnder the Lawe"; a reference to the Mosaic Law in the Old Testament</note> (excellent King), there were both daily sacrifices and freewill offerings. The first proceeded from regular observance; the second from a devout cheerfulness. In the same way, there belongs to Kings from their servants both a tribute of duty and presents of affection. Regarding the first of these, I hope I shall not live to be found wanting, according to my most humble duty and the good pleasure of your Majesty's employments. For the second, I thought it more respectful <note>original: "reſpectiue"</note> to choose a gift <term>oblation</term> <note>an offering or gift, often of a religious or formal nature</note> which might refer to the particular nature <note>original: "proprietie"</note> and excellence of your individual person, rather than to the business of your Crown and State.

Therefore, bringing your Majesty many times to my mind, and beholding you not with

# Of the Advancement of Learning

<gloss>b a</gloss>
I do not look with the inquisitive eye of presumption to discover that which the Scripture tells me is inscrutable <note>original: "inscrutable" — meaning impossible to understand or interpret; beyond human searching</note>; but rather with the observant eye of duty and admiration. Leaving aside the other parts of your virtue and fortune, I have been touched—indeed, possessed—with an extreme wonder at those virtues and faculties of yours which the philosophers call intellectual: the largeness of your capacity, the faithfulness of your memory, the swiftness of your apprehension, the penetration of your judgment, and the facility and order of your elocution <note>original: "elocution" — the skill of clear and expressive speech</note>. 

I have often thought that of all the living persons I have known, your Majesty would be the best instance to prove *Plato's* opinion: that all knowledge is but remembrance, and that the mind of man by nature knows all things. According to this view, the mind only has its own native and original motions revived and restored—motions which were sequestered <note>original: "sequestred" — hidden away or separated</note> by the strangeness and darkness of this "tabernacle of the body" <note>A biblical metaphor (2 Corinthians 5:1-4) referring to the physical human body as a temporary dwelling for the soul</note>. I have observed such a light of Nature in your Majesty, and such a readiness to take flame and blaze from the slightest occasion presented, or the smallest spark of another's knowledge delivered. 

And as the Scripture says of the wisest King <note>Referring to King Solomon; see 1 Kings 4:29</note>: *That his heart was as the sands of the sea*, which, though it is one of the largest bodies, yet consists of the smallest and finest portions; so has God given your Majesty an admirable composition of understanding. You are able to encompass and comprehend the greatest matters, and nevertheless to touch and apprehend the least;

<summary>Francis Bacon praises King James I's intellectual brilliance, comparing his vast yet detailed mind to the "sands of the sea" and suggesting his natural wisdom supports Plato's theory that all learning is actually a form of remembering.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, King James I, Plato, theory of remembrance, King Solomon, intellectual virtues, light of nature, Advancement of Learning</keywords>

whereas it should seem an impossibility in Nature for the same instrument to make itself fit for both great and small works. And regarding your gift of speech, I am reminded of what *Cornelius Tacitus* says of *Augustus Caesar*: "Augustus possessed a flowing eloquence, such as became a prince" <note>original: *Augusto profluens & quæ principem deceret, eloquentia fuit*</note>. 

For if we observe it well, speech that is delivered with labor and difficulty, or speech that savors of the affectation of art and rules, or speech that is modeled after the imitation of some pattern of eloquence (no matter how excellent), all of this has something servile about it, appearing subordinate to the subject. 

But your Majesty's manner of speech is indeed prince-like: it flows as if from a fountain, yet it streams and branches itself into the order of Nature. It is full of facility <note>original: "facilitie" — ease or fluency</note> and felicity <note>original: "felicitie" — an apt or pleasing quality; happiness in expression</note>, imitating no one and impossible for anyone else to imitate. 

And just as in your civil state there appears to be a rivalry and competition between your Majesty’s virtue and your fortune—a virtuous disposition balanced with a fortunate reign <term>regiment</term>; a virtuous expectation (in former times) of your greater fortune, with a prosperous possession of it when the due time came; a virtuous observance of the laws of marriage, along with the most blessed and happy fruit <note>Referring to the King's children and heirs</note> of that marriage; a virtuous and most Christian desire for peace, along with a fortunate inclination toward it among your neighboring princes—so likewise in these intellectual matters, there seems to be no less a competition between the excellence of your Majesty’s natural gifts and the universality and depth of your learning. For I am well assured that this which

# Of the Advancement of Learning

What I shall say is no exaggeration at all, but a positive and measured truth: which is, that there has not been since Christ’s time any King or secular <note>original: "temporall"</note> monarch who has been so learned in all literature and erudition, both divine and human. For let a man seriously and diligently examine <note>original: "reuolue"</note> and peruse the succession of the Emperors of Rome—of whom *Caesar* the Dictator (who lived some years before Christ) and *Marcus Antoninus* <note>Marcus Aurelius, the philosopher-emperor</note> were the most learned—and then descend to the Emperors of Greece, or of the West, and then to the lineages of *France, Spain, England, Scotland*, and the rest, and he shall find this judgment is truly made. 

For it seems a great achievement in a King if, through the concise summaries <note>original: "compendious extractions"</note> of other men’s wits and labors, he can grasp any superficial ornaments and displays of learning, or if he encourages and favors learning and learned men. But to drink indeed from the true fountains of learning—nay, to have such a fountain of learning within himself—is, in a King, and in a King born to the throne, almost a miracle. 

And it is all the more so because there is met in your Majesty a rare conjunction of both divine and sacred literature, as well as secular <note>original: "prophane"</note> and human literature. Thus, your Majesty stands invested with that triplicity <term>triplicity</term> <note>a threefold nature, referring here to the legend of Hermes Trismegistus, or "Thrice-Great Hermes"</note> which, with great veneration, was ascribed to the ancient *Hermes*: the power and fortune of a King; the knowledge and illumination of a Priest; and the learning and universality of a Philosopher. This characteristic <note>original: "propriety"</note>, an inherent and individual attribute in your Majesty, deserves to be expressed, not only in the fame and...

<summary>Bacon argues that King James I is the most learned monarch since the time of Christ, possessing a "triple" excellence of power, priestly knowledge, and philosophical learning.</summary>
<keywords>King James I, Marcus Aurelius, Hermes Trismegistus, triplicity, divine and human learning, royal erudition</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dedication to King James I, following the comparison of the King to Hermes Trismegistus. Bacon transitions from praising the King’s personal intellect to proposing a scholarly work that reflects and honors that brilliance.</meta>

# The First Book

...admiration of the present time, nor in the history or tradition of the ages to come, but also in some solid work, fixed memorial, and immortal monument, bearing a character or signature of both the power of a king and the distinct excellence <note>original: "difference"</note> and perfection of such a king.

Therefore, I concluded within myself that I could not make to your Majesty a better offering <note>original: "oblation"</note> than some treatise tending toward that goal. The substance of this work will consist of these two parts: The first concerning the excellence of learning and knowledge, and the excellence of the merit and true glory found in its increase <note>original: "Augmentation"</note> and spread <note>original: "Propagation"</note>. The second part covers what the particular acts and works are which have been embraced and undertaken for the advancement of learning, and furthermore, what defects and shortcomings <note>original: "undervalues"</note> I find in such particular acts. My goal is that, even though I cannot positively or directly <note>original: "affirmatively"</note> advise your Majesty or propose specific finished plans <note>original: "framed particulars"</note>, I may still stimulate your princely thoughts to visit the excellent treasure of your own mind, and from there to extract details for this purpose that are suitable to your greatness of spirit <note>original: "magnanimity"</note> and wisdom.

At the beginning of the first of these parts, in order to clear the way and, as it were, create silence so that the true testimonies concerning the dignity of learning might be better heard without the interruption of silent <note>original: "tacit"</note> objections, I think it good to deliver it from...

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues Bacon’s introduction to the first part of his work. He begins to address the various groups that have traditionally criticized or discredited learning: theologians, politicians, and even scholars themselves.</meta>

-># Of the Advancement of Learning<-

...the discredit and disgrace which learning has received. All of this comes from ignorance, but an ignorance appearing in various disguises: sometimes in the zeal and jealousy of theologians <note>original: "Divines"</note>; sometimes in the severity and arrogance of politicians <note>original: "Politiques"</note>; and sometimes in the errors and imperfections of learned men themselves.

I hear the first group [the theologians] say that knowledge consists of things that are to be accepted only with great limitation and caution. They argue that aspiring to too much knowledge was the original temptation and sin that led to the Fall of Man; that knowledge has something of the serpent in it, and therefore, when it enters a man, it makes him swell with pride. *Knowledge puffs up* <note>original: "Scientia inflat," 1 Corinthians 8:1</note>. They point out that Solomon gives a judgment <note>original: "Censure"</note>: *Of making many books there is no end, and much study is a weariness of the flesh* <note>Ecclesiastes 12:12</note>. And again in another place: *In much wisdom is much grief, and he who increases knowledge increases sorrow* <note>original: "contristation," Ecclesiastes 1:18</note>. 

They also note that Saint Paul gives a warning <note>original: "Caveat"</note>: *Beware lest anyone spoil you through hollow and deceptive philosophy* <note>Colossians 2:8</note>. Furthermore, they argue that experience demonstrates how learned men have often been arch-heretics, how intellectual eras have been inclined toward atheism, and how the contemplation of "second causes" <note>Natural laws and physical explanations for how the world works</note> detracts <note>original: "derogate"</note> from our dependence upon God, who is the "first cause" <note>The ultimate origin and creator of all things</note>.

To reveal the ignorance and error of this opinion, and the misunderstanding of its foundations, it will clearly appear that these men do not observe or consider one thing: it was not the pure knowledge of nature and the universe <note>original: "universality"</note>—a knowledge by whose light 

<meta>catchword: whereof</meta>

<summary>Bacon identifies the three sources of opposition to learning—theologians, politicians, and scholars—and begins by detailing the religious objections, which claim knowledge leads to pride, sorrow, and atheism.</summary>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues Bacon’s defense of learning against theological critics. He is specifically addressing the argument that the pursuit of knowledge led to the Fall of Man in Genesis.</meta>

->### The First Book###<-

In that state, man gave names to the other creatures in Paradise as they were brought before him, according to their properties <note>original: "proprieties"; the specific natures or characteristics of the animals</note>. This did not cause the Fall. However, it was the proud knowledge of good and evil—with the intent for man to give law to himself and to depend no longer upon God’s commandments—which was the actual nature <note>original: "fourme"</note> of the temptation. 

Nor can any amount of knowledge, no matter how great, make the mind of man "swell" with pride; for nothing can fill, much less expand, the soul of man except God and the contemplation of God. Therefore *Solomon*, speaking of the two principal senses of inquiry <note>original: "Inquisition"</note>, the Eye and the Ear, affirms that *the eye is never satisfied with seeing, nor the ear with hearing* <note>Ecclesiastes 1:8</note>. If there is no fullness, then the container <note>original: "Continent"</note> is greater than the contents. 

He defines knowledge itself and the mind of man (to which the senses are merely reporters) in the same way, using these words. They are placed after that calendar or schedule <note>original: "Ephemerides"; a table showing the predicted positions of heavenly bodies or a daily record of events</note> which he makes of the diversity of times and seasons for all actions and purposes <note>A reference to Ecclesiastes 3, "For everything there is a season"</note>; he concludes thus:

> *God has made all things beautiful or decent in the true return of their seasons. Also, he has placed the world in man's heart, yet man cannot find out the work which God works from the beginning to the end.* <note>Ecclesiastes 3:11</note>

He declares quite clearly that God has framed the mind of man like a mirror or glass, capable of reflecting the image of the entire world, and joyful to receive its impression, just as the

<summary>Bacon argues that the Fall of Man resulted from the desire for moral autonomy rather than the study of nature. He concludes that the human mind is a divinely designed "mirror" intended to perceive the entire universe.</summary>
<keywords>Fall of Man, Solomon, Ecclesiastes, inquiry, properties of nature, human mind as mirror, Ephemerides</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Bacon’s examination of the human mind’s capacity for knowledge, specifically interpreting King Solomon’s views from the book of Ecclesiastes and Proverbs.</meta>

The eye rejoices to receive light, and is not only delighted in beholding the variety of things and the changes <note>original: "vicissitude"</note> of times, but is also raised to find out and discern the laws and decrees which are infallibly observed throughout all those changes. And although he [Solomon] suggests <note>original: "insinuate"</note> that the supreme or summary law of Nature—which he calls *the work which God works from the beginning to the end*—cannot be found out by man, yet that does not take away <note>original: "derogate"</note> from the capacity of the mind. Instead, it may be attributed to impediments such as the shortness of life, the poor coordination of efforts <note>original: "ill conjunction of labors"</note>, the poor transmission of knowledge from hand to hand <note>original: "tradition"</note>, and many other inconveniences to which the condition of man is subject. 

For he [Solomon] establishes in another place that no part <note>original: "parcell"</note> of the world is denied to man’s inquiry and discovery <note>original: "invention"</note> when he says, *The spirit of man is as the lamp of God, with which he searches the inwardness of all secrets* <note>Proverbs 20:27</note>. If, then, such is the capacity and receptivity <note>original: "receit"</note> of the human mind, it is manifest that there is no danger at all in the proportion or quantity of knowledge, however large it may be, as if it might make the mind swell or exceed its own limits. No, it is purely the *quality* of knowledge which—whether the quantity is great or small—if it is taken without its true corrective, contains a nature of venom or malice, and produces the effects of that venom, which is pride <note>original: "ventosity"; literally "windiness," meaning a puffed-up or hollow vanity</note>.

<summary>Bacon argues that the human mind is naturally capable of understanding the universe, and that the limits of our knowledge stem from human frailty rather than divine prohibition. He distinguishes between the quantity of knowledge (which is harmless) and the quality of knowledge (which can lead to pride if not properly balanced).</summary>
<keywords>human mind, Solomon, Ecclesiastes, Proverbs, laws of nature, pride, ventosity, capacity of knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues Bacon’s argument that knowledge is not harmful in quantity, but requires a specific "quality" to be beneficial. He is currently identifying "charity" (altruistic love) as the necessary ingredient to prevent knowledge from becoming a source of pride.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>The first book.</header>
<sig>B 2</sig>

or swelling. This corrective spice—the mixture of which makes knowledge so supreme <note>original: "sovereign"</note>—is charity <note>In this context, "charity" refers to the theological concept of *caritas*, or selfless love for others</note>, which the Apostle [Paul] immediately adds to the previous statement; for he says, *Knowledge puffs up, but charity builds up* <note>1 Corinthians 8:1</note>. This is similar to what he delivers in another place: *If I spoke with the tongues of men and angels, and had not charity, I would be like a clanging cymbal* <note>1 Corinthians 13:1</note>. It is not that speaking with the tongues of men and angels is anything but an excellent thing, but rather that if it is separated from charity and not directed toward the good of men and mankind, it possesses a hollow and unworthy fame <note>original: "glory"</note> rather than a deserving and substantial virtue. 

As for the criticism of Solomon concerning the excess of writing and reading books <note>Ecclesiastes 12:12</note>, the anxiety of spirit that results from knowledge, and the warning of Saint Paul *that we should not be seduced by empty philosophy* <note>Colossians 2:8</note>—if those passages are understood correctly, they excellently define the true bounds and limitations by which human knowledge is confined and circumscribed. Yet, this is done without such restriction <note>original: "coarctation"</note> that would prevent knowledge from encompassing the universal nature of all things. These limitations are three-fold: 

First, *that we do not place our happiness <note>original: "felicity"</note> so entirely in knowledge that we forget our own mortality*. 

Second, *that we apply our knowledge to give ourselves peace and contentment, rather than creating anxiety <note>original: "distaste"</note> or resentment*.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>Bacon argues that knowledge must be tempered by charity to be virtuous and identifies the first two of three divine limitations on human inquiry: remembering one's mortality and seeking contentment rather than anxiety.</summary>
<keywords>Charity, Knowledge, Apostle Paul, Solomon, Philosophy, Felicity, Mortality, Contentment</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page concludes the list of three limitations on human knowledge and begins a detailed explanation of each, specifically addressing Solomon’s views and the psychological effects of learning.</meta>

<header>Of the Advancement of Learning,</header>

The third: that we do not presume, by the contemplation of Nature, to attain to the mysteries of God. As for the first of these, Solomon excellently explains himself in another place in the same book, where he says: *I saw clearly that knowledge recedes as far from ignorance as light does from darkness, and that the wise man’s eyes keep watch in his head, whereas the fool wanders about in darkness; but I also learned that the same mortality overtakes them both.* <note>Ecclesiastes 2:13–14</note> 

Regarding the second point, it is certain that there is no vexation or anxiety of mind resulting from knowledge, except merely by accident. For all knowledge and wonder (which is the seed of knowledge) is an impression of pleasure in itself. However, when men start forming conclusions from their knowledge, applying it to their own personal circumstances <note>original: "particular"</note>, and thereby feeding themselves weak fears or vast desires, then there arises that worry <note>original: "carefulness"</note> and trouble of mind which is spoken of. For then knowledge is no longer a "dry light" <note>original: *Lumen siccum*</note>, of which the profound Heraclitus <note>A Greek philosopher known for his doctrines on change and the "dry soul" being the wisest</note> said, "A dry light is the best soul" <note>original: *Lumen siccum optima anima*</note>; instead, it becomes a "damp or soaked light" <note>original: *Lumen madidum, or maceratum*</note>, being steeped and infused in the moods <note>original: "humors"</note> of the emotions <note>original: "affections"</note>. 

As for the third point, it deserves to be dwelled upon a little and not lightly passed over. For if any man thinks that by the observation and inquiry into these physical <note>original: "sensible"</note> and material things, he can attain that light by which he may reveal to himself the nature or will of God, then indeed is he 

<summary>Bacon explains three limitations of knowledge: it must not replace spiritual happiness, it should not cause anxiety unless biased by emotion, and it cannot be used to fully uncover the nature of God.</summary>

<keywords>Solomon, Heraclitus, dry light, Lumen siccum, nature of God, anxiety of mind, human knowledge, philosophy vs divinity</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the third limitation of knowledge—the danger of trying to reach divine mysteries through natural observation—and addresses the concern that science leads to atheism.</meta>

<header>The First Book.</header>

...spoiled by vain philosophy: for the contemplation of God’s creatures and works produces (when considering the works and creatures themselves) knowledge; but when considering God, it produces no perfect knowledge, but only wonder, which is "broken knowledge." And therefore it was most aptly said by one of Plato’s school <note>likely a reference to Philo Judaeus, a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher</note>, *“That the sense of man carries a resemblance to the Sun, which (as we see) opens and reveals all the terrestrial globe; but then again, it obscures and conceals the stars and the celestial globe. So does the sense discover natural things, but it darkens and shuts out the divine.”* And from this, it is true that it has happened that various great learned men have become heretical, while they have sought to fly up to the secrets of the Deity using the waxen wings of the senses <note>a reference to the myth of Icarus, whose wings melted when he flew too close to the sun</note>. 

And as for the idea <note>original: "conceit"</note> that too much knowledge might incline a man toward atheism, and that ignorance of second causes <note>the natural laws and physical processes through which God acts</note> should create a more devout dependence upon God, who is the first cause: First, it is good to ask the question that Job asked of his friends: *“Will you lie for God, as one man will do for another, to gratify Him?”* <note>Job 13:7</note> For it is certain that God works nothing in nature except through second causes; and if they would have it believed otherwise, it is a mere imposture—a supposed "favor" toward God that is actually nothing else but offering to the Author of Truth the unclean sacrifice of a lie.

Furthermore, it is an assured truth and a conclusion drawn from experience that a little or superficial...

<meta>Catchword: knowledge</meta>

<summary>Bacon argues that while the senses reveal the physical world, they obscure divine truths, much like the sun hides the stars. He defends the study of "second causes" (natural science), arguing that claiming God acts without natural laws is a pious lie that dishonors the truth.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophy, Plato, Senses, Atheism, Second Causes, Job, Theology, Nature, Divine Mystery</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that deep scientific inquiry actually supports religious faith and encourages an endless pursuit of both divine and natural knowledge, before pivoting to address political criticisms of learning.</meta>

# Of the advancement of learning,

Knowledge of **philosophy** may incline the mind of man toward **atheism**, but a deeper progression in it brings the mind back again to **religion**. For at the start of philosophy, when **second causes** <note>the natural, physical laws and processes that occur in the world</note>—which are closest to our senses—present themselves to the human mind, if the mind rests and stays there, it may cause some forgetfulness of the highest cause <note>God</note>. But when a man passes on further and sees the dependency of causes and the works of **providence**, then according to the allegory of the poets, he will easily believe that the highest link of nature's chain must necessarily be tied to the foot of **Jupiter's chair** <note>original: "Iupiters chaire." This refers to a passage in Homer’s *Iliad* where a golden chain connects the earth to the throne of Zeus/Jupiter, symbolizing the connection between the physical world and the divine.</note>. 

To conclude, therefore, let no man—out of a weak conceit of sobriety <note>a misguided idea of being humble or cautious</note> or an ill-applied moderation—think or maintain that a man can search too far or be too well-studied in the book of God's word <note>the Bible or Theology</note> or in the book of God's works <note>Nature or Science</note>; **Divinity** or **Philosophy**. Instead, let men endeavor toward an endless progress or proficiency in both. Only let men beware that they apply both to **charity** and not to **swelling** <note>original: "swelling"; meaning pride or arrogance</note>; to use, and not to ostentation; and again, that they do not unwisely mingle or confound these different types of learning together.

And as for the disgraces which learning receives from **politicians** <note>original: "Politiques"</note>, they are of this nature: that learning softens men's minds and makes them more unfit for the honor and exercise of arms; that it ruins and perverts men's dispositions for...

<summary>Bacon argues that while superficial science might lead to atheism, deep study of natural causes inevitably leads back to God; he encourages the unlimited study of both religion and nature while warning against pride.</summary>
<keywords>philosophy, atheism, religion, second causes, providence, Jupiter's chain, divinity, charity, politics, military discipline</keywords>

Out of this notion, *Cato* surnamed the Censor <note>Cato the Elder (234–149 BC), a Roman statesman famous for his traditionalism and resistance to Greek cultural influence.</note>—indeed one of the wisest men who ever lived—when *Carneades* the philosopher <note>A Greek philosopher who visited Rome as an ambassador in 155 BC; his skeptical arguments famously unsettled Roman traditionalists.</note> came as an ambassador to Rome, and the young men of Rome began to flock around him, drawn by the sweetness and majesty of his eloquence and learning, gave counsel in the open Senate that they should dismiss him with all speed. He feared Carneades might infect and enchant the minds and affections of the youth, and unintentionally bring about a change in the manners and customs of the State. 

Out of the same notion or mood, *Virgil*—turning his pen to the advantage of his country and the disadvantage of his own profession—made a kind of separation between policy and government, and between arts and sciences, in those much-renowned verses <note>original: A reference to the *Aeneid* (6.847–853), where Virgil suggests other nations might excel in arts and science, but the Roman's art is to rule and impose peace.</note>, attributing and claiming the former to the *Romans*, and...

Similarly, we see that *Anytus*, the accuser of *Socrates*, brought it as a formal charge and accusation against him that he used the variety and power of his discourses and debates to withdraw young men from their proper reverence for the laws and customs of their country. He claimed *Socrates* practiced a dangerous and harmful science, which was to make the worse cause seem the better, and to suppress the truth through the power of eloquence and speech. 

But these and similar accusations have a look of seriousness rather than any basis in justice. Experience proves that, in both specific people and specific eras, there has been a meeting and coordination of learning and military skill <note>original: "Armes"</note>, with both flourishing and excelling in the same men and the same ages. 

Regarding individuals, there cannot be a better example than that pair, *Alexander* the Great and *Julius Caesar* the Dictator. The first was *Aristotle’s* student in philosophy, and the other was *Cicero’s* rival in eloquence. Or, if anyone would rather look for scholars who became great generals than generals who were great scholars, let him consider *Epaminondas* the Theban <note>A general and statesman of 4th-century BC Thebes who transformed Greece by breaking Spartan hegemony.</note> or *Xenophon* the Athenian <note>A student of Socrates and a mercenary general whose writings documented his military expeditions.</note>. One was the first to diminish the power of *Sparta*, and the other was the first to pave the way for the overthrow of the monarchy of *Persia*. 

This coordination of learning and military prowess is even more visible in eras than in individuals, since an age is a larger ob- <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; the catchword for the next page is "ject," completing the word "object."</note>

# The First Book.

For in *Egypt, Assyria, Persia, Greece, and Rome*, the same eras that are most renowned for military prowess <note>original: "Armes"</note> are likewise most admired for learning. Consequently, the greatest authors and philosophers, and the greatest captains and governors, have lived in the same ages. Nor could it be otherwise; for just as in a man, the peak strength of both body and mind occurs at roughly the same age (except that physical strength arrives somewhat earlier), so in states, military power and learning—the one corresponding to the body and the other to the soul of man—occur together or in close succession.

Regarding matters of policy and government, the idea that learning should hinder rather than enable them is highly improbable. We see it is considered an error to entrust a physical body to "empirical" physicians <note>In this context, Bacon refers to "empirics"—practitioners who rely solely on trial-and-error experience without understanding the underlying scientific theory or "causes."</note>, who typically have a few pleasant recipes which they apply confidently and boldly, yet know neither the causes of diseases, nor the physical constitutions <note>original: "complexions"</note> of patients, nor the danger of complications <note>original: "accidents"</note>, nor the true method of treatment. We see it is a similar error to rely on advocates or lawyers who are only men of practice and not well-read in their books; they are often easily surprised when a situation arises beyond their experience, to the detriment of the cases they handle. By the same reasoning, it must surely be a risky matter if states are managed by—

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s argument that states are better managed by learned men than by those who rely solely on practical experience. He specifically addresses the common criticism that scholars (derisively called "pedants") are unfit for governance by providing historical counter-examples.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

empirical <note>original: "Emperique"; here meaning people who rely solely on practical experience without theoretical knowledge</note> statesmen who are not well balanced with men grounded in learning. On the contrary, there is almost no instance where a government was disastrous when it was in the hands of learned governors. 

Although it has been common for political men to belittle and discredit learned men by calling them "pedants" <note>original: "Pedantes"; at the time, this was a derogatory term for a schoolmaster or scholar who cared more for academic rules than practical reality</note>, the historical records show many specific examples where the governments of princes in their minority—despite the massive disadvantages of that kind of state—have nevertheless excelled the governments of princes of mature age. This occurred for the very reason that critics try to slander: because, in those instances, the state was in the hands of "pedants." 

For example, the first five years of the state of *Rome* under the young *Nero*, which are so highly praised, occurred while the government was in the hands of *Seneca*, a "pedant." So it was again for ten years or more during the minority of *Gordian* the younger, which was conducted with great applause and satisfaction <note>original: "contentation"</note> while in the hands of *Misitheus* <note>Referring to Timesitheus, a scholar and legal advisor who served as the praetorian prefect</note>, another "pedant." Before that, the minority of *Alexander Severus* saw similar happiness under similar conditions, because the government was led by the royal women who were aided by teachers and tutors <note>original: "Preceptors"</note>. 

Indeed, if one looks into the government of the Popes <note>original: "Bishops of Rome"</note>—specifically the reigns of *Pius V* and *Sixtus V* in our own times—both were initially regarded as nothing more than "scholarly friars" <note>original: "Pedanticall Friers"</note> at their start. Yet, one shall find that such Popes achieve greater things and act upon more solid principles...

If these are followed well and watchfully, there will seldom be a need for those other political tactics, any more than there is a need for medicine <note>original: "Phisicke"</note> in a healthy or well-dieted body. 

Furthermore, the experience of one man’s life cannot provide enough examples and precedents <note>original: "presidents"</note> for the events of even one man’s life. For just as it happens sometimes that a grandchild or other descendant resembles their ancestor more than their father, so many times current events may align better with ancient examples than with those of more recent or immediate times. And lastly, the intelligence of one man can no more equal the power of learning than one man’s wealth can keep pace with a public treasury <note>original: "common purse"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the defense of learning against the charge that it makes men unfit for action or decision-making.</meta>

Of the advancement of learning,

Learning offers a greater strength of medicine or remedy than it offers cause of indisposition or infirmity. For if, by some secret operation, it makes men perplexed and irresolute, on the other hand, by plain precept, it teaches them when and upon what grounds to resolve. Indeed, it even teaches how to carry things in suspense without prejudice until they do resolve. 

If learning makes men too positive and rigid <note>original: "reguler"</note>, it teaches them which things are by nature demonstrative <note>capable of being proved with certainty</note> and which are merely conjectural. It teaches the use of distinctions and exceptions as well as the broad application <note>original: "latitude"</note> of principles and rules. If it misleads by a lack of proportion or by the dissimilarity of examples, it teaches men the force of circumstances, the errors of false comparisons, and all the cautions of application. So, in all these cases, learning rectifies more effectively than it can pervert. 

Learning conveys these medicines into men's minds much more forcibly through the vividness and penetration of examples. For let a man look into the errors of Pope Clement VII, so lively described by Francesco Guicciardini <note>An Italian historian and statesman who served under Clement VII</note>, or into the errors of Cicero, painted by his own pen in his letters to Atticus, and he will fly quickly from being irresolute. Let him look into the errors of Phocion <note>An Athenian statesman known for his rigid honesty and refusal to compromise</note>, and he will beware of being obstinate or inflexible. Let him but read the fable of Ixion <note>In Greek mythology, Ixion embraced a cloud thinking it was the goddess Hera; Bacon uses this to represent chasing illusions</note>, and it will keep him from being vaporous or imaginative. Let him look into the errors of Cato the Younger <note>original: "Cato the second"</note>, and he will never be one of the *Antipodes* <note>People who live on the opposite side of the globe; here meaning those who are out of step with their own time</note>, walking in opposition to the present world.

And as for the conceit <note>notion or opinion</note> that learning should dis-

<summary>Bacon argues that learning provides the intellectual tools to correct its own potential flaws, using historical examples to show how study prevents indecision and stubbornness. He contends that knowledge of history and philosophy makes a person more adaptable and practical, rather than less.</summary>
<keywords>Clement VII, Guicciardini, Cicero, Phocion, Ixion, Cato the Younger, decision-making, historical examples, political wisdom</keywords>

# The First Book

disposed men to leisure and privacy, and make men slothful: it would be a strange thing if that which accustoms the mind to perpetual motion and agitation should induce slothfulness. On the contrary, it may be truly affirmed that no kind of men love business for its own sake, except those who are learned. 

Other persons love business for profit, like a hireling who loves the work for the wages; or for honor, because it sustains them in the eyes of men and refreshes their reputation, which otherwise would fade; or because it reminds them of their fortune and gives them the opportunity to bestow pleasure or displeasure; or because it exercises some faculty in which they take pride, and so keeps them in a good humor and pleasing opinion of themselves; or because it advances any other of their goals. 

So, just as it is said of false bravery—that some men's courage exists only in the eyes of those looking on—so the industry of such men exists only in the eyes of others, or at least in regard to their own private designs. Only learned men love business as an action according to nature, as agreeable to the health of the mind as exercise is to the health of the body, taking pleasure in the action itself and not in the profit. Consequently, of all men, they are the most tireless if they are engaged in any business which can hold or occupy their mind.

And if any man is laborious in reading and study, and yet idle in business and action, this stems from some weakness of body or softness of spirit, such as *Seneca* <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – AD 65), a Roman Stoic philosopher and statesman.</note> speaks of...

<summary>The author argues that learning does not cause sloth; rather, learned men are the only ones who truly love work for its own sake, whereas others only pursue business for profit, status, or vanity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon's defense of learning against common prejudices, specifically addressing the claims that it makes men unfit for business or weakens their respect for the law.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning,

<note>original: "Quidam tam sunt umbratiles, ut putent in turbido esse, quicquid in luce est"</note> "Some are so fond of the shade that they think whatever is in the light is in a state of confusion," as Seneca speaks of; and he is speaking of a specific personality type, not of learning itself. It may well be that such a trait in a man's nature may make him devote himself to learning, but it is not learning that breeds such a trait in his nature.

And as for the claim that learning should take up too much time or leisure, I answer: the most active or busy man that has ever been or can be has (without question) many vacant times of leisure while he awaits the tides and returns of business. This is true unless he is either tedious and slow in his work, or is foolishly and unworthily ambitious to meddle in things that could be better done by others. The question then is simply how those spaces and times of leisure shall be filled and spent: whether in pleasures or in studies. This was well answered by *Demosthenes* to his adversary *Aeschines*, who was a man given to pleasure and told him, "That his orations smelled of the lamp." "Indeed," said *Demosthenes*, "there is a great difference between the things that you and I do by lamp-light." Thus, no man need doubt that learning will drive out business; rather, it will keep and defend the possession of the mind against idleness and pleasure, which otherwise might enter unawares to the prejudice of both.

Again, regarding that other conceit—that learning should undermine the reverence of laws and government—it is assuredly a mere depravity and calumny <note>original: "calumny"; meaning a false and malicious accusation</note> without any shadow of truth. For to say that a blind custom of obedience should be a surer obligation...

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that learning does not make men idle or antisocial, but rather protects the mind from unproductive pleasures during downtime and reinforces rather than weakens civic duty.</summary>

It is beyond all controversy that learning makes the minds of men gentle, generous, manageable, and pliant to government; whereas ignorance makes them churlish, stubborn, and mutinous. The evidence of history clearly supports this assertion, considering that the most barbarous, rude, and unlearned times have been most subject to tumults, seditions, and changes.

And as for the judgment of *Cato* the Censor <note>Marcus Porcius Cato (234–149 BC), a Roman statesman known for his traditionalism and opposition to Greek influence.</note>, he was well punished for his blasphemy against learning in the same way in which he offended. For when he was past sixty years old, he was taken with an extreme desire to go to school again and to learn the Greek tongue in order to study the Greek authors. This clearly demonstrates that his former censure of Grecian learning was an affected gravity <note>A performed or fake seriousness.</note> rather than his own sincere opinion. 

And as for *Virgil’s* verses, though it pleased him to boast to the world by claiming for the Romans the art of empire while leaving the arts of subjects to others <note>original: "Excudent alii spirantia mollius aera... tu regere imperio populos, Romane, memento." # Of the Advancement of Learning

the best or second-best orator, *Marcus Cicero*, known to the memory of man. As for the accusation of *Socrates*, the time when it was prosecuted must be remembered; it was under the Thirty Tyrants, the most base, bloody, and envious persons that have ever governed. No sooner was that revolution of state over than *Socrates*—whom they had treated as a criminal—was recognized as a heroic person. His memory was showered with honors both divine and human, and those discourses of his, which were then termed "the corrupting of manners," were afterward acknowledged as sovereign medicines for the mind and behavior, and have been received as such ever since until this day.

Let this, therefore, serve as an answer to those "Politiques" <note>original: "Politiques"; here meaning cynical or purely pragmatic political thinkers</note> who, in their moody severity or feigned gravity, have presumed to cast insults upon learning. This refutation <note>original: "redargution"</note>, however, would not even be necessary for the present day (except that we do not know if our labors may reach other ages), because of the love and reverence toward learning which has been instilled in all men of position and authority in our nation. This respect has been produced by the example and encouragement of two such learned princes as Queen *Elizabeth* and your Majesty, who are like *Castor* and *Pollux*, *bright stars* <note>original: "Lucida Sydera"</note> of excellent light and most gracious influence.

Now, therefore, we come to that third sort of discredit or loss of reputation that happens to learning because of learned men themselves; this is the kind of criticism that commonly sticks most firmly. It arises either from their fortune,

# The First Book

Fortune, or from their manners, or from the nature of their studies. Regarding the first, it is not in their power; and the second is accidental; only the third is proper to be handled here. But because we are not currently dealing with true measures of worth, but with popular estimation and opinion, it is not out of place to speak somewhat of the former two. The derogations <note>original: "derogations"; here meaning things that take away from the reputation of learning</note>, therefore, which grow to learning from the fortune or condition of learned men, are either in respect of scarcity of means, or in respect of the privacy of their lives and the meanness <note>original: "meanesse"; meaning low status or insignificance</note> of their employments.

Concerning want—and the fact that it is the case for learned men usually to begin with little, and not to grow rich as fast as other men because they do not direct their labors chiefly toward lucre and profit—it would be best to leave the common arguments in praise of poverty for some Friar to handle. Much was attributed to this point by Machiavelli when he said, *That the kingdom of the clergy would have ended long ago, if the reputation and reverence toward the poverty of Friars had not offset the scandal of the superfluities and excesses of Bishops and Prelates.* 

So a man might say that the luxury and delicacy of Princes and great persons would have long ago turned to rudeness and barbarism, if the poverty of learning had not maintained civility and the honor of life. But without any such rhetorical advantages, it is worthy of observation what a revered and honored thing poverty of fortune was for several ages in the Roman State, which was nevertheless a state without paradoxes <note>meaning a practical, realistic society rather than one driven by strange or contradictory theories</note>. For we see what Titus Livius <note>original: "Titus Liuius"; the Roman historian Livy</note>...

<meta>This page continues the defense of learning against the common prejudices of the world, specifically addressing the perceived poverty and obscurity of scholars.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning,

Livy <note>original: "Livius"</note> says in his introduction: *“Otherwise, either the love of the task I have undertaken deceives me, or there was never any republic greater, more sacred, or richer in good examples; nor one into which greed and luxury entered so late, nor where poverty and frugality were honored so highly or for so long.”* <note>original: "Cæterum aut me amor negotij suscepti fallit, aut nulla unquam respublica, nec maior, nec sanctior, nec bonis exemplis ditior fuit; nec in quam tam seræ auaritia luxuriáque immigrauerint, nec ubi tantus ac tam diu paupertati ac parsimoniæ honos fuerit."</note> We see likewise that after the State of Rome was no longer itself, but had degenerated, that the person who took it upon himself to be a counselor to Julius Caesar after his victory—regarding where to begin his restoration of the State—makes it the most essential point of all to take away the high reputation of wealth. *“But these and all other evils will end along with the honor paid to money, if neither magistracies nor other things commonly desired are for sale.”* <note>original: "Verum hæc & omnia mala pariter cum honore pecuniæ desinent; Si neque magistratus, neque alia vulgo cupienda venalia erunt."</note> To conclude this point, as it was truly said that *“A blush is the color of virtue,”* <note>original: "Rubor est virtutis color"</note> though sometimes it comes from vice; so it may be aptly said that *“Poverty is the fortune of virtue,”* <note>original: "Paupertas est virtutis fortuna"</note> though sometimes it may proceed from mismanagement and accident. Surely Solomon has pronounced it both as a judgment—*“He who hastens to riches shall not be innocent”*—and as a precept: *“Buy the truth, and sell it not; and do the same for wisdom and knowledge,”* judging that resources were to be spent upon learning, and not that learning should be used merely to gain resources.

And as for the privacy or obscurity of the lives of contemplative men (as it may be viewed in common opinion), it is a very common theme to extol a private life—provided it is not tainted with sensuality and sloth—in comparison to, and to the disadvantage of, a civil <note>term: "civil life" refers to a life of public service, politics, or business</note> life, for the sake of safety, liberty, pleasure, and dignity, or at least freedom from indignity.

<summary>Bacon argues that poverty is often a companion to virtue and learning, citing Roman history and Biblical wisdom to show that knowledge should be valued above financial gain.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the defense of learning, specifically addressing the social status of learned men and the importance of education.</meta>

# The First Book

This subject is of such a quality that no man handles it without handling it well; it has such a harmony with men’s thoughts in its expression, and with men’s agreement in its approval. I will only add this: that learned men who are forgotten by the State, and who do not live in the public eye, are like the **images of Cassius and Brutus at the funeral of Junia**; of which, because they were not displayed as many others were, <note>Junia was the widow of Cassius and sister of Brutus; their images were barred from her funeral because they had assassinated Julius Caesar.</note> *Tacitus* says, **"They shone all the more brightly because they were not seen."** <note>original: "Eo ipso praefulgebant, quod non visebantur."</note>

And regarding the perceived lowliness of their employment, the thing most often criticized and held in contempt is that the instruction of youth is commonly assigned to them. Because youth is the age of least authority, that lack of status is transferred to the low opinion of those jobs where youth is involved, and which are focused on the young. 

But how unjust this slander is (if you will judge things by the measure of reason rather than by popular opinion) may appear in this: we see that men are more careful about what they put into a new vessel than into a seasoned one; and they are more careful about what soil they lay around a young plant than around a plant that is already strong <term>original: "corroborate"</term>. Thus, the most fragile stages and times of all things usually receive the best care and assistance. 

And will you listen to the **Hebrew Rabbis**? **"Your young men shall see visions, and your old men shall dream dreams,"** <note>A reference to the biblical book of Joel 2:28 or Acts 2:17.</note> they say that youth is the worthier age, because visions are closer to the actual appearances of God than dreams are. And let it be noted that, however much the lifestyle of **pedagogues** <note>original: "Pedantes"; here meaning teachers or schoolmasters.</note> has been mocked on stages as the **"Ape of Tyranny"**;

<summary>Bacon argues that the obscurity of learned men often increases their true glory and defends the teaching of youth as a vital, high-stakes profession despite its low social status.</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Tacitus, Cassius, Brutus, education, pedagogy, youth vs. age, Hebrew Rabbis, social status of scholars</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the perceived discredits of learning, specifically addressing the role of educators and the personal character of scholars.</meta>

And though modern looseness or negligence has taken no due regard for the choice of schoolmasters and tutors, yet the ancient wisdom of the best times did always make a just complaint: that states were too busy with their laws and too negligent in the point of education. This excellent part of ancient discipline has been, in some sort, revived in recent times by the colleges of the Jesuits. Regarding them, although because of their superstition I may say, "The better they are, the worse they are" <note>original: *Quo meliores, eo deteriores*</note>, yet in regard to this and some other points concerning human learning and moral matters, I may say as Agesilaus <note>A King of Sparta</note> said to his enemy Pharnabazus <note>A Persian satrap</note>, "Since you are such as you are, I wish you were on our side" <note>original: *Talis quum sis, utinam noster esses*</note>. And this is enough regarding the discredits drawn from the fortunes <note>term: fortunes; meaning the financial status or social standing</note> of learned men.

As for the manners <note>term: manners; meaning personal character and behavior</note> of learned men, it is a personal and individual matter. No doubt there are among them, as in other professions, people of all temperaments. Yet it is not without truth what is said: that "Studies pass into character" <note>original: *Abeunt studia in mores*</note>; studies have an influence and operation upon the manners of those who are conversant in them.

But upon an attentive and impartial review, I for my part cannot find any disgrace to learning that proceeds from the manners of learned men which is inherent to them specifically as scholars—unless it be a fault (which was the supposed fault of Demosthenes, Cicero, Cato the Younger, Seneca, and many more) that because the times they read about are commonly better than the times they live in, and the duties taught...

<summary>Bacon argues that the state neglects education compared to law, praising the Jesuits' methods despite differing with their theology, and begins defending the personal character of scholars against common prejudices.</summary>
<keywords>education, Jesuits, Agesilaus, Pharnabazus, character of scholars, Demosthenes, Cicero, Cato the Younger, Seneca, pedagogy</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the various impediments to the growth of knowledge, specifically how over-reverence for antiquity and the authority of certain authors has hindered the advancement of the sciences.</meta>

The first Book.

Sciences have suffered from this <note>The author refers to the practice of treating earlier authors as infallible authorities.</note>, as the principal cause that has kept them low, at a standstill without growth or advancement. For from this it has happened that in the **mechanical arts**, the first inventor falls shortest, and time adds to and perfects the work; but in the **sciences**, the first author goes furthest, and time loses <note>original: "leefeth"</note> and corrupts the knowledge. So we see that artillery, sailing, printing, and the like were crudely <note>original: "grossely"</note> managed at first, and were adapted and refined over time. But on the contrary, the philosophies and sciences of *Aristotle*, *Plato*, *Democritus*, *Hippocrates*, *Euclid*, and *Archimedes* were at their most vigorous at the beginning, and have degenerated and been debased over time. 

The reason for this is nothing other than that in the former [mechanical arts], many minds and efforts have contributed together; but in the latter [sciences], many minds and efforts have been spent on the mind of just one person, whom they have often corrupted <note>original: "depraved," meaning distorted or worsened</note> rather than enlightened <note>original: "illustrated"</note>. For just as water will not rise higher than the level of the original spring from which it flows, so knowledge derived from *Aristotle*—if exempt from the liberty of examination—will never rise higher than the knowledge of *Aristotle*. And therefore, although the position is good that "A learner must believe" <note>original: *Oportet discentem credere*</note>, yet it must be coupled with this: "The educated must judge" <note>original: *Oportet edoctum iudicare*</note>. For disciples owe their masters only a temporary belief and a suspension of their own judgment until they are fully instructed, not an absolute resignation or perpetual captivity. And therefore, to conclude this point, I will say no more but this: let great authors

<meta>The previous page concluded the discussion of the three major "diseases" of learning: vain phrasing (delicate learning), vain matter (contentious learning), and vain research (deceitful learning). This page transitions to minor "unhealthy tendencies" in scholarship.</meta>

...have their due, as time—which is the "Author of Authors"—should not be deprived of its due, which is to discover truth further and further. Thus, I have gone over these three diseases of learning; besides these, there are some other **unhealthy tendencies** <note>original: "peccant humors," a medical term referring to an imbalance of bodily fluids that causes minor illness</note> rather than fully formed diseases. Nevertheless, these are not so secret and internal <note>original: "intrinsic"</note> that they do not fall under common observation and criticism <note>original: "traducement"</note>; and therefore they are not to be passed over.

The first of these is the extreme obsession <note>original: "affecting"</note> with two extremes: **Antiquity** and **Novelty**. In this, it seems the children of Time take after the nature and malice of their father <note>In mythology, Saturn (Time) devoured his own children</note>. For as he devours his children, so one of these seeks to devour and suppress the other. Antiquity is jealous that there should be new additions, and Novelty cannot be content to add something new unless it also defaces the old. Surely the advice of the Prophet is the true direction in this matter: "Stand in the ancient ways, and see which is the right and good way, and walk in it" <note>original: *State super vias antiquas, & videte quaenam sit via recta & bona, & ambulate in ea* (Jeremiah 6:16)</note>. 

Antiquity deserves such reverence that men should pause there to discover which is the best way; but once the discovery is clearly made, they should then move forward. And to speak truly, **"The antiquity of the age is the youth of the world"** <note>original: *Antiquitas saeculi iuventus mundi*</note>. These present times are the truly ancient times because the world is now old; they are not those times which we count as ancient by **calculating backward** <note>original: *ordine retrogrado*</note> from ourselves.

Another error induced by the former is a distrust that anything new should now be found out which...

<summary>Bacon identifies the rivalry between traditional antiquity and modern novelty as a hindrance to progress, arguing that the present age—having more experience—is actually the world's "ancient" age. He suggests that we should respect the past to find the right path, but must continue to move forward in our discoveries.</summary>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "errors" or "distempers" of learning, specifically focusing on the failure to connect specific sciences to universal knowledge and the tendency of scholars to rely on internal logic over external observation.</meta>

# The First Book.

Neither is it possible to discover the more remote and deeper parts of any science if you stand only upon the level of that same science and do not ascend to a higher science. <note>Bacon argues that to understand a specific field deeply, one must view it from the perspective of a broader, more universal knowledge.</note>

Another error has proceeded from too great a reverence, and a kind of adoration, of the human mind and understanding. Because of this, men have withdrawn themselves too much from the contemplation of nature and the observations of experience. They have tumbled up and down in their own reason and conceits. Regarding these "intellectualists" (who are nevertheless commonly considered the most sublime and divine philosophers), Heraclitus <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher known for his doctrine of change and his cryptic sayings.</note> gave a just censure, saying: *Men sought truth in their own little worlds, and not in the great and common world.* For they disdain to spell out, and so by degrees to read, the volume of God’s works. On the contrary, by continual meditation and the agitation of their own wit, they urge—and, as it were, call upon—their own spirits to divine and give oracles to them, by which they are deservedly deluded.

Another error that has some connection with this last one is that men are accustomed to infect their meditations, opinions, and doctrines with certain conceits <note>original: "conceits"; here meaning favorite ideas or private fancies</note> which they have most admired, or some sciences which they have most practiced. They give all other things a "tincture" <note>A metaphorical coloring or bias.</note> according to these favorites, in a way that is utterly untrue and improper. So has **Plato** intermingled his philosophy with theology, and **Aristotle** with logic; and the second school of **Plato** <note>Likely referring to the Neoplatonists.</note>—

<summary>Bacon identifies two major errors in scholarship: the failure to look beyond specific fields to universal principles, and the tendency of "intellectualists" to rely on their own internal reasoning rather than the objective observation of nature.</summary>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues Bacon’s critique of the "errors" or "distempers" of learning, moving from the specific style of certain schools to the broader motivations and goals of scholars.</meta>

->The first Book.<-

not to be disallowed. But in the true handling of knowledge, men ought not to fall on the one side into the style <note>original: "veyne"</note> of *Velleius* the *Epicurean*: "Fearing nothing so much as that he might seem to doubt anything" <note>original Latin: *Nil tam metuens, quam ne dubitare aliqua de re videretur*</note>; nor on the other side into *Socrates’* <note>original: "Socrates his"</note> ironic doubting of all things. Instead, they should propose things sincerely, with more or less assertion <note>original: "asseueration"</note>, according to how they stand in a man's own judgment, proved more or less.

There are other errors in the goal <note>original: "scope"</note> that men set for themselves and toward which they direct their efforts. For while the more steady and devoted practitioners of any science ought to aim to make some additions to their field, they instead turn their labors toward certain secondary prizes. These include being a profound interpreter or commentator <note>original: "Cōmenter"</note>; a sharp champion or defender; or a methodical organizer <note>original: "Compounder"</note> or abridger. In this way, the inheritance <note>original: "Patrimonie"</note> of knowledge is sometimes refined <note>original: "improoued"</note>, but seldom increased <note>original: "augmented"</note>.

But the greatest error of all is the misunderstanding or misplacing of the final or ultimate end of knowledge. Men have entered into a desire for learning and knowledge for various reasons: sometimes out of natural curiosity and an inquisitive appetite; sometimes to entertain their minds with variety and delight; sometimes for ornament and reputation; sometimes to enable them to win intellectual battles <note>original: "victorie of wit and contradiction"</note>; and most often for profit <note>original: "lukar"</note> and professional gain <note>original: "profession"</note>. Seldom do they seek it sincerely to give a true account of their gift <note>The text continues on the next page with the word "gift."</note>

<summary>Bacon criticizes scholars who aim for secondary goals like commentary or debate rather than the discovery of new knowledge. He argues that the greatest error is seeking knowledge for vanity or profit instead of its true purpose.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s discussion of the wrong motivations for seeking knowledge, contrasting self-serving goals with the true purpose of learning: the benefit of humanity and the glory of God.</meta>

<header>Of the advancement of learning,</header>

gift of reason, for the benefit and use of men. It is as if people sought in knowledge a couch where a searching and restless spirit might rest; or a terrace for a wandering and fickle <note>original: "variable"</note> mind to walk up and down with a pleasant view <note>original: "faire proſpect"</note>; or a tower of state for a proud mind to raise itself upon; or a fort or commanding ground for strife and contention; or a shop for profit or sale; and not a rich storehouse for the glory of the Creator and the relief of man's estate <note>Bacon’s famous phrase "relief of man’s estate" refers to the practical improvement of the human condition and the easing of suffering through scientific discovery.</note>. But this is what will indeed dignify and exalt knowledge: if contemplation and action may be more nearly and strictly joined and united together than they have been. This would be a conjunction like that of the two highest planets: *Saturn*, the planet of rest and contemplation, and *Jupiter*, the planet of civil society and action. 

However, when I speak of use and action, I do not mean that goal mentioned before—applying knowledge merely for profit <note>original: "luker" (lucre)</note> and professional gain. For I am well aware how much that diverts and interrupts the pursuit and advancement of knowledge. It is like the golden ball thrown before *Atalanta*; while she goes aside and stoops to pick it up, her race is hindered:

> She turns aside from her course and picks up the rolling gold. <note>original Latin: *Declinat cursus, aurumque volubile tollit* (Ovid, *Metamorphoses* 10.667). In the myth, Atalanta loses a race because she stops to gather golden balls intended to distract her.</note>

Neither is it my meaning—as was said of *Socrates*—to call philosophy down from heaven to converse upon the earth; that is, to set aside natural philosophy <note>The study of nature and the physical universe (the precursor to modern science).</note> and apply knowledge only to morality <note>original: "manners"</note> and politics <note>original: "policie"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of knowledge, starting with the final syllable of the word "knowledge" from the previous page.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

...knowledge in the archetype <term>archetype</term> (original: "Arch-tipe") — the original pattern or model from which all things are derived. or first platform, which is found in the attributes and acts of God, as far as they are revealed to man and may be observed with sobriety. In this context, we must not seek it under the name of "Learning," for all learning is knowledge that is acquired, whereas all knowledge in God is original. Therefore, we must look for it by another name: that of wisdom or sapience, as the Scriptures call it.

It is so then, that in the work of the Creation, we see a double outpouring of power <note>original: "emanation of virtue"</note> from God: one referring more properly to power, the other to wisdom. The first is expressed in creating the substance of matter, and the second in arranging the beauty of its form. Given this, it should be observed that, according to the history of the Creation, the confused mass and matter of heaven and earth was made in a moment; however, the ordering and arrangement of that *Chaos* or *Mass* was the work of six days. God chose to place such a mark of distinction between the works of power and the works of wisdom. This is supported by the fact that regarding the former, it is not recorded that God said, "*Let there be Heaven and Earth*" (as is written of the works that followed), but rather, it says that God actually *made* heaven and earth. The one carries the character of a physical production <note>original: "manufacture"</note>, while the other carries the character of a law, decree, or counsel.

To proceed to what comes next in order after God—the spirits: we find, as far as credit is to be given to the "Celestial Hierarchy" of that supposed <note>Referring to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian and philosopher.</note> Dionysius...

<summary>Bacon distinguishes between divine wisdom and human learning, noting that God's creation reflects both His power (the instantaneous creation of matter) and His wisdom (the six-day process of bringing order to chaos).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the hierarchy of spiritual beings and the divine origins of knowledge, following the previous page's introduction to the works of wisdom in the Creation.</meta>

# The First Book

According to Dionysius the Senator of Athens <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century theologian whose work *The Celestial Hierarchy* was highly influential in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note>, the first rank or degree is given to the Angels of love, who are termed *Seraphim*; the second to the Angels of light, who are termed *Cherubim*; and the third and following places to thrones, principalities, and the rest, which are all Angels of power and service <note>original: "ministry"</note>. Thus, the Angels of knowledge and illumination are placed before the Angels of duty <note>original: "Office"</note> and authority.

To descend from spirits and intellectual forms to physical <note>original: "sensible"</note> and material forms, we read that the first form that was created was light. Light has a relationship and correspondence in nature and physical things to what knowledge is in spirits and incorporeal things.

Likewise, in the arrangement of the days of Creation, we see that the day wherein God rested and contemplated His own works was blessed above all the days in which He performed and completed them.

After the Creation was finished, it is recorded for us that man was placed in the Garden to work there. This work appointed to him could be nothing other than the work of contemplation—that is, when the purpose of work is only for exercise and trial <note>original: "experiment"</note>, not for necessity. For there being then no resistance from nature <note>original: "reluctation of the creature"</note> nor sweat of the brow, man's employment must consequently have been a matter of delight in observation and not a matter of labor for utility. consisted of the two summary parts of knowledge: the observation <note>original: "view"</note> of created things <note>original: "Creatures"</note> and the assignment <note>original: "imposition"</note> of names. The assumption <note>original: "supposition"</note> behind this was that God’s commandments or prohibitions were not the true origins of good and evil, but that they had other sources <note>original: "beginnings"</note> which man aspired to know, with the goal of making a total desertion <note>original: "defection"</note> from God and depending entirely upon himself.

Moving on to the first event after the Fall of Man, we see an image of the two states of life—the contemplative state and the active state—represented <note>original: "figured"</note> by the persons of *Abel* and *Cain*. (The Scriptures contain infinite mysteries in this way, without at all violating the historical or literal truth of the story.) These states are shown through the two simplest and most primitive occupations. First is that of the shepherd, who—because of his leisure, his staying in one place, and his living under the open sky <note>original: "in view of heaven"</note>—is a vivid <note>original: "lively"</note> image of a contemplative life. Second is that of the farmer <note>original: "husbandman"</note>. Here we see again that the favor and choice <note>original: "election"</note> of God went to the shepherd and not to the tiller of the ground.

Similarly, in the age before the Flood, the holy records (within those few accounts <note>original: "memorials"</note> that are recorded and registered there) have seen fit to mention and honor the names of the inventors and authors of music and metalworking. In the matter of biology <note>original: "generation"</note>: *Have you not milked me like milk, and curdled me like cheese, etc.?* <note>original: "Annon sicut lac mulsisti me, & sicut caseum coagulasti me, &c."</note> In the matter of minerals: *Silver has the beginnings of its veins, and there is a place for gold where it is refined; iron is taken from the earth, and stone, dissolved by heat, is turned into bronze* <note>original: "Habet argentum venarum suarum principia: & auro locus est in quo conflatur, ferrum de terra tollitur, & lapis solutus calore in æs vertitur"</note>; and so on throughout that chapter.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of divine wisdom, transitioning from the scientific insights found in the Book of Job to King Solomon's divinely inspired natural history and his pursuit of truth as a "game" played with God.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>

# The First Book

God's agreement to this <note>referring to Solomon’s request for wisdom rather than wealth</note> was preferred above all other earthly <note>original: "terrene"</note> and temporary happiness. By virtue of this grant or gift <note>original: "donatiue" <term>Donative: a gift or formal donation, often from a superior to an inferior.</term></note> from God, Solomon became capable not only of writing those excellent parables or aphorisms <note>original: "Aphorismes" <term>Aphorisms: concise statements of principles or general truths.</term></note> concerning divine and moral philosophy, but also of compiling a natural history of all greenery <note>original: "verdor"</note>—from the cedar tree on the mountain to the moss on the wall (which is but a basic form <note>original: "rudiment"</note> between decay and a plant)—and also of all things that breathe or move. Indeed, the same King Solomon, although he excelled in the glory of treasure and magnificent buildings, of shipping and navigation, of service and attendance, and of fame and renown and the like; yet he makes no claim to any of those glories, but only to the glory of the investigation <note>original: "Inquisition"</note> of truth. For as he says expressly: *The glory of God is to conceal a thing, but the glory of the king is to find it out* <note>Proverbs 25:2</note>. It is as if, according to the innocent play of children, the Divine Majesty took delight in hiding his works so that they might be found out; and as if kings could obtain no greater honor than to be God's playfellows in that game, considering their great command of intellect <note>original: "wits"</note> and resources, so that nothing needs to be hidden from them.

Neither did God's way of dealing with the world <note>original: "dispensation"</note> change in the times after our Savior came into the world. For our Savior himself first showed his power to overcome ignorance through his discussion with the priests and doctors of the law, before he showed his power...

<header>Of the Advancement of Learning,</header>

...to subdue nature by his miracles. And the coming of the Holy Spirit was chiefly represented and expressed in the likeness and gift of tongues, which are but the **vehicles of knowledge** <note>original: "Vehicula scientiæ"</note>.

So in the choice <note>original: "election"</note> of those instruments which it pleased God to use for the planting <note>original: "plantation"</note> of the faith, he initially employed persons who were altogether unlearned—except through divine inspiration—to more clearly demonstrate his immediate working and to humble <note>original: "abbase"</note> all human wisdom or knowledge. Nevertheless, as soon as that plan of his was accomplished, in the very next transition <note>original: "vicissitude"</note> and succession, he sent his divine truth into the world accompanied by other forms of learning as if they were servants or handmaidens. For we see that *Saint Paul*, who was the only learned man among the Apostles, had his pen used most frequently in the scriptures of the New Testament.

Again, we find that many of the ancient bishops and Fathers of the Church were excellently read and studied in all the learning of the pagans <note>original: "Heathen"</note>. In fact, the edict of the Emperor *Julian*—by which it was forbidden <note>original: "interdicted"</note> for Christians to be admitted into schools, lectures, or exercises of learning—was esteemed and accounted a more destructive tool and plot <note>original: "pernitious engine and machination"</note> against the Christian faith than all the bloody persecutions <note>original: "sanguinarie prosecutions"</note> of his predecessors. Nor could the rivalry and jealousy of *Gregory* the first of that name, Bishop of *Rome*, ever gain him a reputation for piety or devotion <note>Bacon refers here to Pope Gregory the Great's alleged hostility toward secular classical literature</note>:
