And we see before our eyes that in our own age and that of our fathers, when it pleased God to call the Church of Rome to account for its degenerate manners and ceremonies—and various doctrines that were harmful <note>original: "obnoxious"</note> and designed to uphold those same abuses—it was ordained by divine providence at that very same time that there should also occur a renovation and new spring of all other branches of knowledge. And on the other hand, we see the *Jesuits*, who—partly through their own efforts and partly by the rivalry and provocation of their example—have much revived and strengthened the state of *learning*. We see, I say, what notable service and restoration they have done for the Roman See <note>original: "Romane Sea"</note>.

Therefore, to conclude this part, let it be observed that there are two principal duties and services, besides ornament and illustration, which *philosophy* and human learning perform for faith... <note>The text continues on the next page with the word "faith".</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the two ways human learning serves faith and religion, concluding the section on divine evidence for the dignity of knowledge and beginning the section on human evidence.</meta>

<header>Of the advancement of learning,</header>

...faith and religion. The first is because they are an effective incentive for the exaltation of the glory of God. For as the Psalms and other Scriptures often invite us to consider and magnify the great and wonderful works of God, if we were to stop only at the contemplation of their exterior appearance—as they first present themselves to our senses—we would do an injury to the majesty of God. It would be as if we judged or interpreted the inventory <note>original: "store"</note> of an excellent jeweler based only on what is displayed in the shop window facing the street. 

The second reason is because they provide a unique help and a safeguard <note>original: "preseruatiue"</note> against unbelief and error. For our Savior says, *You err, not knowing the Scriptures, nor the power of God* <note>Matthew 22:29</note>. He places before us two books or volumes to study if we wish to be protected from error: first, the Scriptures, which reveal the will of God; and second, the creatures <note>original: "creatures" — meaning the created world and all things in nature</note>, which express His power. The latter is a key to the former. It does not only open our understanding to conceive the true sense of the Scriptures by the general concepts of reason and the rules of speech, but it primarily opens our belief by drawing us into a proper meditation on the omnipotence of God, which is essentially signed and engraved upon His works. This is enough, therefore, for divine testimony and evidence concerning the true dignity and value of learning.

As for human proofs, it is such a large field that, in a discourse of this nature and brevity, it is more appropriate to select only those things which we shall produce...

<meta>This page continues the comparison between different types of benefits provided by learning, specifically contrasting temporary, localized achievements with universal, lasting ones.</meta>

->Of the Advancement of Learning<-

season, and for a specific area of ground where they fall. But the other kind of benefit is indeed like the blessings of heaven, which are permanent and universal. The former type of merit is mixed with conflict and disturbance, but the latter has the true character of a divine presence, coming in a "gentle breeze" <note>original: *aura leni*</note>, without noise or agitation.

Certainly, that other merit of learning—repressing the troubles that arise between people—is not much inferior to the first merit, which is relieving the hardships that arise from nature. This merit was vividly illustrated by the ancients in the fictional story of *Orpheus’s Theater*, where all beasts and birds assembled. Forgetting their individual appetites—some for prey, some for sport, some for fighting—they all stood sociably together listening to the melodies and harmonies of the *harp*. However, as soon as that sound ceased or was drowned out by some louder noise, every beast returned to its own nature. This aptly describes the nature and condition of human beings, who are full of savage and untamed desires for profit, lust, and revenge. As long as they listen to moral precepts, laws, and religion—when these are sweetly touched by the eloquence and persuasion of books, sermons, and formal speeches—society and peace are maintained. But if these instruments fall silent, or if sedition and tumult make them inaudible, all things dissolve into anarchy and confusion.

# The first Booke.

other governors in commonwealths and popular states <note>original: "popular Estates," referring to republics or representative governments</note> are endowed with learning. For although he might be thought partial to his own profession who said, *“Then people and states would be happy when either kings were philosophers or philosophers were kings,”* <note>Bacon refers here to a famous sentiment from Plato’s *Republic*.</note> yet experience verifies that the best times have always existed under learned princes and governors. For however kings may have their imperfections in their passions and habits, if they are illuminated by learning, they possess those notions of religion, policy, and morality which preserve them and restrain them from all ruinous and destructive <note>original: "peremptory," meaning absolute or final, often in a negative sense here</note> errors and excesses. Learning whispers constantly in their ears when counselors and servants stand mute and silent.

Likewise, senators or counselors who are learned proceed upon safer and more substantial principles than counselors who are merely men of experience. The learned sort keep dangers far away, whereas the others do not discover them until they are close at hand; and then they must trust to the agility of their wit to ward them off or avoid them.

This prosperity <note>original: "felicitie"</note> of the times under learned princes—to remain brief by using only the most eminent and selected examples—is most apparent in the age that passed from the death of the Emperor **Domitian** until the reign of **Commodus**. This period included a succession of six sovereigns, all of whom were learned or were exceptional supporters and promoters of learning. In terms of worldly matters, this age was

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**Antoninus Pius**, who succeeded him, was an excellently learned Prince and possessed the patient and subtle wit of a scholar <note>original: "Schoole man," referring to the detailed logic of medieval Scholasticism</note>. In common speech (which leaves no virtue uncriticized), he was called *Cymini Sector* <note>original: *Cymini Sector*, meaning "a divider of cumin seed"</note>—a carver or a divider of cumin seed, which is one of the smallest seeds. He had such patience and a settled spirit that he could examine the smallest and most exact differences in legal cases. This was undoubtedly a fruit of his mind's exceeding tranquility and serenity. Because his mind was in no way burdened or encumbered by fears, remorses, or doubts—and having been noted as a man of the purest goodness, without any fiction or affectation, who has ever reigned or lived—his mind remained continually present and whole. He also approached a degree nearer to Christianity and became, as **Agrippa** said to **Saint Paul**, *half a Christian* <note>A reference to Acts 26:28, "Almost thou persuadest me to be a Christian"</note>. He held their religion and law in high regard, not only ending their persecution but also allowing for the advancement of Christians.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He was succeeded by the first *divine brothers* <note>original: *Diui fratres*</note>, the two adopted brothers: **Lucius Commodus Verus**, son of **Aelius Verus**, who delighted much in the "softer" kinds of learning <note>such as poetry and rhetoric</note> and was accustomed to calling the poet **Martial** his "**Virgil**"; and **Marcus Aurelius Antoninus**. The latter, who outshone his colleague and survived him by many years, was named "The Philosopher." Just as he excelled all the rest in learning, he also excelled them in the perfection of all royal virtues...

...virtues: so much so that **Julian** the Emperor, in his book titled *The Caesars*—which was a lampoon <note>original: "Pasquill," a satirical piece of writing</note> or satire written to mock all his predecessors—imagined that they were all invited to a banquet of the Gods. **Silenus** the jester sat at the lower end of the table and offered a mocking remark to everyone as they entered. But when **Marcus the Philosopher** <note>Marcus Aurelius</note> came in, Silenus was stumped <note>original: "grauelled"</note> and embarrassed, not knowing how to criticize him; except that, at the last moment, he made a passing remark about Marcus’s patience toward his wife <note>A reference to the rumors of infidelity regarding Empress Faustina the Younger</note>. 

The virtue of this Prince, combined with that of his predecessor, made the name of **Antoninus** so sacred in the world that even though it was extremely dishonored by **Commodus**, **Caracalla**, and **Heliogabalus** (who all bore the name), the reputation of the name endured. When **Alexander Severus** refused the name because he was a stranger to the family, the Senate cried out in unison, "As Augustus was, so also let Antoninus be" <note>original: *Quomodo Augustus sic & Antoninus*</note>. The names of these two Princes were held in such renown and veneration in those days that the Senate wanted the name to be a permanent addition to the titles of all future Emperors. 

During this Emperor’s time, the Church was also largely at peace. Thus, in this sequence of six princes <note>Referring to the era of the "Five Good Emperors" plus Lucius Verus</note>, we see the blessed effects of learning upon sovereign rule, depicted on the grandest stage <note>original: "Table"</note> of the world.

However, for a smaller portrait or depiction (not presuming to speak of Your Majesty while you are still living), the most excellent example in my judgment is that of **Queen Elizabeth**, your immediate predecessor in this part of **Britain**. She was a ruler of such quality that if **Plutarch** <note>A famous Greek biographer known for comparing the lives of great Greeks and Romans</note> were still alive to write biographies...

<meta>This page transitions from discussing the Roman Emperors to a glowing tribute to Queen Elizabeth I, using her as a modern example of a ruler whose governance was perfected by learning.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>
# The first book.

If Plutarch were alive today to write biographies by parallels <note>original: "lynes by parallells," a reference to Plutarch’s *Parallel Lives*, which compared famous Greeks and Romans</note>, I think it would trouble him to find a parallel for her among women. This Lady was endowed with learning that was unique for her sex, and great even when compared to male Princes, whether we speak of learning in languages, or in modern or ancient science, in Divinity <note>theology</note> or the Humanities. And until the very last year of her life, she was accustomed to appoint set hours for reading; scarcely any young student in a university studied more daily or more diligently.

As for her government, I am certain I shall not exaggerate if I affirm that this part of the island never had forty-five years of better times. Yet this was not due to the calmness of the season, but through the wisdom of her rule <note>original: "regimēt"</note>. 

For consider, on the one hand, the truth of religion established; the constant peace and security; the good administration of Justice; the temperate use of the prerogative <note>the sovereign's special powers or rights</note>, which was neither loosened nor overly strained; the flourishing state of learning, suitable for such an excellent patron; the stable state of wealth and resources, both of the Crown and the subject; the habit of obedience, and the moderation of discontent. 

Then consider, on the other hand, the religious differences; the troubles of neighboring countries; the ambition of **Spain** and the opposition of **Rome**; and finally, that she was solitary and independent <note>original: "of her selfe," referring to her status as a sovereign who ruled without a husband or co-regent</note>. 

Considering these things, I say, just as I could not have chosen an example so recent and so appropriate, I also believe I could not have chosen one more remarkable or eminent for the purpose now in hand...

[The matter] currently being discussed; which concerns the union of learning in the Prince with the happiness of the people. Learning does not only influence and operate upon civil merit and moral virtue, or the arts and character <note>original: "temperature," meaning the balanced state or constitution</note> of peace and peaceful government; it also has no less power and effectiveness in enabling martial and military virtue and prowess. This may be notably seen in the examples of **Alexander** the Great and **Caesar** the Dictator mentioned before, but now in a proper place to be resumed. There is no need for a note or recital of their virtues and acts in war, as they have been the wonders of time in that regard. 

But regarding their love for learning and their own excellence in it, it is relevant to say something. **Alexander** was raised and taught under **Aristotle**, the great philosopher, who dedicated several of his books of philosophy to him. He was accompanied by **Callisthenes** <note>a Greek historian and Aristotle's great-nephew</note> and various other learned people who followed him in the military camp throughout his journeys and conquests. The high value and esteem in which he held learning appears notably in these three particulars: 

First, in the envy he used to express toward **Achilles**—specifically, that he had such a good herald <note>original: "Trumpet"</note> of his praises as **Homer’s** verses. 

Secondly, in the judgment or decision he gave concerning that precious casket <note>original: "Cabinet"</note> of **Darius** which was found among his jewels. When the question was asked what item was worthy to be kept in it, he gave his opinion that it should be **Homer’s** works.

Observe again that speech which was usual for him: *that he felt his mortality chiefly in two things—sleep and lust.* See if it were not a speech extracted from the depths of natural philosophy, and more likely to have come from the mouth of *Aristotle* or *Democritus* than from *Alexander*.

See again that speech of humanity and poetry: when his wounds were bleeding, he called to him one of his flatterers who was accustomed to ascribing divine honor to him and said, *“Look, this is real blood; this is not the kind of liquid <note>original: "liquor," referring to ichor, the ethereal fluid that flows in the veins of gods</note> that Homer speaks of, which flowed from Venus’s hand when it was pierced by Diomedes.”*

See likewise his readiness in criticizing <note>original: "reprehension"</note> logic in the speech he used to *Cassander* regarding a complaint made against Cassander's father, *Antipater*. For when *Alexander* happened to say, *“Do you think these men would have come from so far to complain unless they had a just cause for grief?”* and *Cassander* answered, *“Yes, that was the reason: because they thought they would not be proven wrong <note>original: "disproved"</note>,”* *Alexander* said laughing: *“See the subtleties of Aristotle—taking a matter both ways, for and against <note>original: "Pro & Contra"</note>, etc.”*

But note again how well he could use the same art that he criticized to serve his own purposes <note>original: "humor"</note>. While holding a secret grudge against *Callisthenes* because he opposed the new ceremony of bowing to the king as a god <note>original: "adoration"</note>, he was feasting one night where *Callisthenes* was at the table. For when one of *Antipater's* friends praised him to *Alexander* for his moderation—noting that he did not fall into *Persian* pride like the other lieutenants by wearing purple robes, but kept the ancient *Macedonian* custom of wearing black—*Alexander* replied, *"True, but Antipater is all purple on the inside."* <note>Purple was the color of imperial power and luxury; Alexander suggests that while Antipater appears humble, he is inwardly full of royal ambition and arrogance.</note>

Or take that other example, when *Parmenio* came to him on the plain of *Arbela* <note>The site of the Battle of Gaugamela, 331 BCE</note> and showed him the vast multitude of his enemies, especially as they appeared by the infinite number of torches—as if there were a new sky full of stars. *Parmenio* advised him to attack them by night, to which *Alexander* answered that *he would not steal the victory.*

# Of the Advancement of Learning

...said, *"That the one loved Alexander, and the other loved the King"*; describing the principal difference between a prince's best servants: that some, out of affection, love the person, and others, out of duty, love the crown.

Consider also that excellent criticism <note>original: "taxation"</note> of an error common among the counselors of princes: that they advise their masters according to the model of their own minds and fortunes, and not those of their masters. When, upon *Darius's* great offers of peace, *Parmenio* had said, *"Surely, I would accept these offers if I were Alexander"*; *Alexander* replied, *"So would I, if I were Parmenio."*

Lastly, consider that quick and witty reply which he made when he gave such large gifts to his friends and servants. When he was asked what he reserved for himself, he answered, *"Hope."* Consider, I say, whether he had not calculated his accounts correctly, because *Hope* must be the portion of all who resolve to undertake great enterprises. For this was *Caesar's* portion when he first went into *Gaul*, his personal estate being then utterly depleted by his generous spending <note>original: "Largesses"</note>. And this was likewise the portion of that noble prince (however much he was carried away by ambition), *Henry*, Duke of *Guise*, of whom it was commonly said that he was the greatest usurer in *France*, because he had turned all his estate into obligations <note>By giving away his wealth and lands, he made the recipients "obliged" or indebted to him for future political support.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Alexander the Great's intellectual virtues and transitions into an analysis of Julius Caesar’s learning, specifically focusing on his literary and scientific achievements.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>

...steps of learning in those few speeches that are reported of this prince. The admiration of him, when I consider him not as *Alexander the Great* but as *Aristotle’s scholar*, has carried me too far.

As for *Julius Caesar*, the excellence of his learning does not need to be proved by his education, his company, or his speeches; rather, it reveals itself to a further degree in his writings and works, some of which survive today, while others have unfortunately perished. For first, we see that we are left with that excellent history of his own wars, which he titled only a *Commentary*. In this work, all succeeding ages have admired the solid weight of the matter, the realistic passages, and the vivid images of actions and persons, expressed with the greatest propriety <note>Appropriateness and fitness of style.</note> of words and clarity of narration that ever existed. 

That this was not the result of a natural gift, but of learning and instruction, is well-witnessed by that work of his titled *On Analogy* <note>original: *De Analogia*</note>. This was a work of grammatical philosophy in which he labored to make "language as a matter of choice" <note>original: *vox ad placitum*</note> become "language as a matter of law" <note>original: *vox ad licitum*</note>. He sought to reduce the common custom of speech to the consistency of speech, drawing, as it were, the images of words from the life of reason. 

So we receive from him, as a monument both of his power and learning, the then-reformed calculation of the year <note>The Julian calendar, which Caesar introduced to align the calendar year with the solar year.</note>. This well expresses that he believed it to be as great a glory for himself to observe and know the laws of the heavens as to give laws to men upon the earth.

<summary>Bacon concludes his praise of Alexander the Great’s intellect and begins an evaluation of Julius Caesar, highlighting Caesar’s "Commentaries," his grammatical treatise "De Analogia," and his reform of the calendar as evidence of his immense learning.</summary>
<keywords>Alexander the Great, Julius Caesar, Aristotle, De Analogia, Commentaries, Julian calendar, grammar, linguistics, Roman history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Julius Caesar’s intellectual achievements, moving from his scientific work on the calendar to his literary conflicts and his mastery of rhetoric.</meta>

->Of the advancement of learning<-

So likewise in his book titled *Anticato* <note>original: *Anticato*; a lost work by Caesar written as a response to Cicero’s eulogy for Cato the Younger, Caesar’s political enemy.</note>, it easily appears that he aspired as much to a victory of wit as to a victory of war. In that work, he undertook a conflict with the pen against the greatest champion then living, Cicero the Orator.

So again, in his book of *Apothegms* <note>A collection of concise, pithy sayings or remarks.</note> which he collected, we see that he considered it more an honor to make himself merely a pair of "writing tablets" to record the wise and pithy words of others, than to have every word of his own treated as an apothegm or an oracle—as vain princes, through the custom of flattery, pretend to do. And yet, if I were to list several of his own speeches (as I did those of Alexander), they are truly like those which Solomon describes when he says: "The words of the wise are like goads and like nails firmly fixed" <note>original: *Verba sapientum tanquam aculei, & tanquam claui in altum defixi*; a reference to Ecclesiastes 12:11.</note>. Of these, I will recite only three, which are notable not just for their elegant style, but for their admirable vigor and effectiveness.

First, it is reasonable that he be considered a master of words who could appease a mutiny in his army with a single word. This happened as follows: When Roman generals spoke to their army, they used the word *Soldiers* <note>original: *Milites*</note>; but when the magistrates spoke to the people, they used the word *Citizens* <note>original: *Quirites*</note>. The soldiers were in a state of tumult and seditiously begged to be cashiered <term>cashiered: dismissed from military service</term>. They did not actually mean this, but used the demand as a complaint to pressure Caesar into better conditions. Because he was resolute and refused to give way, after a period of silence, he began his speech with the words, "I, Citizens" <note>original: *Ego Quirites*</note>. By using this address, he signaled that they were already dismissed from service; where—

## The first book.

...with which they were so surprised, thwarted, and confused that they would not allow him to continue his speech, but gave up their demands and pleaded to be called by the name of *Soldiers* <note>original: *Milites*. By calling them "Citizens" on the previous page, Caesar had effectively dismissed them from his service, which shamed them into loyalty.</note> once again.

The second speech was this: Caesar greatly desired the title of King. Some people were instigated to salute him as king with popular cheers as he passed by; however, finding the acclaim weak and meager, he brushed it off with a kind of joke, as if they had mistaken his surname: "I am not King, but Caesar" <note>original: *Non Rex sum, sed Cæsar*</note>. If this speech is examined, its vitality and depth can scarcely be fully expressed. First, it was a refusal of the title, yet not a serious one. Second, it signaled an infinite confidence and magnanimity, as if he presumed *Caesar* was the greater title—which, through his excellence, has proven true to this day. But chiefly, it was a speech of great allure toward his own purpose, as if the State were struggling with him only over a name that even ordinary families possessed; for *Rex* (King) was a surname among the Romans, just as *King* is a surname among us.

The last speech which I will mention was directed at Metellus. When Caesar, after war was declared, took possession of the city of Rome, he entered the inner treasury to take the money accumulated there. Metellus, being a Tribune <note>An elected official who represented the plebeians and had the power to veto government actions.</note>, forbade him. To this, Caesar said, "If you do not stop, I will strike you dead on the spot." And immediately checking himself, he added, "Young man, it is harder for me to say it,"

But to return and conclude with Caesar, it is evident that he himself knew well his own perfection in learning and took pride in it. This appeared when, on one occasion, some people spoke of what a strange resolution it was for *Lucius Sylla* to resign his *dictatorship* <note>original: *Dictature*. Lucius Cornelius Sulla (Sylla) was a Roman general who unexpectedly resigned from absolute power in 79 BC.</note>. Caesar, scoffing at him for his own advantage, answered: *That Sylla did not know his letters, and therefore did not know how to dictate.* <note>Caesar makes a pun here: "Letters" refers to being learned or literate. He mocks Sylla by saying that because he was uneducated, he literally didn't know how to "dictate" text, and thus didn't know how to be a "dictator."</note>

And here it would be appropriate to leave this point concerning the harmony of military virtue and learning (for what example could follow with any grace after those two of *Alexander* and *Caesar*?), were it not for the rarity of circumstance that I find in one other particular case. It is a story that passed so suddenly from extreme scorn to extreme wonder: that of *Xenophon* the *Philosopher*. He went from the school of *Socrates* into *Asia* during the expedition of *Cyrus the Younger* against King *Artaxerxes*. This *Xenophon* at that time was very young and had never seen war before; neither did he have any command in the army, but only followed the war as a volunteer for the love and fellowship <note>original: *conuersation*. In this period, "conversation" meant social intimacy and the act of living among others.</note> of his friend *Proxenus*. He was present when *Falinus* came with a message from the Great King to the Greeks after *Cyrus* had been slain in the field, leaving them a mere handful of men left to themselves.

It takes away the wildness, barbarism, and fierceness of men's minds; but indeed, the emphasis needs to be upon the word *faithfully* <note>original: *fideliter*</note>. For a little superficial learning produces the opposite effect. It takes away all levity, rashness <note>original: "temeritie"</note>, and insolence by providing a wealth of doubts and difficulties, and by acquainting the mind with balancing reasons on both sides. It teaches the mind to reject its first impressions and notions <note>original: "conceits"</note> and to accept nothing except what has been examined and tested. It takes away the vain admiration of anything, which is the root of all weakness. For all things are admired either because they are new or because they are great. 

Regarding novelty: no man who wades deeply into learning or contemplation will fail to find it printed in his heart that *there is nothing new under the sun* <note>original: *Nil noui super terram*</note>. Neither can any man marvel at a puppet show if he goes behind the curtain and carefully observes the mechanics <note>original: "the Motion"</note>. 

And as for magnitude: consider Alexander the Great. After he was used to commanding great armies and making great conquests of the spacious provinces in Asia, he received letters from Greece about some fights and military actions there (which were usually over a mountain pass, a fort, or a walled town at most). <meta>This page continues the discussion on how learning improves the human mind and character, specifically focusing on the "reformation" of the self and the transition from discussing moral virtue to the nature of power and authority.</meta>

Therefore, I will conclude with that which includes the essence of the whole <note>original Latin: *rationem totius*</note>: namely, that learning shapes the character of the mind so that it is not fixed or settled in its own defects, but remains capable and open to growth and improvement. For the unlearned man does not know what it is to look deep within himself or to hold himself accountable, nor does he know the pleasure of that *most pleasant life: to feel oneself becoming better every day.* <note>original Latin: *Suavissima vita, indies sentire se fieri meliorem*</note> 

He will learn to show off the good qualities he has to the fullest and use them skillfully, but he will not do much to increase them. He will learn how to hide and disguise the faults he has, but not how to correct them; he is like a poor mower who keeps on mowing but never stops to sharpen his scythe. 

In contrast, it is different for the learned man; he constantly mixes the correction and improvement of his mind with the use and exercise of it. Furthermore, in general and in summary, it is certain that **Truth** <note>original Latin: *Veritas*</note> and **Goodness** <note>original Latin: *Bonitas*</note> differ only as the seal and the wax impression: for Truth leaves the mark of Goodness, while the clouds of error are what descend in the storms of passions and mental distress <note>original: "perturbations"</note>.

From moral virtue, let us pass on to the matter of power and authority <note>original: "commandment"</note>, and consider whether, by right reason, there is any power comparable to that which knowledge grants to and crowns within human nature. --- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between various forms of human authority and the superior power of knowledge, moving from the control of bodies and actions to the control of the human mind and will.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
# The First Book

The authority <note>original: "commandement"</note> that herdsmen have over their flocks is a contemptible thing; to have authority over children, as schoolmasters do, is a matter of small honor; to have authority over galley slaves is a disgrace rather than an honor. Neither is the power of tyrants over people who have lost the nobility <note>original: "Generositie"</note> of their minds much better. 

Therefore, it was always held that honors in free monarchies and commonwealths had a greater sweetness than in tyrannies, because authority extends more over the wills of men, and not only over their actions and service. And so, when Virgil sets out to attribute the greatest of human honors to Augustus Caesar, he does so in these words:

> ->*And as victor, he gives laws to a willing people,*<-
> ->*and paves his way to heaven.*<- <note>original Latin: *Victorque volentes / Per populos, dat iura, viamque affectat Olympo*</note>

But yet the authority of knowledge is even higher than the authority over the will; for it is a command over the reason, belief, and understanding of man, which is the highest part of the mind and gives laws to the will itself. For there is no power on earth that sets up a throne or seat of state <note>original: "chaire of Estate"</note> in the spirits and souls of men—and in their thoughts <note>original: "cogitations"</note>, imaginations, opinions, and beliefs—except for knowledge and learning. 

Therefore, we see the detestable and extreme pleasure that arch-heretics, false prophets, and impostors are overcome <note>original: "transported"</note> with, when they once find within themselves that they have gained a superiority over the faith...

<summary>Bacon argues that authority over the human mind through knowledge is superior to any political or physical power, as it governs the reason and will rather than just outward actions.</summary>
<keywords>authority, knowledge, Virgil, Augustus Caesar, power, reason, will, learning, tyranny</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues Bacon’s argument regarding the power of knowledge over the human mind, transitioning from the dangers of spiritual delusion to the practical and intellectual rewards of learning.</meta>

and the consciences of men. This power is so great that once they have tasted it, it is seldom seen that any torture or persecution can make them relinquish or abandon it. But just as this <note>referring to the power of false prophets mentioned on the previous page</note> is what the Author of Revelation calls the "depths" or "profoundness of Satan," so, by the argument of opposites, the just and lawful sovereignty over the understanding of men—achieved through the appearance of truth rightly interpreted—is that which comes closest to the likeness of divine rule.

As for fortune and advancement, the benefits of learning are not so confined to bringing success only to states and commonwealths that they do not also bring success to private individuals. For it was well noted long ago that *Homer* has provided more men with their livelihoods than either *Sulla*, *Caesar*, or *Augustus* ever did, despite their great bounties <note>original: "largesses"</note>, donations, and distributions of land to so many legions. And no doubt, it is hard to say whether military arms or learning has advanced a greater number of people. In the case of sovereignty, we see that if arms or hereditary descent have secured the *Kingdom*, yet learning has secured the *Priesthood*, which has always been in some competition with the *Empire*.

Furthermore, regarding the pleasure and delight of knowledge and learning, it far surpasses all others in nature. For if the pleasures of the emotions <note>original: "affections"</note> so exceed those of the senses—just as achieving a desire or a victory exceeds a song or a dinner—then must it not follow that the pleasures of the intel- <meta>The text continues as "intellect" on the next page; the catchword is "lect"</meta>

<summary>Bacon argues that the intellectual authority of truth is the closest human equivalent to divine rule and highlights how learning provides greater livelihood and status than even the greatest military leaders.</summary>
<keywords>Sovereignty of the Mind, Revelation, Homer, Sulla, Caesar, Augustus, Priesthood vs Empire, Intellectual Pleasure</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the supreme pleasure of the intellect over the emotions, which began on the previous page with the syllable "intel-".</meta>

# The First Book

lect or understanding exceed the pleasures of the emotions <note>original: "affections"</note>? We see that in all other pleasures, there is satiety <note>state of being overfilled or bored</note>; and after they are experienced, their freshness <note>original: "verdour"</note> fades. This shows clearly that they are but deceptive pleasures and not true pleasures, and that it was the novelty which pleased, and not the quality. Therefore, we see that men of pleasure <note>original: "voluptuous men"</note> often become monks <note>original: "Friers"</note>, and ambitious princes become melancholy. But with knowledge there is no satiety; instead, satisfaction and appetite are perpetually interchangeable. Therefore, knowledge appears to be good in itself simply, without deception or coincidence <note>original: "fallacy or accident"</note>. Nor is that pleasure of small effect or contentment to the mind of man which the poet *Lucretius* describes elegantly:

->*It is pleasant, when the winds disturb the waters on the great sea... etc.* <note>original: "Suave mari magno, turbantibus æquora ventis: &c."</note><-

*It is a delightful view (he says) to stand or walk along the shore and see a ship tossed by a storm at sea; or to be in a fortified tower and see two armies join in battle on a plain. But it is an incomparable pleasure for the mind of man to be settled, landed, and fortified in the certainty of truth; and from there to observe and behold the errors, disturbances, labors, and wanderings up and down of other men.*

Lastly, leaving aside the common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> arguments—that through learning, one man excels another in that very way in which man excels beasts; that by learning man ascends to the heavens and their movements, where in his body he cannot go; and the like—let us conclude with the dignity and excellence of knowledge and learning in that toward which man’s nature most aspires.

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the superiority of knowledge, specifically focusing on its "illumination" which grants a form of immortality to the human spirit, contrasting the fleeting nature of physical monuments with the enduring nature of written works.</meta>

Of the Advancement of Learning,

...illumination, <note>The previous page ends with "the...", and the catchword suggests "illumination" begins this section.</note> which is immortality or continuance; for to this end tends procreation <note>original: "generation"</note> and the raising of houses and families; to this end buildings, foundations, and monuments; to this tends the desire for memory, fame, and celebration; and in effect, the strength of all other human desires. We see, then, how much more durable the monuments of wit <note>In this context, "wit" refers to the intellect or creative genius.</note> and learning are than the monuments of power or of handiwork. For have not the verses of *Homer* continued for twenty-five hundred years or more without the loss of a syllable or letter, during which time countless <note>original: "infinite"</note> palaces, temples, castles, and cities have decayed and been demolished?

It is not possible to have the true pictures or statues of *Cyrus*, *Alexander*, or *Caesar*, nor even of the kings or great personages of much later years; for the originals cannot last, and the copies inevitably lose <note>original: "leeſe"</note> their life and truth. But the images of men’s wits and knowledge remain in books, exempted from the ravages <note>original: "wrong"</note> of time and capable of perpetual renewal <note>original: "renouation"</note>. Neither are they accurately called "images," because they continue to generate and cast their seeds in the minds of others, provoking and causing infinite actions and opinions in succeeding ages. 

So, if the invention of the ship was thought so noble—which carries riches and commodities from place to place and unites <note>original: "consociateth"</note> the most remote regions in the sharing of their fruits—how much more are letters to be magnified? Like ships, they pass through the vast seas of time and allow ages so distant to participate in the wisdom, illumination, and inventions, the one of the other. <note>The text likely continues onto the next page to finish this thought.</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that intellectual works (monuments of wit) are superior to physical monuments because they are immortal, remaining unchanged over millennia while buildings crumble and statues fade. Let this foundation therefore be laid: that all works are accomplished by the greatness <note>original: "amplitude"</note> of reward, by the soundness of direction, and by the joining together of labors. # Of the Advancement of Learning

Places appointed, such as universities, colleges, and schools, for the reception and support <note>original: "comforting"</note> of the same.

The works which concern the seats and places of learning are four: foundations and buildings; endowments with revenues; endowments with franchises <note>meaning legal rights and exemptions</note> and privileges; and institutions and ordinances for government—all tending to the quietness and privateness of life, and the discharge of cares and troubles. This is much like the stations which *Virgil* prescribes for the hiving of bees:

->*First, a seat and a station must be sought for the bees,*<-
->*where the winds may have no access, etc.*<-
<note>original: "Principio sedes Apibus, statioq; petenda: Quo neq; fit ventis aditus, &c." from Virgil's Georgics, Book IV.</note>

The works concerning books are two: first, libraries, which are as the shrines where all the relics of the ancient saints—full of true virtue, and that without delusion or imposture—are preserved and laid to rest. Secondly, new editions of authors, with more correct printings, more faithful translations, more useful glosses <note>explanatory comments</note>, more diligent annotations, and the like.

The works pertaining to the persons of learned men (besides the advancement and countenancing of them in general) are two: the reward and appointment <note>original: "designation"</note> of Readers <note>meaning lecturers or professors</note> in sciences already extant and invented; and the reward and appointment of writers and researchers <note>original: "Enquirers"</note> concerning any parts of learning not sufficiently labored and pursued.

# The Second Book

As for any particular commemorations, I recall what *Cicero* said when he gave general thanks: "It is difficult to omit anyone; it is ungrateful to pass over anyone" <note>original Latin: *Difficile non aliquem; ingratum quenquam præterire*</note>. Let us rather, according to the Scriptures, look toward that part of the race which is before us, rather than looking back at what has already been attained.

First, therefore, among so many great foundations of colleges in *Europe*, I find it strange that they are all dedicated to specific professions, and none are left free for the arts and sciences in general. For if men judge that learning should be applied to action, they judge well; but in this, they fall into the error described in the ancient fable <note>The Fable of the Belly, attributed to Aesop or Menenius Agrippa</note>, in which the other parts of the body supposed the stomach was idle because it neither performed the duty of motion, as the limbs do, nor of sense, as the head does. Yet, nonetheless, it is the stomach that digests and distributes nourishment to all the rest. 

Similarly, if any man thinks philosophy and universality <term>the study of general principles that apply across all fields of knowledge</term> are idle studies, he does not consider that all professions are served and supplied by them. And I take this to be a great cause that has hindered the progress of learning: these fundamental knowledges have been studied only in passing <note>original: "but in passage"</note>. For if you want a tree to bear more fruit than it has been accustomed to do, it is not anything you can do to the boughs that will work; rather, it is the stirring of the earth and the putting of new soil around the roots that must achieve it. # Of the Advancement of Learning

The lack of foundations and endowments <note>original: "Foundations and Dotations"</note> for professional learning has not only had a harmful <note>original: "malign"</note> influence on the growth of the sciences, but has also been prejudicial to states and governments. For from this it follows that Princes find a lack <note>original: "solitude"</note> of capable men to serve them in matters of state, because there is no collegiate education that is "free"—where those who were so inclined might devote themselves to history, modern languages, books of policy, and civil discourse, and other such preparations for state service.

And because founders of colleges "plant," and founders of lectures "water" <note>An allusion to 1 Corinthians 3:6: "I have planted, Apollos watered; but God gave the increase."</note>, it follows in due order to speak of the defect found in public lectures: namely, the smallness and meanness of the salary or reward which in most places is assigned to them, whether they be lectures in the Arts or the Professions. For it is necessary for the progress of the sciences that "Readers" <term>lecturers or professors</term> be the most capable and sufficient men, as those who are ordained for the generating and propagating of sciences, and not for temporary use. This cannot be achieved unless their condition and endowment are such that they satisfy the most capable man, allowing him to dedicate his whole labor and continue his whole life in that function and service. Therefore, the reward must be proportional to that moderate or adequate level of advancement <note>original: "mediocrity or competency of advancement"</note> which might be expected from a profession, or the actual practice of a profession. So, if you will have sciences flourish, you must observe **David's** military law...

## The Second Book

...military law, which was, *that those who stayed with the baggage should have an equal share with those who were in the action*; otherwise, the baggage will be poorly guarded. Similarly, lecturers in the sciences are indeed the guardians of the stores and provisions of knowledge from which men in active careers are supplied, and therefore they ought to receive equal support <note>original: "entertainment"</note>. Otherwise, if the "fathers" in the sciences are of the weakest sort or are poorly maintained:

->And the weak offspring will reflect the fasting of their fathers.<-
<note>original: "Et Patrum invalidi referent ieiunia nati." A paraphrased reference to Virgil’s *Georgics*, suggesting that if the parents (teachers) are malnourished or weak, the children (the sciences) will also be weak.</note>

Another defect I note—in which I shall need some alchemist to help me, as they call upon men to sell their books and build furnaces, abandoning and forsaking Minerva and the Muses as barren virgins and relying upon Vulcan <note>Minerva was the Roman goddess of wisdom; the Muses were the goddesses of the arts; Vulcan was the god of fire and metalworking. Bacon is using them to symbolize the shift from pure theory (books) to practical experimentation (the furnace).</note>. But it is certain that for the deep, fruitful, and effective <note>original: "operatiue"</note> study of many sciences—especially natural philosophy and medicine—books are not the only instruments. In this area, the generosity of men has not been entirely lacking; for we see that spheres, globes, astrolabes, maps, and the like have been provided as tools for astronomy and cosmography, as well as books. We see likewise that some places established for medicine have added the benefit of gardens for medicinal herbs <term>simples: medicinal plants or herbs used to create remedies</term> of all sorts, and likewise provide for the use of dead bodies for dissections <note>original: "Anatomyes"</note>. # Of the Advancement of Learning

...whether they are experiments belonging to **Vulcan** or **Daedalus** <note>original: "Vulcanus or Dedalus." Vulcan represents metallurgy and fire; Daedalus represents mechanical invention and engineering.</note>, the furnace or the engine, or any other kind. And therefore, just as the secretaries and spies of princes and states submit bills for intelligence, so you must allow the spies and intelligence agents of Nature to submit their bills; otherwise, you will be poorly informed <note>original: "aduertised"</note>.

And if **Alexander the Great** made such a generous <note>original: "liberall"</note> grant of treasure to **Aristotle** for the support of hunters, fowlers, fishermen, and the like, so that he might compile a *Natural History*, those who labor in the "arts of nature" <note>the practical and experimental sciences</note> deserve it even more.

Another defect which I note is a pause <note>original: "intermission"</note> or neglect of consultation by those who are governors in universities, and of visitation <note>official inspections or oversight</note> by princes or superior persons. They should take account and consider whether the lectures <note>original: "Readings"</note>, exercises, and other customs belonging to learning—established long ago and continued since—are well-instituted or not, and then base an amendment or reformation on what is found to be unsuitable <note>original: "inconuenient"</note>. For it is one of Your Majesty’s own most wise and princely maxims, *that in all customs and precedents, the times in which they first began should be considered; if those times were weak or ignorant, it detracts from the authority of the custom and leaves it suspect.* Therefore, since most of the customs and orders of the universities were derived from more obscure times <note>the medieval period</note>, it is all the more necessary that they be re-examined. In this regard, I will give an instance...

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s critique of university governance and customs. He is specifically addressing flaws in the curriculum and the methods of academic exercise, arguing that they are poorly timed and poorly structured for real-world application.</meta>

## The Second Book

...an instance or two for example's sake, of things that are the most obvious and familiar. 

The first is a matter which, though it is ancient and general, I still hold to be an error: that scholars in universities come too soon, and while they are too unripe, to the study of logic and rhetoric. These arts are better suited for graduates than for children and novices. For if these two are understood correctly, they are the gravest of sciences, being the "Arts of Arts"—one for judgment and the other for ornament. They provide the rules and directions for how to set forth and arrange subject matter. 

Therefore, for minds that are empty and unstocked <note>original: "vnfraught"</note> with matter, and which have not gathered what **Cicero** calls *Sylva* and *Supellex* <note>original: *Sylua* and *Supellex*. These Latin terms refer to a "forest" of raw materials and the "furniture" or equipment of knowledge—the store of facts and ideas an orator needs before they can speak eloquently.</note>—raw material and variety—to begin with those arts is like trying to learn how to weigh, or measure, or paint the wind. It produces only this effect: that the wisdom of those arts, which is great and universal, is made almost contemptible, and has degenerated into childish sophistry and ridiculous affectation. Furthermore, the untimely learning of these subjects has consequently led to the superficial and unprofitable teaching and writing of them, in a way that is indeed suited to the capacity of children.

Another defect I find is in the exercises used in the universities, which create too great a divorce between "invention" <note>In a rhetorical sense, "invention" refers to the process of finding or creating arguments and subject matter.</note> and memory. Their speeches are either entirely premeditated in *Verbis conceptis* <note>Latin: "in formal, prescribed words"—meaning the speech is written out beforehand and recited from a script.</note>, where nothing is left to invention, or they are merely extemporaneous, where little is left to memory. However, in actual life and action, there is the least use for either of these extremes. Instead, what is needed is a mixture of premeditation and invention, of notes and memory.

<meta>Catchword: So</meta>

<summary>Bacon argues that teaching logic and rhetoric to young, inexperienced students is a mistake, as they lack the "raw material" of knowledge to apply these arts effectively. He also critiques university exercises for failing to balance preparation with spontaneous thinking.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon’s critique of the deficiencies in university education. He previously argued that the timing of certain studies (like logic and rhetoric) is premature and that academic exercises are too disconnected from real-world application.</meta>

So, because the exercise does not fit the practice, nor the image fit the life; and it is always a true rule in exercises that they should be framed as closely as possible to the reality <note>original: "life"</note> of practice. Otherwise, they pervert the motions and faculties of the mind rather than preparing them. The truth of this is clear when scholars enter professional practices or other activities of civic life; when they begin these, this deficiency is soon noticed by themselves, and even sooner by others. 

Regarding this section concerning the improvement of the institutions and regulations of universities, I will conclude with a clause from **Caesar’s** letter to *Oppius* and *Balbus*: "As to how this might be done, some ideas occur to me, and many more can be found; regarding these matters, I ask you to take them into your consideration." <note>original: *Hoc quemadmodum fieri possit, nonnulla mihi in mētē veniant, & multa reperiri possunt: de ijs rebus rogo vos, vt cogitationem suscipiatis.*</note>

Another defect I note rises a little higher than the previous one. For just as the progress <note>original: "proficience"</note> of learning depends much on the regulations and institutions of universities within specific states and kingdoms, so it would be even further advanced if there were more mutual intelligence and communication <note>original: "Intelligēce Mutual"</note> between the universities of *Europe* than there is now. We see that there are many religious orders and foundations which, although they are divided under different sovereignties and territories, still consider themselves to have a kind of contract, fraternity, and correspondence with one another, so much so that they have provincial and general leaders <note>Bacon refers here to international religious orders like the Jesuits, who were governed by "Provincials" and "Generals" across borders.</note>. 

And surely, just as nature creates brotherhood in families, and the mechanical arts establish brotherhoods within guilds <note>original: "communalties"</note>, and the anointing of God [superinduceth...]

<summary>Bacon argues that university exercises must mirror real-world practice to be effective and calls for greater international cooperation and communication between the universities of Europe.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon’s discussion on the institutional requirements for advancing knowledge. <page-num>6</page-num>

...introduces a brotherhood among kings and bishops. In the same way, there must be a fraternity in learning and enlightenment <note>original: "illumination"</note> relating to that Fatherhood <note>original: "Paternity"</note> attributed to God, who is called the "Father of lights" <note>original: "Father of illuminations or lights"; a reference to James 1:17</note>.

The last defect that *I* will note is that there has not been (or has only very rarely been) any public appointment <note>original: "Designation"</note> of writers or researchers <note>original: "Enquirers"</note> to focus on those areas of knowledge that do not appear to have been sufficiently worked on or undertaken yet. To achieve this, it is helpful <note>original: "an Inducement"</note> to survey and examine which branches of learning have been advanced and which have been neglected. Often, the false belief that we have plenty is a cause of our actual poverty. The huge quantity of books creates an appearance of surplus rather than a lack. However, this overabundance <note>original: "surcharge"</note> is not solved by stopping the production of books, but by producing *better* books. These, like the serpent of **Moses**, might devour the serpents of the enchanters <note>original: "Inchantors"; referring to the biblical story in Exodus 7 where Aaron’s rod, acting for Moses, becomes a serpent and swallows the serpents created by Pharaoh’s magicians</note>.

Fixing all the defects I have listed (except for the last one) and managing the practical side of that last one—the actual appointment of writers—are "Royal Works" <note>original: *Opera Basilica*; projects requiring the power and resources of a monarch</note>. Toward these goals, the efforts of a private individual may be like a statue at a crossroads: it can point toward the path but cannot walk it. But the preparatory part of the latter—the survey of learning—can be advanced by private labor <note>original: "travaile"</note>. Therefore, I will now attempt to make a general and faithful tour <note>original: "perambulation"</note> of learning, inquiring which parts of it remain uncultivated <note>original: "lie fresh"</note> and...

<summary>Bacon argues that the greatest obstacle to the advancement of knowledge is the lack of a systematic survey identifying what we do not yet know. He describes the creation of such a survey as a task he can begin as a private citizen, whereas the actual funding of researchers is a "Royal Work."</summary>

In this, nevertheless, my purpose at this time is to note only omissions and deficiencies, and not to make any refutation <note>original: "redargution"</note> of errors or incomplete prosecutions <note>original: "prosecutions"; the carrying out or following through of a study</note>. For it is one thing to set forth what ground lies uncultivated <note>original: "vnmanured"</note>, and another thing to correct ill management <note>original: "husbandry"</note> in that which is cultivated.

In the handling and undertaking of which work, I am not ignorant of what it is that I do now move and attempt, nor insensitive of my own weakness to sustain my purpose. But my hope is that if my extreme love of learning carries me too far, I may obtain the excuse of affection; for **it is not granted to man to love and to be wise** <note>original: "It is not granted to man to loue, and to bee wise"; a common proverb derived from the Latin: *Amare et sapere vix Deo conceditur*</note>. But I know well I can use no other liberty of judgment than I must leave to others, and I for my part shall be equally <note>original: "indifferentlie"</note> glad either to perform it myself or accept from another that duty of humanity: **For he who kindly shows the way to one who is wandering, etc.** <note>original: "Nam qui erranti comiter monstrat viam: &c."; a quote from the Roman poet Ennius, cited by Cicero in *De Officiis*</note>. <meta>This page continues Bacon’s discussion on the feasibility of advancing knowledge. He transitions from addressing the potential difficulty of the task to establishing his famous tripartite division of human and divine learning based on the faculties of the mind.</meta>

<header>The Second Book.</header>

<sig>Bb 3</sig>

Regarding the last point, concerning impossibility, I believe those things should be considered possible which can be performed by some person, though not by everyone; and which can be done by many together, though not by any single individual; and which can be accomplished over a succession of ages, though not within the "hourglass" <note>original: "houre-glasse"; a metaphor for the limited span of a single lifetime</note> of one man’s life; and which can be achieved by public appointment <note>original: "publique designation"</note>, though not by private effort. 

Nevertheless, if any man prefers to apply to himself that saying of Solomon <note>original: "Salomon"</note>, **"The lazy man says, 'There is a lion in the path,'"** <note>Latin original: *Dicit piger, Leo est in via*; from Proverbs 26:13</note> rather than that of Virgil, **"They are able, because they think they are able,"** <note>Latin original: *Possunt, quia posse videntur*; from the *Aeneid*</note> I shall be satisfied if my labors are regarded as nothing more than a better sort of wish. For just as it requires some knowledge to ask a relevant <note>original: "not impertinent"</note> question, it also requires some sense to make a wish that is not absurd.

THE PARTS of human learning relate to the three parts of man’s understanding, which is the seat of learning: **HISTORY** to his **MEMORY**, **POETRY** <note>original: "POESIE"</note> to his **IMAGINATION**, and **PHILOSOPHY** to his **REASON**. 

Divine learning follows the same distribution, for the spirit of man is the same, even though the source of revelation—whether by divine oracle or through the senses—is different. Thus, Theology also consists of the **HISTORY** of the Church; of **PARABLES**, which is Divine Poetry; and of holy **DOCTRINE** or Precept. As for that part which seems extra <note>original: "supernumerarie"</note>, namely **PROPHECY**, it is nothing but Divine History, which has this advantage over human history: the narration can be written before the event occurs just as well as after it.

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s division of learning. Having established that History corresponds to the faculty of Memory, he now subdivides History into four specific branches: Natural, Civil, Ecclesiastical, and Literary.</meta>

<header>Of the advancement of learning</header>

<sig>D 4</sig>

<margin>History of Learning <note>original: *Historia Literarum*</note></margin>
HISTORY IS NATURAL, CIVIL, ECCLESIASTICAL, and LITERARY. Of these, I recognize the first three as existing <note>original: "extant"</note>, but I note the fourth as lacking <note>original: "deficient"</note>. For no man has proposed to himself that the general state of learning should be described and represented from age to age, as many have done for the works of Nature and for Civil and Ecclesiastical states. Without this, the history of the world seems to me to be like the **statue** of **Polyphemus** <note>The one-eyed giant, or Cyclops, from Greek mythology</note> with his eye out; that part is missing which most shows the spirit and life of the person. 

And yet, I am not unaware that in various particular sciences—such as those of the legal scholars <note>original: "Iurisconsults"</note>, the mathematicians, the rhetoricians, and the philosophers—there are recorded some small memorials of the schools, authors, and books. Likewise, there are some meager <note>original: "barren"</note> accounts regarding the invention of arts or customs. 

But a complete <note>original: "iust"</note> story of learning is truly wanting. Such a work would contain the antiquities and origins of different types of knowledge and their sects, their inventions, and their traditions. It would cover their various ways of being managed and administered, their periods of flourishing, their oppositions, decays, depressions, and disappearances <note>original: "obliuions"</note>, and their migrations <note>original: "remoues"</note>—along with the causes and occasions for these shifts and all other events concerning learning throughout the ages of the world. 

I do not design the use and end of such a work merely for curiosity or for the satisfaction of those who are lovers of learning. Rather, I intend it for a more serious and grave purpose, which is this, in few words: it will make

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s argument for a comprehensive history of learning, explaining that such a history would provide practical wisdom. He then transitions to a new topic: the three branches of Natural History.</meta>

<header>The Second Book.</header>

...learning; for such a history would, I am persuaded, as a main ornament, make learned men wise in the use and administration of learning. For it is not the works of Saint Augustine or Saint Ambrose that will make so wise a theologian <note>original: "Divine"</note> as Ecclesiastical History, when it is thoroughly read and observed; and the same logic applies to learning in general.

HISTORY OF NATURE is of three sorts: of NATURE IN ITS NORMAL COURSE; of NATURE ERRING, or VARYING; and of NATURE ALTERED or wrought. These are the HISTORY of CREATURES, the HISTORY of MARVELS, and the HISTORY of ARTS. The first of these, no doubt, exists <note>original: "is extant"</note> and in good perfection. The latter two are handled so weakly and unprofitably that I am moved to note them as deficient. 

<margin>History of Erring Nature <note>original: *Historia Naturæ Errantis*</note></margin>
For I find no sufficient or competent collection of the works of Nature which have a digression or deviation <note>original: "Deflexion"</note> from the ordinary course of generations, productions, and motions—whether they are unique features <note>original: "singularities"</note> of a place and region, the strange events of time and chance, the effects of properties as yet unknown, or instances that are exceptions to general rules <note>original: "generall kindes"</note>. 

It is true that I find a number of books of fabulous experiments and "secrets," and frivolous deceptions <note>original: "Impostures"</note> intended for pleasure and novelty <note>original: "strangeness"</note>. But I do not find a substantial and rigorous <note>original: "severe"</note> collection of the **HETEROCLITES** <note>original: "Heteroclites," a term for things that deviate from the common rule or standard</note>, or IRREGULARS of NATURE, well-examined and described—especially not one with the proper rejection of fables and common errors.

<summary>Bacon argues that a history of learning is essential for wisdom and then divides Natural History into three categories: ordinary nature, "erring" nature (marvels and monsters), and nature altered by human art. He notes that systematic records of nature’s exceptions and irregularities are currently lacking.</summary>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Erring Nature" or the "History of Marvels." Bacon argues that documenting nature’s deviations is not for entertainment, but for scientific rigor and the development of new technologies.</meta>

<header>Of the advancement of learning</header>

For, as things now are, if a falsehood about nature is once established, it is never challenged—partly because of the neglect of examination and the authority of antiquity, and partly because the opinion is used in metaphors and rhetorical ornaments.

The purpose of this work—which follows the precedent set by *Aristotle*—is certainly not to satisfy the curiosity of idle and vain minds, as is the custom of **MIRABILARIES** <note>original: "Mirabilaries," collections of wonders or oddities</note>. Rather, it is for two very important reasons. The first is to correct the bias of axioms <note>original: "Axiomes," or established principles</note> and opinions, which are usually based only on common and familiar examples. The second is because studying the wonders of nature provides the closest information and path toward the wonders of art <note>Bacon refers to "art" as human technology or experimental skill</note>. For it is simply a matter of following and, as it were, hounding nature in her wanderings, so that we may be able to lead her back to that same state again.

Nor do I believe that in this **HISTORY OF MARVELS**, superstitious accounts of sorceries, witchcrafts, dreams, divinations, and the like should be entirely excluded, provided there is reliable and clear evidence of the facts. For it is not yet known in what cases, and to what extent, effects attributed to superstition are actually the result of natural causes. Therefore, although the practice of such things is to be condemned, yet from the study and
