--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, criticizing the academic tendency to ignore "ordinary" or "vulgar" things in favor of grand, rare, or secret knowledge.</meta>

<header>Of the advancement of learning</header>

Supercilious arrogance is justly derided in **Plato**, where he introduces **Hippias**, a boasting sophist <note>A teacher in ancient Greece; here used to mean someone who uses clever but false arguments</note>, disputing with **Socrates**, a true and sincere seeker of truth. When the subject was beauty, **Socrates**—following his wandering manner of reasoning <note>original: "Inductions"</note>—first offered the example of a beautiful maiden, then a beautiful horse, and then a beautiful pot, well-glazed. **Hippias** was offended by this and said that, were it not for the sake of courtesy, he would refuse to debate anyone who would cite such lowly and vulgar <note>original: "Sordide"</note> examples. To this, **Socrates** answered: "*You are right, and it suits you well, being a man so finely dressed...*" and so on, continuing in a tone of irony. 

But the truth is that it is not always the grandest examples that provide the most reliable information. This is well illustrated by the common story of the philosopher who fell into the water while gazing upward at the stars. If he had looked down, he might have seen the stars reflected in the water; but by looking upward, he could not see the water in the stars. In the same way, it often happens that humble and small things reveal great truths better than great things can reveal small ones. Therefore, **Aristotle** notes correctly that the nature of everything is best seen in its smallest portions. For that cause, he investigates the nature of a commonwealth <note>A state or political community</note> by first looking at a family and the simple pairings <note>original: "Coniugatiõs"</note> of husband and wife, parent and child, and master and servant—relationships which are found in every cottage.

<meta>catchword: Even</meta>

<summary>Bacon argues that great truths are often found in small, humble examples rather than grand ones, citing Socrates' debate with Hippias and Aristotle's study of the family as the foundation of the state.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Hippias, Socrates, Aristotle, scientific method, induction, irony, philosophy, commonwealth, observation</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that grand truths are often found in small, humble observations, comparing the study of the universe to the study of a city's smallest parts.</meta>

<header>The second book.</header>

In the same way, the nature of this great city of the world and its workings <note>original: "policie"</note> must first be sought in humble correspondences and small portions. We see, for example, how that secret of nature—the way iron touched by a lodestone <note>A naturally magnetized mineral</note> turns toward the north—was discovered in needles of iron, not in large bars of iron.

But if my judgment carries any weight, the use of **MECHANICAL HISTORY** <note>original: "HISTORIE MECHANICAL"; the study of human crafts, trades, and industrial processes</note> is, of all types of history, the most essential and fundamental to natural philosophy <note>The study of nature and the physical universe; a precursor to modern science</note>. I mean a natural philosophy that will not vanish like smoke in subtle, lofty, or pleasing speculation, but one that will be practical for the enrichment and benefit of human life. For it will not only provide and suggest, for the present, many ingenious practices in all trades—by connecting and transferring the observations of one art to the use of another, when the experiences of various trades <note>original: "misteries"; specialized crafts or guilds</note> are considered together by one person—but further, it will provide a truer and more real enlightenment concerning causes and principles <note>original: "Axiomes"</note> than has been achieved so far. 

For just as a person’s true character is never well known until they are opposed, and just as the sea-god Proteus <note>A Greek god who could change his shape at will to avoid capture</note> never changed his form until he was constrained and held fast, so the workings and variations of nature do not appear as fully when nature is left to its own devices as they do through the trials and pressures of human technology <note>original: "Art"</note>.

<sig>Cc 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: For</meta>

<summary>Bacon argues that "Mechanical History"—the study of trades and technology—is the most vital foundation for science. He asserts that nature reveals its secrets more clearly when "vexed" or pressured by human experimentation than when it is left to itself.</summary>
<keywords>Mechanical History, Natural Philosophy, technology, Proteus, experimentation, scientific method, lodestone</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues Bacon’s classification of knowledge, moving from the study of nature to the study of human society, which he calls "Civil History."</meta>

# Of the advancement of learning

As for **CIVIL HISTORY** <note>original: "CIVILE HISTORY"; history concerning human society and political affairs, as opposed to natural history</note>, it is of three kinds, which may be appropriately compared with three kinds of pictures or images. In pictures or images, we see that some are unfinished, some are perfect <note>original: "parfite"; complete or finished</note>, and some are defaced. Similarly, in history, we find three kinds: **MEMORIALS**, **PERFECT HISTORIES**, and **ANTIQUITIES**. **MEMORIALS** are history unfinished, or the first rough drafts of history. **ANTIQUITIES** are history defaced—the remnants of history that have by chance escaped the shipwreck of time <note>A famous metaphor for how historical records are lost over centuries, leaving only fragments behind</note>.

**MEMORIALS**, or preparatory history, are of two types: the first may be called **COMMENTARIES**, and the other **REGISTERS**. **COMMENTARIES** are those which record a continuous sequence of raw events and actions, without including the motives, designs, advice, speeches, excuses, causes, or other context of the action. This is the true nature of a commentary (though Julius Caesar, in a mix of modesty and greatness, chose to apply the name "commentary" to the best history in the world <note>Bacon refers to Caesar’s *Commentaries on the Gallic War*</note>). **REGISTERS** are collections of public acts, such as council decrees, judicial proceedings, state declarations and letters, orations, and the like, without a complete continuity or a weaving together of the narrative thread.

<meta>catchword: ANTIQUITIES</meta>

<summary>Bacon categorizes "Civil History" into three types—Memorials, Perfect Histories, and Antiquities—using the analogy of unfinished, complete, and damaged artwork. He further defines Memorials as either raw event logs (Commentaries) or collections of official documents (Registers).</summary>
<keywords>civil history, memorials, perfect histories, antiquities, commentaries, registers, Julius Caesar, historiography, shipwreck of time</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues Bacon’s classification of history, specifically defining "Antiquities" and moving into the three main branches of "Perfect History."</meta>

# The Second Book

**ANTIQUITIES**, or "Remnants of History," are, as was said, **like the planks of a shipwreck** <note>original: *tanquam Tabula Naufragij*</note>. Through exact and painstaking diligence and observation, industrious people save and recover some information from the deluge of time using monuments, names, words, proverbs, traditions, private records, and evidence, as well as fragments of stories and passages in books that do not primarily concern history.

In these kinds of imperfect histories, I assign no "deficiency" <note>Bacon is cataloging gaps in human knowledge; here he argues that since antiquities are by definition fragments, their incompleteness is not a "gap" in scholarship but simply their nature</note>, for they are **like things imperfectly mixed** <note>original: *tanquam imperfectè Mista*; a reference to Aristotle’s physics regarding substances that have not yet fully combined or matured</note>, and therefore any deficiency in them is part of their very nature. As for the "Corruptions and Moths of History"—which are **Epitomes** <note>Summaries or abridgments of longer works</note>—the use of them deserves to be banished, as all men of sound judgment have confessed. These abridgments have eaten away and corroded the healthy bodies of many excellent histories, turning them into base and useless dregs.

**HISTORY** which may be called **JUST** and **PERFECT** History <note>meaning full, proper, or complete history</note> is of three kinds, according to the object it proposes to represent. It represents either a **TIME**, a **PERSON**, or an **ACTION**.
1. The first we call **CHRONICLES**.
2. The second we call **LIVES**.
3. The third we call **NARRATIONS** or **RELATIONS**.

Of these, although the first (Chronicles) is the most complete and absolute kind of history and receives the most respect and glory, the second (Lives) excels it in profit and usefulness, and the third (Narrations) excels it in truth <note>original: "veritie"</note>.

<sig>Cc 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>Bacon defines Antiquities as fragments saved from the "shipwreck of time" and divides "Perfect History" into three categories: Chronicles, Lives, and Narrations.</summary>
<keywords>Antiquities, Epitomes, Chronicles, Biography, Narrations, Perfect History, Historiography</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues Bacon’s comparison between the three kinds of "Perfect History": Chronicles (History of Times), Biographies (Lives), and Narrations. He argues that while Chronicles are more prestigious, Biographies and Narrations are often more accurate and insightful.</meta>

...and sincerity. For **HISTORY OF TIMES** <note>also called Chronicles</note> represents the magnitude of actions and the public faces and conduct of people, but passes over in silence the smaller events and motivations of men and matters. 

But since God’s workmanship is such that he hangs the greatest weight upon the smallest wires—**hanging the greatest things from the smallest** <note>original: *Maxima è Minimis suspēdēs*</note>—it happens that such histories set forth the pomp of business rather than its true and inward springs <note>original: "resorts"; the hidden mechanisms or motivations behind events</note>. 

But **LIVES**, if they are well written, choose a specific person to represent, in whom both large and small, public and private actions are blended together. By necessity, they must contain a more true, natural, and lively representation. 

Similarly, **NARRATIONS** and **RELATIONS** of specific actions—such as the **Peloponnesian War**, the **Expedition of Cyrus the Younger** <note>original: *Cyrus Minor*; a reference to Xenophon’s *Anabasis*</note>, or the **Conspiracy of Catiline**—cannot help but be more purely and exactly true than **HISTORIES OF TIMES**. This is because they can choose a subject matter that is within the knowledge and information available to the writer. On the other hand, anyone who undertakes the story of an entire era, especially one of any length, will inevitably encounter many blanks and gaps that he is forced to fill using his own intellect and conjecture.

Regarding the **HISTORY OF TIMES** (by which I mean civil or political history), God’s providence has determined its distribution. For it has pleased God to ordain and highlight two model states in the world for their excellence in military power, learning, moral virtue, statecraft <note>original: "policie"</note>, and laws: the **STATE OF GREECE**, and...

<summary>Bacon argues that Biographies and Narrations of specific events are often more accurate than broad Chronicles (Histories of Times) because they capture the small, private motivations that actually drive great events. He begins to discuss the two great "model states" of history: Greece and Rome.</summary>

<keywords>Chronicles, Biography, Narrations, Peloponnesian War, Cyrus the Younger, Catiline, historical methodology, providence, Greece</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues Bacon’s discussion of the "History of Times." He has just mentioned that God ordained two "Exemplar States" whose histories are particularly worthy of study: Greece and Rome.</meta>

...the **STATE OF ROME**: Its histories occupy the **MIDDLE PART** of time. They are preceded by histories which may, by one common name, be called the **ANTIQUITIES OF THE WORLD**, and followed by histories which may likewise be called by the name of **MODERN HISTORY**.

Now to speak of the deficiencies: As for the **HEATHEN ANTIQUITIES** <note>The history of the pre-Christian world, specifically non-Jewish civilizations</note> of the world, it is pointless to note them as deficient. They are certainly deficient, consisting mostly of fables and fragments, but this deficiency cannot be remedied. For Antiquity is like Fame: *she hides her head among the clouds* <note>original Latin: *Caput inter nubila condit*</note>; her head is muffled and hidden from our sight. As for the **HISTORY OF THE EXEMPLARY STATES** <note>The classical civilizations of Greece and Rome</note>, it exists in good perfection.

Still, I could wish there were a complete course of history for **Greece** from **Theseus** to **Philopoemen** <note>A Greek general known as "the last of the Greeks," marking the end of Greek independence</note> (at which time the affairs of Greece were drowned and extinguished by the affairs of Rome), and for **Rome** from **Romulus** to **Justinian**, who may truly be called the **Last of the Romans** <note>original Latin: *Vltimus Romanorum*</note>. In these sequences of story, the texts of **Thucydides** and **Xenophon** in the one, and the texts of **Livy**, **Polybius**, **Sallust**, **Caesar**, **Appian**, **Tacitus**, and **Herodian** in the other, should be kept entire without any reduction at all, and only supplemented and continued. But this is a matter of grandeur <note>original: "Magnificence"; something requiring great resources or royal patronage</note>, to be praised rather than demanded.

->and<-

<summary>Bacon categorizes civil history into three eras (Antiquities, Exemplary/Classical, and Modern) and calls for a continuous, preserved narrative of Greek and Roman history using the surviving works of classical historians.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s review of the deficiencies in history. Having discussed the histories of the "Exemplary States" of Greece and Rome, he now moves to "Modern History," focusing specifically on the need for a unified history of Great Britain.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

And we speak now of parts of learning that are supplemental, and not of supererogation <note>original: "supererogation"; works performed beyond what is required by duty</note>.

But for **MODERN HISTORIES**, of which there are some few very worthy, but the greater part beneath mediocrity: leaving the care of foreign stories to foreign states, because I will not be a meddler in another's state <note>original Latin: *Curiosus in aliena Republica*</note>, I cannot fail to represent to your Majesty the unworthiness of the history of *England* in its main continuation, and the partiality and bias <note>original: "obliquity"</note> of that of *Scotland* in the latest and most extensive author <note>Likely a reference to George Buchanan’s "History of Scotland" (1582)</note> that I have seen. 

I suppose that it would be an honor for your Majesty, and a very memorable work, if this island of Great *Britain*—as it is now joined in a monarchy for the ages to come—were also joined in one history for the times past. This should be done after the manner of the sacred history <note>The historical books of the Bible</note>, which records the story of the Ten Tribes and of the Two Tribes <note>The divided kingdoms of Israel and Judah</note> together as twins. 

And if it should seem that the greatness of this work might cause it to be performed less exactly, there is an excellent period of a much smaller span of time regarding the story of *England*: that is to say, from the Uniting of the Roses <note>The end of the Wars of the Roses with the accession of Henry VII in 1485</note> to the Uniting of the Kingdoms <note>The Union of the Crowns under James I in 1603</note>. This is a portion of time wherein, to my understanding, there has been the rarest variety of events known in a similar number of successions in any hereditary monarchy. For it begins with the mixed acquisition <note>original: "Adeption"</note> of a crown by both arms and title. It was an entry by battle and an establishment by marriage; and therefore...

As for **BIOGRAPHIES** <note>original: "LIVES"</note>, I find it strange that these times have so little esteemed the virtues of their own era, such that the writing of biographies is not more frequent. For although there are not many sovereign princes or absolute commanders, and states are mostly gathered into monarchies, yet there are many worthy individuals who deserve better than scattered rumors or empty eulogies <note>original: "barren Elogies"</note>. This opinion stems from that root: "We do not despise praise until we have ceased to do things worthy of praise" <note>original Latin: *Non prius laudes contempsimus, quam laudanda facere desivimus*</note>. Yet that will not alter Solomon’s judgment: "The memory of the just is blessed, but the name of the wicked shall rot" <note>original Latin: *Memoria Iusti cum laudibus, at impiorum nomen putrefcet*</note>. The one flourishes, while the other either consumes away into immediate oblivion or turns into a foul odor. And therefore, in those titles or additions which are and have long been well-received and brought into common use—"of happy memory," "of pious memory," "of good memory"—we acknowledge what Cicero says (borrowing it from Demosthenes): "Good fame is the proper possession of the dead" <note>original Latin: *Bona Fama propria possessio defunctorum*</note>. I cannot help but note that in our times, this "possession" lies much in waste, and that in this area there is a deficiency.

As for the narrations and reports of particular actions, it is also to be wished that there were greater diligence. There is no great action that does not deserve a good pen to record it. ## Of the Advancement of Learning

This is especially true with the application that he <note>The author refers here to the Roman historian Tacitus.</note> couples it with: **Annals** and **Journals**, assigning to the former matters of state, and to the latter, *acts* and accidents of a lowlier nature. For while briefly mentioning certain magnificent buildings, he adds: "Since it has been found to be in accordance with the dignity of the Roman people to commit illustrious matters to Annals, and such things as these to the daily acts of the city." <note>original: "Cum ex dignitate populi Romani repertum sit, res illustres annalibus, talia diurnis urbis Actis mandare."</note>

Thus, there is a kind of contemplative **Heraldry** <note>The study of rank and precedence.</note> as well as a civil one. Just as nothing detracts from the dignity of a state more than the confusion of social ranks, so it does not a little to debase the authority of a **History** to intermingle matters of triumph, ceremony, or novelty with matters of state. 

However, the use of a **Journal** has not only been applied to the history of time, but likewise to the history of persons, and chiefly of actions. In ancient times, princes—for reasons of both honor and policy—had journals kept of what passed day by day. We see that the chronicle read before **Ahasuerus** <note>The Persian King from the biblical Book of Esther.</note> when he could not sleep contained matters of state business, but specifically those that had occurred in his own time and very recently. In contrast, the **Journal** of **Alexander's** house expressed every small detail, even concerning his person and court. It is still a well-received custom in memorable enterprises—such as military expeditions, voyages, and the like—to keep **Diaries** of what happens continually.

I cannot likewise be ignorant of a form of writing which some grave and wise men have used,

These are not incorporated into the history itself, but kept separate, and are the primary focus of the author's intention. This kind of ***RUMINATED HISTORY*** <note>original: "RVMlNATED HISTORY"; history that has been "chewed over" or deeply meditated upon with added commentary</note> I think more fit to place among books of policy (which we shall speak of hereafter) than among books of history. For it is the true office of history to represent the events themselves, together with the motives and counsels behind them, and to leave the observations and conclusions to the liberty and faculty of every man's judgment. But "mixtures" are irregular things, which no man can clearly define.

So also is there another kind of history that is manifoldly mixed, and that is the ***HISTORY OF COSMOGRAPHY*** <note>The study of the universe, including geography and astronomy</note>. This is compounded of natural history in respect to the regions themselves; civil history in respect to the habitations, governments <note>original: "Regiments"</note>, and manners of the people; and mathematics in respect to the climates and the configurations of the heavens. This part of learning, above all others in these latter times, has obtained the most proficiency. For it may be truly affirmed to the honor of these times, and in a virtuous emulation of antiquity, that this great building of the world never had "through-lights" <note>windows or openings that allow light to pass all the way through a structure</note> made in it until the age of us and our fathers. ## Of the Advancement of Learning

Even if "the late-reddening evening star brings on the light" <note>original: *sera rubens accedit lumina vesper*; a reference to Virgil's Georgics regarding the setting sun</note>, that might be shown by demonstration and not in actual fact; and if it were achieved by travel, it would require a voyage of only half the globe. But to circle the Earth, as the heavenly bodies do, was neither done nor even attempted until these later times. Therefore, these modern times may justly take as their motto not only "Further Beyond" <note>original: *Plus ultra*</note> in contrast to the ancient "No Further" <note>original: *Non ultra*</note>, and "Imitable Thunder" <note>original: *Imitabile fulmen*; referring to the invention of gunpowder and cannons</note> in contrast to the ancient "Unimitable Thunder"—as it was said of the man who was "Mad, to mimic the storm-clouds and the inimitable thunder" <note>original: *Demens qui nymbos et non imitabile fulmen, &c.*; Virgil's description of Salmoneus, who tried to rival Jupiter</note>—but likewise, "Imitable Heaven." This is because of the many memorable voyages which, after the manner of the heavens, have circled the globe of the earth.

And this proficiency in navigation and discoveries may also foster an expectation of the further proficiency and increase of all sciences. It seems they are ordained by God to be contemporaries—that is, to meet in one age. For so the prophet Daniel, speaking of the latter times, foretells: "Many shall pass through, and knowledge shall be increased" <note>original: *Plurimi pertransibunt, & Multiplex erit Scientia*; Daniel 12:4</note>. It is as if the openness and thorough passage of the world and the increase of knowledge were appointed to happen in the same ages. We see this is already performed in great part, as the learning of these later times does not yield much ground to the former two periods or cycles of learning: the one of the Greeks and the other of the Romans.

Ecclesiastical History receives the same divisions as Civil History; but further in the...

### *The Second Book*

The specific nature of this subject may be divided into the **History of the Church** (as a general name), the **History of Prophecy**, and the **History of Providence**. 

The first describes the times of the militant Church <note>The "Church Militant" refers to the Christian Church on earth, struggling against sin and persecution.</note>; whether it be drifting, like the Ark of Noah; or moving, like the Ark in the Wilderness; or at rest, as the Ark in the Temple. That is to say: the state of the Church in persecution, in transition <note>original: "Remoue"</note>, and in peace. I should not mark this part as deficient in any way; I only wish that its virtue and sincerity were equal to its mass and quantity. However, I am not currently dealing with criticisms, but with omissions.

The second part, which is the **History of Prophecy**, consists of two related elements: the prophecy and its accomplishment. Therefore, the nature of such a work ought to be that every prophecy of the Scripture be matched with the event fulfilling it throughout the ages of the world. This would serve both for the better confirmation of faith and for the better enlightenment of the Church regarding those parts of prophecies which are yet unfulfilled. 

In this, we must nevertheless allow for that latitude which is agreeable and familiar to divine prophecies. They share the nature of their Author, with whom a thousand years are but as one day <note>A reference to Psalm 90:4 and 2 Peter 3:8</note>. Therefore, they are not fulfilled precisely all at once, but have a springing and germinating accomplishment <note>The author suggests that prophecies may have multiple stages of fulfillment over time.</note> throughout many ages, even though the height or fullness of them may refer to one specific age.

<summary>The author divides ecclesiastical history into the history of the Church, Prophecy, and Providence, specifically calling for a chronological study that links biblical prophecies to their historical fulfillments.</summary>
<keywords>Ecclesiastical History, Church Militant, History of Prophecy, Biblical fulfillment, Church in Persecution, Divine Providence</keywords>

### Of the Advancement of Learning

<margin>Prophetic History.</margin> This is a work which I find deficient <note>original: "deficient"; meaning a branch of knowledge that has not yet been adequately developed</note>, but it is to be done with wisdom, sobriety, and reverence, or not at all.

The third part, which is the **History of Providence**, contains that excellent correspondence which exists between God’s revealed will and His secret will. This is often so obscure that, for the most part, it is not legible to the "natural man" <note>A person relying solely on human reason without divine revelation</note>; no, nor many times even to those who behold it from the Tabernacle <note>Those within the church or possessing spiritual insight</note>. Yet, at sometimes it pleases God—for our better establishment in faith and for the confuting of those who live as if there were no God in the world—to write it in such large and capital letters that, as the Prophet says, *he that runs by may read it* <note>A reference to Habakkuk 2:2</note>. This means that even "recreant" <note>original: "recrestentiall" / "recreant"; those who are negligent or cowardly in faith</note> persons, who hasten past God’s judgments and never bend or fix their thoughts upon them, are nevertheless forced to discern it during their passage and race through life. Such are the notable events and examples of God’s judgments, chastisements, deliverances, and blessings. This is a work which has been labored upon by many authors, and therefore I cannot present it as something omitted or overlooked.

There are also other parts of learning which are appendices to History. Since all the exterior proceedings of man consist of **Words** and **Deeds**, History properly receives and retains the "Deeds" in memory; and if it includes "Words," it is only as they are inducements and passages leading to Deeds. Therefore, there are other books and writings which are appropriated to the custody and reception of **Words** only; which likewise...

<summary>Bacon defines Prophetic History and the History of Providence, noting that while God's will is often obscure, it is sometimes made undeniably clear through historical judgments and blessings.</summary>

## The Second Book

<margin>Appendices to History</margin>
These likewise are of three sorts: **ORATIONS**, **LETTERS**, and **BRIEF SPEECHES** or **SAYINGS**. 

**ORATIONS** include legal pleadings, speeches of counsel, <term>laudatives</term><note>original: "Laudatiues"; speeches of praise</note>, <term>invectives</term><note>speeches of harsh denunciation</note>, apologies, <term>reprehensions</term><note>formal expressions of disapproval</note>, orations of formality or ceremony, and the like. 

**LETTERS** vary according to all the variety of occasions, such as: advertisements <note>in this context, notifications or news</note>, advice, directions, propositions, petitions, letters of recommendation, letters of protest, letters of apology, letters of courtesy, letters of pleasure, letters of discourse, and all other passages of action. 

In my judgment, letters written by wise men are the best of all human words; for they are more natural than orations and public speeches, and more considered than informal conferences or spontaneous speeches. So again, letters concerning affairs of state, written by those who manage them or are privy to them, are the best instructions for history of all others; and to a diligent reader, they are the best histories in themselves.

<summary>Francis Bacon categorizes the "appendices" of history into orations, letters, and brief sayings, arguing that personal and official letters provide the most authentic and insightful historical record.</summary>
<keywords>Orations, Letters, Historical methodology, Civil Knowledge, Rhetoric, Primary Sources</keywords>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

This concludes what I have to say concerning History, which is that part of learning corresponding to one of the cells, *dwellings* <note>original: "Domiciles"</note>, or offices of the human mind: that of the Memory.

**POETRY** <note>original: "POESIE"</note> is a part of learning that is mostly restrained in the measure of its words, but in all other respects is extremely free. It truly refers to the Imagination, which, not being tied to the laws of matter, may join together at will what Nature has separated, and separate what Nature has joined. In this way, it creates unlawful matches and divorces of things. As the saying goes, *Painters and poets have always had an equal right to dare anything* <note>original: "Pictoribus atque Poetis &c." from Horace's Ars Poetica</note>.

Poetry is understood in two senses: in respect to its words or its matter. In the first sense, it is merely a style of writing and belongs to the arts of speech <note>such as rhetoric or grammar</note>, and is not relevant for our present discussion. In the latter sense, it is (as has been said) one of the principal portions of learning. It is nothing else but **FICTIONAL HISTORY** <note>original: "FAINED HISTORY"</note>, which may be written in prose as well as in verse.

The use of this **FICTIONAL HISTORY** has been to give some shadow of satisfaction to the human mind in those areas where the nature of things denies it. The world, in its reality, is inferior in proportion to the human soul. Because of this, the spirit of man craves a more ample greatness, a more exact goodness, and a more absolute variety than can be found in the actual nature of things. Therefore, because the acts of...

<summary>Francis Bacon defines Poetry as "fictional history" belonging to the Imagination, arguing that it allows the human spirit to experience a greatness and morality that the real world lacks.</summary>
<keywords>Poetry, Imagination, Fictional History, Memory, Human Soul, Literary Theory, Francis Bacon</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the different branches of human learning, specifically focusing on the nature and purpose of Poetry as a form of "feigned history."</meta>

### The Second Book

Or, because the events of *true history* do not have that magnitude which satisfies the mind of man, *Poetry* invents acts and events that are greater and more heroic. Because *true history* presents the successes and outcomes of actions as not always being agreeable to the merits of virtue and vice, *Poetry* invents them to be more just in their retribution, and more in accordance with revealed Providence. Because *true history* represents actions and events as more ordinary and less varied, *Poetry* endows them with more rarity and more unexpected and alternating variations. 

Thus, it appears that *Poetry* serves and contributes to magnanimity <note>original: "Magnanimitie"; greatness of soul or courage</note>, morality, and to delight. Therefore, it was always thought to have some participation in divinity, because it raises and uplifts the mind by submitting the appearances of things to the desires of the mind; whereas reason buckles and bows the mind to the nature of things. 

We see that by these charms and harmonies with human nature and pleasure—joined also with the agreement and comfort it shares with music—poetry has found access and esteem in uncivilized times and barbarous regions where other forms of learning were excluded.

The division of *Poetry* which is most appropriate to its own nature (besides those divisions which it shares with history, such as feigned chronicles, feigned lives, and the appendices of history, such as feigned epistles <note>letters</note>, feigned orations, and the rest) is into POETRY...

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that poetry is superior to history in its ability to satisfy the human spirit by depicting a more heroic, just, and varied world than reality provides. He defines poetry as a tool that aligns the world with our desires, whereas reason forces us to align our minds with reality.</summary>
<keywords>Poetry, History, Providence, Magnanimity, Morality, Reason vs. Imagination, Literary Theory, Heroic Acts</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the different types of Poetry as a branch of human learning.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>
# Of the Advancement of Learning

NARRATIVE, REPRESENTATIVE, and ALLUSIVE. The NARRATIVE is a mere imitation of history with the excesses previously mentioned; choosing for its subject commonly wars and love, rarely statecraft, and sometimes pleasure or mirth. REPRESENTATIVE is like a visible history, and is an image of actions as if they were present, just as history is an image of actions in nature as they are (that is) past. ALLUSIVE or PARABOLICAL is a narration applied only to express some special purpose or concept <note>original: "conceit"</note>. 

This latter kind of parabolical wisdom was much more in use in ancient times, as may appear by the fables of Aesop, the brief sentences of the Seven Sages, and the use of *hieroglyphics*. The reason for this was that it was then necessary to express any point of reason—which was sharper or more subtle than the common understanding—in that manner, because men in those times lacked both a variety of examples and subtlety of thought. And just as *hieroglyphics* existed before letters, so parables existed before formal arguments. Nevertheless, now and at all times, they retain much life and vigor, because abstract reason cannot be made so tangible <note>original: "sensible"</note>, nor are historical examples always so perfectly fitting.

But there remains yet another use of PARABOLICAL POETRY <note>original: "POESY"</note>, opposite to the one we last mentioned. For while that first type tends to demonstrate and illustrate what is taught or delivered, this other type tends to withdraw and obscure it. That is, when the secrets and mysteries of religion, policy, or philosophy [are hidden...]

<summary>Francis Bacon defines three types of poetry—narrative, representative, and allusive—noting that parables were historically used to make complex ideas understandable before formal logic and literacy were widespread.</summary>
<keywords>Poetry, Narrative, Representative, Parabolical, Aesop, Hieroglyphics, Philosophy, Rhetoric</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the different branches of learning, specifically focusing on "Poesy" or poetry, and its use in conveying philosophical truths through allegory.</meta>

### The Second Book

<margin>The use of Parables.</margin>
Philosophy is often wrapped in fables or parables. In divine poetry, we see this use is authorized. In heathen poetry, we see that the interpretation of fables sometimes turns out with great success. For example, there is the fable that after the Giants were overthrown in their war against the Gods, the Earth, their mother, brought forth Fame in revenge.
*Mother Earth, provoked by anger against the Gods, brought her forth last (as they say), a sister to Coeus and Enceladus.* <note>original Latin: "Illam terra Parens ira irritata Deorum, Extremam, ut perhibent, Coeo Enceladoque Sororem Progenuit." From Virgil's Aeneid.</note>
This is interpreted to mean that when Princes and Monarchs have suppressed actual and open rebels, then the malice of the people (which is the mother of rebellion) brings forth libels, slanders, and criticisms of the state. These are of the same nature as rebellion, but more "feminine" <note>meaning indirect or verbal rather than physical</note>. 

So too in the fable that the rest of the Gods conspired to bind Jupiter, but Pallas called Briareus with his hundred hands to his aid. This is interpreted to mean that Monarchies need not fear any curbing of their absolute power by mighty subjects, as long as they use wisdom to keep the hearts of the common people, who will be sure to side with the monarch. 

Also, in the fable that Achilles was brought up under Chiron the Centaur, who was part man and part beast, it is interpreted ingeniously (though corruptly) by Machiavelli <note>Niccolò Machiavelli, author of *The Prince*</note>. He suggests that it belongs to the education and discipline of Princes to know how to play the part of the Lion in violence and the Fox in guile, as well as the part of the Man in virtue and justice. Nevertheless, in many similar cases, I tend to think that the fable was created first and the interpretation was devised later.

<summary>Francis Bacon discusses how ancient fables and parables serve as allegories for political and philosophical truths, citing examples like the birth of Fame and the education of Achilles.</summary>
<keywords>Poesy, Parables, Allegory, Mythology, Machiavelli, Political Philosophy, Fame, Rebellion, Monarchy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of Poesy (Poetry) and its role in human knowledge, transitioning from the imaginative arts to the rational sciences.</meta>

<header>Of the Advancement of Learning</header>

than that the moral was first, and the fable framed upon it. For I find it was an ancient vanity in *Chrysippus* <note>A Stoic philosopher known for his complex logic and interpretations.</note>, who troubled himself with great effort to fasten the assertions of the *Stoics* upon the fictions of the ancient *Poets*. But yet, I offer no opinion that all the fables and fictions of the *Poets* were merely for pleasure and not figurative. Surely, regarding those *Poets* who are now extant—even *Homer* himself (notwithstanding he was made a kind of Scripture by the later schools of the *Grecians*)—I should without any difficulty pronounce that his fables had no such hidden meaning in his own intention. But what they might have possessed based on a more original tradition is not easy to affirm, for he was not the inventor of many of them. 

In this third part of learning, which is *Poesy* <note>original: "Poesie"; here referring to all imaginative literature and creative writing.</note>, I can report no deficiency. For being like a plant that comes from the lust of the earth <note>Spontaneous growth without cultivation.</note> without a formal seed, it has sprung up and spread abroad more than any other kind of knowledge. To ascribe to it what is due: for the expressing of emotions, passions, corruptions, and customs, we are more beholden to *Poets* than to the *Philosophers'* works; and for wit and eloquence, we owe them not much less than to the *Orators'* harangues. But it is not good to stay too long in the theater; let us now pass on to the judicial place or palace of the mind, which we are to approach and view with more reverence and attention.

The knowledge of man is like the waters: some descending

<summary>Bacon concludes his survey of poetry, arguing that while poets provide better insights into human passion than philosophers, the field is already well-developed and it is time to move on to the study of the rational mind.</summary>

## ## *The Second Book*

Knowledge descends from above or springs from beneath; the one is informed by the light of Nature, the other inspired by divine revelation. The light of Nature consists of the notions of the mind and the reports of the senses. As for the knowledge which man receives by teaching, it is cumulative <note>original: "Cumulatiue"; meaning added or built up over time</note> and not original—much like a body of water that, besides its own spring-head, is fed by other springs and streams. So then, according to these two differing illuminations or origins, knowledge is first of all divided into **DIVINITY** and **PHILOSOPHY**.

In **PHILOSOPHY**, the contemplations of man either penetrate *toward God*, are directed toward *Nature*, or are reflected back upon *himself*. Out of these several inquiries, there arise three branches of knowledge: **DIVINE PHILOSOPHY**, **NATURAL PHILOSOPHY**, and **HUMAN PHILOSOPHY** (or **HUMANITY**). For all things are marked and stamped with this triple character: the power of God, the difference of Nature, and the use of Man. 

However, the distributions and partitions of knowledge are not like separate lines that meet in a single angle and so touch only at a point. Instead, they are like the branches of a tree that meet in a stem. This stem has a dimension and quantity of entireness and continuity before it begins to separate and break itself into arms and boughs. Therefore...

<summary>Francis Bacon outlines the primary division of knowledge into Divinity and Philosophy, further categorizing Philosophy into Divine, Natural, and Human branches based on their subject of study.</summary>

## Of the Advancement of Learning

Therefore, it is good—before we enter into the previously mentioned distribution of knowledge—to establish and constitute one universal science by the name of *First Philosophy* <note>original: "Philosophia Prima"</note>, *Primitive Philosophy*, or *Summary Philosophy*. This should serve as the main and common highway, before we reach the point where the paths part and divide themselves. Whether I should report this science as deficient or not, I remain doubtful. 

For I find a certain rhapsody <note>a disorganized collection or medley</note> of *Natural Theology*, and of various parts of *Logic*; and of that part of *Natural Philosophy* which concerns *Principles*; and of that other part of *Natural Philosophy* which concerns the *Soul or Spirit*. All of these are strangely mixed together and confused. But when examined, it seems to me to be a plunder of other sciences—elevated and exalted by high-sounding terms—rather than anything solid or substantive in itself. 

Nevertheless, I cannot be ignorant of the common distinction currently in use: that the same things are handled but in different respects. For example, that *Logic* considers many things as they exist in *Notion* <note>as mental concepts</note>, and this *Philosophy* considers them as they exist in *Nature*; the one in *Appearance*, the other in *Existence*. But I find this difference is better stated than pursued. For if they had considered *Quantity, Similarity, Diversity*, and the rest of those *External Characters of things* as *Philosophers* and in *Nature*, their inquiries must have been of...

For does any of them, in handling **Quantity**, speak of the force of union—how, and how far, it multiplies power <note>original: "vertue"</note>? Does any give the reason why some things in Nature are so common and in such great mass, while others are so rare and in such small quantity? Does any, in handling **Similitude** and **Diversity**, assign the cause why iron should not move toward iron (which is more like itself), but move toward the **lodestone** <note>a naturally magnetized mineral</note> (which is less like it)? 

<margin><term>Similitude</term></margin>
Why, in all the **diversities** of things, should there be certain **"participles" in Nature** <note>entities that share characteristics of two different categories; transitional forms</note> which are almost ambiguous as to which kind they should be referred? But there is a complete and deep silence touching the nature and operation of those **common adjuncts** <note>general properties or characteristics</note> of things as they exist in Nature; there is only a resuming and repeating of the force and use of them in speech or argument. 

<margin><term>Common adjuncts</term></margin>
Therefore, because in a writing of this nature I avoid all unnecessary subtlety, my meaning touching this **Original or Universal Philosophy** is expressed in this plain and simple description by negative: that it be a **receptacle for all** such profitable observations and **axioms** as do not fall within the compass of any of the special parts of philosophy or sciences, but are more common and of a higher stage.

# Of the Advancement of Learning

And is there not a true coincidence between commutative and distributive justice, and arithmetic and geometric proportion? Is this not an axiom of justice as well as of mathematics? Is not that other rule—*things which agree with a third thing, agree also with each other* <note>original: "Quæ in eodem tertio conueniunt, & inter se conueniunt"</note>—a rule taken from mathematics, yet so potent in logic that all syllogisms are built upon it? Is not the observation that *all things change, but nothing perishes* <note>original: "Omnia mutantur, nil interit"</note> a contemplation in philosophy? In physics, it means that the <term>quantum</term> <note>The total amount or bulk of matter.</note> of nature is eternal. In natural theology, it means that it requires the same omnipotence to turn something into nothing as it did at the first to make something out of nothing. This is according to the Scripture: *I have learned that all the works which God has made continue forever; we cannot add anything to them, nor take anything away* <note>original: "Didici quod omnia opera quæ fecit Deus, perfeuerent in perpetuum, non possumus eis quicquam addere, nec auferre"; see Ecclesiastes 3:14.</note>.

Was not the <term>Persian Magic</term> <note>The wisdom of the Magi, which Bacon interpreted as the study of the harmony between the rules of nature and the rules of government.</note> a reduction or correspondence of the principles and architectures of nature to the rules and policy of governments? 

Is not the precept of a musician—to move from a discord or harsh accord to a concord or sweet accord—equally true in the human affections? Are not the organs of the senses of the same kind as the organs of reflection—the eye like a mirror, the ear like a cave or a narrow, bounded passage? This science, therefore (as I understand it), I may justly report as **deficient**; <margin>First Philosophy, or the Fountainhead of the Sciences</margin> <note>original: "Philosophia prima, sive de Fontibus Scientiarum"</note> for I see sometimes that the more profound wits, in handling some particular argument, will now and then draw a bucket of water out of this well for their present use. <margin>Of the light of Nature</margin>
This knowledge may be truly termed Divine in respect to its object, and Natural in respect to its source of light. The boundaries of this knowledge are these: it is sufficient to refute Atheism, but not to inform Religion. Therefore, God never performed a miracle to convert an atheist, because the light of Nature <note>The "light of Nature" refers to human reason and the observation of the physical world without the aid of divine revelation.</note> should have led him to acknowledge a God. However, miracles have been performed to convert idolaters and the superstitious, because the light of Nature does not extend so far as to declare the will and true worship of God. 

For just as all works show the power and skill of the workman, but not his actual image, so it is with the works of God. They show the omnipotence and wisdom of the Maker, but not His image. In this regard, the Heathen <note>Refers to pre-Christian Greek and Roman philosophers.</note> opinion differs from the Sacred truth. They supposed the world to be the image of God, and Man to be an extract or miniature image of the world <note>original: "compendious Image"; referring to the concept of man as a "microcosm."</note>. But the Scriptures never deign to attribute such honor to the world as to call it the image of God; they call it only <margin>Of the works of his hands</margin> *the work of His hands*. Neither do the Scriptures speak of any other image of God except Man.

But on the other hand, to seek to derive the articles of religion out of the contemplation of Nature, or from the foundation of human knowledge...

# The Second Book

To use human knowledge to induce any certainty or persuasion concerning the points of Faith is, in my judgment, not safe: *Give to faith the things that belong to faith* <note>original: "Da fidei, quae fidei sunt"</note>. For the Heathen themselves conclude as much in that excellent and divine fable of the Golden Chain: *That men and Gods were not able to draw Jupiter down to the Earth, but contrariwise, Jupiter was able to draw them up to Heaven.* 

Therefore, we ought not to attempt to draw down or submit the mysteries of God to our Reason; but on the contrary, we must raise and advance our Reason to the Divine Truth. In this part of knowledge, touching Divine Philosophy <note>The study of God through the light of nature and reason</note>, I am so far from noting any deficiency that I rather note an excess. I have digressed into this point because of the extreme prejudice which both Religion and Philosophy have received, and may receive, by being mixed together; for such a mixture will undoubtedly create an heretical religion and an imaginary and fabulous philosophy.

Otherwise, it is different regarding the nature of Angels and Spirits, which is an appendix of Theology—both Divine and Natural—and is neither inscrutable nor forbidden. For although the Scripture says, *Let no man deceive you in sublime discourse touching the worship of Angels, pressing into that which he knows not, etc.* <note>A reference to Colossians 2:18</note>, yet nevertheless, if you observe that precept well, it may appear thereby that there are only two things forbidden: Adoration...

But the sober and grounded inquiry which may arise out of the passages of Holy Scriptures, or out of the gradations of nature, is not restrained. Likewise, regarding degenerate and revolted spirits <note>original: "reuolted fpirites"; referring to fallen angels or demons</note>, conversing with them or employing them is prohibited, and much more so any veneration toward them.

### The Second Book

The truth of Nature lies hidden in certain deep mines and *causes*. If it is also true, as the Alchemists <note>original: "Alchymists"</note> so frequently emphasize, that *Vulcan* <note>The Roman god of fire, here representing the art of metallurgy and chemistry</note> is a second Nature—imitating skillfully and efficiently what Nature produces through long, winding paths <note>original: "ambages"</note> and great lengths of time—it would be beneficial to divide Natural Philosophy into the "Mine" and the "Furnace." We might create two professions or occupations for Natural Philosophers: some to be pioneers <note>original: "Pionners"; here meaning miners or excavators</note> and some to be smiths; some to dig, and some to refine and hammer. 

I certainly most approve of a division of that kind, though expressed in more familiar and academic terms. Namely, that these are the two parts of Natural Philosophy: the **INQUIRY INTO CAUSES** and the **PRODUCTION OF EFFECTS**; the **SPECULATIVE** and the **OPERATIVE**; **NATURAL SCIENCE** and **NATURAL PRUDENCE**. 

Just as in civil matters there is a wisdom of theory and a wisdom of practice, so it is in natural matters. Here, I will make a request: that for the latter part (or at least for a portion of it), I may revive and restore the misapplied and abused name of **NATURAL MAGIC**. In its true sense, this is nothing but **NATURAL WISDOM** or **NATURAL PRUDENCE**, understood according to its ancient definition <note>original: "acception"</note> and purged of vanity and superstition. Now, although it is true—and I know it well—that there is a connection between *causes* and *effects*, so that both these branches of knowledge, *speculative* and *operative*, have a great...

## Of the Advancement of Learning

There is a great connection between these fields; yet because all true and fruitful **Natural Philosophy** <note>The study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern science.</note> has a double scale or ladder—**Ascending** and **Descending**—ascending from experiments to the discovery of causes, and descending from causes to the discovery of new experiments; therefore, I judge it most necessary that these two parts be considered and handled separately.

**Natural Science**, or **Theory**, is divided into **Physics** and **Metaphysics**. Regarding this, I desire it to be understood that I use the word "Metaphysics" in a different sense from how it is commonly received. In the same way, I doubt not but it will easily appear to men of judgment that in this and other particulars, wherever my conception and notion may differ from the Ancient ones, I am still careful to keep the **Ancient Terms**. 

Hoping to prevent any misunderstanding by the orderly and clear expression of what I propose, I am otherwise eager and devoted to depart as little from Antiquity—either in terms or opinions—as is consistent with truth and the progress of knowledge. In this, I cannot help but marvel at the philosopher **Aristotle**, who proceeded in such a spirit of difference and contradiction toward all Antiquity. He undertook not only to frame new words of science at his pleasure, but also to defeat and extinguish all ancient wisdom; so much so, that he never names <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "nameth" indicates the sentence continues on the next page.</note>

# The Second Book

Aristotle rarely names or mentions an ancient author or opinion except to refute and reprove them. In this, he took the right course for seeking glory and attracting followers and disciples. For certainly, that which was noted and pronounced in the highest truth also occurs and has a place in human truth: *I have come in my Father’s name, and you do not receive me; if another comes in his own name, him you will receive* <note>original Latin: "Veni in nomine Patris, nec recipitis Me, Si quis venerit in nomine suo, eum recipietis." A reference to John 5:43.</note>. 

But in this divine aphorism (considering to whom it was applied—namely to the Antichrist, the greatest deceiver), we may clearly discern that coming in one's own name, without regard for antiquity or the "fathers" of knowledge <note>original: "paternities"</note>, is no good sign of truth, even if it is joined with the good fortune and success of being "received" by the world. 

As for this excellent person Aristotle, I believe he learned that ambitious temperament from his pupil, Alexander the Great, with whom he seemed to compete: the one striving to conquer all opinions, as the other strove to conquer all nations. In this regard, Aristotle might receive a title from men of a bitter disposition similar to the one his pupil received:

*A fortunate robber of lands, who gave the world a harmful example* <note>original Latin: "Felix terrarum Praedo, non utile mundo Editus exemplum." A quote from Lucan regarding Alexander the Great.</note>.

So too:

*A fortunate robber of learning.*

But for my part, desiring as much as lies within my power to establish a friendly intercourse between antiquity and modern progress <note>original: "Proficience"</note>, it seems best to keep company with antiquity as far as possible <note>original Latin: "usque ad aras," literally "even to the altars," meaning to the furthest extent allowed by religion or truth.</note>. ## Of the Advancement of Learning

Even though I sometimes alter the uses and definitions of terms, I follow the moderate approach used in civil government; where, although there may be some actual change, that principle holds which *Tacitus* wisely noted: *The titles of the offices remain the same.* <note>original Latin: "Eadem Magistratuum vocabula." Tacitus was a Roman historian describing how Augustus kept old Republican titles while changing the underlying power structure.</note>

To return, therefore, to the use and accepted meaning of the term **Metaphysics**, as I now understand the word: it appears from what has already been said that I intend for **First Philosophy** (or **Summary Philosophy**) and **Metaphysics**—which until now have been confused as one thing—to be two distinct things. I have established the first as a parent or common ancestor to all knowledge. The other, **Metaphysics**, I have now categorized as a branch or descendant of **Natural Science**. 

It likewise appears that I have assigned to **Summary Philosophy** those common principles and axioms which are shared by and applicable to several different sciences. I have also assigned to it the inquiry into the operation of the *relative and external attributes of essences*—such as *Quantity, Similarity, Diversity, Possibility*, and the rest—with this distinction and provision: that they be handled according to how they function in nature, and not merely as logical concepts. 

It appears likewise that **Natural Theology**, which until now has been handled in a confused manner alongside **Metaphysics**, I have enclosed...

In this matter, I may—without prejudice—preserve this much of the concept of Antiquity: that **PHYSICS** <note>original: "PHISICKE"; here referring to the study of nature and the physical world, not the practice of medicine.</note> should contemplate that which is inherent in matter, and therefore transitory; and **METAPHYSICS**, that which is abstracted and fixed. And again, that **PHYSICS** should handle that which supposes in Nature only a being and moving, and **METAPHYSICS** should handle that which supposes further in Nature a reason, understanding, and platform <note>original: "platforme"; meaning a design, plan, or pattern.</note>. 

But the difference, when clearly expressed, is most familiar and sensible. For as we divided **NATURAL PHILOSOPHY** in general into the **INQUIRY OF CAUSES** and the **PRODUCTION OF EFFECTS**, so that part which concerns the **INQUIRY OF CAUSES** we do subdivide according to the received and sound division of **CAUSES**. The one part, which is **PHYSICS**, inquires into and handles the **MATERIAL** and **EFFICIENT CAUSES**; and the other, which is **METAPHYSICS**, handles the **FORMAL** and **FINAL CAUSES** <note>Bacon is using the four Aristotelian causes: Material (what it's made of), Efficient (how it's made), Formal (its essence or design), and Final (its purpose).</note>.

the causes, but VARIABLE or RELATIVE CAUSES, and METAPHYSICS the FIXED and CONSTANT CAUSES.

<margin>As this clay hardens, and as this wax melts. <note>original: "Limus ut hic durescit, & haec ut Cera liquescit." This is a quote from Virgil's Eclogues.</note></margin>
<margin>By one and the same fire. <note>original: "Uno eodemque igni."</note></margin>

Fire is the cause of hardening <term>induration</term>, but only in relation to clay; fire is the cause of melting <term>colliquation</term>, but only in relation to wax. Therefore, fire is not a constant cause of either hardening or melting. So then, the Physical causes are only the Efficient cause <note>the agent that produces an effect</note> and the Matter. PHYSICS has three parts: two of these regard Nature *united or collected*, and the third contemplates Nature *diffused or distributed*. Nature is collected either into one entire whole, or else into the same principles or seeds.

So, the first doctrine is CONCERNING the CONTEXT or CONFIGURATION of THINGS, such as *On the World* <note>original: "De Mundo"</note> or *On the Universe of Things* <note>original: "de universitate Rerum"</note>. The second is the doctrine CONCERNING the PRINCIPLES or ORIGINS of THINGS. The third is the doctrine CONCERNING all VARIETY and PARTICULARITY of THINGS, whether it be of the differing substances or their differing qualities and natures. There is no need to list these individually, as this part is merely like a GLOSS or PARAPHRASE that accompanies the text of NATURAL HISTORY. Of these three, I cannot report any as deficient. As to the truth or perfection with which they are handled, I make no judgment now. But

<summary>Francis Bacon distinguishes between Physics, which deals with variable and material causes, and Metaphysics, which deals with constant causes. He further divides Physics into the study of the universe's structure, its fundamental principles, and the variety of natural substances.</summary>
<keywords>Physics, Metaphysics, Efficient Cause, Matter, Natural History, Induration, Colliquation, Principles of Nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the branches of Natural Philosophy, specifically focusing on the definition and utility of Metaphysics.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>The second book.</header>

But they are parts of knowledge not deserted by the labor of man.

For **Metaphysics**, we have assigned to it the inquiry of **Formal** and **Final Causes**. <note>Formal causes refer to the essential nature or "blueprint" of a thing; Final causes refer to the purpose or "end" for which a thing exists.</note> This assignment, regarding the former of them, may seem to be trifling <note>original: "Nugatorie"</note> and useless because of the long-held and deep-seated opinion that human inquiry is not capable of finding out *essential forms* or *true differences*. Regarding this opinion, we will hold this position: that the discovery of forms is, of all other parts of knowledge, the worthiest to be sought, if it is possible to be found. As for the possibility, they are poor explorers who think there is no land simply because they can see nothing but sea. 

It is manifest that *Plato*, in his theory of *Ideas*, possessed a mind of such high elevation—as if situated upon a cliff—that he did discern *that forms were the true object of knowledge*. However, he lost the real fruit of his opinion by considering forms as being absolutely abstracted from matter, rather than confined and determined by matter. Thus, he turned his opinion toward *Theology*, with which his Natural Philosophy is infected. 

But if any man shall keep a continually watchful and rigorous eye upon action, operation, and the practical use of knowledge, he may observe and take notice of what the *forms* are, the disclosures of which are fruitful and important to the condition of mankind. For as to the *forms of substances* (excepting only Man, of whom it is said, *He formed man from the dust of the ground, and breathed into his face the breath of life* <note>original Latin: "Formavit hominem de limo terrae, et spiravit in faciem eius spiraculum vitae"; see Genesis 2:7</note>), and...

<summary>Bacon defines Metaphysics as the study of Formal and Final Causes, defending the search for "Forms" against skeptics while criticizing Plato for separating these forms too far from physical matter.</summary>
<keywords>Metaphysics, Formal Causes, Final Causes, Plato, Theory of Ideas, Natural Philosophy, Forms of Substances, Theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the division of the sciences, specifically focusing on the distinction between Physics and Metaphysics in the study of nature.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

The creation of man was not like that of all other creatures, of whom it was said, "Let the waters bring forth, let the earth bring forth" <note>original Latin: *Producent aqua, producat terra*; a reference to Genesis 1:20-24</note>. The <term>forms</term> <note>In this context, "forms" refers to the essential nature or structural laws of a thing</note> of substances—as they are now multiplied by compounding and transplanting—are so complex that they are not to be investigated in that state. It would be no more possible or useful than seeking to understand the "forms" of every complex sound that makes up a word, which are infinite due to the composition and transposition of letters. 

But on the other hand, to investigate the forms of those sounds or voices which make simple letters is easily understandable. Once known, these reveal the forms of all words which consist of and are compounded from them. In the same manner, to inquire into the "form" of a lion, of an oak, or of gold—or even of water and air—is a vain pursuit. 

Instead, we should investigate the forms of sense, of voluntary motion, of vegetation, of colors, of gravity and lightness <note>original: *Grauuitie and Leuitie*</note>, of density, of thinness <note>original: *Tenuitie*</note>, of heat, of cold, and all other natures and qualities. Like an alphabet, these are not many in number, yet they are the essences (supported by matter) of which all creatures consist. To investigate the true forms of these is that part of **Metaphysics** which we are now defining. 

This is not to say that **Physics** does not also inquire into and consider these same natures, but it does so differently. Physics only looks at the material and efficient causes <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, the "material cause" is what something is made of, and the "efficient cause" is the agent or force that brings it into being</note> of these things, and not their "forms." For example, if the cause of whiteness in snow or froth is investigated, and the explanation is given that "the subtle intermixture of air and water is the cause," the explanation is correct, but neverthe—

<summary>Bacon argues that Metaphysics should focus on discovering the "alphabet" of nature—the simple forms like heat and gravity—rather than trying to study complex individual substances like lions or gold directly.</summary>
<keywords>Metaphysics, Physics, Forms, Alphabet of Nature, Material Cause, Efficient Cause, Natural Philosophy, Francis Bacon</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the branches of Natural Philosophy, specifically transitioning from the limitations of Physics to the necessity of Metaphysics in understanding the "forms" of things.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
# The Second Book

Nevertheless, is this the "form" of whiteness? No; it is the efficient cause <note>original: "efficient," the agent or force that produces an effect</note>, which is always but the *carrier of the form* <note>original: "vehiculum formae"</note>. This part of **Metaphysics** <margin>Metaphysics: concerning forms and purposes of things</margin> I do not find labored or performed. I do not marvel at this, because I hold it impossible to be discovered by that course of invention which has been used; for men (which is the root of all error) have made too untimely a departure and too remote a withdrawal from particulars <note>Specific, observable facts or data</note>.

But the use of this part of **Metaphysics** which I report as deficient is, of all the rest, the most excellent in two respects. The first is because it is the duty and virtue of all knowledge to abridge the infinity of individual experience as much as the conception of truth will permit, and to remedy the complaint that *life is short, but art is long* <note>original: "vita brevis, ars longa"</note>. This is performed by uniting the notions and conceptions of the sciences. 

For knowledges are as **Pyramids**, whereof **History** is the **Base**. So, of **Natural Philosophy**, the **Base** is **Natural History**; the stage next to the base is **Physics**; the stage next to the vertical point is **Metaphysics**. As for the **Vertical Point**—*the work which God works from the beginning even to the end* <note>original: "Opus quod operatur deus a principio usque ad finem"</note>, or the **Summary Law of Nature**—we know not whether man's inquiry can attain unto it. But these three are the *true stages* of knowledge; though to those who are depraved <note>In this context, those with distorted or prideful minds</note>, they are no better than the *Giants' Hills* <note>A reference to the Greek myth of the giants piling mountains (Ossa and Pelion) on top of one another to storm heaven</note>.

### Of the Advancement of Learning

*Three times they attempted to pile Mount Ossa upon Pelion,*
*And indeed, to roll leafy Olympus upon Ossa.*
<note>original: "Ter sunt conati imponere Pelio Offam: Scilicet atque Offa frondosum inuoluere Olympum." This is a reference to the Greek myth of the Giants attempting to scale heaven to challenge the gods.</note>

But to those who refer all things to the glory of God, these levels of knowledge are like the three acclamations: *Holy, Holy, Holy*. God is holy in the description or expansion of His works; holy in the connection or linking of them; and holy in their union within a perpetual and uniform law. Therefore, the theory held by *Parmenides and Plato* was excellent—though in their case it was merely a theory—that all things ascend to unity by a scale or ladder. 

Thus, the worthiest knowledge is always that which is burdened with the least amount of variety. This appears to be <term>Metaphysics</term> <note>In Bacon's time, Metaphysics referred to the study of formal and final causes, or the fundamental principles of nature.</note>, as it considers the *simple forms or differences of things*. These forms are few in number, yet their *degrees and arrangements* create all the variety we see in the world. 

The second reason to value and commend this part of <term>Metaphysics</term> is that it liberates human power to its greatest extent, allowing for the widest possibility of works and effects. For <term>Physics</term> <note>original: "Phisicke"; here meaning the study of material and efficient causes in the natural world.</note> carries men along narrow and restricted paths, subject to many accidental obstacles and imitating the ordinary, winding courses of Nature. But *the ways of the wise are open on every side*. To wisdom (which was anciently defined as *the knowledge of things divine and human* <note>original: "Rerum diuinarum & humanarum scientia"</note>), there is always a choice of means. For <term>Physical</term> causes—

<summary>Bacon argues that Metaphysics is the highest form of knowledge because it seeks the simple, unifying laws of nature, which grants humans greater freedom and power to act than the specific, restricted paths of Physics.</summary>

...causes give light to new inventions in *similar matter* <note>original: "Simili materia"</note>. But whoever knows any "Form" <note>In Bacon's philosophy, a "Form" is the fundamental law or internal structure that governs a physical property</note> knows the utmost possibility of introducing that nature upon any variety of matter. Such a person is less restricted in their operation, being limited neither by the base material nor the specific conditions of the efficient cause. This kind of knowledge is elegantly described by Solomon, though in a more divine manner: *Your steps shall not be hampered, and when you run, you shall not stumble* <note>original: "Non arctabuntur gressus tui, & currens non habebis offendiculum"; Proverbs 4:12</note>. The ways of wisdom <note>original: "sapience"</note> are not easily hindered by specific obstacles or by chance.

The second part of **METAPHYSICS** is the **INQUIRY OF FINAL CAUSES** <note>Final Causes: The purpose or "end" for which a thing exists or is done</note>, which I am moved to report not as being omitted, but as being misplaced. If it were merely a fault in the order of presentation, I would not speak of it, for order is a matter of clarity but does not affect the substance of the sciences. However, this misplacing has caused a deficiency, or at least a great lack of progress, in the sciences themselves. 

The handling of final causes—when mixed with the rest in physical inquiries—has interrupted the severe and diligent inquiry of all real and physical causes. It has given men the excuse to stop at these *satisfactory and plausible causes* <note>original: "satisfactorie and specious cauſes"</note>, to the great delay and prejudice of further discovery. I find this has been done not only by **Plato**, who always anchors upon that shore, but also by **Aristotle**, **Galen**, and others, who usually run aground upon these shallows of "discursive causes." For example, to say that the hairs of the...

<summary>Bacon argues that understanding the fundamental "Forms" of nature allows for greater scientific freedom and asserts that "Final Causes" (teleology) should be separated from physical science because they discourage the investigation of mechanical causes.</summary>
