## Of the Advancement of Learning

Eyelids are for a quick-set <note>original: "quic-sette"; a living hedge or fence</note> and defense around the sight; or, that the firmness of the skins and hides of living creatures is to defend them from the extremities of heat or cold; or, that the bones are for the columns or beams upon which the frame of the bodies of living creatures are built; or, that the leaves of trees are for protecting the fruit; or, that the clouds are for watering the earth; or, that the solidity of the earth is for the station and mansion <note>original: "Manſion"; a permanent dwelling place</note> of living creatures: and the like. Such things are well inquired into and collected in **Metaphysics**, but in **Physics** they are impertinent <note>original: "impertinent"; irrelevant or not belonging to the subject</note>. 

Nay, they are indeed but remoras <note>term: A "remora" is a sucking-fish believed by ancients to have the power to slow down or stop a ship by attaching to it.</note> and hindrances to stay and sluggishly delay the ship from further sailing. They have brought it to pass that the search for **Physical Causes** has been neglected and passed over in silence. 

And therefore the Natural Philosophy of Democritus <note>A Greek philosopher (c. 460–370 BC) who proposed an atomic theory of the universe.</note> and some others—who did not suppose a Mind or Reason in the frame of things, but attributed the form of the world (being able to maintain itself) to infinite essays or proofs of Nature, which they term "fortune"—seems to me (as far as I can judge by the recitals and fragments which remain to us) more real and better inquired into regarding the particularities of Physical causes than that of Aristotle and Plato. Both of those men intermingled **final causes** <note>term: "Final causes" refer to the purpose or "end" for which a thing exists (e.g., "eyes are for seeing"). Bacon argues this belongs to philosophy/religion, not hard science.</note>: the one as a part of Theology, and the other as a part of Logic, which were the favorite studies respectively of both those persons. Not because...

<summary>Bacon argues that seeking the "purpose" of natural features (Final Causes) belongs to Metaphysics and actually hinders scientific progress in Physics. He praises the materialist approach of Democritus over the teleological approaches of Plato and Aristotle.</summary>

<keywords>Metaphysics, Physics, Final Causes, Democritus, Aristotle, Plato, Natural Philosophy, scientific method</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 32 regarding the distinction between "final causes" (purpose or design) and "physical causes" (material or mechanical processes) in natural philosophy.</meta>

# The Second Book

This is not because those final causes <note>original: "finall cauſes"; the ultimate purpose or "why" behind a natural phenomenon</note> are not true and worthy to be investigated, provided they are kept within their own province; rather, it is because their excursions into the limits of physical causes <note>original: "Phiſicall cauſes"; the mechanical or material "how" of a phenomenon</note> have created a wasteland and a neglect of study in that field. Otherwise, if they keep to their own boundaries and borders, men are extremely deceived if they think there is any enmity or inconsistency between them at all. 

For example, the cause given that the hairs around the *eyelids* are for the protection of the sight does not contradict the cause given that *pilosity* <note>hairiness</note> naturally occurs at the *openings* of *moisture*—as in the phrase, "mossy fountains," etc. <note>original Latin: "Muſcoſi fontes"; a reference to Virgil's Eclogues, suggesting that where there is moisture, growth naturally follows.</note> Nor does the cause given that the toughness of hides is for the armor of the body against extremes of heat or cold contradict the cause given that the contraction of pores naturally occurs in the outermost parts of a body because of their proximity to foreign or unlike substances. The same applies to the rest; both causes are true and compatible, with one declaring an intention <note>the divine purpose</note> and the other merely a consequence <note>the physical result of natural laws</note>. 

Neither does this approach call into question or detract from Divine Providence, but rather it highly confirms and exalts it. For just as in civil actions, he is the greater and deeper politician who can make other men the instruments of his will and ends without ever acquainting them with his purpose—so that they do what he desires and yet do not know what they are doing—rather than the one who must explain his meaning to those he employs. In the same way, the wisdom of God is more admirable when...

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that seeking the physical, mechanical causes of natural phenomena does not conflict with or disprove their divine purpose (final causes), but rather illustrates the sophisticated "politics" of God's providence.</summary>
<keywords>final causes, physical causes, natural philosophy, Divine Providence, teleology, scientific method, Francis Bacon</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
Nature intends one thing, and Providence draws forth another. This would be the case if He had communicated the characters and impressions of His Providence to particular creatures and motions. This concludes my discussion of <term>Metaphysics</term> <note>original: METAPHISICKE; the branch of philosophy dealing with first principles and the nature of being</note>, the latter part of which I acknowledge as currently existing, but I wish to see it confined to its proper place.

Nevertheless, there remains yet another part of <term>Natural Philosophy</term> <note>the study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern science</note> which is commonly treated as a principal branch and holds equal rank with specific <term>Physics</term> and <term>Metaphysics</term>: this is *Mathematics*. However, I think it more agreeable to the nature of things and to the clarity of logical order to place it as a branch of *Metaphysics*. 

The subject of Mathematics is *Quantity*. This is not *Indefinite Quantity*—which is merely a *Relative* concept and belongs to *First Philosophy* <note>original: Philoſophia Prima; the foundational universal principles common to all sciences</note> as previously stated—but rather *Determined* or *Proportionable Quantity*. It appears to be one of the essential forms of things, acting as a cause in nature for a number of *effects*. Indeed, we see in the schools of both *Democritus* and *Pythagoras* that the former ascribed *Figure* <note>shape or geometric form</note> to the first seeds of things, and the latter supposed *numbers* to be the *principles* and *origins* of things. It is also true that of all other forms (as we understand forms), quantity is the most abstracted and separable from matter, and therefore it is most properly suited to *Metaphysics*, which has...

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that Mathematics should be classified as a branch of Metaphysics rather than a primary division of Natural Philosophy, noting that quantity is an abstract form that shapes the physical world.</summary>

# The Second Book

This has likewise been the cause why it has been better labored and researched than any of the other *forms*, which are more immersed in matter. For it is the nature of the mind of man (to the extreme prejudice of knowledge) to delight in the spacious liberty of generalities, as if in an open plain, and not in the enclosures of particularity. Therefore, the **MATHEMATICS**, of all other knowledge, were the most beautiful fields to satisfy that appetite. But as for the placement of this science, it is not of great importance; we have only endeavored in these our partitions <note>original: "Partitions"; referring to the divisions or classifications of knowledge</note> to observe a kind of perspective, so that one part may cast light upon another.

The **MATHEMATICS** are either **PURE** or **MIXED**. To the **PURE MATHEMATICS** belong those sciences which handle <term>determinate quantity</term> <note>Quantity that is fixed or defined by measurement or number</note> entirely severed from any axioms of **NATURAL PHILOSOPHY**. These are two: **GEOMETRY** and **ARITHMETIC**; the one handling <term>quantity</term> continued <note>Continuous magnitude, such as lines or surfaces</note>, and the other <unclear>dissevered</unclear> <note>Discrete quantity, such as individual numbers</note>.

**MIXED** mathematics has for its subject some axioms or parts of Natural Philosophy, and considers <term>quantity</term> determined as it is auxiliary and incidental unto them. For many parts of Nature can neither be discovered with sufficient subtlety, nor demonstrated with sufficient clarity, nor accommodated

# Of the Advancement of Learning

unto use with sufficient dexterity, without the aid and intervention of Mathematics; of which sort are *Perspective*, *Music*, *Astronomy*, *Cosmography* <note>The study of the general features of the universe, including geography and astronomy.</note>, *Architecture*, *Engineering* <note>original: "Inginarie"</note>, and various others. In Mathematics, I can report no deficiency, except perhaps that men do not sufficiently understand the excellent use of pure Mathematics, in that they remedy and cure many defects in the mind <note>original: "Wit"</note> and *Intellectual* faculties. For, if the mind be too dull, they sharpen it; if too wandering, they fix it; if too inherent in the senses <note>meaning: too focused on physical objects rather than abstract thought</note>, they abstract it. So that, just as Tennis is a game of no use in itself, but of great use because it creates a quick eye and a body ready to put itself into all postures; so in Mathematics, that use which is collateral and intervening is no less worthy than that which is principal and intended. And as for Mixed Mathematics, I may only make this prediction: that there cannot fail to be more kinds of them as Nature grows further disclosed.

Thus much of NATURAL SCIENCE, or the SPECULATIVE part of Nature.

For NATURAL PRUDENCE <note>The practical application of knowledge; wisdom in action.</note>, or the OPERATIVE part of NATURAL PHILOSOPHY, we will divide it into three parts: EXPERIMENTAL, PHILOSOPHICAL, and MAGICAL. HISTORY, PHYSICS <note>original: "Phisicke"</note>, and METAPHYSICS: For many operations have been invented sometimes by a casual incident and occurrence, and sometimes by a purposeful experiment. Of those which have been found out by an intentional experiment, some have been found out by varying or extending the same experiment, and some by transferring and compounding diverse experiments one into the other—a kind of invention that an empiric <note>term: "Emperique"; a practitioner who relies on trial and error rather than underlying theory</note> may manage. Again, by the knowledge of physical causes, there cannot fail to follow many indications and designations of new particulars, if men in their speculation will keep one eye upon use and practice.

But these are but coastings along the shore, *hugging the uneven coast* <note>original Latin: "Premendo littus iniquum"</note>. For it seems to me there can hardly be discovered any radical or fundamental alterations and innovations in Nature, either by the fortune and essays of experiments, or by the light and direction of physical causes. If, therefore, we have reported METAPHYSICS deficient, it must follow that we do the like of <margin>Natural Magic</margin> NATURAL MAGIC, which has a relation thereunto. 

For as for the <margin>Natural Magic, or the operative part of Physics</margin> NATURAL MAGIC of which there is now mention in books—containing certain credulous and superstitious conceits and observations of sympathies and antipathies, and hidden properties <note>original: "Proprieties"</note>, and some frivolous experiments, strange rather by disguisement than in themselves—it is as far differing in truth of Nature from such a knowledge as we require, as the story of King *Arthur* of

of Britain, or *Hugh of Bordeaux* <note>original: "Hughe of Burdeaux"; a hero of popular medieval French romance</note>, differs from Caesar’s commentaries in the truth of the story. For it is manifest that Caesar did greater things in reality <note>original: "de vero"</note> than those *imaginary heroes* were feigned to do. But he did them not in that fabulous manner. Of this kind of learning, the fable of Ixion was a symbol: who designed to enjoy *Juno*, the goddess of power, and instead of her, had copulation with a cloud; from which mixture were fathered Centaurs and Chimeras. 

So, whoever shall entertain high and vaporous imaginations instead of a laborious and sober inquiry of truth, shall father hopes and beliefs of strange and impossible shapes. And therefore we may note in these sciences—which hold so much of imagination and belief, such as this degenerate natural magic, alchemy, astrology, and the like—that in their propositions, the description of the means is always more monstrous than the intended goal or end. 

For it is a thing more probable that he who knows well the natures of *weight*, of *color*, of *pliancy*, and *fragility* in respect to the hammer, of *volatility* and *fixity* in respect to the fire, and the rest, may superimpose upon some metal the nature and form of gold by such mechanics as belong to the production of the qualities previously rehearsed, than that some grains of the "medicine" <note>The "Philosopher's Stone" or elixir believed by alchemists to transform base metals into gold</note> projected should in a few moments of time turn a sea of quicksilver <note>mercury</note> or other material into gold.

So it is more probable that he who knows the nature of *drying* <note>original: "Arefection"</note>; the nature of *assimilation* (how nourishment becomes part of the thing nourished); the manner of *growth* and the *clearing of spirits*; the manner of the *preying* <note>original: "depredations"</note> which *spirits* <note>In early science, "spirits" were subtle fluids thought to govern vital functions.</note> make upon the *fluids* <note>original: "humours"</note> and *solid parts*: shall, by the *indirect paths* <note>original: "Ambages"</note> of *diets*, *bathings*, *anointings*, *medicines*, *exercises* <note>original: "motions"</note>, and the like, *prolong life* or *restore some degree of youth* or *vitality* <note>original: "viuacitie"</note>, than that it can be done with the use of a few *drops* or *tiny amounts* <note>original: "scruples"</note> of a *liquid* or *recipe*.

To conclude, therefore, the true NATURAL MAGIC—which is that great *liberty* and *latitude* of *operation* that *depends* upon the *knowledge of forms* <note>The essential nature or "blueprints" of things.</note>—I must report as deficient, just as its *relative* <note>Metaphysics</note> is. Regarding this part, if we are *serious* and do not *lean* toward *vanities* and *superficial talk* <note>original: "plausible discourse"</note>, there are two very *useful* points to consider besides *deriving* and *deducing* the *operations* themselves from METAPHYSICS: one by way of *preparation*, the other by way of *caution*.

<margin>An Inventory of Human Wealth.</margin>The first is that there should be made a *calendar* resembling an *inventory* of the *estate of man*. This would contain all the *inventions* (being the *works* or *fruits* of *Nature* or *Art*) which are now *existing* and which *man* already *possesses*. From this would *naturally* result a *list* of what *things* are still held to be *impossible* or *not yet invented*. This *calendar* will be the more *skillful* <note>original: "artificiall"</note> and *serviceable* if, to every *reputed impossibility*, you *add* what *thing* currently exists which...

comes the nearest in degree to that *impossibility*, to the end that by these *opiates* <note>original: "opiatiues"; here meaning remedies that dull the senses or provide false comfort</note> and *potentials*, man's inquiry may be the more awake in deducing the direction of works from the speculation of causes. And secondly, that those <term>experiments</term> should not only be valued which have an immediate and present use, but those principally which are of most universal consequence for the invention of other <term>experiments</term>, and those which give most light to the discovery of causes. For the invention of the <term>mariner's needle</term> <note>the magnetic compass</note>, which gives the direction, is of no less benefit for <term>navigation</term> than the invention of the sails which provide the motion.

Thus have I passed through <term>natural philosophy</term> and the deficiencies thereof. In this, if I have differed from the ancient and received doctrines, and thereby shall move contradiction; for my part, as I do not aim to dissent, so I do not purpose to contend, if it be truth.

> We do not sing to the deaf; the woods echo every note. <note>original Latin: "Non canimus surdis respondent omnia sylvae" from Virgil's Eclogues.</note>

The voice of Nature will consent, whether the voice of man does or not. And as <term>Alexander Borgia</term> <note>Pope Alexander VI</note> was wont to say of the expedition of the French for <term>Naples</term>, that they came with <term>chalk</term> in their hands to mark up their lodgings, and not with weapons to fight; so I prefer that entry of truth which comes peaceably with <term>chalk</term>, to mark up those minds which are capable to lodge and harbor it, than that which comes with pugnacity and contention.

philosophy according to the *Report of the Inquiry*, and nothing concerning the matter or subject; and that is POSITIVE and CONSIDERATIVE: when the inquiry reports either an Assertion or a Doubt. These *doubts or "it is not clear"* <note>original: "Non Liquets," a legal term used when a verdict cannot be reached due to lack of evidence</note> are of two sorts: *Particular* and *Total*. For the first, we see a good example in *Aristotle’s Problems*, which deserved to have had a better continuation; but nevertheless, there is one point of which warning must be given and taken. 

The registering of doubts has two excellent uses. The first is that it saves philosophy from errors and falsehoods: when that which is not fully apparent is not gathered into an assertion (whereby one error might lead to another) but is instead reserved as a doubt. The other use is that the recording of doubts acts like so many suckers or sponges to draw in the use of knowledge. Consequently, something that a man would never have noticed—but would have passed over without remark if a doubt had not preceded it—is instead attended to and studied because of the suggestion and urging of those doubts. 

But both these advantages scarcely outweigh a disadvantage which will intrude itself if it is not barred: which is that when a doubt is once received, men labor more on how to keep it a doubt than how to solve it, and they direct their wits accordingly. We see a familiar example of this in lawyers and scholars; if either has once admitted a doubt, it remains authorized as a doubt forever after. But that use of wit and knowledge is to be

# Of the Advancement of Learning

Those methods are to be praised which labor to make doubtful things certain, rather than those which labor to make certain things doubtful. Therefore, I commend these *Calendars of Doubts* <note>original: "Kalenders of doubts"; systematic lists of unresolved scientific questions</note> as excellent tools, provided this caution is used: once they are thoroughly examined and resolved, they should be removed and discarded, rather than continued in a way that encourages men to remain in a state of doubting.

<margin>The continuation of problems in Nature.</margin> To this *Calendar of Doubts or Problems*, I advise that another *Calendar* be attached—one just as important, if not more so. This is a *Calendar of Popular Errors*. I mean

<margin>A catalog of falsehoods prevalent in natural history.</margin> chiefly those errors in natural history that are commonly spoken of and believed, yet have been clearly detected and proven false. This is necessary so that human knowledge is not weakened or degraded by such dross and vanity.

<margin>Nature.</margin> As for the *Doubts or "It is not clear"* <note>original: "Non liquets"; a legal term used when a verdict cannot be reached due to lack of evidence</note> regarding general or total systems, I refer to those differences of opinion concerning the principles of Nature and its fundamental points. These have caused the diversity of sects, schools, and philosophies, such as those of *Empedocles, Pythagoras, Democritus, Parmenides*, and the rest. For although *Aristotle*—as if he were of the race of the *Ottoman* Sultans <note>A reference to the practice of "royal fratricide," where a new Sultan might execute his brothers to prevent civil war</note>—thought he could not reign unless his first act was to kill all his brothers, it must seem a matter of great profit to those who seek *truth* and not *mastery* <note>original: "Magistralitie"; the authoritative status of a teacher</note> to see before them the various opinions regarding the foundations of Nature. ## The Second Book

The same *astronomical phenomena* <note>original: "Phenomena in Astronomie"</note> are explained by the standard astronomy of the daily motion and the specific motions of the planets—with their *eccentrics* and *epicycles* <note>terms for the circular paths planets were thought to follow in the Ptolemaic system</note>—and likewise by the theory of *Copernicus*, who supposed the Earth to move. The calculations are equally consistent with both systems. In the same way, the ordinary appearance and view of experience is often satisfied by several different theories and philosophies, whereas finding the real truth requires a different level of rigor and attention. For, as *Aristotle* says, children at first will call every woman "mother," but afterward they come to distinguish according to the truth. So experience, while it is in its childhood, will call every *philosophy "mother"*; but when it reaches maturity, it will discern the true mother.

<margin>On Ancient Philosophies <note>original: "De Antiquis Philosophijs"</note></margin>

Therefore, in the meantime, it is good to examine the various interpretations and opinions on nature, in which it may be that every philosopher has seen more clearly than his fellows on some specific point. Therefore, I wish for a collection to be made, painstakingly and with understanding, *concerning ancient philosophies* <note>original: "de Antiquis Philosophijs"</note> from all the possible light that remains to us of them. I find this kind of work to be lacking. But here I must give warning that it should be done distinctly and rigorously: the philosophies of each individual should be presented entirely by themselves, and not packed and bundled together by topic, as has been done by *Plutarch*. For it is the internal harmony of a philosophy that gives it clarity and credibility; whereas if it is singled...

### Of the Advancement of Learning

<note>original: "gled and broken"</note> scattered and fragmented, it will seem more foreign and discordant. For example, when I read the actions of Nero or Claudius in the works of Tacitus, presented with the circumstances of the times, the motives, and the occasions, I do not find them so strange. But when I read them in Suetonius Tranquillus, gathered into categories and bundles rather than in chronological order, they seem more monstrous and incredible. The same is true of any philosophy: when reported as a whole, it makes sense, but when dismembered into separate articles, it loses its coherence. 

I do not exclude the opinions of more recent times from being likewise represented in this calendar of philosophical sects. This includes the work of Theophrastus Paracelsus <note>A famous Swiss physician and alchemist (1493–1541) who challenged traditional medical views.</note>, which was eloquently organized into a harmonious system by the pen of Severinus the Dane. It also includes the work of Telesio and his student Donius, which is like a "pastoral" philosophy—full of sensory observation but lacking great depth. I would also include Fracastoro, who, though he did not claim to create a new philosophy, used his own independent judgment to reinterpret the old systems. Finally, I include our countryman Gilbert <note>William Gilbert (1544–1603), an English physician and physicist famous for his work on magnetism.</note>, who revived the opinions of Xenophanes with some alterations and demonstrations, along with any other system worthy of being admitted.

Thus, we have now dealt with two of the three beams of human knowledge. These are the **Direct Beam** <note>original: "Radius Directus"</note>, which is directed toward Nature; and the **Refracted Beam** <note>original: "Radius Refractus"</note>, which is directed toward God, but cannot report the truth accurately because of the inequality of the medium <note>Bacon suggests that our knowledge of God is distorted, like light passing through water, because of the limitations of the human mind.</note>. There remains the **Reflected Beam** <note>original: "Radius Reflexus"</note>, by which man beholds and contemplates himself.

We come, therefore, now to that knowledge to which the ancient Oracle <note>A reference to the famous Greek maxim "Know Thyself," inscribed on the Temple of Apollo at Delphi.</note> directs us, which...

<page-num>36</page-num>
<header>The second book.</header>

This is the knowledge of ourselves, which deserves more accurate handling because it touches us more closely. This knowledge, while it is the goal and end-point of Natural Philosophy <note>original: "Naturall Philosophy"; in this context, the study of the physical world and its laws</note> in the mind of Man, is nevertheless only one portion of Natural Philosophy within the broader scope of Nature. 

Generally, let this be a rule: that all divisions of knowledge should be accepted as *lines and veins* rather than as *sections and separations*, and that the continuity and wholeness of knowledge be preferred. The contrary approach has caused particular sciences to become barren, shallow, and erroneous, because they have not been nourished and maintained from the common fountain. 

So we see *Cicero* the Orator complained of *Socrates* and his school, saying he was the first who separated Philosophy and Rhetoric; because of this, Rhetoric became an empty and verbal art. We may also see that the opinion of *Copernicus* regarding the rotation of the earth—which Astronomy itself cannot disprove because it does not contradict any of the observed phenomena <note>original: "Phainomena"</note>—may yet be corrected by Natural Philosophy. We see also that the science of Medicine, if it is abandoned and forsaken by Natural Philosophy, is not much better than an empirical practice <note>term: "Empeirical practize"; here meaning medical treatment based on trial and error or habit rather than scientific understanding</note>. 

With this reservation, therefore, we proceed to HUMAN PHILOSOPHY or HUMANITY, which has two parts: the one considers Man *segregated, or individually*; the other *congregated, or in society*. Thus, HUMAN PHILOSOPHY is either SIMPLE and PARTICULAR,

# Of the Advancement of Learning

Whether social <note>original: "conjugate"</note> or civil, **HUMANITY PARTICULAR** consists of the same parts of which Man consists: that is, of **KNOWLEDGE WHICH RESPECTS THE BODY**, and of **KNOWLEDGE THAT RESPECTS THE MIND**. 

But before we categorize these further, it is good to establish a general foundation. For I consider the general and broad consideration of **HUMAN NATURE** to be fit to be set free <note>original: "emancipate"</note> and made a branch of knowledge by itself. This is not so much because of those delightful and elegant discourses which have been made concerning the dignity of Man, his miseries, his state and life, and similar perspectives <note>original: "Advants"</note> on his common and undivided nature; rather, it is chiefly because of the knowledge concerning the **SYMPATHIES AND CONCORDANCES BETWEEN THE MIND AND BODY**. Because these are mixed, they cannot be properly assigned to the sciences of either the mind or the body alone.

This knowledge has two branches; for just as all alliances and friendships consist of mutual intelligence and mutual duties <note>original: "Offices"</note>, so this alliance of mind and body has these two parts: *How the one discloses the other, and how the one works upon the other*—Discovery and Impression. 

The first of these has given rise to two arts, both of *Prediction* or *Foreknowledge* <note>original: "Prenotion"</note>. One of these is honored by the inquiry of *Aristotle* <note>Aristotle (384–322 BCE), the Greek philosopher who wrote on physiognomy</note>, and the other by *Hippocrates* <note>Hippocrates (c. 460–370 BCE), the Greek physician known as the father of medicine</note>. And although in recent times they have often been coupled with superstitious...

## *The Second Book.*

superstitious and fantastical arts; yet being purged and restored to their true state, they both have a solid ground in nature and a profitable use in life. The first is **Physiognomy**, <note>The study of a person's facial features or body structure as an indication of character.</note> which discovers the disposition of the mind by the features of the body. The second is the **Interpretation of Natural Dreams**, which discovers the state of the body by the imaginations of the mind. In the former of these, I note a deficiency. For Aristotle has very ingeniously and diligently handled the structures of the body, but not the gestures of the body; which are no less understandable by art, and of greater use and advantage. For the features of the body do disclose the disposition and inclination of the mind in general; but the motions of the countenance and limbs do not only do that, but further disclose the present mood and state of the mind and will. For as your Majesty says most aptly and elegantly: *As the tongue speaks to the ear, so the gesture speaks to the eye.* And therefore a number of subtle persons, whose eyes dwell upon the faces and manners of men, well know the advantage of this observation, as it constitutes the better part of their abilities. Neither can it be denied that it is a great tool for discovering dissimulation <note>original: "dissimulations"; the act of concealing one's true thoughts or feelings.</note> and a great guide in business.

For the consideration is double: either **HOW, AND HOW FAR THE HUMORS AND AFFECTIONS OF THE BODY DO ALTER OR WORK UPON THE MIND;** or again, **HOW AND HOW FAR THE PASSIONS OR APPREHENSIONS OF THE MIND DO ALTER OR WORK UPON THE BODY.**

The former of these has been inquired into and considered as a part and appendix of Medicine, but much more as a part of Religion or superstition. For the Physician <note>original: "Philistian"; likely an OCR error or archaic variant for Physician</note> prescribes cures of the mind in frenzies and melancholy passions; and also claims to provide medicines to exhilarate the mind, to confirm courage, to clarify the wits, to strengthen the memory, and the like. 

But the scruples and superstitions regarding diet and other regimens of the body in the sect of the *Pythagoreans*, in the heresy of the *Manicheans*, and in the law of *Muhammad* <note>original: "Mahumet"</note> are excessive. So likewise, the ordinances in the Ceremonial Law <note>The Mosaic Law found in the Old Testament</note>—forbidding the eating of blood and fat, and distinguishing between beasts clean and unclean for meat—are many and strict. Nay, the Christian faith itself, being clear and serene from all clouds of ceremony, yet retains the use of fastings, abstinences, and other macerations <note>term: macerations; the act of weakening or wearing down the body through fasting or hardship</note> and humiliations of the body, as things real and not merely figurative. The root and life of all these prescriptions is (besides the religious ceremony) the consideration of that dependency to which the affections of the mind are submitted...

## The Second Book.

The mind is subject to the state and disposition of the body. If any man of weak judgment should conceive that this suffering of the mind caused by the body either questions the immortality or detracts from the sovereignty of the soul, he may be taught by simple examples. For instance, the infant in the mother’s womb is affected by the mother <note>original: "is compatible with," meaning sharing the same conditions or suffering together</note>, and yet is a separate being. Likewise, the most absolute monarch is sometimes led by his servants, yet without being in subjection to them.

As for the reciprocal knowledge—which is the operation of the thoughts and passions of the mind upon the body—we see that all wise physicians, in the prescriptions of their regimens for their patients, always consider the *accidents of the mind* <note>original: "Accidentia animi"; refers to mental states, emotions, or psychological factors</note> as having great force to either further or hinder remedies and recoveries. 

More specifically, there is an inquiry of great depth and worth concerning **IMAGINATION**: how, and how far, it alters the physical body of the person imagining. For although it has a manifest power to do harm, it does not follow that it has the same degree of power to help. One can no more conclude this than one can conclude that because there are pestilent airs <note>original: "Ayres"; referring to the "miasma" theory that disease was spread by bad air</note> able to suddenly kill a healthy man, there must therefore be sovereign <note>meaning: supreme or highly effective</note> airs able to suddenly cure a sick man. 

But the investigation of this area is of great use, though it requires, as **Socrates** said, *a Delian diver* <note>original: "A Delian diver"; a proverbial expression for a task requiring great skill and depth, referring to the deep-sea divers of Delos</note>, being difficult and profound. This knowledge has been attempted and is debated, but it deserves to be much better investigated. For the opinion of *Plato*, who placed the *understanding* in the *brain*; *animosity* (which he unjustly called *anger*, as it has a greater mixture of *pride*) in the *heart*; and *concupiscence* <note>original: "Concupiscence"; intense sexual desire or lust</note> or *sensuality* in the *liver*, deserves not to be despised, but much less to be fully accepted. So then, we have established (according to our own judgment and advice) the inquiry CONCERNING HUMAN NATURE AS A WHOLE as a proper branch of knowledge to be handled on its own.

The knowledge that concerns man's body is divided in the same way the "good" of man's body is divided, to which it refers. The good of man's body is of four kinds: *Health*, *Beauty*, *Strength*, and *Pleasure*. Accordingly, the types of knowledge are *Medicine* (or the *Art of Cure*); the *Art of Decoration* (which is called *Cosmetic*); the *Art of Activity* (which is called *Athletic*); and the *Voluptuary Art*, which *Tacitus* truly calls *educated luxury* <note>original: "Eruditus Luxus"</note>. This subject of man's body is, of all other things in nature, most capable of receiving a remedy; but then, that *remedy* is most liable to error. For the same *subtlety* <note>original: "Subtilitie"; here meaning complexity or intricacy</note> of the subject causes both great *possibility* for success and an easy *failure*; therefore, the inquiry ought to be all the more exact.

To speak therefore of *Medicine*, and to resume what we have said by ascending a little higher: the ancient opinion...

### The Second Book

The opinion that Man was a *Microcosm* <note>original: "Microcosmus"; a "little world" that mirrors the universe</note>, an abstract or model of the world, has been fantastically strained by *Paracelsus* and the *Alchemists*. They suggest that there are certain correspondences and parallels to be found in the human body which relate to all varieties of things—such as stars, planets, and minerals—that exist in the great world. 

But this much is evidently true: that of all substances which Nature has produced, the human body is the most extremely complex. For we see that herbs and plants are nourished by earth and water; beasts, for the most part, by herbs and fruits; but Man is nourished by the flesh of beasts, birds, fish, herbs, grains, fruits, water, and the manifold alterations, dressings, and preparations of these several bodies before they become his food and sustenance. Add to this that beasts have a simpler way of life and fewer changes of emotions to affect their bodies, whereas man—in his housing, sleep, exercise, and passions—has infinite variations. It cannot be denied that the *body of Man*, above all other things, is of the most complex composition. 

The soul, on the other hand, is the simplest of substances, as is well expressed: 

*And there remains a pure, heavenly sense, and the fire of simple air.* <note>original: "Purumque reliquis Aethereum sensum, atque Aurai simplicis ignem" — from Virgil's Aeneid, describing the purified soul.</note>

So it is no marvel, though the soul is so placed, that it enjoys no rest, if that principle be true that *the motion of things is rapid outside of their proper place, but calm within their place.* <note>original: "Motus rerum est rapidus extra locum. Placidus in loco."</note> But to the purpose: this variable composition of the human body has made it like an instrument easily put out of tune; and therefore the Poets did well to

### Of the Advancement of Learning

They join **Music** and **Medicine** in the god Apollo, because the duty of medicine is simply to tune this curious harp of man's body and to restore it to **Harmony**. Thus, the **Subject** (the human body) being so **Variable**, has consequently made the **Art** more **conjectural** <note>original: "coniecturall"; based on guesswork or incomplete information</note>. Because the **Art** is **Conjectural**, it has left much more room for deception. 

Almost all other arts and sciences are judged by their **Acts**, or "masterpieces" as I may call them, and not by their final successes or outcomes. The **Lawyer** is judged by the virtue of his pleading and not by the final issue of the case. The **Master** of a **Ship** is judged by directing his course correctly and not by the fortune of the **Voyage**. 

But the **Physician** <note>original: "Philiſtian" (likely an OCR error for Physician)</note>, and perhaps the **Politician** <note>original: "Politique"</note>, has no particular **Acts** that clearly demonstrate his ability; instead, he is judged mostly by the event. This outcome is always open to interpretation; for who can tell, if a **Patient** dies or recovers, or if a **State** is preserved or ruined, whether it was the result of **Art** or **Accident**? Therefore, many times the **Impostor** is praised, and the man of true merit is criticized. 

Indeed, we see that the weakness and gullibility of men is such that they will often prefer a **Mountebank** <note>a person who deceives others, especially a seller of quack medicines</note> or a **Witch** over a learned **Physician**. Therefore, the **Poets** were clear-sighted in discerning this extreme folly when they made **Aesculapius** <note>the god of medicine</note> and **Circe** <note>the enchantress/witch</note> brother and sister, both children of the **Sun**, as in the verses:

*The Father himself, with a thunderbolt, hurled the son of Apollo, the discoverer of such medicine and art, down to the Stygian waters.* <note>original Latin: "Ipſe repertorem medicinæ talis & artis, Fulmine Phœbigenam ſtygias detruſit ad vndas" from Virgil's Aeneid. It describes Jupiter striking down Aesculapius for bringing the dead back to life.</note>

### The second book.

And again:
*Where the wealthy daughter of the Sun occupies inaccessible groves, etc.* <note>original Latin: "Dives inaccessos ubi Solis filia Lucos, &c." from Virgil's Aeneid, referring to Circe the enchantress.</note>

For in all times, in the opinion of the common people, witches, old women, and impostors have been in competition with physicians. And what follows? Even this: that physicians say to themselves, as Solomon expressed it on a higher occasion: *If it happens to me as it happens to the fool, why should I labor to be more wise?* <note>A reference to Ecclesiastes 2:15.</note>

And therefore I cannot much blame physicians if they commonly tend to pursue some other art or practice which they enjoy more than their profession. For you shall find among them: antiquaries, poets, humanists, statesmen, merchants, and divines <note>term: "divines" refers to theologians or members of the clergy</note>; and in every one of these, they are better respected than in their own profession. No doubt this is because they find that mediocrity and excellence in their art make no difference in profit or reputation toward their fortune. For the weakness of patients, the sweetness of life, and the nature of hope make men depend upon physicians despite all their defects.

But nevertheless, these things which we have spoken of are courses of action born from a small excuse and a great deal of sloth and neglect. For if we will excite and awake our observation, we shall see in familiar instances what a predominant faculty the *subtlety of the spirit* has over the *variety of matter or form*. Nothing is more variable than faces and countenances; yet men can hold in their memory the infinite distinctions between them. Nay, a painter with a few shells of colors...

<summary>Bacon argues that because the public cannot distinguish between a skilled doctor and a fraud, physicians often neglect their medical studies for other hobbies; however, he insists that human intellect is capable of mastering even the most complex subjects.</summary>
<keywords>medicine, physicians, reputation, Solomon, Circe, human intellect, observation, arts and sciences</keywords>

### Of the Advancement of Learning,

colors, and the benefit of his eye and the habit of his imagination can imitate all that ever have been, are, or may be, if they were brought before him. Nothing is more variable than voices, yet men can likewise discern them personally; indeed, you shall find a jester <note>original: "Buffon"</note> or a mime <note>original: "Pantomimus"</note> who will express as many as he pleases. Nothing is more variable than the differing sounds of words, yet men have found the way to reduce them to a few simple letters. 

So, it is not the *insufficiency or incapacity of man's mind*, but it is the *remote standing or placing of it* that breeds these mazes and failures of understanding. For just as the sense is full of mistakes from far away but is exact at hand, so it is with the understanding. The remedy for this is not to sharpen or strengthen the organ of the mind, but to go nearer to the object. Therefore, there is no doubt that if physicians <note>original: "Philistians"; here Bacon likely uses the term to refer to practitioners of the physical arts or perhaps a play on "physicians"</note> will learn and use the true approaches and avenues of nature, they may achieve as much as the poet says:

> *And since diseases vary, we will vary our arts;*
> *There are a thousand species of evil, and there shall be a thousand means of healing.*
> <note>original Latin: "Et quoniam variant Morbi, variabimus artes, Mille Mali species, mille Salutis erunt." From Ovid's Remedia Amoris.</note>

That they should do this is deserved by the nobility of their art. This was well foreshadowed by the poets when they made Aesculapius <note>The Greek god of medicine and healing.</note> to be the son of the Sun <note>Apollo</note>; the one being the fountain of life, the other acting as the second stream. But this art is infinitely more honored by the example of our Savior, who made the body of man the object of his miracles, just as the soul was the object of his doctrine. For we do not read that he ever deigned to perform any miracle concerning honor,

<summary>Bacon argues that the human mind is capable of mastering complex natural variations if it approaches them closely, specifically urging physicians to advance the noble art of medicine, which was honored by Christ's own miracles.</summary>

## The Second Book.

or money (except for that one instance regarding the giving of tribute to *Caesar*), but only about preserving, sustaining, and healing the body of man.

*Medicine* is a science which has been (as we have said) more professed than labored upon, and yet more labored upon than advanced; the labor having been, in my judgment, rather in a circle than in progression. For I find much repetition <note>original: "Iteration"</note>, but small addition. It considers the *causes of diseases, along with their triggers or impulses*; the *diseases themselves, along with their symptoms* <note>original: "Accidents"</note>; and the *cures, along with the preventions*. The deficiencies which I think good to note—being a few out of many, and those of a more open and manifest nature—I will list individually rather than categorize.

<margin>Medical Narratives <note>original: "Narrationes Medicinales"</note></margin>
The first deficiency is the discontinuance of the ancient and serious diligence of *Hippocrates*, who used to set down a *narrative* of the special cases of his patients: how the illness proceeded, and how they were resolved by recovery or death. Therefore, having a proper example in the "father of the art," I shall not need to cite a foreign example, such as the wisdom of the lawyers, who are careful to report new cases and decisions for the direction of future judgments. This continuation of *medical history* I find deficient. I do not mean for this to be so infinite as to extend to every *common case*, nor so reserved as to admit nothing but *wonders* or miracles. For many things are new in their *manner* of appearing which are not new in their *kind*; and if men will intend to observe, they shall find much worthy of observation.

<summary>Francis Bacon critiques the field of medicine for its lack of progress and identifies the first major deficiency as the loss of detailed clinical case histories, a practice started by Hippocrates.</summary>
<keywords>Medicine, Hippocrates, medical history, clinical observation, scientific progress, legal precedents, pathology</keywords>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the general state of medicine and the need for a more systematic approach to the study of the human body. This page continues that critique by focusing on the limitations of contemporary anatomical study.</meta>

## Of the Advancement of Learning

<margin>Comparative Anatomy.</margin> In the inquiry which is made by *Anatomy*, I find much deficiency. For physicians inquire into the *parts* and their *substances, figures*, and *locations* <note>original: "collocations"</note>; but they do not inquire into the *diversities* of those parts, the *secrets* of the *passages*, and the *seats* or *nesting* <note>original: "neafling"</note> of the *humors* <note>The four fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—thought to determine health and temperament.</note>. Nor do they inquire much into the *footsteps* and *impressions* of *diseases*. I suppose the reason for this omission is because the first inquiry can be satisfied by viewing one or a few *anatomies* <note>dissections</note>; but the latter, being comparative and based on specific cases, must arise from the viewing of many. 

As to the diversity of parts, there is no doubt that the structure or framing of the inward parts is as full of difference as the outward parts. In that structure lies the *containing cause* <note>original: "Cause Continent"; the immediate internal cause of a disease</note> of many diseases. Because this is not observed, physicians many times blame the humors which are not at fault, when the fault actually lies in the very frame and *mechanics* of the part itself. Such a fault cannot be removed by alterative medicine <note>medicines intended to alter the humors</note>, but must be accommodated and palliated by diets and familiar medicines. 

And regarding the passages and pores, it is true what was anciently noted: that the more subtle of them do not appear in dissections because they are shut and hidden in dead bodies, though they are open and manifest in the living. This being granted, although the inhumanity of *vivisection* <note>original: "Anatomia vivorum"; the dissection of living subjects</note> was justly reproved by *Celsus* <note>A Roman medical writer of the 1st century AD.</note>, yet in regard to the great use of this observation, the inquiry did not need to be so lightly abandoned by him altogether, or referred only to the casual practices of surgery. It might have been well redirected toward the dissection of living beasts.

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that anatomical study is too limited, focusing only on general structures rather than the individual variations and mechanical flaws that cause disease. He suggests that comparative anatomy and the dissection of animals could reveal physiological processes that are invisible in human corpses.</summary>

<keywords>Anatomy, Comparative Anatomy, Humors, Pathology, Celsus, Vivisection, Medical Research, Physiology</keywords>

### The Second Book

Even though their parts are dissimilar, these examples may sufficiently satisfy this inquiry. As for the humors <note>original: "humors"; the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—thought to determine health and temperament</note>, they are commonly passed over in anatomical studies as if they were merely waste products. However, it is most necessary to observe what cavities, nests, and receptacles these humors find within the body's parts, along with the differing kinds of humors lodged and received there. 

As for the traces of diseases and their devastation of the inward parts—such as abscesses <note>original: "imposthumations"</note>, ulcerations, ruptures of continuity, putrefactions, wasting diseases <note>original: "consumptions"</note>, contractions, swellings, convulsions, dislocations, obstructions, and over-fullness <note>original: "repletions"</note>—together with all unnatural substances like stones, fleshy growths <note>original: "carnosities"</note>, excrescences, worms, and the like: these ought to have been exactly observed through a multitude of dissections <note>original: "Anatomies"</note> and the contribution of various men's experiences. They should be carefully recorded both historically, according to how they appeared, and scientifically, with reference to the diseases and symptoms that resulted from them in cases where the dissection is of a deceased patient. Currently, however, upon the opening of bodies, these details are passed over slightly and in silence.

<margin>Further inquiry into incurable diseases.</margin> In the inquiry into diseases, physicians abandon the cure of many patients—some because the diseases are considered incurable by nature, and others because they are judged to be past the point of recovery. Consequently, Sulla and the Triumvirs <note>Roman leaders known for "proscriptions," or lists of enemies sentenced to death</note> never condemned so many men to die as these physicians do by their ignorant edicts; many of these patients actually escape death with less difficulty than those did during the Roman proscriptions. Therefore, I will not hesitate to note it as a deficiency that they do not investigate...

<summary>Bacon critiques the medical field for failing to study the physical effects of disease on internal organs during dissections and for prematurely declaring many illnesses incurable.</summary>

the perfect cures of many diseases, or extreme cases of diseases; instead, by pronouncing them incurable, they enact a law of neglect and exempt their own ignorance from discredit.

<margin>Concerning external Euthanasia <note>original: De Euthanasia exteriore</note></margin>
Nay, further, I esteem it the office of a physician not only to restore health, but to mitigate pain and sorrows—and not only when such mitigation may lead to recovery, but even when it may serve to make a fair and easy passage. For it is no small happiness which *Augustus Caesar* used to wish for himself, that same *Euthanasia* <note>An easy or painless death</note>, and which was specially noted in the death of *Antoninus Pius*, whose death was in the fashion and likeness of a gentle and pleasant sleep. So it is written of *Epicurus*, that after his disease was judged desperate, he drowned his stomach and senses with a large draught and gulping of wine, whereupon the epigram was made: *From here, he drank the Stygian waters while intoxicated* <note>original: Hinc stygias Ebrius hausit aquas. The Styx was the river of the underworld in Greek mythology.</note>. He was not sober enough to taste any bitterness of the Stygian water. But physicians, on the contrary, make a kind of moral scruple and religious point of leaving the patient after the disease is despaired of; whereas, in my judgment, they ought both to research the skill and provide the attendance necessary for facilitating and assuaging the pains and agonies of death.

<margin>Experimental medicines <note>original: Medicine experimentales</note></margin>
In the consideration of the cures of diseases, I find a deficiency in "specific recipes" <note>original: Receipts of propriety; refers to medicines uniquely suited to a particular ailment</note> regarding the particular cures of diseases. For physicians have frustrated the fruit of tradition and experience by their "magistralities" <note>The practice of prescribing custom-mixed formulas based on theoretical rules rather than proven, specific remedies</note>, in adding and taking out and...

<margin>Nature in baths and medicinal waters <note>original: Natura in Balneis, & Aquis Medicinalibus</note></margin>
and changing one thing for another <note>original: Quid pro quo</note> in their prescriptions at their pleasure, commanding the medicine in such a way that the medicine cannot command the disease. For except for Treacle <note>A complex medicinal compound, often used as an antidote</note>, Mithridatum <note>A semi-mythical remedy with many ingredients, believed to cure all poisons</note>, and recently *Diascordium* <note>An electuary made from water germander, used to treat the plague and other fevers</note>, and a few others, they bind themselves to no prescriptions strictly or religiously. As for the commercial confections sold in shops, they are made for readiness and not for specific suitability; they are intended for general purposes like purging, opening, comforting, or altering the body, and are not specifically tailored to particular diseases. 

This is the reason why empirics <note>Unlicensed practitioners who rely on experience rather than formal theory</note> and old women are often more successful in their cures than learned physicians: because they are more faithful in sticking to their proven medicines. Therefore, the deficiency I find here is that physicians—partly from their own practice, partly from the consistent trials reported in books, and partly from the traditions of empirics—have not recorded and handed down certain *experimental medicines* for the cure of particular diseases, beyond their own *conjectural* and *magisterial* <note>Prescriptions formulated on the spot by a physician's authority</note> descriptions. 

Just as the men of the best character in the State of **Rome** were those who, being Consuls, favored the people, or being Tribunes, favored the Senate; so in the matter we are now handling, the best physicians are those who, being learned, lean toward the traditions of experience, or being <margin>Imitation <note>original: Imitatio</note></margin> empirics, lean toward the methods of learning. 

In the preparation of medicines, I find it strange—especially considering how mineral medicines have

Not only that, but they are discerned and distinguished by what particular *mineral* they receive their *tincture* <note>The characteristic quality or medicinal infusion</note>, such as *sulfur*, *vitriol* <note>Sulfuric acid or metallic sulfates</note>, *steel*, or the like. <margin>The Thread of Medicine, or of the Successions of Remedies.</margin>But lest I grow more detailed than is agreeable either to my intention or to the proportion of this work, I will conclude this part with the note of one more deficiency, which seems to me of greatest consequence. This is that the prescriptions currently in use are too brief and simplified to attain their end. To my understanding, it is a vain and flattering opinion to think any *medicine* can be so sovereign or so effective that the single receipt or use of it can work any great effect upon the body of man. It would be a strange speech which, spoken once or even spoken often, should reclaim a man from a vice to which he was by nature subject. It is order, pursuit, sequence, and the interchange of application which is mighty in nature. Although this requires more exact knowledge in prescribing and more precise obedience in observing the treatment, it is compensated by the magnitude of the effects. Not that every scrupulous or superstitious prescription is effectual, no more than every straight way is the way to heaven, but the *truth of the direction* must precede *severity of observance*.

For *Cosmetics* <note>original: "Cosmetique"</note>, it has parts that are civil and parts that are effeminate. For cleanliness of body was ever esteemed to proceed from a due reverence to God, to society, and to ourselves. As for artificial decoration <note>referring here to makeup or "painting" the face</note>, it is well worthy of the deficiencies which it has: being neither fine enough to deceive, nor handsome to use, nor wholesome to please.

For *Athletics* <note>original: "Athletique"</note>, I take the subject of it largely; that is to say, for any point of ability whereunto the body of man may be brought, whether it be of *Activity* or of *Patience*. Of these, *Activity* has two parts: *Strength* and *Swiftness*. And *Patience* likewise has two parts: *Hardness against wants and extremities* <note>the ability to withstand deprivation or harsh environments</note>, and *Endurance of pain or torment*. We see the practices of these in tumblers, in savages, and in those that suffer punishment. Nay, if there be any other faculty which falls not within any of the former divisions—such as in those that dive, who obtain a strange power of containing respiration <note>holding one's breath</note>, and the like—I refer it to this part. Of these things the practices are known, but the philosophy that concerns them is not much inquired into; the rather, I think,

Although this is not true, I still refrain from noting any deficiencies here; for the Olympian Games have long since ended, and a moderate skill in these things is sufficient for practical use. As for excellence in them, it serves for the most part only for mercenary display <note>original: "ostentation"</note>.

Regarding the *arts of sensual pleasure*, the chief deficiency in them is a lack of *laws* to repress them. For as it has been well observed, the arts that flourish in times when virtue is growing are *military*; while virtue is at its peak <note>original: "in State"</note>, they are *liberal* <note>The "liberal arts," such as rhetoric and philosophy, associated with a free and stable society.</note>; and while virtue is in decline, they are *voluptuary* <note>Devoted to luxury and physical pleasure.</note>. Thus, I fear that this age of the world is somewhat on the downward turn of the wheel. With *voluptuary* arts, I couple *jocular* practices <note>original: "practises Iocularie"; referring to sleight-of-hand or illusions.</note>; for deceiving the senses is one of the pleasures of the senses. As for games of recreation, I hold that they belong to civil life and education. And this concludes the discussion of that particular Human Philosophy **WHICH CONCERNS THE BODY**, which is but the tabernacle <note>A temporary dwelling or shelter.</note> of the mind.

For **HUMAN KNOWLEDGE WHICH CONCERNS THE MIND**, it has two parts: the one that inquires into **THE SUBSTANCE OR NATURE OF THE SOUL OR MIND**; the other, that inquires into the **FACULTIES OR FUNCTIONS**—

### The Second Book

Functions thereof: *unto the first of these,* <note>The "first of these" refers to the study of the substance or nature of the soul.</note> *the considerations of the origin of the soul—whether it is native <note>original: "Native"; meaning inherent or born within the body</note> or adventive <note>original: "aduentive"; meaning coming from the outside</note>; and how far it is exempted from the laws of matter; and of its immortality; and many other points—do appertain. These have been no more laboriously inquired into than they have been variously reported; so that the work undertaken therein seems to have been more like wandering in a maze than following a clear path.* 

But although I am of the opinion that this knowledge may be more realistically and soundly inquired into even within nature than it has been, yet I hold that in the end it must be bounded by religion; otherwise, it will be subject to deceit and delusion. For just as the substance of the soul during Creation was not extracted out of the mass of heaven and earth by the blessing of a "Let it bring forth" <note>original Latin: "Producat"; referring to Genesis 1:24, where God commands the earth to bring forth living creatures</note>, but was immediately inspired by God; so it is not possible that the soul should be (otherwise than by accident) *subject to the laws of heaven and earth, which are the subject of the sciences.* 

And therefore, the true knowledge of the nature and state of the soul must come by the same inspiration that gave it its substance. To this part of knowledge concerning the soul, there are two appendices which, in the way they have been handled, have rather exhaled fables than kindled truth: Divination and Fascination <note>original: "Fascination"; the power of charming or influencing others, often associated with the "evil eye" or hypnotic influence</note>.

Divination has been anciently and appropriately divided into *Artificial* and *Natural*. *Artificial* divination is when the mind makes a prediction by argument, concluding based upon signs and tokens. Natural...

<summary>Bacon argues that because the human soul was divinely inspired rather than created from earthly matter, its true nature must be understood through religion rather than just natural philosophy. He also introduces the study of divination and fascination as secondary branches of this knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>soul, immortality, creation, divinity, natural philosophy, divination, fascination, artificial divination, natural divination</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the faculties of the soul, specifically focusing on the nature of Divination and human foresight.</meta>

### Of the Advancement of Learning

Natural divination occurs when the mind has a premonition by an internal power, without being prompted by an external sign. *Artificial* divination is of two types: either when the argument is coupled with a derivation of causes (which is *rational*); or when it is only grounded upon a coincidence of the effect (which is *experimental*). This latter type is, for the most part, superstitious. Examples include the observations made by the Heathens upon the inspection of sacrifices, the flights of birds, and the swarming of bees; as well as *Chaldean Astrology* <note>The ancient Babylonian practice of predicting individual fates and world events based on planetary positions</note> and similar practices. 

As for *Artificial Divination*, its various kinds are distributed among specific fields of knowledge. The *Astronomer* has his predictions, such as conjunctions, aspects, eclipses, and the like. The Physician has his predictions regarding death, recovery, and the symptoms and outcomes of diseases. The Politician has his predictions, such as the saying: *"O city for sale, and doomed to perish, if it can find a purchaser!"* <note>original: "O urbem venalem [corrected from versatem], si emptorem invenerit"; a quote attributed to Jugurtha regarding the corruption of Rome</note>—a prediction which did not wait long to be fulfilled, first under *Sulla* and later under *Caesar*. 

Since these types of predictions belong to other fields, they are irrelevant here and should be referred to their respective sciences. But the *Divination* which springs from the internal nature of the soul is what we are now discussing. This has been categorized into two sorts: *Primitive* and by *Influxion* <note>The idea of divine or spiritual influence flowing into the soul from without</note>. *Primitive* divination is grounded upon the supposition that the mind—when it is withdrawn and collected into itself, and not diffused into the organs of the body—has some extent and range of foreknowledge. This, therefore, appears most often in sleep, in ecstasies <note>Trance-like states or intense spiritual experiences</note>, and near death, and more rarely in waking apprehensions; and is induced...

<summary>Bacon distinguishes between "artificial" divination (based on science or superstition) and "natural" divination, which he suggests arises from the soul's inherent power when detached from bodily senses.</summary>
<keywords>divination, astrology, astronomy, medicine, politics, Sulla, Caesar, soul, premonition, ecstasies</keywords>

# The Second Book

<page-num>46</page-num>

...produced and furthered by those abstinences and observances which make the mind most self-contained. Divination by "influxions" <note>original: "influxions"; the idea of divine or spiritual influence flowing into the mind</note> is grounded upon the concept that the mind, like a mirror or glass, should receive illumination from the foreknowledge of God and spirits. The same mental discipline <note>original: "Regiment"</note> also leads to this state. For the retiring of the mind within itself is the state most susceptible to divine influences—except that in this case, it is accompanied by a fervor and elevation (which the ancients called "fury" <note>original: "furie"; referring to divine frenzy or poetic inspiration</note>) and not with a repose and quiet, as it is in the other state.

Fascination <note>The power of the mind or eye to influence others, often associated with "the evil eye" or hypnosis</note> is the power and act of the Imagination when it is directed intensely upon bodies other than the body of the person imagining. (We spoke of the imagination's effect on one's own body in the proper place). In this matter, the school of Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist</note> and the disciples of so-called "Natural Magic" have been so immoderate that they have exalted the power of the imagination to be almost the same as the power of miracle-working faith. 

Others, who come closer to probability, consider the secret passages of things—especially the way contagion passes from body to body. They conceive it to be equally agreeable to Nature that there should be some transmissions and operations from spirit to spirit without the mediation of the senses. From this have grown the concepts (now almost made visible) of the "Mastering Spirit," the force of confidence, and the like. Related to this is the inquiry into how to raise and fortify the imagination; for if the imagination...

<summary>Bacon explores how the mind receives divine illumination through "fury" and examines "fascination"—the controversial theory that the imagination can exert a physical influence over other people and objects.</summary>

<keywords>divination, influxions, divine fury, fascination, imagination, Paracelsus, natural magic, contagion, mastering spirit, psychology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the power of the imagination and transitions into the study of the human mind's faculties.</meta>

<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>*Of the Advancement of Learning,*</header>

If a fortified imagination has power, then it is important to know how to strengthen and elevate it. *And herein* enters, in a distorted and dangerous way, a justification for a great part of *Ceremonial Magic*. For it may be claimed that *Ceremonies, Characters, and Charms* work not by any *silent or sacramental contract* <note>original: "Tacite or Sacramentall contract"</note> with evil spirits, but serve only to strengthen the imagination of the person using them; just as *Images* are said by the *Roman Church* to fix the thoughts and raise the devotions of those who pray before them.

But in my own judgment, even if it is admitted that *Imagination* has power, and that *Ceremonies* fortify *Imagination*, and that they are used sincerely and intentionally for that purpose, I would still hold them unlawful. They oppose that first edict which God gave to man: *In the sweat of your face you shall eat bread* <note>original: "In sudore vultus comedes Panem tuum"</note>. For these practices suggest that those noble results—which God has ordained for man to buy at the price of labor—can instead be attained by a few easy and slothful observances. I will report no specific deficiencies in these areas of knowledge, other than the general deficiency: that it is not known how much of them is truth and how much is vanity.

### THE KNOWLEDGE WHICH RESPECTS THE FACULTIES OF THE MIND OF MAN
This knowledge is of two kinds: the one respecting his Understanding and Reason, and the other his Will, Appetite, and Affection; the former produces DIRECTION or DECREE, the latter ACTION or EXECUTION. It is true that the *Imagination* is an *Agent, or Messenger* <note>original: "Nuntius"</note>.

<summary>Bacon argues against the use of ceremonial magic as a shortcut to power, asserting that it violates the divine decree that man must labor for his achievements, before transitioning to a classification of the mind's intellectual and moral faculties.</summary>

## The Second Book.

The imagination acts as an agent in both provinces: the *judicial* <note>original: "Iudiciall"; relating to judgment or the evaluation of truth</note> and the *ministerial* <note>original: "Ministeriall"; relating to the execution of actions</note>. For the senses send information over to the *Imagination* before *Reason* has judged it; and *Reason* sends information over to the *Imagination* before a decree can be acted upon. This is because *Imagination* always precedes *Voluntary Motion*. However, this *Janus* <note>The Roman god of beginnings and transitions, usually depicted with two faces looking in opposite directions.</note> of *Imagination* has different faces. The face turned toward *Reason* bears the print of Truth, but the face turned toward *Action* bears the print of Goodness—which, nevertheless, are faces "such as sisters' faces should be" <note>original: "Quales decet esse sororum"; a quote from Ovid's Metamorphoses suggesting they are similar but distinct.</note>.

Neither is the *Imagination* simply and only a *messenger*; rather, it is invested with—or at least usurps—no small authority in itself, beyond the mere duty of delivering the message. For it was well said by *Aristotle*: "That the mind has over the *Body* that commandment which a lord has over a *bondman* <note>original: "Bond-man"; a slave or serf</note>; but that *Reason* has over the *Imagination* that *commandment* which a *magistrate* has over a free *citizen*, who may also come to rule in his turn." 

For we see that in matters of *Faith and Religion*, we raise our *Imagination* above our *Reason*. This is the reason why *Religion* has always sought access to the mind through *similitudes*, *types*, *parables*, *visions*, and *dreams*. Furthermore, in all persuasions wrought by eloquence and other impressions of a similar *nature*—which paint and disguise the true appearance of things—the chief recommendation to *Reason* comes from the *Imagination*. Nevertheless, because I do not find any *science* that properly or fitly pertains to the *IMAGINATION*, I see no cause

And if it be a work, we speak not now of such parts of learning as the *imagination* produces, but of such *sciences* as handle and consider the *imagination* itself. This is no different than how we shall not speak now of such *knowledges* as reason produces (for that extends to all *philosophy*), but of such *knowledges* as handle and inquire into the faculty of *reason*. So, *poetry* had its true place <note>Bacon previously categorized Poetry under the faculty of Imagination in his tripartite division of History (Memory), Poetry (Imagination), and Philosophy (Reason).</note>. 

As for the power of the *imagination* in nature and the manner of strengthening it, we have mentioned it in the *Doctrine of the Soul* <note>original: *De Anima*</note>, where it most appropriately belongs. And lastly, for *imaginative* or *insinuative reason*—which is the subject of *rhetoric*—we think it best to refer it to the *arts of reason*. Therefore, we content ourselves with the former division: that *human philosophy*, which respects the faculties of the mind of man, has two parts: *RATIONAL* and *MORAL*.

The part of *human philosophy* which is rational is, to most wits, the least delightful of all knowledges; it seems to be but a net of subtlety and thorniness <note>original: "spinosity," meaning prickly or difficult like a brier</note>. For as it was truly said that *knowledge* is the *food of the soul* <note>original: *Pabulum animi*</note>, so in the nature of men's appetite for this food, most men have the taste and stomach of the *Israelites* in the desert. They would gladly have returned *to the flesh-pots* <note>original: *Ad ollas carnium*; a reference to Exodus 16:3, where the Israelites longed for the meat they ate in Egypt</note> and were weary of *manna*, which, though it was celestial, yet seemed less nutritious and comforting. So, generally, men taste

## The Second Book.

Knowledge that is immersed in "flesh and blood"—such as *Civil History, Morality, and Policy*, which revolve around human emotions, reputations, and fortunes—is well understood. However, this same *dry light* <note>original: "Lumen ficcum"; a reference to Heraclitus's idea that a dry soul is the wisest and best</note> parches and offends the watery and soft natures of most men. But to speak truly of things according to their actual worth, rational forms of knowledge are the keys to all other arts. For as Aristotle says aptly and elegantly, *the hand is the instrument of instruments, and the mind is the form of forms* <note>original: "Anima est forma formarum"</note>; so these intellectual arts may truly be called the "Art of Arts." They do not only provide direction, but also confirm and strengthen the mind, just as the habit of practicing archery not only enables one to aim more accurately but also to draw a stronger bow.

The INTELLECTUAL ARTS are four in number, divided according to the ends for which they are intended. Human labor aims to either *invent* <note>original: "invent"; in this context, to discover or find</note> that which is *sought* or *proposed*; to *judge* that which is *invented*; to *retain* that which is *judged*; or to *deliver* over to others that which is *retained*. Consequently, there must be four arts: the ART of ENQUIRY or INVENTION; the ART of EXAMINATION or JUDGMENT; the ART of CUSTODY or MEMORY; and the ART of ELOCUTION or TRADITION <note>original: "tradition"; here meaning the delivery or communication of knowledge</note>.

INVENTION is of two very different kinds: the first is the invention of ARTS and SCIENCES, and the other is the invention of SPEECH and ARGUMENTS. The former of these I report as deficient. This seems to me to be a deficiency similar to one found in the making of...

<summary>Bacon defines the four "Intellectual Arts"—Invention, Judgment, Memory, and Tradition—as the essential tools for all knowledge, while noting a significant lack in the current methods for discovering new arts and sciences.</summary>

## Of the Advancement of Learning

In an inventory regarding the estate of a deceased person, it would be noted if there is no ready money. Just as money can acquire all other commodities, this specific knowledge <note>The art of invention and discovery</note> is what should purchase all the rest. And just as the West Indies would never have been discovered if the use of the mariner’s needle <note>The magnetic compass</note> had not been discovered first—even though the former are vast regions and the latter is a small device—it should not be considered strange if the sciences are not further explored when the very art of invention and discovery has been overlooked.

That this part of knowledge is missing is, in my judgment, plainly admitted. First, Logic does not claim to invent sciences or the axioms <note>Fundamental principles or self-evident truths</note> of sciences; instead, it passes over the responsibility by saying, "Every person must be trusted in their own art" <note>original: *Cuique in sua arte credendum*</note>. Celsus <note>A Roman medical writer</note> acknowledges this solemnly when speaking of the Empirical and Dogmatic sects of physicians: he says that medicines and cures were first found by chance, and only afterward were their reasons and causes discovered; the causes were not found first to then reveal the medicines and cures by their light. 

Plato also notes well in his *Theaetetus* that specific details are infinite, and the highest generalities do not provide sufficient direction. He argues that the "pith" <note>original: *pythe*; the essential core or strength</note> of all sciences—which distinguishes the skilled craftsman from the amateur—lies in the "middle propositions." In every specific field of knowledge, these are derived from tradition and experience. Therefore, we see that those who discuss the inventions and origins of things refer them more to chance than to art, and more to beasts and birds...
