## The Second Book

This form of induction <note>original: "Induction"; the process of reaching a general conclusion from specific instances</note>, I say, is utterly flawed and inadequate. Their error is all the more shameful because it is the duty of *Art* to perfect and exalt Nature; but they, on the contrary, have wronged, abused, and misrepresented Nature. For he who attentively observes how the mind gathers this excellent dew of knowledge—similar to that which the poet describes as "the celestial gift of honey from the air" <note>original: "Aerei mellis caelestia dona"; a reference to Virgil’s Georgics regarding the gathering of honey</note>, distilling and refining it out of specific natural and artificial examples, like the flowers of the field and garden—shall find that the mind, by its own nature, manages and performs an induction much better than they describe it. 

To reach a conclusion based on an *enumeration of particulars without a contradictory instance* <note>original: "Enumeration of particulars without instance contradictorie"; concluding something is true simply because no counter-example has been seen yet</note> is not a true conclusion, but a mere conjecture. For who can be certain (in many subjects) based only on those particulars which appear on one side, that there are not others on the contrary side which do *not* appear? It is as if *Samuel* should have settled on those sons of *Jesse* <note>original: "Iſſay"</note> who were brought before him, and missed *David*, who was away in the field. 

This form of reasoning is, to tell the truth, so crude that it would not have been possible for wits as subtle as those who have managed these things to have offered it to the world, except that they hurried toward their *theories* and *dogmas* <note>original: "Dogmaticals"</note>. They were arrogant and scornful toward specific details <note>original: "particulars"</note>, which they were accustomed to use only as *lictors* and *messengers* <note>original: "Lictores and Viatores"; Roman officers who cleared the way for high-ranking officials</note>—acting as sergeants and ushers <note>original: "Wiſſlers"; officials who clear the way for a procession</note>—*to move the crowd aside* <note>original: "Ad summouendam turbam"</note> and make way for their own ideas.

<summary>The author critiques the traditional method of induction for being overly simplistic and prone to error, arguing that true knowledge requires looking for contradictory evidence rather than just confirming existing theories.</summary>

It is true that in popular sciences, such as *ethics*, *laws*, and the like—and even *divinity* (because it pleases God to apply himself to the capacity of the simplest people)—that form of logic may have use. Bacon argues that traditional logic wins arguments but does not produce new inventions or works.</note>.

For *arguments* consist of *propositions*, and *propositions* consist of *words*, and *words* are but the *common tokens* or *marks* of popular *notions* of *things*. If these *notions* are crudely and inconsistently collected out of *particulars* <note>Specific observations or data points.</note>, then no amount of laborious examination—either of the *consequences* of *arguments* or of the truth of *propositions*—can ever correct that *error*. # The Second Book

(as physicians speak) in the first digestion; and therefore it was not without cause that so many excellent philosophers became *Skeptics* and *Academics* <note>The "Academics" refers to the followers of the Platonic Academy, particularly in its later phases when it embraced skepticism.</note>, and denied any certainty of knowledge or comprehension, holding the opinion that the knowledge of man extended only to appearances and probabilities. 

It is true that in *Socrates*, this was supposed to be merely a form of *irony*: "He feigned knowledge by pretending to be ignorant" <note>original: *Scientiam dissimulando simulavit*</note>. For he used to disparage his own knowledge to the end of enhancing his reputation for knowledge—much like the *disposition of Tiberius* <note>Tiberius was the second Roman Emperor, known for his secretive and deceptive nature.</note> in the beginning of his reign, who wished to rule but would not openly acknowledge it. 

In the later *Academy*, which *Cicero* embraced, this opinion of *acatalepsia* <note>term: *acatalepsia* — the philosophical conviction that things cannot be fully understood or perceived with absolute certainty.</note> was also (I suspect) not held sincerely. It seems that all those who excelled in a great abundance of speech chose that sect because it was best suited to bring glory to their eloquence and varied discourses; these were more like *pleasurable strolls* than journeys toward a specific destination. 

But assuredly, many individuals scattered throughout both *Academies* did hold this view with subtlety and integrity. However, here was their chief error: they blamed the deceit upon **the senses**. In my judgment—notwithstanding all their trivial objections—the senses are quite sufficient to certify and report the truth. Though they may not always do so immediately, they can do so through comparison, by the help of instruments, and by bringing things that are too subtle for the senses to some effect that the senses *can* comprehend, and other similar methods.

I say this not to discourage the mind of man, but to stir it up to seek help. For no man, no matter how skillful or practiced he is, can draw a perfectly straight line or a perfect circle by steadiness of hand alone; yet this may be easily done with the help of a ruler or a compass.

<margin>Literate experience and the interpretation of Nature.</margin> This part of <term>Invention</term> <note>original: "Inuention"; here meaning the discovery of new knowledge or arts</note> concerning the <term>Invention</term> of *Sciences*, I purpose (if God gives me leave) hereafter to propound. I have digested it into two parts: the one I term <term>Literate experience</term> <note>original: "Experientia literata"; the method of organizing scattered experiments into a written, systematic order</note>, and the other the <term>Interpretation of Nature</term> <note>original: "Interpretatio Naturae"; the higher process of deriving true axioms from organized experience</note>. The former is but a degree and a starting point for the latter. But I will not dwell too long, nor speak too grandly upon a promise.

The <term>Invention</term> of speech or argument is not properly an <term>Invention</term>. To *invent* is to discover that which we do not know, and not to recover or summon again that which we already know. The use of this type of <term>Invention</term> is nothing more than to draw forth from the *knowledge* our mind already possesses, or to call before us, that which may be pertinent to the purpose we are considering. So, to speak truly, it is not <term>Invention</term>, but a *Remembrance* or *Suggestion* combined with an <term>Application</term>. This is why the schools place it after <term>Judgment</term>, as something that follows rather than precedes it. Nevertheless, because we account it a chase, as well

And since it has already obtained the name, let it be called *Invention*; so long as it is perceived and discerned that the scope and end of this *Invention* is the readiness and immediate use of our knowledge, and not the addition or amplification of it.

To procure this ready use of knowledge, there are two courses: PREPARATION and SUGGESTION. The former of these seems scarcely a part of knowledge, consisting more of diligence than of any technical <note>original: "artificiall"</note> erudition. And herein *Aristotle* wittily, but hurtfully, does deride the *Sophists* <note>Ancient Greek teachers often criticized for prioritizing persuasion over truth.</note> near his time, saying: *They acted as if one who professed the art of shoemaking should not teach how to make a shoe, but only display a ready stock of many shoes of all fashions and sizes.* 

But yet a man might reply that if a shoemaker should have no shoes in his shop, but only work as he is commissioned, he would have very few customers. Furthermore, our *Saviour*, speaking of *Divine Knowledge*, says: *That the kingdom of heaven is like a good householder, who brings forth both new and old store* <note>Matthew 13:52</note>. And we see the ancient writers of *Rhetoric* give it as a rule: that pleaders <note>Legal advocates or orators.</note> should have the "Places" <note>term: Commonplaces; pre-prepared arguments or themes used in debate.</note> which they use most continually already handled in every possible variety. For example, they should be ready to speak for the literal interpretation of the *Law* against *Equity* <note>The principle of fairness or the spirit of the law versus its strict letter.</note>, and *contrary*; and to speak in favor of legal presumptions.

The other part of *Invention*, which I term **SUGGESTION**, assigns and directs us to certain *marks* or *places* <note>original: "Topica" or "Topics"</note> which may excite our mind to return and produce such knowledge as it has formerly collected, to the end that we may make use of it. Neither may these places serve only to prompt our <term>Invention</term> <note>In this context, "Invention" refers to the rhetorical art of finding or discovering arguments and ideas.</note>; but also to direct our inquiry. For a faculty of wise interrogating is half of knowledge; for as *Plato* says, *Whosoever seeks, knows that which he seeks for in a general notion; otherwise, how shall he know it when he has found it?* <note>original: "Qui quaerit, scit illum quod quaerit, in notione generali; aliter quomodo sciet, cum invenerit?"</note> And therefore, the larger your anticipation is, the more direct and concise is your search. But the same *Places* <note>In logic, "Places" (or Topics) are categories or "seats" of arguments used to organize thought.</note> which will help us what to produce from that which we know already, will also help us—if a man of experience were before us—what questions to ask; or if we have books and authors, to instruct us what points to search and revolve. So, I cannot report that this part of Invention, which is what the Schools call <term>Topics</term>, is deficient.

Nevertheless, <term>Topics</term> are of two sorts: general and special. We have spoken to the general; but the particular has been touched by some, but generally rejected as unartful and variable. But leaving the humor which has reigned too much in the Schools (which is to be vainly subtle in a few things which are within their command, and to reject the rest), I do receive particular <term>Topics</term>—that is, places or directions of <term>Invention</term> and <term>Inquiry</term> in every particular knowledge—as things of great use. These are mixtures of <term>Logic</term> with the matter of sciences; for in these it holds true that *the art of discovery grows along with the discoveries themselves.* <note>original: "Ars inveniendi adolescit cum Inventis"</note> For as in walking a path, we do not only gain that part of

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that the art of "Invention" (discovery) is aided by "Topics," which provide a framework for asking the right questions. He advocates for "special topics" tailored to specific sciences, noting that the method of discovery evolves as more knowledge is found.</summary>
<keywords>Invention, Logic, Plato, Topics, Inquiry, Methodology, Epistemology</keywords>

<meta>The previous page discussed the methods of discovery and the initial stages of inquiry. This page continues the exploration of how human knowledge progresses through the arts of judgment and logical reasoning.</meta>

## Of the Advancement of Learning

Just as we gain a better view of the path ahead by looking back at the way we have already traveled, every degree of progress in a science provides light for what follows. If we strengthen that light by expanding it into specific questions or areas of inquiry, we greatly advance our pursuit of knowledge.

Now we pass to the **Arts of Judgment**, which handle the nature of *proofs* and *demonstrations*. Regarding *induction* <note>The process of reaching a general conclusion from specific observations.</note>, judgment coincides with *invention* <note>In this context, "invention" refers to the discovery of arguments or ideas.</note>. In all inductions, whether they are formed correctly or incorrectly, the same mental action that *discovers* also *judges*; it is just like the way our physical senses work. However, it is different in proof by *syllogism* <note>A form of reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two given or assumed premises.</note>. Because the proof in a syllogism is not immediate but relies on a middle term, the *discovery* of that middle term is one thing, and the *judgment* of the logical *consequence* is another. The first only *stimulates* the mind, while the other *examines* it. Therefore, for the real and exact form of judgment, we refer back to what we have already said regarding the *interpretation of nature*.

As for the other type of judgment by *syllogism*, it is something very agreeable to the human mind, and thus it has been pursued with great intensity and excellence. The nature of man extremely desires to have something fixed and immovable in his understanding to serve as a rest and support for the mind. Therefore, as *Aristotle* endeavors to prove, in all motion there is some...

<summary>Francis Bacon distinguishes between the judgment used in induction and the formal logic of the syllogism, noting that humans naturally crave the certainty and stability that logical structures provide.</summary>
<keywords>Arts of Judgment, Induction, Syllogism, Aristotle, Logic, Interpretation of Nature, Epistemology</keywords>

# The Second Book
<page-num>54</page-num>

...some point remains at rest. He elegantly explains the ancient fable of *Atlas* (who stood fixed and held up the heavens to keep them from falling) to refer to the poles or the axle-tree of the heavens, upon which the rotation of the universe is accomplished. So, assuredly, men have a desire to have an *Atlas* or axle-tree within themselves to keep them from fluctuation, which feels like a perpetual peril of falling. Therefore, men hastened to establish certain principles around which the variety of their disputations might turn.

So then, this Art of **Judgment** is nothing but the **reduction** of **propositions** to **principles** by means of a **middle term**. The **principles** are to be agreed upon by all and exempted from **argument**. The **middle term** <note>In logic, the term that connects the subject and predicate of a conclusion in a syllogism.</note> is to be selected at the liberty of every man’s **invention**. This **reduction** is of two kinds: **direct** and **inverted**. 

The first is when the **proposition** is reduced to the **principle**, which they term a **direct proof** <note>original: "Probation offensive"</note>. The other is when the contradictory of the **proposition** is reduced to the contradictory of the **principle**; this is what they call **by way of inconvenience** <note>original: "Per Incommodum"</note>, or **pressing an absurdity** <note>Also known as *reductio ad absurdum*.</note>. The number of **middle terms** depends on how many **degrees** the **proposition** stands, more or less, removed from the **principle**.

But this art has two distinct methods of **instruction** <note>original: "Doctrine"</note>: the one by way of **direction**, the other by way of **caution**. The former frames and sets down a true **form of consequence** <note>The logical structure of a valid inference.</note> by the variations...

<summary>Bacon explains the Art of Judgment as a logical process of anchoring arguments to fixed principles, comparing the need for certainty to the mythological Atlas supporting the heavens.</summary>

Toward the composition and structure of which form, it is necessary to handle the parts thereof, which are *Propositions*, and the parts of *Propositions*, which are *Simple Words*. And this is that part of *Logic* which is comprehended in the *Analytics* <note>Aristotle's works on logic, specifically the Prior and Posterior Analytics, which deal with the structure of syllogisms and demonstration.</note>.

The second method of teaching was introduced for speedy use and for the sake of certainty; discovering the more subtle forms of *Sophisms* <note>term: Sophisms; deceptive arguments that appear true but are actually false.</note> and *Entanglements* <note>original: "Illaqueations"</note>, with their *refutations* <note>original: "redargutions"</note>, which is that which is termed *ELENCHES* <note>term: Elenches; the branch of logic dealing with the refutation of fallacious arguments.</note>. For although in the more obvious types of fallacies it happens (as *Seneca* makes the comparison well) as in juggling feats, where though we do not know how they are done, yet we know well it is not as it seems to be; yet the more subtle sort of them does not only leave a man without an answer, but does many times deceive his judgment.

This part concerning *ELENCHES* is excellently handled by *Aristotle* in *Precept* <note>In his work "On Sophistical Refutations."</note>, but more excellently by *Plato* in *Example*: not only in the persons of the *Sophists*, but even in *Socrates* himself. Socrates, professing to affirm nothing but to weaken <note>original: "infirme"</note> that which was affirmed by another, has exactly expressed all the forms of objection, fallacy, and refutation. ### The Second Book
<note>original: "The ſecond Booke"</note>

The corrupt use of logic is for trickery <note>original: "caption"</note> and contradiction, which is regarded as a great skill and is undoubtedly of very great advantage. However, there is a good distinction made between orators and sophists <note>original: "Sophifters"; professional debaters who use clever but false arguments</note>: the one is like the greyhound, which has his advantage in the race; the other is like the hare, which has her advantage in the turn. Thus, it is the advantage of the weaker creature.

But furthermore, this doctrine of **Elenches** <note>original: "ELENCHES"; the detection and refutation of fallacious arguments</note> has a wider scope and extent than is usually perceived, reaching into various parts of knowledge—some of which are well-studied, while others are neglected. 

First, I believe (though it may seem somewhat strange at first) that the branch of study which is variously referred sometimes to Logic and sometimes to Metaphysics, concerning the common attributes of essences, is actually nothing but a study of fallacies. For the greatest sophism of all sophisms is the equivocation or ambiguity of words and phrases, especially of those words which are most general and intervene in every inquiry. 

It seems to me that the true and fruitful use of the inquiry into **Majority**, **Minority**, **Priority**, **Posteriority**, **Identity**, **Diversity**, **Possibility**, **Actuality** <note>original: "Actio"</note>, **Totality**, **Parts**, **Existence**, **Privation**, and the like (leaving aside vain subtleties and speculations), is simply to serve as wise cautions against the ambiguities of speech. Similarly, the distribution of things into certain tribes, which we call **Categories** or **Predicaments**—

## Of the Advancement of Learning

*Predicaments* <note>original: "Predicaments"; here referring to Aristotle’s categories of being</note> are nothing more than *cautions* against the confusion of *definitions* and *divisions*.

Secondly, there is a form of deception that works through the strength of the *impression* rather than the subtlety of the *entrapment* <note>original: "Illaqueation"; the act of catching in a snare or trap</note>. This does not so much perplex the *reason* as it overrules it by the power of the *imagination*. However, I think it is more appropriate to handle this part when I speak of *rhetoric*.

But lastly, there is a much more important and profound kind of *fallacy* in the *mind* of *man*, which I find has not been observed or investigated at all. I think it is good to place it here, as it is the thing that, above all others, is most relevant to rectifying *judgment*. The force of these fallacies is such that they do not merely dazzle or snare the understanding in specific instances; instead, they more generally and inwardly *infect* and corrupt its very state. For the mind of *man* is far from having the nature of a clear and even mirror, in which the rays of things reflect according to their true incidence. Instead, it is more like an enchanted mirror, full of superstition and *imposture*, unless it is liberated and restored to its proper state. 

For this purpose, let us consider the false appearances that are imposed upon us by the general *nature* of the *mind*. Let us observe them in an example or two: first, in that instance which is the root of all superstition—namely, *that it is natural to the minds of all men for the affirmative or active side to affect*...

So it is that a few instances of success or presence outweigh many instances of failure or absence. This was well answered by Diagoras to the person who showed him the great number of pictures in Neptune's Temple of those who had escaped shipwreck and had paid their vows to Neptune. The person said, "Consider now, you who think it is folly to call upon Neptune in a storm." "Yes," said Diagoras, "but where are the pictures of those who were drowned?" 

Let us behold this in another instance: namely, that the spirit of man, being of an equal and uniform substance, usually supposes and invents a greater equality and uniformity in Nature than truly exists. From this it comes that mathematicians cannot satisfy themselves unless they reduce the motions of the celestial bodies to perfect circles, rejecting spiral lines and laboring to be rid of eccentrics <note>original: "Eccentriques"; astronomical models where the center of motion is not the center of the Earth</note>. 

From this it also comes that, whereas there are many things in Nature that are, as it were, unique and independent <note>original: "Monodica: sui Iuris"; Latin for "singular: of its own right"</note>, yet the thoughts of man invent for them relatives, parallels, and conjugates <note>terms used to describe things that are linked or paired together</note> where no such thing exists. For example, they have invented an "Element of Fire" simply to keep a square balance with Earth, Water, and Air, and other such things. Indeed, it is incredible, until it is pointed out, what a number of fictions and fantasies have been brought into natural philosophy by the imitation of human actions and arts, together with the making of Man the common measure <note>original: "Communis Mensura"</note> of all things. <meta>This page continues the discussion from Book One regarding the "peccant humors" or errors of the mind, specifically focusing on the distortions caused by language.</meta>

I have given many examples in one of the errors, or "peccant humors" <note>original: "peccant humours"; a medical metaphor for the unhealthy tendencies or "diseases" of the mind</note>, which we ran over briefly in our first book.

And lastly, let us consider the false appearances that are imposed upon us by words, which are framed and applied according to the concepts and capacities of the common people. Although we think we govern our words, and prescribe the rule well—*to speak like the common people, but think like the wise* <note>original: "Loquendum ut Vulgus, sentiendum ut sapientes"</note>—yet it is certain that words, like a Tartar's bow <note>The Tartars were famous for shooting arrows backward while retreating; Bacon means words can "recoil" and strike the speaker's own mind</note>, do shoot back upon the understanding of the wisest, and mightily entangle and pervert the judgment. 

So it is almost necessary in all controversies and disputations to imitate the wisdom of the mathematicians by setting down, in the very beginning, the definitions of our words and terms. This ensures that others may know how we accept and understand them, and whether they agree with us or not. For it happens, for want of this, that we are sure to end exactly where we ought to have begun: in questions and differences about words. 

To conclude, therefore, it must be confessed that it is not possible to divorce ourselves entirely from these fallacies and false appearances, because they are inseparable from our nature and condition of life. Nevertheless, the caution against them—for all *elenches* <term>logical refutations or ways of exposing false arguments</term>, as was said, are but cautions—is extremely important for the true conduct of human judgment.

<margin>On the Analogy of Demonstrations.</margin> There remains one part of Judgment of great excellence which, in my understanding, is so slightly touched upon that I may report it also as deficient: this is the application of different kinds of proofs to different kinds of subjects. For there being but four kinds of demonstrations—that is, by the immediate consent of the **mind** or **sense**; by **induction**; by **sophism**; and by **congruity** (which is what **Aristotle** calls **demonstration in a circle** <note>original: *Demonstration in Orbe*</note>, and not from **things better known** <note>original: *a Notioribus*; referring to the logical principle that a proof should proceed from established facts to the unknown</note>)—every one of these has certain subjects in the field of sciences in which they respectively have the chief use, and certain others from which they ought to be excluded. The excessive rigor and fussiness in requiring the most severe proofs in some things, and especially the ease in contenting ourselves with weaker proofs in others, has been among the greatest causes of harm and hindrance to knowledge. I note the distribution and assignment of demonstrations, according to their appropriateness to specific sciences, as deficient.

The custody or retaining of knowledge is either in **writing** or **memory**. Of these, **writing** has two parts: the nature of the **character** <note>the script or symbols used</note> and the order of the **entry**.

## The Second Book

As for the art of *Characters* <note>original: "Characters"; here meaning systems of shorthand, symbols, or ciphers</note>, or other visible notes of words or things, it has the closest connection with Grammar, and therefore I refer it to its proper place. Regarding the *Disposition* and *Arrangement* of that knowledge which we preserve in writing, it consists of a good digest of *Commonplaces* <note>original: "Common Places"; notebooks where authors gathered useful quotations, arguments, and observations under general headings</note>. I am not ignorant of the prejudice directed at the use of *Commonplace Books*, as they are accused of slowing down one's reading and causing some laziness or relaxation of the memory. 

However, because it is a counterfeit thing in the pursuit of knowledge to be quick and clever unless a man is also deep and full, I hold the entry of commonplaces to be a matter of great use and essence in studying. It is that which ensures an abundance of invention <note>original: "copie of Inuention"; meaning a wealth of creative ideas or arguments</note> and focuses judgment into a source of strength. But it is also true that, among the *Methods of Commonplaces* that I have seen, there is none of sufficient worth. All of them merely carry the appearance of a *School* rather than a *World*; they refer to common matters and pedantic divisions that lack all life or regard for practical action.

For the other principal part of the custody of knowledge, which is **MEMORY**, I find that faculty has been, in my judgment, weakly investigated. There is an art of memory currently in existence, but it seems to me that there are better precepts than that *Art* provides, and better practices of that *Art* than those currently accepted. It is certain that the *Art* (as it is) may be raised to points of prodigious ostentation <note>meaning impressive but superficial displays of memorization</note>; but in actual use, as it is now managed...

### Of the Advancement of Learning,

And therefore, I value the repeating of a great number of names or words upon once hearing them no more than I value the pouring forth of a number of verses or rhymes *extempore* <note>original: "ex tempore"; meaning spontaneously or without preparation</note>. Nor do I value the making of a *satirical simile* for everything, or the turning of everything into a jest, or the falsifying or contradicting of everything by petty objections, or the like. Within the faculties of the *mind*, there is a great abundance of such things, which by cleverness and practice may be raised to an extreme degree of wonder. However, I value them no more than I do the tricks of *tumblers, tightrope walkers* <note>original: "funambuloes"</note>, and *dancers* <note>original: "baladynes"</note>; the one being the same in the *mind* as the other is in the body: matters of strangeness without worthiness.

This *Art of Memory* is built upon only two intentions: the one is *Prenotion* <note>Foreknowledge or prior conception</note>; the other is *Emblem*. *Prenotion* eliminates the endless searching for what we wish to remember and directs us to seek within a narrow compass; that is, something that has a logical connection with our *Place of Memory* <note>A reference to the "Method of Loci," a mnemonic technique where information is mentally placed in specific physical locations</note>. *Emblem* reduces intellectual concepts to sensible images <note>Images that can be perceived by the senses</note>, which strike the *memory* more effectively. From these *axioms* <note>Established principles</note>, a much better practice may be drawn than the one currently in use; and besides these *axioms*, there are several more concerning the help of *memory* that are not inferior to them. But I did in the beginning distinguish—

# The Second Book

I do this so as not to report those things as deficient which are merely poorly managed.

There remains the fourth kind of **Rational Knowledge**, which is transitive, concerning the *expressing* or *transferring* of our *knowledge* to others; I will *term* this by the *general* name of **Tradition** or **Delivery**. **Tradition** has three *parts*: the *first* concerning the **Organ of Tradition** <note>The medium or instrument through which information is conveyed.</note>; the *second* concerning the **Method of Tradition**; and the *third* concerning the **Illustration of Tradition** <note>The use of rhetoric and style to make knowledge persuasive and clear.</note>.

Regarding the **Organ of Tradition**, it is either **Speech** or **Writing**. For *Aristotle* says well: *Words are the images of thoughts, and letters are the images of words.* But yet it is not a necessity that *thoughts* be *expressed* through the *medium* of *words*. For whatever is capable of *sufficient differences*, and *those differences are perceptible* by the *senses*, is in *nature competent* to *express thoughts*. 

And therefore we see in the *commerce* of *barbarous people* <note>original: "barbarous People"; referring to foreign cultures whose languages were unknown to the author.</note> who do not understand one another's *language*, and in the *practice* of *various people* who are *dumb* and *deaf*, that *men's minds* are *expressed* in *gestures*; though not *exactly*, yet enough to *serve the purpose*. And we understand further that it is the *custom* of *China* and the *kingdoms* of the *High Levant* <note>The Far East.</note> to write in *real characters* <note>Ideograms or pictograms that represent concepts directly rather than sounds.</note>, which *express neither letters nor words* in the aggregate, but *things* or *notions*. This is so much the case that *countries* and *provinces* which do not understand one another's *language* can nevertheless read one another's *writings*, because the...

<summary>Bacon introduces "Tradition" (the delivery of knowledge) as the fourth branch of rational knowledge, identifying speech, writing, gestures, and "real characters" (like Chinese script) as its primary organs.</summary>
<keywords>Rational Knowledge, Tradition, Delivery, Organ of Tradition, Aristotle, Speech, Writing, Gestures, Real Characters, China, Communication</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transmission of knowledge, specifically focusing on how thoughts are expressed through signs and characters beyond spoken language.</meta>

## Of the Advancement of Learning

The *characters* <note>original: "Characters"; here meaning written symbols or signs</note> are accepted more generally than the *languages* themselves extend; and therefore they have a vast multitude of *characters*, as many (I suppose) as there are radical words.

These *notes of thoughts* <note>original: "Notes of Cogitations"</note> are of two sorts: the one where the *note* has some *similitude* <note>resemblance</note> or *congruity* with the *notion*; the other *at pleasure* <note>original: "Ad Placitum"; a Latin legal term meaning "at will" or by arbitrary choice</note>, having force only by *contract* or *social acceptance*. Of the former sort are *hieroglyphics* and *gestures*. As for *hieroglyphics* (things of ancient use, embraced chiefly by the *Egyptians*, one of the most ancient nations), they are essentially continued *impresas* <note>original: "Impreſaes"; heraldic devices or emblems with a motto</note> and *emblems*. And as for *gestures*, they are like *transitory hieroglyphics*. They are to *hieroglyphics* what *spoken words* are to *written words*, in that they do not endure; yet they always have, just like the others, an affinity with the things signified. 

For example, when *Periander* <note>A tyrant of Corinth (c. 627–585 BC)</note> was consulted on how to preserve a newly usurped tyranny, he told the *messenger* to wait and report what he saw him do. He went into his garden and chopped off all the highest flowers, signifying that the secret consisted in the cutting off and keeping low of the *nobility* and *grandees*. 

Those signs that are *at pleasure* <note>original: "Ad Placitum"</note> are the "real characters" mentioned before, and *words*. This is true although some people have been willing—through *curious inquiry*, or rather by clever invention—to derive the original naming of things from reason and intent. This is an elegant speculation, and one that is *reverent* because it searches into *antiquity*; but it is sparingly mixed...

<summary>Bacon distinguishes between signs that represent ideas through natural resemblance (hieroglyphics and gestures) and those that represent ideas by arbitrary social convention (words and characters).</summary>

mixed with truth, and of small fruit. This portion of knowledge, touching the *Notes of things* <note>original: *De Notis Rerum*, concerning the signs of things</note> and thoughts in general, I find not inquired into, but deficient. And although it may seem of no great use, considering that *words and writings by letters* do far excel all the other ways; yet because this part concerns, as it were, the "Mint of Knowledge"—for *words* are the tokens current and accepted for ideas, just as *money* is for value, and it is fit men be not ignorant that *money* may be of another kind than gold and silver—I thought it good to propose it for better inquiry.

## Concerning SPEECH and WORDS, the consideration of them has produced the Science of GRAMMAR. 

For *Man* still strives to reintegrate himself into those blessings from which, by his own fault, he has been deprived. And as he has striven against the first general curse <note>The curse of labor following the Fall of Man in Genesis.</note> by the invention of all other arts, so has he sought to escape the second general curse (which was the confusion of tongues <note>The division of languages at the Tower of Babel.</note>) by the Art of GRAMMAR. The use of this art in one's own tongue is small; in a foreign tongue it is greater; but it is greatest in such *foreign tongues* as have ceased to be *vulgar tongues* <note>term: common spoken languages</note> and have become only *learned tongues* <note>Such as Latin, Greek, or Hebrew in the 17th century.</note>. The duty of Grammar is of two natures: the one *Popular*, which is for the speedy and perfect attaining of languages, as well for the intercourse of speech as for the understanding of authors; the other *Philosophical*, examining the power and nature of words...

<summary>Bacon argues that while words are the primary "currency" of ideas, other signs of thought deserve study; he further defines Grammar as a tool to overcome the linguistic confusion of Babel.</summary>

Therefore, I cannot report it as deficient, though I think it very worthy to be organized into a science by itself.

To *GRAMMAR* also belongs, as an appendix, the consideration of the "accidents" <note>In this context, "accidents" refers to the non-essential qualities or properties of words</note> of words, which are measure, sound, and elevation (or accent), and the sweetness and harshness of them. In this area, although men in learned tongues <note>referring to classical languages like Latin and Greek</note> tie themselves to the ancient measures, yet in modern languages, it seems to me as free to make new measures of verses as of dances. For a dance is a measured pace, just as a verse is a measured speech. In these things, the *sense* <note>meaning the physical ear or aesthetic feeling</note> is a better judge than the *art* <note>meaning formal rules or theory</note>.

## The Second Book

The kinds of <term>ciphers</term> <note>original: "cyphars"; systems for secret writing</note> (besides the <term>simple ciphers</term> with changes and mixtures of <term>nulls</term> and <term>non-significants</term> <note>characters that have no meaning, used to confuse a codebreaker</note>) are many, according to the nature or rule of the encoding: <term>wheel-ciphers</term>, <term>key-ciphers</term>, <term>doubles</term>, etc. But the virtues of them, by which they are to be preferred, are three: that they are not laborious to write and read; that they are impossible to decipher; and in some cases, that they are without suspicion. The highest degree of this is to write <term>all things by all things</term> <note>original: "omnia per omnia"; a method where any letter can represent any other letter</note>; which is undoubtedly possible, with a ratio of five-to-one at most between the outer writing and the inner message, and no other restraint whatsoever. This <term>art of ciphering</term> has a related <term>art of deciphering</term>; which in theory is useless, but as things currently stand, is of great use. For even if ciphers were well managed, there are multitudes of them which exclude the <term>decipherer</term>. But because of the rawness and lack of skill of the hands through which they pass, the greatest matters are many times carried in the weakest ciphers.

In the listing of these private and retired <term>arts</term>, it may be thought I seek to make a great <term>muster-roll of sciences</term> <note>a formal list or inventory of all branches of knowledge</note>; naming them for show and ostentation, and for little other purpose. But let those who are skillful...

### Of the Advancement of Learning

Let them judge whether I bring these subjects in only for appearance, or whether in what I say of them (though in few marks <note>original: "Markes"; here meaning brief points or characteristics</note>) there is not some seed of progress. And this must be remembered: just as there are many people of great importance in their own local countries and provinces who, when they come up to the seat of the government <note>original: "Seate of the Estate"</note>, are but of average rank and scarcely noticed; so these arts, being placed here alongside the principal and supreme sciences, seem like petty things. Yet, to those who have chosen to spend their labor and studies on them, they seem like great matters.

Regarding the **Method of Tradition** <note>The systematic way in which knowledge is organized and handed down</note>, I see it has sparked a controversy in our time. But as in civil business, if there is a meeting and men fall into arguments, there is commonly an end to the matter for that time and no progress at all; so in learning, where there is much controversy, there is often little actual inquiry. For this part of knowledge concerning **Method** seems to me so weakly investigated that I shall report it as deficient.

**Method** has been placed—and not wrongly—within **Logic** as a part of judgment. For just as the doctrine of **syllogisms** <note>A form of reasoning in which a conclusion is drawn from two given or assumed premises</note> encompasses the rules of judgment upon that which is discovered <note>original: "inuented"; in the classical sense of 'inventio' or finding arguments</note>, so the doctrine of **Method** contains the rules of judgment upon that which is to be delivered.

<summary>Bacon argues that the "Method of Tradition"—the way knowledge is organized for delivery—is a neglected field of study, despite its importance in the logical process of judgment.</summary>
<keywords>Method of Tradition, Logic, Syllogisms, Advancement of Learning, Inquiry, Pedagogy, Intellectual Organization</keywords>

# The Second Book

red, for Judgment precedes Delivery, just as it follows Invention. Neither is the METHOD or the NATURE of the TRADITION <note>original: "Tradition"; here meaning the act of handing down or communicating knowledge</note> important only to the *Use* of Knowledge, but likewise to the *Progression* of Knowledge. For since the labor and life of one man cannot attain to the perfection of Knowledge, the *Wisdom* of the *Tradition* is what inspires the success of its continuance and advancement. And therefore, the most significant distinction of *Method* is between METHOD REFERRED TO USE and METHOD REFERRED TO PROGRESSION; the one may be termed MAGISTERIAL <note>authoritative teaching intended for immediate use</note>, and the other of PROBATION <note>testing or examination for further discovery</note>.

The latter of these seems to be a *deserted and closed-off path* <note>original: "Via deserta & interclusa"</note>. For as branches of knowledge are now delivered, there is a kind of *Contract* of Error between the Deliverer and the Receiver. He that delivers knowledge desires to deliver it in such a form as may be best believed, and not as may be best examined; and he that receives knowledge desires present satisfaction rather than expectant Inquiry, and so prefers not to doubt rather than not to err. Glory makes the Author hide his weaknesses, and sloth makes the Disciple fail to know his own strength.

But knowledge that is delivered as a thread to be spun further ought to be delivered and suggested, if it were possible, *in the same Method wherein it was invented*. And so it is possible for knowledge

# Of the Advancement of Learning

But yet, nevertheless, *to a greater or lesser degree* <note>original: "Secundum majus & minus"</note>, a man may revisit and descend unto the foundations of his knowledge and belief. In doing so, he may transplant it into another person's mind just as it grew in his own. 

For it is with types of knowledge as it is with *plants*: if you mean to use the plant, the roots do not matter; but if you mean to remove it to grow elsewhere, then it is more certain to rely upon roots than *cuttings* <note>original: "slippes"</note>. So the delivery of knowledge (as it is now used) is like the fair bodies of trees without the roots: good for the carpenter, but not for the planter. But if you want sciences to grow, the shaft or body of the tree matters less, provided you look well to the taking up of the roots. 

<margin>On the sincere method, or the transmission of knowledge to the "sons of science."</margin> Of which kind of delivery, the *methods* of *mathematics* have some shadow in that subject; but generally, I see it neither put into practice <note>original: "put in vre"</note> nor even investigated. Therefore, I note it as deficient.

Another diversity of *METHOD* there is, which has some affinity with the former, used in some cases by the discretion of the *ancients*. However, it has been disgraced since then by the impostures of many vain persons, who have used it as a false light for their counterfeit merchandise; and that is the distinction between the **Enigmatic** and the **Disclosed**. # The Second Book

The purpose of this method is to prevent common minds from being admitted to the secrets of knowledge, reserving them for selected auditors or wits of such sharpness that they can pierce the veil.

Another diversity of **METHOD**, which has great consequences, is the delivery of knowledge in **APHORISMS** <note>original: "Aphorismes"; concise statements of principle or truth</note> or in **METHODS** <note>here meaning formal, systematic arrangements of a subject</note>. Regarding this, we may observe that it has become too much of a custom to take a few *axioms* or *observations* on any subject and turn them into a solemn and formal art. This is done by filling the work with various discourses, illustrating it with examples, and organizing it into a plausible **Method**. However, writing in **APHORISMS** has many excellent virtues which the writing in **Method** does not approach.

For first, it tests the writer to see whether he is superficial or solid. **Aphorisms**, unless they are to be ridiculous, cannot be made of anything but the pith and heart of sciences. In this format, explanatory discourse is cut off; the recital of examples is cut off; discourse regarding connection and order is cut off; and descriptions of practice are cut off. So, nothing remains to fill the **Aphorisms** but a substantial amount of solid observation. Therefore, no man is capable of—nor will any man in his right mind attempt—writing **Aphorisms** unless he is sound and well-grounded in his subject. But in **Methods**,

<note>original: "Tantum" (Only/So much)</note>

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that writing in aphorisms is superior to formal systematic methods because it forces the author to present only the essential "pith" of a science, preventing superficiality.</summary>
<keywords>Method, Aphorisms, Axioms, Scientific Communication, Pedagogy, Intellectual Rigor</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transmission of knowledge, specifically comparing the effectiveness of systematic methods versus aphorisms.</meta>

<header>Of the advancement of learning,</header>

->*So much power do sequence and connection possess;*<-
->*so much honor is added to things taken from the middle.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Tantum Series iuncturaque Pollet, Tantum de Medio sumptis, accedit honoris" — Horace, Ars Poetica. This refers to the power of arrangement in making common subjects appear significant.</note>

In this way, a man may make a great show of an art which, if it were taken apart, would amount to very little. Secondly, *Methods* are better suited to win consent or belief, but less suited to guide *Action*. This is because they carry a kind of demonstration in a globe or circle, where one part illuminates another, and therefore they satisfy the mind. But specific details, being scattered, best agree with scattered directions for practice. And lastly, *Aphorisms*, representing knowledge that is broken or incomplete, invite men to inquire further; whereas *Methods*, carrying the appearance of a complete total, make men feel secure, as if they had reached the furthest limits of the subject.

Another diversity of *METHOD*, which is likewise of great weight, is the handling of knowledge by *Assertions* and their *Proofs*, or by *Questions* and their *Determinations* <note>The formal resolution or conclusion of a scholarly debate.</note>. This latter kind, if it is followed immoderately, is as prejudicial to the progress of learning as it is to the progress of an army to attempt to besiege every little fort or stronghold. For if the field is held and the main goal of the enterprise is pursued, those smaller things will surrender on their own. Indeed, a man would not want to leave some important enemy position at his back; in the same manner, the use of confutation <note>The act of proving an argument wrong.</note> in the delivery of sciences ought to be very sparing.

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that while systematic methods of teaching provide a satisfying sense of completeness, aphorisms are superior for encouraging further inquiry and practical action. He also warns that over-analyzing every minor question can hinder the overall progress of knowledge, much like a distracted army.</summary>

Another diversity of *Methods* is *According to the Subject or Matter* which is handled. For there is a great difference in the delivery of *Mathematics*, which are the most abstracted of knowledges, and *Policy* <note>original: "Policie"; here meaning political science or statecraft</note>, which is the most immersed <note>involved in practical, material affairs</note>. And however much contention has been raised regarding a uniformity of *Method* across a *Multiformity of Matter*, yet we see how that opinion—besides its inherent weakness—has been harmful to learning. It takes the path of reducing learning to certain empty and barren generalities, which are but the very husks and shells of sciences; all the kernel is forced out and expelled by the torture and pressure of the *Method*. And therefore, just as I spoke in favor of *particular topics for invention* <note>specific frameworks for discovering new ideas</note>, so I also approve of *particular methods of tradition* <note>original: "Tradition"; referring to the delivery or transmission of knowledge</note>.

Another diversity of *Judgment* in the delivery and teaching of knowledge is *According to the light and presuppositions of that which is delivered*. For knowledge that is new and foreign to received opinions must be delivered in a different form than that which is agreeable and familiar. And therefore *Aristotle*, when he intends to criticize *Democritus*, actually commends him when he says: *If we shall indeed dispute, and not follow* <note>original: "If wee ſhall indeede diſpute, and not followe"—Bacon is citing Aristotle's *De Caelo*, where Aristotle notes that Democritus follows reason rather than mere appearances.</note>

...follow after *similitudes* <note>original: "Similitudes"; analogies or comparisons used for illustration</note>, and so on. For those whose conceptions <note>original: "conceites"</note> are based on popular opinions only need to prove their point or engage in dispute. But those whose conceptions go beyond popular opinions face a double labor: first, to make themselves understood, and second, to prove and demonstrate their point. Therefore, it is a necessity for them to resort to similitudes and metaphors <note>original: "translations"</note> to express themselves. 

And therefore, in the infancy of learning and in primitive times—when those ideas that are now commonplace <note>original: "triuiall"</note> were then new—the world was full of *parables* and *similitudes*. Without them, men would have either ignored what was offered without notice <note>original: "Marke"</note>, or rejected it as a paradox before they had understood or judged it. Similarly, in divine learning <note>theology</note>, we see how frequent *parables* and figures of speech <note>original: "Tropes"</note> are. For it is a rule that *whatever science is not consistent with existing assumptions must call upon the help of similitudes.*

<margin>On the wisdom of delivery <note>original: *De prudentia Traditionis*</note>.</margin>
There are also other varieties of **METHODS** that are common and accepted: such as that of *resolution* (or *analysis*), of *constitution* (or *system-building* <note>original: *Systasis*</note>), and of *concealment* (or the *cryptic method*), and so on. I approve of these, though I have focused on those methods that are least handled and observed. I have mentioned all of these for this purpose: because I wish to establish and constitute one general inquiry (which seems to me lacking) concerning the *wisdom of delivery* <note>original: "Tradition"; the art of transmitting knowledge to others</note>.

And therefore, *Method* considers not only the arrangement of the *argument* or *subject*, but also the *propositions*: not regarding their *truth* or *subject matter*, but their *scope* and *form*. 

In this regard, *Ramus* <note>Petrus Ramus (1515–1572), a French humanist and logician known for his reform of logic and pedagogy.</note> deserved much more credit for reviving the sound rules of *propositions*—such as *universal primary* and *of every instance* <note>original Greek: Καθόλου πρῶτον Κατὰ παντόϛ (Katholou proton kata pantos); these are Aristotle’s conditions for scientific definitions that apply always and everywhere.</note>—than he did for introducing the destructive habit <note>original: "Canker"</note> of *Epitomes* <note>summaries or abridgments that simplify complex subjects too much, potentially losing vital detail.</note>. And yet (as is the condition of human affairs—where according to ancient fables, *the most precious things have the most dangerous guardians*), it happened that his attempt at the former led him into the latter. 

For he would need to be well-guided who tries to make *axioms convertible* <note>logical statements where the subject and predicate can be swapped while remaining true, such as "all humans are rational animals" and "all rational animals are humans."</note>, to ensure he does not also make them *circular*, *non-progressive* <note>original Latin: *Non promouent*; not moving the argument forward</note>, or *repetitive* <note>original: "Incurring into themselves"</note>. Nevertheless, his intention was excellent.

The other considerations of *Method* regarding *propositions* mainly concern the ultimate propositions that define the boundaries <note>original: "Dimensions"</note> of sciences. For every branch of knowledge can be said to have—besides *depth* (which is its truth and substance, making it *solid*)—a *length* and a *width*. We measure the width in relation to other sciences, and the length toward *action*—that is, the path from the highest level of generality down to the most specific instruction. The one...

[The first rule] gives a rule for how far one branch of knowledge ought to interfere within the province of another, which is the rule they call *essential relationship* <note>original Greek: Καθ’αυτο (Kath’auto); an Aristotelian principle that a proposition must be true of its subject by its very nature.</note>. The other gives a rule for what degree of detail a branch of knowledge should descend. I find this latter rule has been passed over in silence, which in my judgment is the more important matter. For certainly, something must be left to practice, but how much is worthy of inquiry. We see that remote and superficial generalities only offer knowledge to the scorn of practical men; they are no more helpful to practice than an universal map by *Ortelius* <note>Abraham Ortelius (1527–1598) was the creator of the first modern atlas, the *Theatrum Orbis Terrarum*.</note> is for directing someone on the road between *London* and *York*. Now we descend to that part which concerns the **ILLUSTRATION OF TRADITION**, contained within that science which we call **RHETORIC, OR THE ART OF ELOQUENCE**. This is an excellent science and one that has been excellently well studied <note>original: "laboured"</note>. For so *Solomon* says: *The wise in heart shall be called prudent, but the sweetness of the lips increases learning* <note>original Latin: *Sapiens Corde appellabitur Prudens, sed dulcis Eloquio Maiora reperiet*; Proverbs 16:21</note>. This signifies that profound wisdom will help a man gain a reputation or admiration, but it is eloquence that prevails in an active life. Regarding the study of this art, the rivalry <note>original: "Emulation"</note> of *Aristotle* with the rhetoricians of his time, and the experience of *Cicero*, has made them exceed themselves in their works on rhetoric. Furthermore, the excellence of the examples of eloquence found in the orations of *Demosthenes* and *Cicero*, added to the perfection of the rules <note>original: "Precepts"</note> of eloquence, has doubled the progress in this art. handmaids attend the art, rather than focusing on the rules or the use of the art itself.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Nevertheless, to stir the earth a little around the roots of this science, as we have done with the rest: the duty and office of *Rhetoric* is **to apply Reason to the Imagination for the better moving of the Will.** For we see that *Reason* is disturbed in its administration by three means: by entanglement <note>original: "Illaqueation"</note> or fallacy <note>original: "Sophisme"</note>, which pertains to *Logic*; by imagination or sensory impression, which pertains to *Rhetoric*; and by passion or feeling <note>original: "Affection"</note>, which pertains to *Morality*. And just as in negotiations with others, men are influenced by cunning, by persistence <note>original: "Importunitie"</note>, and by intensity; so in this negotiation within ourselves, men are undermined by logical inconsistencies <note>original: "Inconsequences"</note>, urged and pestered by impressions or perceptions <note>original: "Obuersations"</note>, and carried away by passions. Neither is the nature of man so unfortunately constructed that those powers and arts should have the force to disturb Reason without also having the power to establish and advance it. For the end <note>goal</note> of *Logic* is to teach a form of argument to secure Reason, and not to entrap it. The end of *Morality* is to persuade the feelings to obey Reason, and not to invade it. The end of *Rhetoric* is to fill the imagination so as to support <note>original: "second"</note> Reason, and not to overwhelm it; for these abuses of the arts only arise indirectly <note>original Latin: *Ex obliquo*</note>, as a matter of caution.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And therefore it was a great injustice in *Plato*—though springing out of a just hatred of the rhetoricians of his time—to regard *Rhetoric* as nothing but a "voluptuary art" <note>an art focused solely on pleasure or decoration</note>...

<summary>Bacon defines Rhetoric as the tool that applies Reason to the Imagination to influence the Will, arguing that its true purpose is to support Reason against the distractions of fallacy and passion.</summary>
<keywords>Rhetoric, Logic, Morality, Reason, Imagination, Will, Plato, Sophistry, psychology of persuasion</keywords>

<header>The Second Book</header>

art, resembling it to cookery, which ruined <note>original: "marre"</note> wholesome meats and helped unwholesome ones by a variety of sauces for the pleasure of the taste. For we see that speech is much more concerned <note>original: "conversant"</note> with adorning that which is good than with disguising <note>original: "colouring"</note> that which is evil; for there is no man but speaks more honestly than he can act or think. And it was excellently noted by *Thucydides* regarding *Cleon* <note>Cleon was a populist Athenian politician during the Peloponnesian War</note> that because he used to hold the wrong side in matters of state, he was always railing <note>original: "inveying"</note> against eloquence and good speech, knowing that no man can speak well of courses of action that are sordid and base. 

And therefore, as *Plato* said elegantly: *That virtue, if she could be seen, would move great love and affection.* Since she cannot be shown to the senses by a physical shape, the next best step is to show her to the *imagination* in a lively representation. To show her to *Reason* only through the subtlety of argument was a thing always ridiculed in *Chrysippus* <note>A Stoic philosopher known for his complex logic</note> and many of the *Stoics*, who thought to thrust virtue upon men by sharp disputations and logical conclusions, which have no resonance <note>original: "Sympathy"</note> with the will of man.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Again, if the passions <note>original: "affections"</note> were in themselves flexible <note>original: "plyant"</note> and obedient to Reason, it would be true that there would be no great use for persuasions and subtle appeals <note>original: "insinuations"</note> to the will, any more than there is for bare propositions and proofs. But in regard to the continual mutinies and rebellions <note>original: "Seditions"</note> of the passions:

> I see the better path and approve of it; yet I follow the worse. <note>original Latin: *Video meliora, proboque; deteriora sequor*</note>

Reason would become captive and enslaved, if

<summary>Bacon defends Rhetoric against the charge that it is merely "cookery" for the mind, arguing that its true purpose is to vividly represent virtue to the imagination so that the will might follow Reason rather than rebellious passions.</summary>

--- Page 230 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Rhetoric, arguing that its purpose is to align the human imagination with reason to overcome the immediate impulses of the passions.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

*Eloquence of Persuasions* did not work upon and win the *Imagination* away from the side of the *passions* <note>original: "affections"</note>, and form a *Confederacy* between *Reason* and *Imagination* against the *Passions*. *For the Passions* themselves always carry a desire for what is good, just as *Reason* does. The difference is *that Passion looks merely at the present*, while *Reason looks at the future and the sum of time.* And therefore, because the *Present* fills the *Imagination* more, *Reason* is commonly defeated. But after the force of *Eloquence and persuasion* has made future and remote things appear as if they were *present*, then, upon the turning <note>original: "revolt"</note> of the *Imagination*, *Reason* prevails.

We conclude, therefore, that *Rhetoric* can no more be blamed for "coloring" <note>Coloring: giving a deceptive or attractive appearance to something</note> the worse side than *Logic* can be blamed for *Sophistry* <note>Sophistry: the use of clever but false arguments</note>, or *Ethics* <note>original: "Moralitie"</note> for Vice. For we know that the *study of opposites* <note>original: "Doctrines of Contraries"</note> is the same, even though their application is opposite. It appears also that *Logic* differs from *Rhetoric* not only as the fist differs from the palm—the one being compact and the other expanded—but much more in this: that *Logic* handles *Reason* in its exact truth, while *Rhetoric* handles it as it is planted in popular opinions and social customs <note>original: "Manners"</note>. And therefore, *Aristotle* wisely places *Rhetoric* as a bridge between *Logic* on one side and *Moral* or *Civil Knowledge* on the other, as it participates in both. For the *Proofs and Demonstrations of Logic* are the same and indifferent toward all men; but the *Proofs and persuasions of Rhetoric* ought to differ...

<summary>Bacon argues that rhetoric is essential for making long-term rational goals more appealing than immediate emotional impulses, and he defines rhetoric as a bridge between pure logic and social ethics.</summary>

<keywords>Rhetoric, Logic, Reason, Imagination, Passions, Aristotle, Sophistry, Ethics, Persuasion</keywords>

[A speaker's style should] differ according to the listeners, like
*Orpheus in the woods, or Arion among the dolphins;* <note>original: "Orpheus in Syluis, inter Delphinas Arion;" Orpheus was a legendary musician who could charm animals and trees; Arion was a poet saved by dolphins. The phrase means one must adapt their "music" or speech to their specific environment or audience.</note>

<margin>On the wisdom of private speech.</margin>
This application of speech, in its perfect *Idea*, ought to extend so far that if a man should speak of the same thing to several different persons, he should speak to them all respectively and in different ways. Yet this *political* part of eloquence in private speech is something the greatest orators often lack. While they observe their well-rehearsed forms of speech, they lose the flexibility <note>original: "volubilitie"</note> of application. Therefore, it would be beneficial to recommend this for better inquiry, without being overly concerned whether we categorize it here or in the part concerning "Policy" <note>Policy: the art of government or social conduct.</note>.

<margin>Colors of good and evil, simple and comparative.</margin>
Now, therefore, I will descend to the deficiencies which (as I said) are but accompaniments <note>original: "Attendances"</note>. First, I do not find that the wisdom and diligence of *Aristotle* has been well pursued. He began to make a collection of the popular signs and "colors" <note>Colors: rhetorical labels or descriptions used to make a thing seem better or worse than it is.</note> of good and evil, both simple and comparative, which are like the *Sophisms* <note>Sophism: a clever but false argument intended to deceive.</note> of *Rhetoric* (as I mentioned before). For example:

### SOPHISM.

> *What is praised is good; what is criticized is evil.* <note>original: "Quod laudatur, bonum: Quod vituperatur, malum."</note>

### REFUTATION.

> *He praises his wares who wishes to push them off on others.* <note>original: "Laudat venales, qui vult extrudere merces." Quote from the Roman poet Horace, suggesting praise is often just a sales tactic.</note>
> *“It is bad, it is bad,” says the buyer; but when he has gone away, then he will boast.* <note>original: "Malum est, Malum est (inquit Emptor) sed cum recesserit, tum gloriabitur." A reference to Proverbs 20:14, describing a buyer who disparages an item to lower the price, then boasts of the bargain later.</note>

The defects in the work of *Aristotle* are three: one, that there are but a few examples out of many; another, that their refutations <note>original: "Elenches" – from the Greek 'elenchos,' meaning a cross-examination or refutation of an argument.</note> are not annexed...

<summary>Bacon argues that effective rhetoric must be tailored to specific audiences and critiques Aristotle's incomplete collection of the "colors" (persuasive appearances) of good and evil.</summary>

[...perceiv]ed; and thirdly, that he conceived only a part of their use. For their use is not only in proof <note>original: "probation"</note>, but much more in **Impression** <note>Impression: the emotional or psychological impact a speech has on an audience, rather than just its logical validity.</note>. For many forms are equal in **Meaning**, but differ in **Impression**. Just as there is a great difference in the piercing of something sharp compared to something flat, even if the strength of the strike is the same; for there is no man who will not be a little more stirred by hearing it said: *Your enemies will be glad of this,*

> *This the Ithacan would desire, and the sons of Atreus would buy at a great price,* <note>original: "Hoc Ithacus velit, & magno mercentur Atridæ;" a quote from Virgil's Aeneid. than by hearing it said only, *This is bad for you.*

Secondly, I also resume what I mentioned before regarding **Provision** or **Preparatory store** for the equipment of speech and readiness of invention <note>Invention: the rhetorical art of finding and developing arguments.</note>. This appears to be of two sorts: the one resembles a shop of parts not yet assembled; the other, a shop of things already finished. Both are to be applied to what is frequent and most in demand. The former of these I will call ***Antitheta*** <note>Antitheta: opposing arguments or "antitheses."</note>, and the latter ***Formulae*** <note>Formulae: prepared phrases or set expressions.</note>.

<margin>Opposing arguments on things.</margin>***Antitheta*** are theses argued **for** and **against**, wherein men may be more detailed and thorough. However (for those who are able to do it), to avoid wordiness in recording them, I wish the seeds of the various arguments to be condensed into some brief and sharp sentences. These are not meant to be cited directly, but to be like skeins or balls <note>original: "Bottomes"</note> of thread, to be unwound at length when they are used, providing authorities and examples by reference.

<summary>Bacon argues that rhetoric is about emotional impact ("impression") as much as logic, and recommends that orators keep a "storehouse" of pre-prepared arguments (Antitheta) and set phrases (Formulae).</summary>

## For the words of the law,

## For the intent of the law.
<note>original: "Pro sententia Legis"</note>

> The meaning is to be drawn out of all the words, which explains each individual part.
> <note>original: "Ex omnibus verbis est Elucendus sensus, qui interpretatur singula"</note>

**Formulae** are simply appropriate and fitting passages or transitions of speech, which may serve various subjects indifferently, such as a *Preface, Conclusion, Digression, Transition, Excuse, etc.* For just as in buildings there is great pleasure and use in the well-designed layout of the staircases, entries, doors, windows, and the like, so in speech, the transitions and passages provide special ornament and effect.

### A conclusion in a deliberative speech.

> In this way we may redeem past faults and prevent future inconveniences.

There remain two appendices concerning the transmission <note>original: "tradition"</note> of knowledge: the one is **Critical**, the other is **Pedagogical** <note>original: "Pedanticall"; here meaning the art of teaching rather than being overly fussy.</note>. For all knowledge is either delivered by teachers or attained by a person’s own efforts. Therefore, just as the main part of the transmission of knowledge concerns the writing of **books**, so the related part concerns the reading of **books**. To this subject, several considerations happen to belong.

The first concerns the true correction and editing of authors, in which, however, reckless diligence has done great damage. For these **Critics** have often presumed that whatever they do not understand is written incorrectly. Like the priest who, finding it written of St. Paul that he "was let down in a basket," <note>original: "Demissus est per sportam," referring to Acts 9:25.</note> amended his book and made it "he was [sent away...]" <note>The text cuts off here. Bacon is likely referring to a famous clerical error where a scribe, not knowing the word "sportam" (basket), changed it to "portam" (gate).</note>

Secondly, the consideration of where to begin with the easiest and so proceed to the more difficult; and in what courses to press the more difficult first and then turn them to the more easy. For it is one...

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s discussion of "Pedagogical" knowledge (the art of teaching), specifically moving from the structure of schools to the specific methods and psychological insights required to train the mind effectively.</meta>

A method to practice swimming with bladders <note>Inflatable bags used as a flotation aid, similar to modern "water wings."</note>, and another to practice dancing with heavy shoes. 

A third method is the application of learning according to the suitability <note>original: "propriety"</note> of different intellects. For there is no defect in the intellectual faculties that does not seem to have a specific cure contained in certain studies. For example, if a child is <term>bird-witted</term>—that is, he lacks the faculty of attention—the <term>mathematics</term> provides a remedy for that; because in those studies, if the mind wanders for even a moment, one has to begin all over again. And just as specific sciences have a suitability toward certain faculties for their cure and help, so do mental faculties or powers have a natural affinity <note>original: "Simpathy"</note> toward certain sciences for achieving excellence or rapid progress. Therefore, it is an inquiry of great wisdom to determine what kinds of minds and natures are most fit and proper for which sciences.

Fourthly, the ordering of exercises is a matter of great consequence that can either hinder or help. For as is well observed by *Cicero* <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the Roman orator and philosopher.</note>, when men exercise their faculties, if they are not well-advised, they may practice their faults and acquire ill habits as well as good ones. Thus, great judgment must be used regarding the continuation and the pausing of exercises. It would be too long to go into detail about many other considerations of this nature—things that seem insignificant <note>original: "of meane appearance"</note> but are of remarkable effectiveness. 

For just as the harming or nurturing of seeds and young plants is the most important factor in their thriving, it was also noted that the first six kings <note>The legendary early rulers of Rome (from Romulus to Tarquinius Superbus) who established the city's religious, social, and legal foundations.</note> served in truth as the "tutors" of the state of <term>Rome</term> during its infancy, which was the principal cause of its immense greatness...

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s discussion of the profound impact of education and training on the human mind, transitioning from the methods of practice to the lasting effects of early habits and the power of rhetorical skill.</meta>

...crookedness of that state which followed: so the cultivation and nourishment <note>original: "manurance"</note> of minds in youth has such a powerful (though unseen) operation, that hardly any length of time or intensity of labor can counteract <note>original: "countervaile"</note> it afterwards. And it is also worthwhile to observe how small and humble abilities acquired through education, when they are applied by great men or to great matters, produce significant and important effects. We see a notable example of this in *Tacitus* <note>Publius Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56 – c. 120 AD), a Roman historian known for his analysis of power.</note> concerning two stage-actors, *Percennius* and *Vibulenus*, who, by their skill in acting, threw the *Pannonian* armies into an extreme state of riot and chaos <note>original: "combustion"</note>. 

For a mutiny arose among them upon the death of *Augustus Caesar*; *Blaesus*, the lieutenant, had imprisoned some of the mutineers, who were suddenly rescued. At this point, *Vibulenus* obtained a hearing to speak, which he did in this manner: 

*“These poor innocent wretches, condemned to a cruel death, you have restored to see the light of day. But who shall restore my brother to me, or life unto my brother? He was sent here with a message from the legions of Germany to negotiate for our common cause; yet Blaesus has murdered him this last night using some of the gladiators <note>original: "fencers"</note> and ruffians he keeps around him as executioners of soldiers. Answer me, Blaesus: what has been done with his body? Even the deadliest enemies do not deny burial. When I have performed my last duties to the corpse with kisses and tears, then command me to be slain beside him, so that these, my comrades—witnesses to our good intentions and our true loyalty to the legions—may have permission to bury us both.”*

With this speech, he threw the army into an infinite fury and uproar; whereas the truth was that he had no brother...

<meta>This page concludes the story of the mutinous soldier Vibulenus, who was revealed to be a mere actor, and begins Bacon's systematic classification of "Rational Knowledges"—the arts of logic, speech, and reason.</meta>

...fellow soldiers, may bury us together, who are killed not for any crime, but only for seeking the welfare of the army.”

He had no such brother, nor was there any such matter; but he played the part merely as if he had been upon the stage.

But to return: we have now come to a period of **RATIONAL KNOWLEDGES** <note>Rational knowledge refers to the intellectual arts, including grammar, logic, and rhetoric, which govern how we reason and communicate.</note>, wherein if I have made the divisions different from those that are commonly received, yet I would not be thought to disallow all those divisions which I do not use. For there is a double necessity imposed upon me to alter the divisions.

The first is because it serves a different end and purpose to sort together those things which are next in *Nature*, and those things which are next in use. For if a Secretary of *State* <note>original: "Estate"</note> should sort his papers, it is likely that in his study, or general *Cabinet* <note>A private room or a storage chest for important documents and curiosities.</note>, he would sort together things of a similar *Nature*, such as *Treaties*, *Instructions*, and so on. But in his boxes, or particular *Cabinet*, he would sort together those that he was likely to use together, though they are of several different *Natures*.

So in this general *Cabinet* of knowledge, it was necessary for me to follow the divisions of the *Nature* of things; whereas if I myself had been handling any particular branch of knowledge, I would have respected the *divisions fittest for use*.

The other reason is because the introduction of the *deficiencies* <note>Bacon's term for "missing" areas of science and learning that have not yet been fully developed.</note> by consequence altered the *partitions* <note>The way a subject is categorized or divided into sub-topics.</note> of the rest. For let the knowledge currently existing (for the sake of demonstration) be 15. Let the knowledge including the deficiencies be 20. The parts of 15 are not the same as the parts of 20; for the parts of 15 are 3 and 5, whereas the parts of 20 are 2, 4, 5, and 10. Thus, these things are without *contradiction* and could not be otherwise.
