# Of the Advancement of Learning

We proceed now to that knowledge which considers the **APPETITE** and **WILL** of man <note>Bacon is moving into the field of Ethics, or Moral Philosophy, which he defines as the study of human desire and the faculty of choice.</note>, of which Solomon says: "Above all things, my son, keep your heart, for out of it proceed the actions of life" <note>original Latin: *Ante omnia fili custodi cor tuum, nam inde procedunt actiones vitæ.* (Proverbs 4:23)</note>. In the treatment of this science, those who have written on it seem to me to have acted like a man who claimed to teach writing but only showed beautiful examples of alphabets and joined letters, without giving any rules or directions for how to move the hand and form the letters.

In the same way, they have produced good and beautiful models and copies, showing the outlines and portraits of Good, *Virtue*, *Duty*, and *Happiness* <note>original: "Felicity"</note>, presenting them well-described as the true objects and goals of man's will and desires. But as for how to reach these excellent targets, and how to shape and tame the will of man to become true and consistent with these pursuits, they pass over it entirely, or treat it slightly and unprofitably.

For it is not enough to debate whether moral virtues are in the mind of man by habit or by nature; or to distinguish that noble <note>original: "generous"</note> spirits are won over by teaching and persuasion, while the common people <note>original: "vulgar sort"</note> are moved by reward and punishment. These and similar passing remarks and observations cannot excuse the absence of this part of study.

The reason for this omission, I suppose, is that hidden rock upon which both this and many other ships <note>original: "barques"</note> of knowledge have been wrecked: that men have looked down on dealing with ordinary and common matters, even though the wise management of such things is the most profound doctrine. (For life...

<summary>Bacon begins his discussion on Moral Philosophy (the Appetite and Will), arguing that previous authors have spent too much time describing what virtue is and too little time teaching the practical methods of how to actually train the human will to achieve it.</summary>
<keywords>Appetite, Will, Ethics, Solomon, Virtue, Duty, Happiness, Moral Philosophy, Education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the critique of moral philosophers who focus on describing the ideal of virtue rather than providing practical instructions on how to cultivate it. # The Second Book

<note>This sentence continues from the previous page's discussion of why certain sciences have been neglected.</note> ...does not consist in novelties or subtleties. On the contrary, they have put together sciences primarily out of a certain resplendent or polished mass of material chosen to give glory either to the subtlety of debates <note>original: "disputacions"</note> or to the eloquence of speeches. But Seneca gives an excellent warning <note>original: "check"</note> to eloquence: "Eloquence harms those in whom it creates a desire for itself, rather than for the subject matter" <note>original: *Nocet illis eloquentia, quibus non rerum cupiditatem facit sed sui*</note>. Teachings should be such that they make men in love with the lesson and not with the teacher, being directed toward the benefit of the listeners <note>original: "Auditors"</note> and not the praise of the author. And therefore, the right kind of teachings are those that can be concluded as Demosthenes <note>The famous Greek orator (384–322 BCE).</note> concludes his counsel: "If you do these things, you will not only praise the speaker at the moment, but you will also praise yourselves before long, because your affairs will be in a much better state" <note>original: *Quae si feceritis non, Oratorem duntaxat inpresentia laudabitis sed vosmetipsos etiam non ita multo post statu rerum vestrarum meliore.*</note>.

Nor did men of such excellent abilities <note>original: "parts"</note> need to despair of a reputation <note>original: "Fortune"</note>—such as the poet Virgil promised himself and indeed achieved—for he gained as much glory for his eloquence, wit, and learning in expressing observations on farming <note>original: "husbandry"</note> as he did for the heroic deeds of Aeneas.

> *I am well aware in my mind how great a task it is to conquer these things with words,*
> *and to add honor to such humble subjects.*
> <note>original: *Nec sum animi dubius verbis ea vincere magnum, / Quam sit & angustis his addere rebus honorem.* (Virgil, *Georgics* III, 289-90)</note>

And surely, if the purpose is sincerely not just to write at leisure what people may read at leisure, but to truly instruct and support <note>original: "suborne"</note> action and active life, then these "Georgics of the mind" <note>Named after Virgil's *Georgics*, a poem on agriculture; Bacon uses the term to describe the practical "tillage" or cultivation of the human soul.</note> concerning its husbandry and tillage are no less worthy than the heroic descriptions of virtue, duty, and happiness <note>original: "felicity"</note>. Therefore, the main and primary division of moral knowledge seems...

<summary>Bacon argues that moral philosophy should focus on practical "husbandry of the mind"—the actual cultivation of character—rather than just the eloquent description of virtues. He uses Virgil’s agricultural poetry as a metaphor for the dignity of studying these "humble" practical methods.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of moral philosophy, which Bacon divides into two parts: the theory of what is "good" and the practical application of how to train the mind to achieve it.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning.

...seems to be divided into the **MODEL or PLATFORM OF GOOD**, and the **GOVERNMENT or CULTURE OF THE MIND**. One describes the nature of Good, while the other prescribes rules on how to subdue, apply, and adapt the will of man to it.

The teaching concerning the **PLATFORM or NATURE of GOOD** considers it as either **SIMPLE** or **COMPARED**—that is, either the kinds of Good or the degrees of Good. In the latter category, those endless debates concerning the supreme degree of good—which they call *Felicity*, *Blessedness* <note>original: "Beatitude"</note>, or the highest Good—have been dismissed by the Christian faith. These ancient doctrines were essentially the theology of the heathens. And as *Aristotle* says, "Young men may be happy, but only through hope" <note>Aristotle argues that true happiness requires a complete life, which the young have not yet lived.</note>; so must we all acknowledge our legal minority <note>Bacon means our status as "minors" or children in this mortal life compared to the next.</note> and embrace the happiness which comes through hope for the future world.

Therefore, having been freed and delivered from this doctrine of the philosophers' heaven—by which they imagined a higher elevation of human nature than actually exists (for we see in what a lofty style *Seneca* writes: "It is a truly great thing to have the fragility of a human, yet the security of a God" <note>original: *Vere Magnum, habere fragilitatem hominis, securitatem Dei*</note>)—we may receive the rest of their inquiries and labors with more sobriety and truth. 

Regarding the **Nature of Positive or Simple Good**, they have set it down excellently by describing the forms of *Virtue* and *Duty*, with their situations and postures, and by dividing them into their kinds, parts, provinces, actions, and administrations, and the like. Furthermore, they have recommended these virtues to human nature and spirit 

<summary>Bacon divides moral philosophy into the study of the nature of "Good" and the practical "Culture of the Mind." He argues that Christian faith has resolved ancient debates about the "highest good" by placing ultimate happiness in the hope of the afterlife.</summary>

## The Second Book

...with great quickness of argument and beauty of persuasion; indeed, they have fortified and entrenched these ideas (as much as rational discourse can) against corrupt and popular opinions. Furthermore, regarding *the degrees and the comparative nature of Good*, they have also handled this excellently in their three-fold classification of *Good* <note>original: "triplicity of Good"; this usually refers to the division of goods into those of the mind, the body, and external circumstances</note>. They have explored this through comparisons between a *contemplative* and an *active* life, in the distinction between virtue achieved through struggle <note>original: "reluctation"</note> and virtue that is secure and effortless, in their debates between integrity <note>original: "honesty"</note> and profit, in their balancing of one virtue against another, and the like. Therefore, this part of philosophy deserves to be recorded as having been excellently researched.

Nevertheless, before they had arrived at the popular and commonly accepted notions of virtue and vice, pleasure and pain, and the rest, if they had stayed a little longer on the inquiry concerning the roots of good and evil—and the fibers of those roots—they would have, in my opinion, shed great light on everything that followed. Especially if they had consulted with Nature itself, they would have made their doctrines less wordy <note>original: "prolixe"</note> and more profound. Since these matters were partly omitted by them and partly handled with much confusion, we will endeavor to revisit and explain them in a clearer manner.

There is formed in every thing a double nature of Good: the first is as every thing is a whole or independent entity <note>original: "a Total or substantive"</note> in itself; the second is as it is a part or member of a larger body. Of these...

<summary>Bacon praises ancient philosophers for their work on the comparative nature of virtue but argues they failed to investigate the fundamental "roots" of good and evil in nature. He introduces his own theory that all things possess a "double nature of good": one as an individual and one as part of a larger system.</summary>

<page-type>text</page-type>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

The latter is in degree the greater and the worthier because it tends toward the conservation of a more general form. Therefore, we see that iron, in a particular sympathy, moves toward the *lodestone* <note>original: "Loadstone"; a naturally magnetized mineral</note>; but yet if it exceeds a certain quantity, it forsakes its affection for the *lodestone* and, like a good patriot, moves toward the *Earth*, which is the region and home of *massive bodies* <note>original: "Massie Bodyes"; solid, heavy matter</note>. So may we go forward and see that *water and massive bodies move toward the center of the earth*; but rather than suffer a division <note>original: "divulsion"; a breaking apart</note> in the continuity of Nature, they will move upwards from the center of the Earth, forsaking their duty to the *Earth* in regard to their duty to the *World* <note>Bacon refers to the ancient belief that Nature abhors a vacuum; substances will move "up" against their nature to ensure no empty space is created in the universe</note>. 

This double nature of *Good*, and the comparison between its parts, is much more deeply engraved upon *man*—if he does not degenerate. To him, the conservation of duty to the public ought to be much more precious than the conservation of life and being. This is according to that memorable speech of *Pompey the Great* <note>original: "Pompeius Magnus"</note>: when he was commissioned to provide food during a famine at *Rome*, and was dissuaded with great vehemence and urgency by his friends from risking his life at sea during an extreme storm, he said only to them: *“It is necessary that I go, but not that I live”* <note>original Latin: "Necesse est ut eam, non ut viuam"</note>. 

But it may be truly affirmed that there was never any philosophy, religion, or other discipline which did so plainly and highly exalt the good which is *communicative* <note>social or shared good</note> and diminish the good which is *private* and *particular* as the *Holy Faith*. # The Second Book

...those laws of nature to the inanimate creatures that we spoke of before; for we read that the elected saints of God have wished themselves accursed <note>original: "Anathemized"</note> and erased from the Book of Life, in an ecstasy of charity and an infinite feeling of *communion*.

This being established and strongly rooted, it judges and determines most of the controversies with which *moral philosophy* is concerned. First, it decides the question regarding the preference of the contemplative or active life, and it decides it against *Aristotle*. For all the reasons that he brings forward for the contemplative life are private, respecting the pleasure and dignity of oneself (in which respects, undoubtedly, the contemplative life has the preeminence). 

This is not much unlike that comparison which *Pythagoras* <note>A Greek philosopher (c. 570–495 BC) often credited with coining the term "philosopher"</note> made to honor and magnify philosophy and contemplation. When he was asked what he was, he answered: *That if Hiero <note>The tyrant of Syracuse</note> were ever at the Olympian games, he knew the custom: some came to try their fortune for the prizes, and some came as merchants to sell <note>original: "utter"</note> their goods, and some came to enjoy good food and meet their friends, and some came only to look on; and he was one of those who came to look on.* 

But men must know that in this theater of man’s life, it is reserved only for God and angels to be onlookers. It also settles the controversies between **Zeno** and **Socrates** and their schools and successors on the one side—who placed happiness <note>original: "felicity"</note> in virtue, whether alone or accompanied by other goods, the actions and exercises of which chiefly embrace and concern society—and on the other side, the **Cyrenaics** and **Epicureans**, who placed it in pleasure. All of these views clearly tend toward private peace and contentment rather than the interests of society.

It also criticizes the philosophy of **Epictetus**, which presupposes that happiness <note>original: "felicity"</note> must be placed in those things within our power, so that we are not subject to fortune and disturbance. It is as if it were not much happier to fail in good and virtuous goals for the public good than to obtain everything we could wish for ourselves in our own personal fortune. As **Consalvo** <note>Gonzalo Fernández de Córdoba (1453–1515), a celebrated Spanish general known as "The Great Captain."</note> said to his soldiers while showing them **Naples**: he declared he would rather die one foot forward than have his life secured for a long time by retreating one foot. The wisdom of that heavenly Leader <note>A reference to God or the author of the Proverbs.</note> has confirmed this, affirming that ***a good conscience is a continual feast*** <note>Proverbs 15:15.</note>. This plainly shows that the consciousness of good intentions—no matter how they turn out—is a more constant joy to human nature than all the preparations that can be made for personal security and peace.

It likewise criticizes that abuse of philosophy which became common around the time of **Epictetus**, turning it into a profession or occupation. It was as if the purpose were not to resist and extinguish mental disturbances <note>original: "perturbations"</note>, but to flee and avoid the causes of them, and to shape a specific kind of life to that end. This introduced a type of "health of mind" similar to the "health of body" mentioned by **Aristotle** regarding **Herodicus** <note>A Greek physician who was said to have spent his entire life doing nothing but nursing his own sickly constitution.</note>, who <meta>did</meta>

<meta>This page continues the critique of philosophical systems that prioritize personal tranquility over social duty. <header>Of the Advancement of Learning</header>

...did nothing all his life but attend to <note>original: "intend"</note> his health. On the other hand, if people commit themselves to the duties of society, it follows that just as the best bodily health is that which is most able to endure all changes and extremes, so too is that health of mind most proper which can go through the greatest temptations and disturbances. Thus, the opinion of **Diogenes** is to be accepted; he praised not those who abstained, but those who sustained—those who could restrain their mind even on a precipice <note>original: *in Precipitio*</note>, and could give to the mind (as is practiced in horsemanship) the shortest stop or turn <note>These are equestrian terms for a horse’s sudden halt or pivot, symbolizing mental agility and control.</note>.

Finally, this perspective criticizes the over-sensitivity and lack of practical application in some of the most ancient and respected philosophers and philosophical men who retired too easily from public life <note>original: "Civil business"</note> to avoid indignities and disturbances. In contrast, the resolve of truly moral men ought to be like what **Consalvo** <note>Gonzalo Fernández de Córdoba (1453–1515), a Spanish general.</note> said: that the honor of a soldier should be "of a coarser weave" <note>original: *e tela crassiore*</note>, and not so fine that every little thing should catch on it and put it in danger.

To return to the subject of **private or particular good**, it can be divided into **Active Good** and **Passive Good**. This difference in "Good"—not unlike the distinction expressed by the Romans in the household terms **Promus** and **Condus** <note>original: *Promus* (the steward who distributes or serves) and *Condus* (the steward who stores or hoards).</note>—is found in all things. It is best revealed in the two distinct appetites found in all creatures: one to preserve or continue themselves, and the other to expand <note>original: "dilate"</note> or multiply themselves. Of these, the latter seems to be the worthier. For in nature, the heavens, which are the more worthy, are the...

<header>The Second Book.</header>

[a]gent <note>The text begins mid-word, continuing "agent" from the previous page.</note>, and the earth, which is the less worthy, is the **Patient** <note>In philosophy, the "agent" is the one who acts, and the "patient" is the one who is acted upon.</note>. In the pleasures of living creatures, the pleasure of generation <note>procreation</note> is greater than that of food. In divine doctrine, it is said: **"It is more blessed to give than to receive"** <note>original: *Beatius est dare quam accipere*</note>. And in life, there is no man’s spirit so weak that he does not value the achieving of something he has set his heart on more than sensual pleasure. 

This priority of the **Active Good** is strongly supported by the consideration that our state is mortal and exposed to the whims of fortune. If we could have a permanent and certain grasp on our pleasures, their status would increase their value. But when we see that it is only a matter of **"valuing it highly to die a little later"** <note>original: *Magni aestimamus Mori tardius*</note> and **"Do not boast about tomorrow, for you do not know what a day may bring forth"** <note>original: *Ne glorieris de crastino. Nescis Partum diei*</note>, it makes us desire to have something secured and exempted from Time—which are only our deeds and works, as it is said: **"Their works follow them"** <note>original: *Opera eorum sequuntur eos*</note>. 

The excellence of this Active Good is likewise supported by the natural human affection for variety and progress. In the pleasures of the senses (which is the principal part of **Passive Good**), there can be no great range or variety. **"Think how long you have been doing the same things: eating, sleeping, and playing. One runs in this same circle; a person may wish to die not only because they are brave, miserable, or wise, but simply because they are bored"** <note>original: *Cogita quamdiu eadem feceris Cibus, Somnus Ludus per hunc Circulum curritur, mori velle non tantum fortis aut miser aut prudens sed etiam fastidiosus potest*</note>. 

But in the enterprises, pursuits, and purposes of life, there is much variety, which men experience with pleasure in their beginnings, progressions, setbacks, recoveries, approaches, and attainments of their goals. As it was well said: **"Life without a purpose is listless and wandering"** <note>original: *Vita sine proposito languida & vaga est*</note>. Nor is this Active Good identical with the Good of Society, although in some cases they may coincide—

We have spoken of **Active Good** and categorized it as a part of private and particular good. And rightly so: for there is impressed upon all things a triple desire or appetite proceeding from self-love: one of **preserving and continuing** their form; another of **advancing and perfecting** their form; and a third of **multiplying and extending** their form upon other things. <header>The Second Book.</header>

For to preserve something in its current state is the lesser goal; to preserve it with advancement is the greater. So it is in man:

"A fiery vigor is in them, and a celestial origin." <note>original: *Igneus est ollis vigor, & cælestis origo*; a quote from Virgil’s *Aeneid*, VI.730, referring to the divine spark within the human soul.</note> 

His approach or **assumption** <note>original: *Assumptiõ*; the act of being taken up into or identifying with.</note> to a divine or **angelic nature** is the perfection of his form. The error or false imitation of this good is the tempest of human life: while man, following the instinct for an advancement that is **formal** and **essential** (improving his very being), is instead carried away to seek an advancement that is merely **local** (changing his position).

For just as those who are sick and find no remedy tumble up and down and change places—as if by a **local removal** (moving the body) they could obtain an **internal removal** (ending the illness)—so it is with men in their ambition. When they fail in the means to exalt their **nature**, they are in a perpetual **agitation** <note>original: *estuation*; a boiling or turbulent state, from the Latin *aestuare*.</note> to exalt their **place** or status. So then **Passive Good** is, as was said, either **conservative** or **perfective**.

To return to the good of conservation or **comfort**, which consists in the **enjoyment** <note>original: *fruicion*; the pleasure derived from possession or use.</note> of that which is **agreeable to our natures**: it seems to be the most pure and natural of pleasures, yet also the softest and the lowest. And this also admits a distinction which has neither been well-judged nor well-investigated. For the good of enjoyment or contentment is placed either in the **purity** <note>original: *sincereneße*.</note> of the enjoyment, or in the **intensity** <note>original: *quickneße*.</note> and vigor of it: the one brought about by **consistency** <note>original: *Æquality*.</note>, the other by **change** <note>original: *Vicicitude*.</note>. The former has a smaller mixture of evils; the latter makes a stronger impression of good. Whether of these is the greater good is a debated question, but whether...

<summary>Bacon defines the two types of Passive Good: "Perfective," which seeks to elevate human nature toward the divine, and "Conservative," which seeks comfort in what is natural. He argues that political ambition is often a misguided attempt to achieve internal growth through mere external promotion.</summary>

--- Page 250 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Passive Good," exploring whether human nature can simultaneously experience both the "Good of Preservation" (peace and stability) and the "Good of Advancement" (growth and the fulfillment of desire).</meta>

<header>Of the Advancement of Learning.</header>

Whether man's nature may be capable of both is a question that has not been explored.

The former question was debated between *Socrates* and a *Sophist*. *Socrates* placed happiness in an equal and constant peace of mind, while the *Sophist* placed it in desiring much and enjoying much. They moved from rational argument to insults. The *Sophist* said that *Socrates’* happiness was the happiness of a block of wood or a stone; *Socrates* replied that the *Sophist’s* happiness was the happiness of someone with a skin rash <note>original: "itch"</note> who did nothing but itch and scratch. 

Neither of these opinions lacks support. For *Socrates’* view is largely upheld by the general consensus—even by the *Epicureans* <note>Followers of Epicurus, who sought "ataraxia" or freedom from fear and pain.</note> themselves—that virtue plays a major role in happiness. If this is true, it is certain that virtue is more useful for clearing away disturbances <note>original: "perturbations"</note> than for fulfilling desires. The *Sophist’s* opinion is supported by the claim we just discussed: that the good of **Advancement** is greater than the good of simple **Preservation**. This is because every time a desire is fulfilled, it has the appearance of advancement, just as movement in a circle has the appearance of moving forward.

But when the second question is decided the right way, it makes the first question unnecessary. For can it be doubted that there are some people who take more pleasure in enjoying pleasures than others, and yet are nevertheless less troubled by the loss or leaving of them? Therefore, this mindset—"To not use things so that you do not desire them, and to not desire them so that you do not fear losing them, are the signs of a small and cowardly mind" <note>original: *Non uti, ut non appetas: Non appetere, ut non metuas, sunt animi pusilli & diffidentis.*</note>. It seems to me that most of the doctrines of the philosophers are more fearful and cautious than...

<summary>Bacon examines the debate between Socratic peace of mind and Sophistic desire-fulfillment, arguing that a strong mind should be able to enjoy pleasures without becoming enslaved by the fear of losing them.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Sophists, happiness, felicity, Epicureans, virtue, Advancement vs. Preservation, desire, philosophy of mind</keywords>

--- Page 251 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Passive Good," specifically critiquing philosophers who prioritize absolute peace of mind at the expense of active engagement with the world.</meta>

# The Second Book

...than the nature of things requires. In this way, they have increased the fear of death by trying to cure it. For when they insist that a person's entire life should be nothing but a discipline or preparation for death, they inevitably make people think that death is a terrible enemy against whom one must never stop preparing. The poet [Juvenal] says it better:

> *Who considers the end of life among the gifts of nature.*
> <note>original: "Qui finem vitae extremum inter Munera ponat Naturae."</note>

Likewise, they have fought to make men’s minds too uniform and harmonious by not training them enough to handle conflicting motivations. I suppose the reason for this is that these philosophers were themselves dedicated to a private, free, and unengaged lifestyle. For, just as we see on a lute or similar instrument, a **ground** <note>A simple, repeating melody or bass line.</note>—though it may be sweet and seem to have many variations—does not train the hand for the difficult fingerings and complex passages required by a **set song** <note>A formal, pre-composed piece.</note> or a **voluntary** <note>An improvised musical composition.</note>. The difference between a **philosophical** and a **civic** life is very much the same. 

Therefore, men should imitate the wisdom of **jewelers**: if there is a flaw <note>original: "graine"</note>, a blemish <note>original: "cloude"</note>, or a crack <note>original: "ise"</note> in a gemstone that can be ground away without removing too much of the stone, they fix it. But if fixing it would shrink or diminish the stone too much, they will not touch it. In the same way, men should only seek **serenity** so long as they do not destroy **magnanimity** <note>Greatness of soul; the courage to take on large tasks.</note>.

Having, therefore, examined the **Good of Man** regarding private and individual matters as far as seems appropriate, we will now return to that **Good of Man** which concerns and [be-] <note>This sentence continues on the next page: "belongs to society."</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that over-preparing for death creates unnecessary fear and that true virtue requires a "civic life" capable of handling complex challenges, rather than a "philosophical life" of isolated peace.</summary>
<keywords>stoicism, death, music analogy, jewelry analogy, serenity vs. magnanimity, civic life vs. private life</keywords>

--- Page 252 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of individual "Passive Good" to "Duty," which Bacon defines as the ethical obligations one has toward others and society.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

[This study] considers society, which we may term **Duty**; because the term "duty" is more appropriate for a mind well-shaped and oriented toward others, just as the term "**virtue**" is applied to a mind well-formed and composed within itself. However, a person can neither understand virtue without some relation to society, nor duty without an inward disposition. This part of study may seem at first to belong to civil and political science; but it does not if it is observed closely. For it concerns the **governance** <note>original: "Regiment"</note> and government of every man over himself, and not over others. 

As in architecture, the instructions for framing the posts, beams, and other parts of a building are not the same as the method for joining them and erecting the building. And in mechanics, the instructions for how to frame an instrument or engine are not the same as the method for setting it to work and employing it; and yet, nevertheless, in describing the one, you incidentally describe its fitness for the other. Likewise, the doctrine of the **joining together** <note>original: "Coniugation"</note> of men in society differs from the doctrine of their individual conformity to it.

This part of Duty is subdivided into two parts: the common duty of every man as a human being or member of a state; and the respective or special duty of every man in his profession, vocation, and position. The first of these is **extant** <note>Already in existence; well-documented.</note> and well-studied, as has been said. The second, likewise, I may report as being scattered rather than deficient—and I acknowledge that this style of scattered writing is best for this kind of argument. For who can take it upon himself to write of the proper duty, virtue, and character...

<summary>Bacon distinguishes between "Virtue" (self-composition) and "Duty" (social orientation), arguing that moral philosophy should focus on how individuals govern themselves within a social context. He divides Duty into general civic obligations and specific professional responsibilities.</summary>
<keywords>Duty, Virtue, Ethics, Self-governance, Civil Science, Social Contract, Vocation, Professional Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 253 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "respective" or special duties belonging to specific professions. Bacon argues that those who actually practice a craft are better suited to write about its duties than theoretical scholars.</meta>

# The Second Book

...and right, of every individual vocation, profession, and position. For although sometimes a bystander may see more than a player, and there is a proverb more arrogant than sound, *That the valley best reveals the hill*; yet there is little doubt that men can write best and most realistically and substantially in their own professions. Furthermore, the writing of theoretical <note>original: "speculative"</note> men on practical <note>original: "Active"</note> matters, for the most part, seems to men of experience like **Phormio’s** argument on the art of war seemed to **Hannibal**—to be nothing but dreams and foolishness. <note>Phormio was a philosopher who gave a long lecture on military strategy to Hannibal, one of history's greatest generals; Hannibal famously ridiculed him for lecturing on a subject he had never practiced.</note>

There is only one vice which accompanies those who write in their own professions: they tend to glorify them to excess. But generally, it is to be wished (as something that would make learning truly solid and fruitful) that practical men would or could become writers.

In this regard, I cannot help but mention, **out of respect** <note>original: "Honoris causa"</note>, Your Majesty’s excellent book concerning the duty of a king: a work richly composed of **theology** <note>original: "Divinity"</note>, **morality**, and **politics** <note>original: "Policy"</note>, with a great infusion of all other arts. In my opinion, it is one of the most sound and healthy writings that I have read. It is not overheated by intense imagination <note>original: "invention"</note> nor chilled by negligence. It is not dizzy with confusion <note>original: "Dusinesse"</note>, as those works are whose authors lose themselves in their own organization; nor is it afflicted by **convulsions**, like those who get stuck <note>original: "Crampe"</note> on irrelevant matters. It does not smell of perfumes and makeup <note>original: "paintings"</note> as those do who seek to please the reader more than nature allows. Above all, it is well-disposed in its spirit, being consistent with truth and suitable for action, and far removed from that natural **infirmity**... <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "infir-".</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that practitioners, rather than theorists, write best about their own professions, and he praises King James I's "Basilikon Doron" as a primary example of a practical man writing wisely about his own duty.</summary>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

...weakness <note>original: "infirmity"</note> which I noted in those who write about their own professions: they tend to glorify them excessively. For Your Majesty has truly described, not a king of **Assyria** or **Persia** in their external glory, but a **Moses** or a **David**, who were the **pastors** <note>leaders or shepherds</note> of their people.

Nor can I ever lose <note>original: "leese"</note> from my memory what I heard Your Majesty—in that same sacred spirit of government—declare during a significant legal case: *That kings rule by their laws just as God rules by the laws of Nature, and they should use their supreme prerogative as rarely as God uses His power to perform miracles.* And yet, in your book on *Free Monarchy* <note>Referring to King James's *The True Law of Free Monarchies* (1598)</note>, you clearly help men understand that you know the full extent <note>original: "plenitude"</note> of a king's power and rights, as well as the scope <note>original: "Circle"</note> of his office and duty.

Thus I have presumed to cite <note>original: "alledge"</note> this excellent writing by Your Majesty as a prime or distinguished example of **treatises** <note>original: "Tractates"</note> concerning special and relative <note>original: "respective"</note> duties. I would have said just as much if it had been written a thousand years ago. Nor am I swayed by certain courtly conventions <note>original: "decencies"</note> which consider it flattery to offer praise in a person's presence. No, it is flattery to praise in "absence"—that is, when either the virtue being praised is missing, or the proper occasion is missing. In such cases, the praise is not natural but forced, either in its truth or its timing. 

But let **Cicero** be read in his *Speech for Marcellus* <note>original: "Oration pro Marcello"; a speech of praise Cicero delivered directly to Julius Caesar</note>, which is nothing but an excellent portrait <note>original: "Table"</note> of **Caesar’s** virtue, delivered to his **face**. There are examples of many other excellent people, far wiser than those who worry about such etiquette, which show we should never hesitate, when the occasion warrants it, to give just...

## The Second Book

...praises to those who are present or absent.

But to return: there is a further part belonging to the discussion of the duties of professions and vocations. This is a *relative* or *opposite* study concerning the frauds, tricks <note>original: "cautels"</note>, impostures, and vices of every profession, which has also been addressed by others. But how? It has been handled more as a *satire* and *cynically*, rather than seriously and wisely. For men have sought through wit to deride and disparage <note>original: "traduce"</note> much of what is good in professions, rather than using judgment to discover and separate <note>original: "sever"</note> what is corrupt. 

For as *Solomon* says: "He who comes to seek knowledge with a mind to scorn and censure will be sure to find material for his own mood, but no material for his instruction." Knowledge hides itself from the scorner who seeks it, but meets the studious man halfway <note>original Latin: "Quarenti derisori Scientiam, ipsa se abscondit: sed Studioso fit obuiam"</note>. 

However, managing this argument with integrity and truth—which I note as a deficiency—seems to me to be one of the best fortifications for honesty and virtue that can be established. For, as the fable of the *basilisk* goes, if he sees you first, you die; but if you see him first, he dies. So it is with deceits and evil arts: if they are spotted first, they lose <note>original: "leese"</note> their power <note>original: "life"</note>, but if they strike first <note>original: "prevent"</note>, they cause danger. 

Thus, we are much indebted to *Machiavelli* and others who write about what men actually do, and not what they ought to do. For it is not possible to join serpentine wisdom with dove-like innocence <note>original: "Columbine Innocency"</note> unless men know exactly all the conditions of the *serpent*: his lowliness and crawling on his belly, his shifty and slippery nature <note>original: "volubility and lubricity"</note>, his envy and sting, and all the other forms and natures of evil. For...

<summary>Bacon argues that a serious, non-satirical study of professional corruption and "evil arts" is necessary to protect virtue. He famously credits Machiavelli for describing human behavior as it is, rather than as it should be, to better equip the innocent.</summary>

<header>Of the Advancement of Learning.</header>

Without this, virtue lies open and unprotected <note>original: "unfenced"</note>. Indeed, an honest man can do no good in reclaiming those who are wicked without the help of the knowledge of evil. For people <note>original: "mē", a common abbreviation for "men"</note> with corrupt minds assume that honesty grows out of a simple-mindedness <note>original: "Simplicitye"</note> of character, and from believing preachers, schoolmasters, and people’s outward words. Thus, unless you can make them perceive that you understand the furthest reaches of their own corrupt opinions, they will despise all morality. "A fool does not receive the words of wisdom, unless you say those things which are already in his heart" <note>original Latin: *Non recipit stultus verba prudentiae, nisi ea dixeris, quae versantur in corde eius*, from Proverbs 18:2</note>.

To this section concerning *respective duty* <note>meaning the duties specific to one's role, rank, or relationship</note> also belong the duties between husband and wife, parent and child, master and servant. Likewise, it includes the laws of friendship and gratitude, the civil bonds of companies, colleges, and political bodies, neighborhoods, and all other related duties—not as they are parts of government and society, but as they relate to the shaping of the minds of individual persons.

The knowledge concerning *good that relates to society* handles the subject not only *simply* on its own, but also *comparatively*. This involves the weighing of duties between person and person, case and case, and between private <note>original: "particular"</note> and public interests. We see this in the legal action of *Lucius Brutus* <note>A founder of the Roman Republic who famously executed his own sons for conspiring to restore the monarchy</note> against his own sons, which was highly praised; yet what was said of it?
> "Unhappy man, however later generations may report those deeds." <note>original Latin: *Infelix, utcunque ferent ea fata Minores*, from Virgil’s *Aeneid*</note>

So the case was doubtful, and there were opinions

# The Second Book

on both sides. Again, we see that when Marcus Brutus and Cassius invited certain people to a supper whose opinions they meant to gauge <note>original: "feele"</note>—to see whether they were fit to be made their associates—they raised the question concerning the *killing of a tyrant* who is an usurper. They were divided in opinion: some held that servitude was the extreme of evils, while others held that tyranny was better than a civil war. There are a number of similar cases regarding comparative duty. 

Among these, the most frequent is the question of whether a great deal of good should follow from a small injustice. Jason of Thessaly <note>Jason of Pherae, a 4th-century BC ruler</note> decided this against the truth, saying: "Some things must be done unjustly, so that many things may be done justly" <note>original Latin: *Aliqua sunt iniustè facienda, vt multa iuſtè fieri poſſint*</note>. But the reply is good: "You have a guarantor for present justice, but you have no guarantor for the future" <note>original Latin: *Authorem præſentis Iuſtitiæ habes ſponſorem futuræ non habes*</note>. Men must pursue things which are just in the present and leave the future to divine providence. So then, we pass on from this general part concerning the model <note>original: "Exemplar"</note> and description of Good.

<margin>Concerning the cultivation of the mind <note>original Latin: *De cultura Animi*</note></margin>
Now, therefore, that we have spoken of this "fruit of life," it remains to speak of the husbandry <note>meaning the practical cultivation or farming of the soul</note> that belongs to it. Without this part, the former discussion seems to be no better than a fair image or statue, which is beautiful to contemplate but is without life and motion. Aristotle himself agrees with this in these words: "It is necessary, of course, to speak of virtue: both what it is and from what it is produced. For it would be almost useless to know what virtue is, but to be ignorant of the ways and means of acquiring it. For we must inquire not only into what kind of thing virtue is, but also how it may be attained..." <note>original Latin: *Neceſſe eſt ſcilicet de virtute dicere, & quid ſit, & ex quibus gignatur. Inutile enim fere fuerit, virtutem quidem noſſe, acquirendæ autem eius modos & vias ignorare Non enim de virtute tantum, qua ſpecie ſit, quærendum eſt, ſed & quomodo ſui copiam faciat...*</note>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

...for indeed we wish both to know the thing itself and to become masters of it: <note>original Latin: *enim volumus, et rem ipsam nosse & eius compotes fieri*</note> *This, however, will not succeed according to our wish, unless we know both of what things it consists and in what manner.* <note>original Latin: *Hoc autem ex voto non succedet, nisi sciamus & ex quibus & quo modo*</note> In such full words and with such repetition <note>original: "iteration"</note> does he emphasize <note>original: "inculcate"</note> this part. So says Cicero in great commendation of Cato the Younger <note>original: "Cato the second"</note>, that he had applied himself to philosophy, *“not for the purpose of arguing, but for the purpose of living.”* <note>original Latin: *Non ita disputandi Causa, sed ita viuendi*</note>

And although the neglect of our times—wherein few men hold any consultations regarding the reformation of their life (as Seneca excellently says, *“Everyone deliberates about the parts of life, but no one about the whole”* <note>original Latin: *De partibus vitæ quisque deliberat, de summa nemo*</note>)—may make this part seem superfluous; yet I must conclude with that aphorism of Hippocrates: *“Those who are seized by a serious disease and yet feel no pain, their mind is diseased.”* <note>original Latin: *Qui graui morbo correptì dolores non sentiunt, ijs mens ægrotat*</note> They need medicine not only to soothe the disease but to awaken the senses. 

And if it be said that the cure of men's minds belongs to sacred divinity, it is most true. But yet moral philosophy may be presented to her as a wise servant and humble handmaid. For as the Psalm says, *“That the eyes of the handmaid look perpetually toward the mistress,”* <note>Psalm 123:2</note> and yet no doubt many things are left to the discretion of the handmaid to discern the mistress's will; so ought moral philosophy to give constant attention to the doctrines of divinity, and yet in such a way that it may yield from itself (within due limits) many sound and profitable directions.

This part, therefore, because of its excellence, I cannot but find it exceedingly strange that it is not reduced to written inquiry—especially because it consists of much matter wherein both speech and action...

It is reasonable, therefore, that we set it forth in greater detail, both because of its worthiness and so we may justify ourselves for reporting it as lacking <note>original: "deficient"</note>—which seems almost incredible and is otherwise conceived and presupposed by those themselves who have written on it. We will therefore list some headings or points concerning it, so that it may better appear what it is and whether it exists <note>original: "is extant"</note>.

First, therefore, in this—as in all things which are practical—we ought to calculate <note>original: "cast up our account"</note> what is in our power and what is not. For the one may be dealt with by way of alteration, but the other only by way of application. The farmer <note>original: "husbandman"</note> cannot control <note>original: "command"</note> either the nature of the earth or the seasons of the weather; no more can the physician control the constitution of the patient nor the variety of medical complications <note>original: "accidents"</note>. So in the cultivation <note>original: "Culture"</note> and cure of the mind of man, two things are outside our command: points of nature <note>natural disposition</note> and points of fortune <note>external circumstances</note>. For our work is limited and tied to the foundation <note>original Latin: *Basis*</note> of the one and the conditions of the other. In these things, therefore, it is left to us to proceed by application: *"All fortune is to be overcome by enduring"* <note>original Latin: *Vincenda est omnis fortuna ferendo*</note>; and so likewise, *"All nature is to be overcome by enduring."* <note>original Latin: *vincenda est omnis Natura ferendo*</note> But when we speak of "suffering" <note>original: "sufferinge," here meaning to endure or undergo</note>, we do not speak of a dull and neglected suffering, but of a wise and industrious suffering which draws,

# Of the Advancement of Learning

...and contrives use and advantage out of that which seems adverse and contrary; which is that property which we call *Accommodating* or *Applying*. Now the wisdom of *Application* rests principally in the exact and distinct knowledge of the preceding state, or disposition, to which we apply it: for we cannot fit a garment unless we first take measure of the body.

So then, the first article of this knowledge is to set down sound and true classifications <note>original: "distributions"</note> and descriptions of the several characters and tempers of men’s natures and dispositions—especially having regard to those differences which are most fundamental <note>original: "radical"</note> in being the sources and causes of the rest, or most frequent in their combination <note>original: "concurrence or commixture"</note>. In this matter, the handling of just a few of them in passing—merely to better describe the middle ground <note>original: "mediocrities"</note> of virtues—cannot satisfy this intention. For if it deserves to be considered *that there are minds which are suited to great matters, and others to small* (which Aristotle handles, or ought to have handled, under the name of *Magnanimity* <term>Greatness of soul; the virtue of being worthy of great things and knowing it</term>), does it not also deserve to be considered *that there are minds suited to attend to many matters and others to few?* 

So it is that some people can divide their attention <note>original: "divide themselves"</note>, while others can perhaps do exactly well, but it must be only in a few things at once. And so there comes to be a *narrowness of mind* as well as a *pusillanimity* <term>Small-mindedness; a lack of courage or determination</term>. And again, *that some minds are suited to that which may be dispatched at once or within a short period of time; others to that which begins far off, and is to be won with length of*

<meta>catchword: pursuit</meta>

<summary>Bacon argues that the first step in "cultivating the mind" is to create a detailed taxonomy of human dispositions, noting that minds differ not just in scale (magnanimity) but in their capacity for multitasking and their orientation toward time.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Cultivation of the Mind," specifically the need for a systematic classification of human characters and dispositions to better understand how to influence or educate them.</meta>

# The Second Book

...pursuit—"even then she aims and cherishes" <note>original: *iam tum tenditque fovetque*; a reference to Virgil’s Aeneid describing Juno’s persistent ambition</note>. So that there may fitly be said to be a <term>long-suffering</term> <note>original: <term>longanimity</term></note>, which is commonly also ascribed to God, just as <term>greatness of soul</term> <note>original: <term>magnanimity</term></note> is. 

Furthermore, it deserved to be considered by Aristotle *that there is a disposition in conversation (assuming it involves things that do not at all touch or concern a person’s self) to soothe and please; and a contrary disposition to contradict and cross others*. And does it not deserve much better to be considered *that there is a disposition—not in conversation or talk, but in matters of a more serious nature (and still assuming it concerns things that are merely indifferent)—to take pleasure in the good of another, and a contrary disposition to take distaste at the good of another*? This is what we properly call <term>good nature</term> or <term>ill nature</term>, <term>kindness</term> <note>original: <term>benignity</term></note> or <term>malice</term> <note>original: <term>malignity</term></note>. 

Therefore, I cannot marvel enough that this part of knowledge regarding the several characters of natures and dispositions should be omitted both in morality and politics <note>original: "policy"</note>, considering it is of such great service and support <note>original: "ministry and suppeditation"</note> to them both. 

A person shall find in the traditions of <term>astrology</term> some clever and appropriate divisions of men’s natures according to the dominance of the <term>planets</term>: *lovers of quiet, lovers of action, lovers of victory, lovers of honor, lovers of pleasure, lovers of arts, lovers of change, and so forth*. 

One shall find in the wisest types of those reports <note>original: "relations"</note> which the Italians make regarding <term>papal elections</term> <note>original: <term>conclaves</term></note>, the natures of the various <term>cardinals</term> handsomely and vividly portrayed. 

Finally, a person shall encounter in every day’s conversation the descriptions of *sensitive, dry, formal, real, humorous <note>original: "humorous"; in the 17th century, this referred to a person’s "humors" or temperament—meaning moody or driven by a particular disposition rather than "funny"</note>, certain, "a man of first impressions" <note>original: *huomo di prima impressione*; one who is stubborn once an opinion is formed</note>, "a man of final impressions" <note>original: *huomo di ultima impressione*; one who is easily swayed by the last thing they hear</note>*, and the like.

<summary>Bacon argues that moral and political philosophy have neglected the systematic study of human character types. He points to astrology, Italian diplomatic reports, and common social labels as existing—though incomplete—attempts to categorize these diverse dispositions.</summary>

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that while observations about human nature exist in literature and experience, they have not yet been systematized into a practical science for improving the mind.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

And yet, nevertheless, these kinds of observations wander in words but are not fixed as a subject of inquiry. For though many of these distinctions are found, we conclude no practical rules <note>original: "precepts"</note> based on them. In this, our fault is the greater because history, poetry, and daily experience are like beautiful fields where these observations grow. Of these, we make a few bouquets <note>original: "poesies," a pun on "poetry" and a small bunch of flowers</note> to hold in our hands, but no man brings them to the laboratory <note>original: "confectionary"; in this period, a place where medicinal recipes or "receipts" were prepared</note> so that prescriptions might be made from them for the practical use of life.

Of a very similar kind are those natural impressions which are imposed upon the mind *by sex, by age, by region, by health and sickness, by beauty and deformity, and the like*, which are inherent and not external. And again, there are those which are caused by external fortune, such as *sovereignty, nobility, obscure birth, riches, want, magistracy, private life, prosperity, adversity, constant fortune, variable fortune, rising by a leap <note>original: *per saltum*</note>, rising by degrees <note>original: *per gradus*</note>*, and the like. And therefore we see that Plautus makes it a wonder to see an old man who is generous, saying: "The kindness of this man is like that of a young man" <note>original: *Benignitas huius ut adolescentuli est*</note>. Saint Paul concludes that severity of discipline was to be used toward the Cretans—"Rebuke them sharply" <note>original: *Increpa eos durè*</note>—based on the disposition of their country: "Cretans are always liars, evil beasts, lazy bellies" <note>original: *Cretenses semper mendaces, malæ Bestiæ, ventres pigri*</note>. Sallust notes that it is usual with kings to desire contradictory things: "but for the most part, the wills of kings, as they are vehement, so are they unstable, and often contradictory to themselves" <note>original: *sed plerunque Regiæ voluntates, ut vehementes sunt, sic mobiles, sæpeque ipsæ sibi aduersæ*</note>. Tacitus ob- <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the full word is "observes."</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that observations of human nature found in history and poetry should be turned into practical "prescriptions" for life, noting how factors like age, health, and social status influence the mind.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s argument that human character and the effects of fortune must be systematically studied as part of "Moral Philosophy," comparing this need to the scientific approach used in agriculture and medicine.</meta>

# The Second Book

...observes how rarely the raising of one's fortune improves their character: **"only Vespasian was changed for the better"** <note>original: *solus Vespasianus, mutatus in melius*; a quote from Tacitus regarding the Roman Emperor Vespasian</note>. Pindar makes an observation that great and sudden fortune for the most part defeats men **"who cannot digest great prosperity"** <note>original: *Qui magnam fœlicitatem concoquere non possunt*</note>. So the Psalm shows it is easier to keep a moderate balance in the enjoyment of fortune than in the increase of fortune: **"If riches increase, do not set your heart upon them"** <note>original: *Divitiæ si affluant, nolite Cor apponere*; Psalm 62:10</note>. These observations and the like, I do not deny, are briefly mentioned by Aristotle in passing in his *Rhetoric*, and are handled in some scattered discourses; but they were never incorporated into Moral Philosophy, to which they essentially belong. This is just as the knowledge of the diversity of soils and earths belongs to agriculture, and the knowledge of the diversity of temperaments <note>original: "Complexions"</note> and constitutions belongs to the physician—unless we mean to follow the lack of judgment shown by empirics <note>original: "indiscretion of Emperiques"; practitioners who rely on trial and error or experience without scientific theory</note>, who administer the same medicines to all patients.

Another article of this knowledge is the inquiry regarding the emotions <note>original: "affections"</note>. For as in the treatment of the body, it is necessary first to know the various temperaments and constitutions, secondly the diseases, and lastly the cures; so in the treatment of the mind, after knowing the various characters of men's natures, it follows in order to know the diseases and infirmities of the mind, which are nothing other than the perturbations and imbalances <note>original: "distempers"</note> of the emotions.

<summary>Bacon argues that Moral Philosophy must move beyond generalities to study how different characters react to fortune and emotions, using the systematic methods of medicine and agriculture as a model.</summary>
<keywords>Vespasian, Pindar, Aristotle, Moral Philosophy, Psychology of Emotions, Empirics, Medical Analogy, Fortune and Character</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "culture of the mind," specifically moving from the study of human character to the study of emotions (affections). It begins by completing the word "affections" (emotions) from the previous page.</meta>

[...emotions] <note>original: "affections"</note>. For as the ancients who wrote on politics in popular states <note>democracies or republics where the people held power</note> were accustomed to compare the people to the sea, and the orators to the winds—because the sea would naturally be calm and quiet if the winds did not move and trouble it—so the people would be peaceable and manageable if seditious orators did not set them in motion and agitation. Similarly, it may be aptly said that the mind, by its very nature, would be temperate and steady if the emotions, like winds, did not throw it into tumult and disturbance.

And here again, I find it strange (as I noted before) that *Aristotle* should have written several volumes on *Ethics* and yet never handled the emotions, which are the principal subject of that field. Yet in his *Rhetoric*, where they are considered only collaterally and in a secondary degree (insofar as they may be moved by speech), he finds a place for them and handles them well in terms of quantity; but where their true place is, he omits <note>original: "pretermitteth"</note> them. For his discussions about pleasure and pain cannot satisfy this inquiry, any more than a person who generally handles the nature of light can be said to handle the nature of *colors*. For pleasure and pain are to the particular emotions what light is to particular *colors*. 

I suppose the *Stoics* had undertaken better labors <note>original: "trauailes"</note> in this subject, as far as I can gather from what we have second-hand. But yet it is likely their work was, according to their manner, focused more on the subtlety of definitions (which in a subject of this nature are mere curiosities <note>refined points of intellectual interest but little practical use</note>) rather than on practical and thorough descriptions and observations. So likewise, I find some particular [writings...]

He criticizes Aristotle for neglecting emotions in his Ethics and notes that the Stoics' approach was too focused on abstract definitions rather than practical observation.</summary>

### The second book.

...writings of an elegant nature touching on some of the **emotions** <note>original: "affections"</note>, such as **anger**, **comfort** following **misfortune** <note>original: "adverse accidents"</note>, **shyness** <note>original: "Tendernesse of Countenance"</note>, and others. But the poets and writers of histories are the best teachers <note>original: "Doctors"</note> of this knowledge. In their works, we may find depicted with great vitality how emotions are kindled and incited; how they are pacified and restrained; and how they are kept from being put into action or increasing further. We see how they reveal themselves, how they work, how they vary, how they grow and strengthen, how they are entangled with one another, and how they fight and encounter each other, along with other similar details. 

Among these, this last point is of special use in moral and civil matters: how, I say, to set one emotion against another and to master one by using another. This is just as we use one animal to hunt another or one bird to catch another, which we perhaps otherwise could not capture so easily. Upon this foundation is built that excellent use of **reward and punishment** <note>original: "Præmium and pæna"</note> by which civil states exist, employing the dominant emotions of fear and hope to suppress and bridle the rest. For just as in the government of states it is sometimes necessary to bridle one faction with another, so it is in the internal government of the self.

Now we come to those points that are within our own command and have power and influence over the mind to affect the will and desire <note>original: "Appetite"</note> and to alter **behavior** <note>original: "Manners"</note>. In this area, writers should have addressed **habit** <note>original: "Custome"</note>...

<header>Of the Advancement of Learning.</header>

*Exercise, Habit, Education, example, Imitation, Emulation, Company, Friends, praise, Reproof, exhortation, fame, laws, Books, studies:* these have specific uses in moral matters. The mind is influenced by them, and from these, such **prescriptions and regimens** <note>original: "receipts & Regiments"; rules of conduct and spiritual exercises</note> are composed and described that they may seem to recover or preserve the health and good state of the mind, as far as pertains to human medicine. We will examine one or two of these as examples of the rest, because it would take too long to pursue them all; therefore, we resume the discussion of **custom and habit**.

The opinion of Aristotle seems to me to be a negligent opinion. He says that among those things which consist by nature, nothing can be changed by custom, using this example: that if a stone is thrown upward ten thousand times, it will not learn to ascend on its own, and that we do not learn to see or hear better by often seeing or hearing. 

For although this principle is true in things where nature is **absolute** <note>original: "Peremptory"</note> (the reason for which we cannot stop to discuss now), it is otherwise in things where nature allows for **flexibility** <note>original: "latitude"</note>. For he might have seen that a tight glove will fit more easily with use, and that a **rod** <note>original: "wand"</note> will, through use, bend in a direction other than the way it grew. By the use of the voice, we speak louder and stronger; and by the habit of enduring heat or cold, we endure it better, and so on. These latter examples have a closer resemblance to the subject of **behavior** <note>original: "Manners"</note> which he handles than the instances he cites. But allowing

<meta>catchword: his</meta>

<summary>Bacon challenges Aristotle’s view that nature cannot be altered by custom, arguing that while some natural laws are absolute, human behavior and physical capabilities possess a "latitude" that allows them to be shaped through habit and exercise.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s critique of Aristotle’s approach to ethics. While the previous page argued that nature is flexible and can be shaped by habit, this page begins a list of practical "precepts" or rules for how one should actually train the mind and body to acquire these habits.</meta>

<header>The Second Book. 74</header>

Since his conclusion was *that virtues and vices consist in habit*, he ought to have taught all the more the method of cultivating <note>original: "superinducing"</note> that habit. For there are many precepts regarding the wise ordering of the exercises of the mind, just as there are for ordering the exercises of the body, of which we will recite a few.

The first shall be that we beware that we do not take, at the start, either too high a strain or too weak a one. For if the strain is too high, you discourage a hesitant <note>original: "different"</note> nature; in a confident nature, you breed a false sense of ease <note>original: "an opinion of facility"</note> and thus laziness; and in all natures, you create a further expectation that cannot be maintained, leading to dissatisfaction in the end. Conversely, if the strain is too weak, you cannot expect to perform or overcome any great task.

Another precept is to practice all things chiefly at two separate times: the one when the mind is best disposed, and the other when it is worst disposed. By the first, you may gain a great step forward; by the second, you may work out the knots and obstacles <note>original: "Stondes"</note> of the mind, and make the intermediate times more easy and pleasant.

Another precept is one that Aristotle mentions in passing: to always lean toward the contrary extreme of that toward which we are by nature inclined. This is like rowing against the stream, or making a rod <note>original: "wand"</note> straight by bending it in the opposite direction of its natural crookedness.

Another precept is that the mind is led to anything better, and with more sweetness and happiness, if the goal you aim for <note>original: "whereunto you pretend"</note> is not first in the

Is this not why those excellent books and discourses of the ancient writers are of so little effect toward leading an honest life? <meta>This page continues Bacon’s discussion on the "culture" or cultivation of the mind. He is examining how to encourage good mental habits and eliminate bad ones, comparing the process of moral development to the way nature creates a whole organism at once, rather than piece by piece.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning.

...at other times in a more depraved <note>original: "depraued"; meaning corrupted or immoral</note> state. Therefore, the purpose of this practice is to fix and cherish the good moments of the mind and to obliterate and remove the evil. 

The fixing of the good has been practiced by two means: vows (or constant resolutions) and observances (or exercises). These are not to be valued so much for themselves, but because they keep the mind in continual obedience. 

The obliteration of evil has been practiced by two means: some kind of redemption or atonement for what is past, and a fresh start <note>original: *de Novo*</note> for the time to come. But this part seems sacred and religious, and rightly so; for all good moral philosophy (as was said before) is merely a handmaid to religion.

Therefore, we will conclude with this last point, which is the most concise <note>original: "compendious"</note> and direct of all means—and furthermore, the most noble and effective way to bring the mind toward virtue and a good state. This involves choosing and setting for oneself good and virtuous goals for one's life, such as may be reasonably within one's power to achieve. 

For if these two things are assumed—first, that a man sets honest and good goals before himself, and second, that he remains resolute, constant, and true to them—it follows that he shall mold himself into all virtues at once. This is indeed like the work of nature, whereas the other course is like the work of the hand. For when a carver makes a statue <note>original: "image"</note>, he shapes only 그 part on which he is working; if he is working on *the face*, that part which...

<summary>Bacon argues that the most effective way to attain virtue is to commit to noble life goals, which allows the mind to develop all virtues simultaneously—much like nature grows a whole organism at once—rather than trying to learn virtues one by one.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s comparison between how art (like sculpture) and nature (like a growing plant) create form. He argues that while a sculptor works on one part of a statue at a time, nature develops all parts of an organism simultaneously. ### The Second Book

...shall be; the rest of the body is but a rough stone still, until such time as he gets to it. But on the contrary, when Nature makes a flower or a living creature, she forms the beginnings <note>original: "rudiments"</note> of all the parts at once. Similarly, in obtaining virtue through *habit*, while a man practices temperance, he does not gain much in fortitude or similar virtues. *But* when he dedicates and applies himself to *good goals*, whatever virtue the pursuit and journey toward those goals recommends to him, he is already invested with a prior disposition to conform himself to it. 

*Aristotle* expresses this state of mind excellently, saying that it should not be called *virtuous*, but *divine*. His words are these: "It is fitting to oppose brutality with that virtue which is above humanity: heroic or divine virtue" <note>original: *Immanitati autem consentaneum est, opponere eam, quae supra humanitatem est, heroicam sive divinam virtutem.*</note>. And a little later: "For just as a wild beast has neither vice nor virtue, neither does God. But this state [the divine] is something higher than virtue, just as the other [the bestial] is something different from vice" <note>original: *Nam ut fera, neque vitium, neque virtus est; sic neque Dei. Sed hic quidem status altius quiddam virtute est, ille aliud quiddam a vitio.*</note>. 

Therefore, we can see what a high degree <note>original: "celsitud"</note> of honor *Pliny the Younger* <note>original: "Plinius secundus"</note> attributes to the Emperor *Trajan* in his panegyric <note>original: "funeral oration"; Pliny's famous speech was a Panegyricus, or formal speech of praise, rather than a funeral oration.</note>, where he said: *That men needed to make no other prayers to the gods but that they would continue to be as good lords to them as Trajan had been.* It was as if he had not only been an imitation of divine nature, but a pattern for it. 

But these are heathen and secular <note>original: "profane"</note> passages, having only a shadow of that divine state of mind to which religion and the holy faith conduct men. They do this by imprinting upon their souls Charity, which is excellently called the "bond of perfection" <note>A reference to the biblical verse Colossians 3:14: "And above all these things put on charity, which is the bond of perfectness."</note>, because it encompasses and fastens all virtues together. --- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page continues Bacon’s discussion on the "divine" state of mind, contrasting the formal, often clumsy methods of moral philosophy with the transformative power of charity (divine love).</meta>

->Of the Advancement of Learning.<-

...which is but a false imitation of divine love. "Love is a better teacher for human life" <note>original: *Amor melior Sophista, ad humanam vitam*</note>; Love teaches a man to carry himself better than the **Sophist** <note>A teacher of philosophy or rhetoric, often associated here with rigid or clever rules.</note> or **Teacher** <note>original: *Preceptor*</note> can. The author calls the teacher "left-handed" because, with all his rules and precepts, he cannot shape a man as dexterously, nor with that ease to value himself and govern himself, as love can do. 

So, certainly, if a man’s mind is truly inflamed with charity, it works him suddenly into greater perfection than all the doctrine of morality can do, which is but a sophist in comparison to the other. Furthermore, as **Xenophon** <note>A Greek historian and student of Socrates.</note> truly observed, all other affections, though they raise the mind, do so by distorting it through the unseemliness of ecstasies or excesses; but only love exalts the mind while, at the same instant, settling and composing it. 

So it is in all other excellencies: though they advance nature, they are subject to excess. Only charity admits no excess. For as we see, by aspiring to be like God in power, the angels transgressed and fell: "I will ascend and be like the Most High" <note>original: *Ascendam, et ero similis altissimo*</note>. By aspiring to be like God in knowledge, man transgressed and fell: "You shall be like gods, knowing good and evil" <note>original: *Eritis sicut Dii scientes bonum et malum*</note>. But by aspiring to a likeness of God in goodness or love, neither man nor angel ever transgressed, nor shall ever transgress. For we are called to that imitation: "Love your enemies, do good to those who hate you, and pray for those who persecute and slander you, so that you may be the children of your Father who is in heaven, who makes his sun to rise upon..." <note>original: *Diligite inimicos vestros, Benefacite eis qui oderunt vos, et orate pro persequentibus et Calumniantibus vos ut sitis filii patris vestri qui in caelis est, qui solem suum orire facit super...* from Matthew 5:44-45</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that divine love (charity) is a more effective and balanced teacher of virtue than formal moral philosophy. He notes that while seeking power or knowledge led to the fall of angels and men, seeking to imitate God's goodness through love is the only ambition that cannot lead to excess or sin.</summary>

> *...with the second youth who shall stand up in his stead.* <note>original: *adolescente secundo qui consurgit pro eo* (Ecclesiastes 4:15)</note>

Here is expressed that which was noted by **Sulla** <note>original: *Sylla*; Roman general and dictator.</note> first, and after him by **Tiberius** <note>The second Roman emperor.</note>: *“More people adore the rising sun than the setting or the midday sun.”* <note>original: *Plures adorant solem orientem, quam occidentem vel meridianum.*</note>

> *If the spirit of the ruler rises up against you, do not leave your place; for healing causes great sins to cease.* <note>original: *Si spiritus potestatem habentis ascenderit super te, locum tuum ne dimiseris, quia Curatio faciet cessare peccata maxima.* (Ecclesiastes 10:4)</note>

Here caution is given that upon facing displeasure, retiring is the most unfit course of all; for a man leaves things at their worst and deprives himself of the means to make them better.

> *There was a little city, and few men in it; and there came a great king against it, and besieged it, and built great bulwarks around it. Now there was found in it a poor wise man, and he by his wisdom delivered the city; yet no man remembered that same poor man.* <note>original: *Erat Ciuitas parua & pauci in ea viri; venit contra eam rex magnus, & vallauit eam, instruxitque munitiones per Gyrum, & perfecta est obsidio, inuentusque est in ea vir pauper & sapiens, & liberauit eam per sapientiam suam, & nullus deinceps recordatus est hominis illius pauperis;* (Ecclesiastes 9:14–15)</note>

Here the corruption of **states** <term>Political bodies or governments</term> is set forth: they do not esteem virtue or merit longer than they have a use for it.

> *A soft answer breaks anger.* <note>original: *Mollis responsio frangit iram.* (Proverbs 15:1)</note>

Here it is noted that silence or a rough answer exasperates, but a prompt and temperate answer pacifies.

> *The way of the slothful is like a hedge of thorns.* <note>original: *Iter pigrorum, quasi sepes spinarum.* (Proverbs 15:19)</note>

Here is vividly represented how laborious **sloth** <note>Laziness or procrastination.</note> proves to be in the end; for when things are deferred until the last instant, and nothing is prepared beforehand, every step finds a briar or **impediment** <note>Obstacle.</note> which catches or stops it.

> *Better is the end of a speech than the beginning.* <note>original: *Melior est finis orationis quam principium.* (Ecclesiastes 7:8)</note>

Here is criticized the vanity of formal speakers, who study more...
