> *He who shows partiality in judgment does not do well; for such a person will desert the truth for a piece of bread.* <note>original: *Qui cognoscit in iudicio faciem, non bene facit, iste et pro buccella panis deseret veritatem.* (Proverbs 28:21)</note> 

Here it is noted that it would be better for a judge to be a bribe-taker than a respecter of persons; for a corrupt judge does not offend as easily as a weak-willed <note>original: *facile*; meaning easily influenced or yielding</note> one.

> *A poor man who oppresses the poor is like a sweeping rain which leaves no food;* <note>original: *Vir pauper calumnians pauperes, similis est imbri vehementi, in quo paratur fames;* (Proverbs 28:3)</note> 

here is expressed the extremity of extortion born of necessity, illustrated in the ancient fable of the full and the hungry leech. <note>The fable suggests that a hungry leech (or a poor, desperate ruler) is more dangerous because it sucks more blood than one that is already full.</note>

> *A righteous man falling down before the wicked is like a troubled fountain and a corrupt spring;* <note>original: *Fons turbatus pede, & vena corrupta, est iustus cadens coram impio:* (Proverbs 25:26)</note> 

here it is noted that one judicial and public <note>original: *exemplar*; serving as a public example</note> iniquity in the eyes of the world troubles the fountains of justice more than many individual injuries that are overlooked through connivance <term>deliberate ignoring of wrongdoing</term>.

> *Whoever robs his father or his mother and says, "It is no transgression," is a companion to a murderer;* <note>original: *Qui subtrahit aliquid a patre & a matre, & dicit hoc non esse peccatum, particeps est homicidij;* (Proverbs 28:24)</note> 

here it is noted that while men, when wronging their best friends, tend to minimize <note>original: *extenuate*</note> their fault—as if they might presume or be bold with them—it actually aggravates their fault instead, and turns it from a simple injury into an act of impiety.

> *Make no friendship with an angry man, and with a furious man you shall not go;* <note>original: *Noli esse amicus homini iracundo, nec ambulato cum homine furioso;* (Proverbs 22:24)</note> 

here caution is given that in the choice of our friends, we should principally avoid those who are impatient, as they are the ones who will draw <note>original: *espouse*</note> us into many factions and quarrels.

## The Second Book

*He who troubles his own house shall inherit the wind:* <note>original: *Qui conturbat domum suam possidebit ventum* (Proverbs 11:29)</note> here it is noted that in domestic separations and breaches, men promise themselves peace of mind and contentment, but they are always deceived in their expectations, and it turns to wind. <note>In this context, "wind" signifies something empty, chaotic, or vanishing.</note>

*A wise son makes a glad father, but a foolish son is a sorrow to his mother.* <note>original: *Filius sapiens lætificat patrem, filius vero stultus mæstitia est matri suæ* (Proverbs 10:1)</note> Here a distinction is made: fathers find the most comfort in the "good proof" <note>meaning the demonstrated success or character</note> of their sons, while mothers find the most discomfort in their failure. This is because women are thought to have little discernment of virtue but focus instead on fortune. <note>original: *fortune*; here referring to a person's outward success, wealth, or status.</note>

*He who conceals a transgression seeks friendship, but he who repeats a matter separates allies;* <note>original: *Qui celat delictum quærit amicitiam, sed qui altero sermone repetit, separat fœderatos* (Proverbs 17:9)</note> here caution is given that reconciliation is better managed by an *Amnesty* <term>a formal forgetting of past offenses</term> and passing over what is past, rather than by *Apologies* and *excuses.* <note>original: *excusations*</note>

*In every good work there will be abundance, but where there are many words, there is frequently poverty:* <note>original: *In omni opere bono erit abundantia, ubi autem verba sunt plurima, ibi frequenter egestas* (Proverbs 14:23)</note> here it is noted that talk and discourse abound most where there is idleness and want.

*The first to plead his case seems just, but the other party comes and examines him;* <note>original: *Primus in sua causa iustus, sed venit altera pars, & inquiret in eum* (Proverbs 18:17)</note> here it is observed that in all cases, the first story told carries great weight; consequently, the bias <note>original: *prejudice*</note> created by it will be difficult to remove, unless some abuse or falsehood in the information is detected.

*The words of a double-tongued man are as if they were simple, and they reach into the innermost parts of the belly;* <note>original: *Verba bilinguis quasi simplicia, & ipsa perveniunt ad interiora ventris* (Proverbs 18:8)</note> here a distinction is made regarding flattery and insinuation which seems natural and artless,

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon’s commentary on the Proverbs of Solomon, using them as foundational examples for his "civil knowledge" or "policy." It concludes his specific analysis of these maxims before justifying why he spent so much time on them.</meta>

...does not sink far; but that which has the appearance of nature, liberty, and simplicity enters deeply.

*He who corrects a mocker brings shame on himself; and he who rebukes a wicked man gets himself a blot.* <note>original: *Qui erudit derisorem, ipse sibi iniuriam facit & qui arguit Impium sibi maculam gerit* (Proverbs 9:7)</note> Here caution is given regarding how we offer criticism to arrogant and scornful natures, whose habit is to regard it as an insult <note>original: *contumely*</note> and return it accordingly.

*Give opportunity to a wise man, and he will be yet wiser.* <note>original: *Da sapienti occasionem & addetur ei sapientia* (Proverbs 9:9)</note> Here a distinction is made between wisdom that has become a deep-seated habit and that which is merely verbal and floating only in the imagination <note>original: *conceite*</note>. For the former is quickened and doubled when an opportunity is presented; the latter is stunned and confused.

*As faces are reflected in water, so are the hearts of men clear to the wise.* <note>original: *Quo modo in aquis resplendent vultus prospicientium, sic corda hominum manifesta sunt prudentibus* (Proverbs 27:19)</note> Here the mind of a wise man is compared to a mirror <note>original: *glasse*</note>, in which the images of every variety of natures and customs are represented; from this representation comes that practical application:

*He who is wise will be adapted to countless ways of life.* <note>original: *Qui sapit innumeris moribus aptus erit* (Ovid, *Ars Amatoria*)</note>

Thus I have dwelled somewhat longer upon these political maxims of Solomon than is suited to the scale of an example. I was led by a desire to give authority to this branch of knowledge (which I previously noted as deficient) by using such an excellent precedent. I have also accompanied them with brief observations which, to my understanding, do no violence to the meaning, though I know they may be applied to a more sacred <note>original: *diuine*</note> use.

<summary>Francis Bacon concludes his analysis of Solomon’s Proverbs, illustrating how they provide practical psychological insights into human nature and social conduct. He justifies this long digression as a way to prove that "civil knowledge" is a legitimate and necessary field of study.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon’s justification for using aphorisms and examples to teach "civil knowledge." He transitions from discussing the Proverbs of Solomon to the general use of parables and fables in antiquity, ultimately arguing that historical examples are the most effective way to teach the art of business and government.</meta>

The Second Book 92

But it is accepted even in theology that some interpretations—and indeed some writings—have more of the "eagle" <note>This refers to the eagle as a symbol of high, soaring insight, traditionally associated with the Gospel of John.</note> in them than others. However, treating these as instructions for life, they might have been expanded into a long discourse if I had chosen to break them down and illustrate them with deductions <note>original: "diducements"</note> and examples.

Nor was this practice used only by the Hebrews; it is generally found in the wisdom of ancient times. As men discovered any observation they thought was beneficial for life, they would collect and express it as a parable, aphorism, or fable. But as for fables, they served as substitutes <note>original: "vicegerents"</note> and supplements where real-life examples were lacking. Now that our era is rich in history, the aim is more accurate when the target is a living example <note>Bacon means that historical facts are better "marks" or targets for study than fictional fables.</note>. Therefore, the form of writing which of all others is best suited for this changing subject of business <note>original: "negotiation"</note> and public affairs is the one Machiavelli chose so wisely and aptly for government: namely, discourse based on histories or examples. For knowledge drawn fresh and firsthand from specific details <note>original: "particulars"</note> knows the way back to specific applications best. It also has much more vitality for practice when the commentary follows the example, rather than when the example is forced to follow the commentary. This is not just a matter of arrangement, as it might first appear, but a matter of substance. For when the example is the foundation, being set down in a full historical account, it is presented with all its circumstances—which can sometimes correct or limit the commentary.

<summary>Bacon argues that while ancient wisdom relied on fables when examples were scarce, modern political and "civil" knowledge is best taught through historical examples, praising Machiavelli's method of analyzing history to derive practical rules.</summary>

...thereupon made, and sometimes supply it as a very pattern for gain. On the other hand, the examples alleged for the sake of the discourse itself are cited briefly and without specific detail; they carry a subservient <note>original: "servile"</note> appearance toward the discourse which they are brought in to support.

But this distinction is worth remembering: just as a history of *Times* (chronicles) is the best foundation for the discourse of Government—such as *Machiavelli* handles—so *Histories of Lives* (biographies) are the most proper for the discourse of business, because business is more concerned with private *Actions*. Indeed, there is a foundation of discourse for this purpose that is even better than both of those: *discourse upon letters*. These are letters that are wise and weighty, such as many of *Cicero’s letters to Atticus* <note>original: "Cicero ad Atticum"</note> and others. For letters provide a greater and more detailed representation of business than either *Chronicles* or *Biographies*. Thus, we have spoken of both the matter and form of this part of *Civil* knowledge concerning *Negotiation* <note>In this context, "negotiation" refers to the general conduct of business and affairs, not just bargaining.</note>, which we note to be lacking <note>original: "deficient"; Bacon uses this term to identify branches of knowledge that have not yet been adequately developed.</note>.

Yet there is another division of this topic which differs as much from what we have discussed as *being wise* <note>original: "sapere"</note> differs from *being wise for oneself* <note>original: "sibi Sapere"</note>. The one moves, as it were, toward the circumference (the world at large), while the other moves toward the center (the individual). For there is a wisdom of counsel (giving advice to others), and then there is a wisdom of pressing a man's own fortune; and these two sometimes meet, but often separate. For many are wise in their own ways who are weak for...

# The Second Book

...government or counsel, like ants, which are wise creatures for themselves but very harmful to the garden. This self-interested wisdom was well recognized by the Romans; "For truly, the wise man fashions his own fortune for himself" <note>original: *Nam pol sapiens... Fingit fortunam sibi*</note> (says the comic poet <note>Plautus, in his play *Trinummus*</note>), and it grew into an adage: "Every man is the maker of his own fortune" <note>original: *Faber quisque fortunae propriae*</note>. Livy attributes this quality to Cato the Elder: "In this man there was such great strength of mind and character that, no matter what rank he had been born into, it seemed he would have made his own fortune" <note>original: *In hoc viro tanta vis animi & ingenij inerat, ut quocunque loco natus esset sibi ipse fortunam facturus videretur*</note>.

If this idea or position is declared and professed too openly, it has been thought unwise and unlucky. This was observed in Timotheus the Athenian, who, having performed many great services for the state during his government and giving an account of them to the people (as was the custom), concluded every specific achievement with this clause: "And in this, fortune had no part." It so happened that he never prospered in anything he undertook afterward. This attitude is too haughty and arrogant, resembling what Ezekiel says of Pharaoh: "You say, 'The river is mine, and I have made it for myself'" <note>original: *Dicis: fluvius est meus & ego feci memet ipsum*</note>; or what another prophet says: "That men offer sacrifices to their nets and snares" <note>Habakkuk 1:16; meaning they worship their own tools and skills rather than God</note>. It is also what the poet <note>Virgil, in the *Aeneid*, describing the tyrant Mezentius</note> expresses: "My right hand is my god, and the weapon I now poise. Be present now!" <note>original: *Dextra mihi Deus, & telum quod inutile libro. Nunc adsinte*</note>.

For such boasts were always unholy and unblessed. Therefore, those who were truly great statesmen <note>original: "Pollitiques"</note> always ascribed their successes to their good fortune <note>original: "felicitie"</note>, and not to their own skill or virtue.

<summary>Bacon explores the Roman concept that individuals create their own destiny, while warning that claiming total credit for one's success without acknowledging "fortune" or divine favor is both arrogant and self-defeating.</summary>

So Caesar said to the master of the ship, "You carry Caesar and his fortune" <note>original: *Caesarem portas & fortunam eius*</note>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But nevertheless, these positions—"Every man is the maker of his own fortune" <note>original: *Faber quisque fortunae suae*</note>, "The wise man will rule the stars" <note>original: *sapiens dominabitur astris*</note>, "No way is impassable to virtue" <note>original: *Invia virtuti nulla est via*</note>, and the like—when taken and used as spurs to industry, and not as stirrups for insolence, and for inner resolution rather than for presumption or outward boasting, have always been thought sound and good. They are, without question, imprinted in the greatest minds, who are so sensitive to this opinion that they can scarcely keep it to themselves. As we see in Augustus Caesar (who was different from his uncle <note>Julius Caesar</note> rather than inferior in virtue), who, when he died, asked his friends around him to give him a "round of applause" <note>original: *Plaudite*; the traditional request for applause at the end of a Roman play</note>, as if he were conscious that he had played his part well upon the stage. This branch of knowledge we also report as deficient—not because it is not practiced (it is practiced too much), but because it has not been systematically committed to writing. <meta>This page continues Bacon's discussion on the "architecture of fortune"—the practical art of advancing one's career and life. He argues that learning should not just be a lofty, theoretical pursuit but should also be applicable to the practical world.</meta>

<header>The second book.</header>

...and **political**, as to be truly **moral**. But the handling of this matter concerns learning greatly, both in honor and in substance. It concerns learning in honor, because practical <note>original: "pragmaticall"</note> men should not walk away with the opinion that learning is like a lark, which can fly high and sing and please herself and nothing else. Instead, they should know that she behaves as much like a hawk, which can soar aloft but can also descend and strike upon the prey.

In substance, it concerns learning because it is the perfect law for the inquiry of truth: *that nothing should exist in the physical world <note>original: "globe of matter"</note> which should not likewise exist in the world of the intellect <note>original: "globe of crystal, or Form"</note>.* That is, there should not be anything in existence and action which should not be drawn and collected into contemplation and teaching <note>original: "doctrine"</note>. Neither does learning admire or value this **architecture of fortune** <note>the art of building one's own success</note> except as an inferior work. For no man's fortune can be an end worthy of his being, and many times the worthiest men willingly abandon their fortune for better reasons. Nevertheless, fortune, as a tool <note>original: "organ"</note> of virtue and merit, deserves consideration.

First, therefore, the precept <note>rule of conduct</note> which I believe to be most essential for prevailing in fortune is to obtain that "window" which *Momus* <note>In Greek mythology, the god of mockery who criticized the Creator for not putting a window in the human chest to see a person's thoughts</note> required. He, seeing such angles and recesses in the frame of the human heart, found fault that there was not a window to look into them. That is: to obtain good information on the details concerning people—their natures, their desires and goals, and their customs...

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s discussion on the practical knowledge needed for the "architecture of fortune." He emphasizes that to succeed, one must understand the specific details of people’s characters and the current political or social climate.</meta>

...and fashions, their helps and advantages, and by what means they chiefly stand. Likewise, one must know their weaknesses and disadvantages, and where they lie most open and vulnerable <note>original: "obnoxious"</note>; their friends, factions, and dependencies; and again, their opposites, enviers, and competitors; their moods and timing—*you alone knew the easy approaches and the right times for the man* <note>original Latin: "Sola viri molles adytus, & tempora noras" (from Virgil's Aeneid, referring to the ability to influence someone at the right moment)</note>—their principles, rules, and observations, and the like. 

And this applies not only to persons but also to actions: what is underway <note>original: "on foot"</note> from time to time, and how those actions are conducted, favored, or opposed; how much they matter <note>original: "import"</note>, and so forth. For the knowledge of current actions is not only important in itself, but without it, the knowledge of persons is very prone to error. This is because men change along with the actions they are involved in; while they are in pursuit of a goal, they act one way, and when they return to their natural state, they act another. 

This information about specific details regarding persons and actions serves as the **minor** propositions in every active **syllogism** <note>a form of logical reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two premises. In this metaphor, the "major" is the general rule of human behavior, and the "minor" is the specific fact about a person or situation</note>. No excellence of general observations (which are the **major** propositions) can be enough to reach a solid conclusion if there is an error or mistake in the minor details.

That this knowledge is possible, **Solomon** is our guarantee, for he says: *Counsel in the heart of man is like deep water, but a man of understanding will draw it out* <note>original Latin: "Consilium in corde viri tanquam aqua profunda, sed vir prudens exhauriet illud" (Proverbs 20:5)</note>. And although the knowledge itself does not fall under a universal rule <note>original: "precept"</note> because it deals with **individuals**, the instructions for obtaining it certainly can.

We will begin, therefore, with this rule <note>original: "precept"</note>, according to the ancient opinion: that the **sinews** <note>the source of strength or power; here referring to the essential foundations of strategy</note> of...

He cites Solomon to assert that a wise person can uncover these hidden motivations like drawing water from a deep well.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s advice on the "architecture of fortune," focusing on the practical skills needed to read and understand other people. Having previously discussed the importance of knowing a person's nature and current circumstances, Bacon now moves to the specific signs—facial expressions, words, and deeds—that reveal a person’s true intentions.</meta>

# The second book.

...of wisdom, are slowness of belief and distrust: that more trust should be given to countenances <note>facial expressions</note> and deeds than to words; and regarding words, more trust should be given to sudden remarks and words spoken off guard than to set and intentional words. Neither should we fear the saying, "No trust is to be placed in the face" <note>original Latin: *fronti nulla fides*</note>, which refers to a general outward behavior and not to the private and subtle movements and efforts of the face and gesture, which, as Quintus Cicero elegantly says, is the "gate of the mind" <note>original Latin: *Animi Janua*</note>. No one was more secretive than Tiberius, and yet Tacitus says of Gallus, "For he had guessed his offense from his expression" <note>original Latin: *Etenim vultu offensionem coniectaverat*</note>. 

So again, noting the differing character and manner in which he commended Germanicus and Drusus in the Senate, Tacitus says concerning the fashion in which he delivered his speech about Germanicus: "With words decorated more for appearance than so that he might be believed to feel them deeply" <note>original Latin: *Magis in speciem adornatis verbis, quam ut penitus sentire crederetur*</note>; but of Drusus he says: "In fewer words, but more earnest and with a sincere speech" <note>original Latin: *Paucioribus sed intentior, & fida oratione*</note>. And in another place, speaking of his style of speech when he did anything that was gracious and popular, Tacitus says that in other things he spoke "as if his words were struggling to come out" <note>original Latin: *velut eluctantium verborum*</note>; but then again, "He spoke more freely when he was coming to someone's aid" <note>original Latin: *Solutius loquebatur quando subveniret*</note>. 

So it is that there is no such master of deception <note>original: "artificer of dissimulation"</note>, nor any such commanded countenance <note>original Latin: *vultus iussus*; a forced or faked expression</note>, that can separate from a fake story some of these signs: either a manner that is too slight and careless, or too stiff and formal, or too tedious and rambling, or coming from a man more drily and with more difficulty.

Neither are deeds such assured guarantees, as that...

He uses historical examples of the Emperor Tiberius to show how even the most secretive people reveal their true feelings through subtle shifts in their manner of speaking and expression.</summary>

--- Page 284 ---
<meta>This page continues Bacon’s discussion on how to "read" people as part of the "architecture of fortune." After discussing facial expressions on the previous page, Bacon moves on to interpreting deeds and words, warning that even seemingly positive actions or "small favors" can be deceptive tools used to lower a person's guard.</meta>

Of the Advancement of Learning.

deeds may be trusted without a judicious consideration of their magnitude and nature; "Fraud establishes trust for itself in small things so that it may deceive with greater profit" <note>original Latin: *Fraus sibi in parvis fidem praestruit, ut maiore emolumento fallat*</note>. Indeed, the Italian thinks himself on the point of being bought and sold when he is treated better than he is accustomed to being without a clear cause. For small favors only lull men to sleep, regarding both their **caution** and their **industry**, and are, as Demosthenes calls them, "nourishment for laziness" <note>original Latin: *Alimenta socordiae*</note>.

So again we see how false the nature of some deeds is in the specific instance which Mutianus practiced upon Antonius Primus, during that hollow and unfaithful reconciliation made between them. At that time, Mutianus promoted many of Antonius’s friends: "At the same time, he bestowed governorships and tribunships on his friends" <note>original Latin: *Simul amicis eius praefecturas & tribunatus largitur*</note>. In this, under the pretense of strengthening him, he actually isolated him and won away his supporters.

As for words (though they are like urine samples to physicians <note>original: "waters to Physicians." Bacon is comparing the interpretation of words to uroscopy, the diagnostic practice of examining urine, which he suggests is often deceptive or unreliable</note>, full of flattery and uncertainty), they are not to be despised, especially when combined with the advantage of passion and emotion. For so we see Tiberius, upon a stinging and inciting speech by Agrippina, stepped out of his dissimulation <note>original: *dissimulacion*; the act of concealing one's true feelings or character</note> when he said, "You are hurt because you do not reign." Regarding this, Tacitus says, "When these things were heard, they drew a rare word from his hidden heart; and having seized her, he warned her with a Greek verse that she was only offended because she did not rule" <note>original Latin: *Audita haec, raram occulti pectoris vocem elicuere: correptamque Graeco versu admonuit: ideo laedi quia non regnaret*</note>. Therefore, the poet elegantly calls passions "tortures" that urge men to confess their secrets.

->**"Tortured by wine and anger."** <note>original Latin: *Vino tortus & ira*</note><-

### The Second Book

And experience shows that there are few men so true to themselves and so settled, but that sometimes out of passion, sometimes out of a desire to show off, sometimes out of kindness, and sometimes out of trouble of mind and weakness, they reveal themselves. This is especially true if they are pressured with a "counter-dissimulation" <note>original: "counter-dissimulatiõ," meaning a deceptive tactic used to trick someone else into being honest</note>, according to the Spanish proverb: *Tell a lie and find a truth* <note>original Spanish: *Di mentira, y sacarás verdad*</note>.

As for knowing men at second hand from **reports**: men’s weaknesses and faults are best known from their enemies; their virtues and abilities from their friends; their customs and habits from their servants; and their private thoughts and opinions from their close friends with whom they talk most. General reputation is unreliable, and the opinions held by superiors or equals are often deceitful, for men wear more of a "mask" around such people. As the saying goes, "A more truthful reputation emanates from one's own household" <note>original Latin: *Verior fama è domesticis emanat*</note>.

But the most reliable way of disclosing and explaining men is by their **natures** and **ends** <note>original: "endes," meaning their ultimate goals or objectives</note>. In this, the weakest sort of men are best interpreted by their natures, and the wisest by their ends. For it was both humorously and wisely said (though I think very untruthfully) by a nuncio <note>a diplomatic representative of the Pope</note> of the Pope upon returning from a certain nation where he served as a <term>ledger</term> <note>original: "LIDGER," an archaic term for a resident ambassador or permanent representative</note>. When his opinion was asked regarding the appointment of a successor to go in his place, he wished that, whatever they did, they should not send anyone who was too wise. This was because no truly wise man would ever imagine what the people in that country [were...]

# Of the Advancement of Learning.

...were likely to do. And certainly, it is a frequent error for men to overreach and to assume deeper motives and more complex schemes than actually exist. The Italian proverb is elegant and, for the most part, true:

->*Of money, of wisdom, and of faith,*<-
->*There is less than you believe:*<-
<note>original Italian: *Di danari, di senno, e di fede, C’n’è manco che non credi*</note>

There is commonly less money, less wisdom, and less good faith than men account for.

But for a very different reason, Princes are best interpreted by their natures, and private persons by their goals <note>original: "ends"</note>. For Princes, being at the peak of human desires, mostly have no specific goals to which they aspire—there is no distant point by which a man might measure and weigh the rest of their actions and desires. This is one of the causes that makes their hearts more inscrutable. 

Furthermore, it is not enough to inform ourselves only of the variety of men's goals and natures; we must also know their predominance—which "humor" <note>original: "humour," referring to a person’s dominant temperament or mood</note> reigns most, and what goal is principally sought. For so we see, when **Tigellinus** <note>a powerful prefect and advisor to the Emperor Nero</note> saw himself outstripped by **Petronius Turpilianus** in catering to **Nero's** appetite for pleasure, he probed Nero's fears <note>original Latin: *Metus eius rimatur*</note>, and by that means he brought about the other's downfall.

But for all this part of inquiry, the most efficient way involves three things: First, to have general acquaintance and intimacy with those who themselves have general acquaintance and look most into the world—and especially according to the...

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s advice on the "Architecture of Fortune," or the practical art of personal advancement. He is concluding his first major point about how to gather information and observe others effectively before moving to the next principle.</meta>

...the diversity of business and the diversity of persons, to have privacy and conversation with at least one friend who is expert and well-informed in every specific field. The second is to maintain a good balance <note>original: "mediocrity"</note> between freedom of speech and secrecy—using openness in most things, but secrecy where it is important. For freedom of speech invites and provokes others to be open in return, which brings much to a man’s knowledge; on the other hand, secrecy fosters trust and intimacy. The last is to train oneself into a watchful and calm habit, making it a point and purpose in every conversation and action to observe as well as to act. 

For as <term>Epictetus</term> <note>a Greek Stoic philosopher (c. 50–135 AD) who taught that we should focus on what is within our control</note> would have a philosopher say to himself in every specific action, "I want to do this, and also to keep my principles" <note>original Latin: *Et hoc volo, & etiam institutum servare*</note>; so a man of affairs <note>original: "politique man"</note> should say to himself in everything, "I want to do this, and also to learn something" <note>original Latin: *Et hoc volo, ac etiam aliquid addiscere*</note>. 

I have dwelled longer on this principle of obtaining good information because it is a major part of the subject and applies to all the rest. But above all things, caution must be taken that men maintain good self-control <note>original: "stay and hold of themselves"</note>, and that knowing so much does not lead to meddling too much. For nothing is more unfortunate than careless and rash interference in many matters. Thus, this variety of knowledge ultimately serves only one purpose: to allow us to make a better and freer choice of those actions which concern us, and to carry them out with fewer errors and more skill <note>original: "dexterity"</note>.

# Of the Advancement of Learning.

...for men to take good information regarding their own person and to understand themselves well; knowing that as Saint James says, though men look often in a mirror <note>original: "glasse"</note>, yet they suddenly forget themselves. Whereas the divine mirror is the Word of God, the political mirror is the state of the world, or the times in which we live, in which we are to behold ourselves.

For men ought to take an impartial view of their own abilities and virtues, and again of their needs and impediments; accounting these <note>one's faults</note> at the highest value, and those others <note>one's virtues</note> at the lowest, and from this view and examination to frame the following considerations.

First, to consider how the constitution of their nature fits <note>original: "forteth"</note> with the general state of the times. If they find it agreeable and fit, then in all things they should give themselves more scope and liberty; but if it is different and dissonant, then in the whole course of their life they should be more private, retired, and reserved. As we see in Tiberius, who was never seen at a play and did not come into the Senate in twelve of his last years; whereas Augustus Caesar lived always in men's eyes, which Tacitus observes: *Tiberius had a different way of behavior.* <note>original Latin: "Alia Tiberio morum via"</note>

Secondly, to consider how their nature fits with professions and courses of life, and accordingly to make a choice if they are free to do so; and if they are already engaged, to make their departure at the first opportunity. As we see was done by Duke Valentine <note>Cesare Borgia (1475–1507), the son of Pope Alexander VI, known for his ambition and ruthlessness</note>, who was designated by his father for a priestly <note>original: "sacerdotal"</note> profession, but quitted it soon after because of his natural talents and inclination—which were such, nevertheless, that a man cannot well tell whether they were worse...

<meta>catchword: for</meta>

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon’s "Architecture of Fortune," offering practical advice for personal advancement by aligning one's choices with their unique nature and the competitive landscape.</meta>

## The Second Book

...whether it be for a prince or for a priest.

Thirdly, one should consider how they align <note>original: "sorte"</note> with those who are likely to be their competitors and rivals, and to choose the path where there is the most "solitude" <note>meaning a field with less competition</note>, and where they themselves are likely to be most eminent. This is as Julius Caesar did; at first, he was an orator or pleader, but when he saw the excellence of Cicero, Hortensius, Catulus, and others in eloquence, and saw there was no man of reputation in the wars except for Pompey—upon whom the State was forced to rely—he abandoned the course he had begun toward civil and popular greatness and transferred his designs to military <note>original: "marshall"</note> greatness.

Fourthly, in the choice of their friends and dependents, they should proceed according to the composition of their own nature. We may see this in Caesar, all of whose friends and followers were active and effective men, rather than those who were merely formal or held high reputations.

Fifthly, one must take special heed of how they guide themselves by examples, thinking they can do as they see others do, whereas perhaps their natures and behaviors <note>original: "carriages"</note> are far different. It seems Pompey fell into this error, of whom Cicero says that he was often accustomed to say, "Sulla could do it; why can’t I?" <note>original Latin: "Sylla potuit; Ego non potero?"</note> In this, he was much deceived <note>original: "abused"</note>, for the natures and proceedings of himself and his example were the most unlike in the world; Sulla being fierce, violent, and driving straight to the point, while Pompey was formal and full of majesty and ceremony <note>original: "circumstance"</note>, and therefore less effective.

<summary>Bacon advises that personal success requires choosing a career path with less competition, selecting compatible associates, and avoiding the mistake of imitating others whose temperaments differ from one's own.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Bacon’s discussion of the "Architecture of Fortune," moving from self-knowledge to the strategic art of self-presentation and how to effectively manage one's public reputation.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning.

But this precept regarding the practical <note>original: "politicke"</note> knowledge of ourselves has many other branches that we cannot dwell upon here.

Next to the thorough understanding and discerning of oneself, there follows the skillful opening and revealing of oneself. In this, we often see that the more capable a man is, the less of a show he makes. For there is a great advantage in the effective presentation of one’s virtues, fortunes, and merits—and likewise in the artful covering of one’s weaknesses, defects, and disgraces. This involves dwelling upon the former and glossing over <note>original: "flyding," meaning sliding or slipping away</note> the latter, highlighting the one by emphasizing circumstances, and excusing the other through explanation <note>original: "gracing the other by exposition"</note>, and so on. 

In this we see what *Tacitus* <note>Cornelius Tacitus (c. 56–120 AD), Roman historian</note> says of *Mucianus* <note>Gaius Licinius Mucianus, a general and statesman who helped Vespasian to the throne</note>, who was the greatest politician of his time: "By a certain art, he was a showman of everything he had said or done" <note>original: "Omnium quae dixerat feceratque, arte quadam ostentator"</note>. This indeed requires some art, lest it become tedious and arrogant; yet, even so, ostentation (though it is the highest degree of vanity) seems to me a vice in social manners rather than in political strategy <note>original: "Policye"</note>. For as it is said, "Slander boldly, for something always sticks" <note>original: "Audacter calumniari, semper aliquid haeret"</note>, so, unless it is done to a ridiculous degree of deformity, "Promote yourself boldly, for something always sticks" <note>original: "Audacter te vendita semper aliquid haeret"</note>. 

For it will make an impression on the more ignorant and inferior sort of men, even though men of wisdom and rank may smile at it and despise it; and yet, the authority won with the many outweighs the disdain of the few. But if this self-promotion is carried out with decency and self-control <note>original: "gouernement"</note>, in a natural, pleasant, and clever <note>original: "ingenious"</note> fashion—or at times when it is mixed with some peril and safety...

<summary>Bacon discusses the strategic importance of self-presentation, arguing that artfully highlighting one's strengths and concealing weaknesses is a vital tool for personal advancement, even if it borders on vanity.</summary>

It greatly adds to one's reputation; and surely quite a few steady <note>original: "solide"</note> natures, who lack this vanity <note>original: "ventositye," literally windiness or puffery</note> and cannot navigate <note>original: "faile," likely meaning sail</note> when the winds are high, suffer some prejudice and disadvantage because of their moderation.

But regarding these flourishes and enhancements of virtue, as they are perhaps not unnecessary, it is at least necessary that virtue not be devalued and debased below its fair price. This is done in three ways: By offering and thrusting <note>original: "obtruding"</note> oneself forward, wherein men think they have rewarded a man simply by accepting him. By doing too much, which will not give that which is well done time to settle, and in the end induces boredom <note>original: "saciety"</note>. And by finding the fruit of one’s virtue too soon in commendation, applause, honor, and favor—wherein, if a man is pleased with a little, let him hear what is truly said: *Take care that you do not seem unaccustomed to greater things, if this small matter delights you as much as if it were great* <note>original: "Caue ne insuetus rebus maioribus videaris, si hac te res parua sicuti magna delectat"</note>.

However, the covering of defects is of no less importance than the highlighting <note>original: "valewing"</note> of good qualities <note>original: "parts"</note>. This may likewise be done in three ways: by *Caution*, by *Color*, and by *Confidence*. *Caution* is when men do

<meta>This page continues Francis Bacon’s advice on the "Architecture of Fortune," specifically the art of "covering defects" or "discretion." He is discussing how a person can strategically manage their reputation by hiding their weaknesses and amplifying their perceived strengths.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning.

...ingeniously and discreetly avoid being put into those situations for which they are not suited; whereas, on the contrary, bold and restless spirits will thrust themselves into matters indiscriminately, and so publish and proclaim all their deficiencies. Color <note>In 17th-century rhetoric, "color" refers to a pretext or a way of framing an action to make it look better than it is.</note> is when men create a way for themselves to have a favorable interpretation made of their faults or deficiencies, as if they proceeded from a noble cause or were intended for some other purpose. For of the former, it is well said:

->*Vice often hides in the proximity of good*<-
<note>original Latin: "Sæpe latet vitium proximitate boni."</note>

And therefore, whatever deficiency a man has, he must see to it that he claims the virtue that shadows it. For example, if he is *dull*, he must affect *gravity*; if a *coward*, *mildness*; and so with the rest. For the second tactic, a man must frame some probable cause why he should not do his best, and why he should hide his abilities; and for that purpose, he must practice hiding those abilities that are well known in him, to give the impression <note>original: "colour"</note> that his true deficiencies are merely intentional acts and deceptions <note>original: "industries and dissimulations"</note>. As for *confidence*, it is the last but the surest remedy: namely, to belittle and seem to despise whatever a man cannot attain, following the clever principle of the *merchants*, who endeavor to raise the price of their own commodities and to beat down the price of others. But there is a confidence that goes beyond this: which is to brazenly deny <note>original: "face out"</note> one's own defects by appearing to believe that he is best in those things where he is actually failing; and to reinforce that, to appear on the other side that he has

<summary>Bacon explains three strategies for hiding personal defects: framing a fault as a related virtue, pretending a lack of ability is actually a deliberate choice, and using confidence to belittle what one cannot achieve.</summary>

# The Second Book

...the least opinion of himself in those things wherein he is best. Just as we commonly see in poets: if they show their verses and you object to any, they will say, *"That line cost them more labor than any of the rest,"* and immediately they will seem to disparage and suspect rather *some other line*, which they know well enough to be the best in the poem. But above all, in this managing and assisting of a man’s self in his own conduct <note>original: "carriage"</note>, he must take care that he does not show himself defenseless <note>original: "dismantled"</note> and exposed to scorn and injury by too much sweetness, goodness, and being too easy-going <note>original: "facility of nature"</note>. Instead, he should show some sparks of independence <note>original: "liberty"</note>, spirit, and assertiveness <note>original: "edge"</note>. This kind of guarded conduct, featuring a ready defense <note>original: "rescuing"</note> of oneself from insults, is sometimes forced upon men by some circumstance in their person or fortune, but it always results in good success <note>original: "felicity"</note>.

Another rule of this knowledge is to endeavor, by all possible means, to frame the mind to be pliant and obedient to the occasion. For nothing hinders men’s fortunes as much as this: *He remained the same, when the same no longer became him* <note>original Latin: "Idem manebat, neque idem decebat." Cicero said this of the orator Hortensius, who failed to adapt his style as he aged.</note>. Men stay where they were even when circumstances change. Therefore, regarding Cato—whom the historian Livy describes as a true "architect of fortune"—Livy adds that he possessed a *versatile mind* <note>original Latin: "Versatile Ingenium"</note>. From this it follows that those grave, solemn minds—which must always be like themselves and cannot adapt to change <note>original: "make departures"</note>—have more dignity than success <note>original: "felicity"</note>. But in some people, it is their nature to be somewhat rigid <note>original: "viscous"</note> and entangled <note>original: "inwrapped" </note>...

<summary>Bacon advises on the importance of self-conduct, suggesting that one should defend against being seen as too "easy" and emphasize the need for a versatile, adaptable mind to achieve success.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Architecture of Fortune," specifically focusing on the psychological flexibility required for success.</meta>

And it is not easy to turn. In some people, it is a fixed idea—almost a second nature—where men can hardly make themselves believe they ought to change their course when they have found success with it in past experience. For Machiavelli <note>original: "Macciaue"</note> wisely notes how Fabius Maximus would have continued his policy of delay, according to his old habit, even when the nature of the war had changed and required hot pursuit. 

In others, it is a lack of sharpness and penetration in their judgment; they do not discern when circumstances have reached a turning point <note>original: "periode"</note>, but instead react too late after the opportunity has passed. As Demosthenes compares the people of Athens to country fellows when they practice in a fencing school: if they receive a blow, only then do they move their weapon to that guard, and not before. 

In others still, it is a reluctance to lose the value of past labors, and a conceit that they can force circumstances to bend to their will. Yet in the end, when they see no other remedy, they finally submit to the change at a disadvantage. This is like King Tarquin <note>original: "Tarquinius"</note>, who paid triple the price for the final third of the Sibyl's books <note>The Sibylline Books were a collection of prophecies; Tarquin refused to buy them until the Sibyl burned two-thirds of the set, eventually paying the full price for the remainder.</note>, when he might at first have had all three for the original price. But from whatever root or cause this stubbornness <note>original: "Restiuenesse"</note> of mind proceeds, it is a most prejudicial thing. Nothing is more politically wise than to make the wheels of our mind concentric and fluid with the wheels of fortune.

Another precept of this knowledge, which has some affinity with what we last spoke of, but with a difference, is that which is well expressed: *Approach the gods and the heavens.* <note>original Latin: "fatis accede deisque"</note>

<summary>Bacon argues that success requires mental flexibility and the ability to adapt one's behavior to changing circumstances, warning against the stubbornness that leads to missed opportunities.</summary>

# The Second Book

<margin>Gods and men</margin> <note>original: "Deisque"</note>, that men do not only turn with the occasions but also run with the occasions. They should not strain their credit or strength to over-hard or extreme points, but choose in their actions that which is most passable. This will preserve men from failure <note>original: "foyle"</note>, prevent them from being occupied too much with a single matter, win a reputation for moderation, please the most people, and create an appearance of perpetual success <note>original: "fœlicitye"</note> in all they undertake, which cannot but greatly increase their reputation.

Another part of this knowledge seems to have some conflict with the former two, but not as I understand it; it is that which *Demosthenes* expresses in high terms: *And just as it is the accepted rule that the General should lead the army, so also should wise men lead the affairs themselves, so that the things they see fit to be done are carried out, and they are not forced to merely follow after events.* <note>original: "Et quemadmodum receptum est, ut exercitum ducat Imperator: sic & a cordatis viris res ipsa ducendæ, ut quæ ipsis videntur, ea gerantur, & non ipsi euentus persequi cogantur."</note> For if we observe, we shall find two differing kinds of ability <note>original: "sufficiency"</note> in the managing of business: some can make use of occasions aptly and dexterously but plot little; others can urge and pursue their own plots well but cannot adapt to or incorporate new circumstances. Either of these is very imperfect without the other.

Another part of this knowledge is observing a good middle ground <note>original: "mediocrity"</note> in declaring or not declaring one's intentions. For although depth of secrecy and making one's way—*like the path of a ship in the sea*, which the French call *underhanded dealings* <note>original: "Sourdes Menées"</note>, when men set things in motion without revealing themselves at all—be sometimes

<summary>Bacon discusses the importance of flexibility, proactive leadership, and the strategic balance between transparency and secrecy in achieving success.</summary>

times both prosperous and admirable: yet many times *dissimulation breeds errors which ensnare the dissembler himself.* <note>original: "Dissimulatio errores parit, qui dissimulatorem ipsum illaqueant."</note> And therefore we see the greatest politicians <note>original: "pollitiques"</note> have in a natural and free manner professed their desires, rather than being reserved and disguised in them. For so we see that *Lucius Sylla* made a kind of profession, *that he wished all men happy or unhappy as they stood his friends or enemies.* So *Julius Caesar*, when he went first into *Gaul*, made no scruple to profess, *that he had rather be first in a village, than second at Rome.* So again, as soon as he had begun the war, we see what *Cicero* says of him: *The other* (meaning *Caesar*) *does not refuse, but in a way demands, that he be called what he is: a Tyrant.* <note>original: "Alter non recusat, sed quodam modo postulat, ut (ut est) sic appelletur Tyrannus."</note> 

So we may see in a letter of *Cicero* to *Atticus*, that *Augustus Caesar* in his very entrance into affairs, when he was a darling of the Senate, yet in his speeches to the people, would swear, *May it be permitted me to attain the honors of my father* <note>original: "Ita parentis honores consequi liceat"</note> (which was no less than the Tyranny), except that to help it, he would stretch forth his hand towards a statue of *Caesar's* that was erected in the place. And men laughed and wondered and said, "Is it possible?" or "Did you ever hear the like?" And yet, though he meant no harm, he did it so handsomely and ingenuously, and all these men were prosperous. 

Whereas *Pompey*, who tended toward the same ends but in a more dark and dissembling manner—as *Tacitus* says of him, *He was more secretive, but no better* <note>original: "Occultior non melior"</note>—wherein *Sallust* concurs, describing him as having *an honest face, but a shameless mind.* <note>original: "ore probo, animo inuerecundo"</note>

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that successful historical leaders like Caesar and Augustus were often openly ambitious, whereas those who relied on excessive secrecy and dissimulation, like Pompey, were viewed with more suspicion.</summary>

# The Second Book

<sig>Ddd 1</sig>

Pompey <note>original: "do" (likely a typo for "he" or "Pompey" based on context)</note> made it his design by infinite secret engines to cast the state into an absolute anarchy and confusion, so that the state might cast itself into his arms for necessity and protection. In this way, the sovereign power would be put upon him without him ever appearing to seek it. And when he had brought it (as he thought) to that point where he was chosen *Consul* alone—an honor never before given to any man—yet he could make no great matter of it, because men did not understand his intentions. He was forced in the end to go the beaten track of getting arms into his hands, under the pretext of fearing *Caesar’s* designs. So tedious, casual, and unfortunate are these deep dissimulations <note>term: dissimulation; the act of concealing one's true intentions or character</note>, of which it seems *Tacitus* made this judgment: that they were a cunning of an inferior form compared to true policy. He attributed the latter to *Augustus* and the former to *Tiberius*, where speaking of *Livia*, he says: *She was well-matched, combining the arts of her husband with the dissimulation of her son* <note>original: "Et cum artibus mariti simulatione filij bene composita"</note>. For surely the continual habit of dissimulation is but a weak and sluggish cunning, and not greatly political.

Another precept of this Architecture of Fortune is to accustom our minds to judge of the proportion or value of things as they lead to, and are material to, our particular ends; and to do that substantially and not superficially. For we shall find the logical part (as I may term it) of some men’s minds is good, but the mathematical part is erroneous. That is, they can well judge of consequences, but not of proportions and weights.

<summary>Bacon argues that excessive secrecy and dissimulation often fail because they confuse the public, and he advises that a successful person must accurately weigh the value of their actions against their ultimate goals.</summary>
<keywords>Pompey, Tacitus, Augustus, Tiberius, Livia, dissimulation, Architecture of Fortune, political strategy, logic vs mathematics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the practical advancement of learning and the strategic management of one's personal fortune and career.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

comparison, preferring things of show and sense before things of substance and effect. So some fall in love with access to Princes, others with popular fame and applause, supposing they are things of great value <note>original: "purchase"</note>, when in many cases they are but matters of envy, peril, and impediment. So some measure things according to the labor and difficulty, or the persistence, which are spent about them; they think if they are ever moving, they must necessarily advance and proceed. This is similar to what *Caesar* says in a despising manner of *Cato* the younger, when he describes how laborious and tireless he was to no great purpose: *He did all these things with great effort* <note>original: "Haec omnia magno studio agebat"</note>. So in most things, men are ready to deceive themselves by thinking the greatest means to be the best, when it should be the fittest.

As for the true ordering <note>original: "marshalling"</note> of men's pursuits toward their fortune according to how essential they are, I hold them to stand thus: First, the improvement <note>original: "amendment"</note> of their own minds. *For the removal of the impediments of the mind will sooner clear the passages of fortune, than the obtaining of fortune will remove the impediments of the mind.* In second place, I set down wealth and means, which I know most men would have placed first because of the general use it has toward all variety of occasions. But that opinion I may condemn with the same reason as *Machiavelli* does that other: that money was the "sinews of war," whereas (says he)

<summary>Bacon argues that people often mistake superficial success for true progress and outlines a hierarchy for personal advancement, prioritizing the improvement of the mind over the accumulation of wealth.</summary>
<keywords>Julius Caesar, Cato the Younger, Machiavelli, personal fortune, self-improvement, psychology of success, wealth vs. wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 299 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the practical advancement of fortune and the strategic management of one's life and resources.</meta>

The true sinews of war are the sinews of men's **arms**—that is, a valiant, populous, and **military** nation. The author aptly cites the authority of **Solon**, who, when **Croesus** showed him his treasury of gold, said to him that if another man came who had better **iron**, he would be master of his **gold**. In like manner, it may be truly affirmed that it is not money that is the "sinews of fortune," but rather the sinews and steel of men's minds: **wit, courage, audacity, resolution, temper, industry**, and the like. 

In the third place, I set down **reputation**, because of the decisive tides and currents it possesses; if these are not taken at their due time, they are seldom recovered, it being extremely hard to play a "recovery game" <note>original: "after game"</note> once reputation is lost. And lastly, I place **honor**, which is more easily won by any of the other three—and much more so by all of them together—than any of those three can be purchased by honor alone. 

To conclude this precept: just as there is order and priority in **matter**, so there is in **time**. The backward <note>original: "preposterous"</note> placing of these is one of the most common **errors**, occurring when men fly toward their ultimate goals when they should be attending to their beginnings. They do not take things in the order of time as they arise, but instead arrange them according to their perceived greatness rather than their immediate urgency, failing to observe the good precept: *Let us do what is now at hand* <note>original: "Quod nunc instat agamus"</note>.

Another precept of this knowledge is not to embrace any matters which occupy too great a quantity of time, but to have that phrase sounding in a man's ears...

<summary>The author argues that mental fortitude and reputation are more vital to success than wealth, emphasizing the importance of prioritizing immediate tasks over distant goals.</summary>
<keywords>Solon, Croesus, sinews of war, reputation, honor, time management, strategic priority, Quod nunc instat agamus</keywords>

--- Page 300 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the practical knowledge of advancement in life and the strategies for personal fortune.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

*But meanwhile, time flies—it flies, never to be recovered* <note>original: "Sed fugit interea, fugit irreparabile tempus" (Virgil, Georgics)</note>, and that is the reason why those who take their course of rising by demanding professions—such as lawyers, orators, hardworking <note>original: "painefull"</note> divines, and the like—are not commonly so politically savvy <note>original: "politique"</note> for their own fortune, except in their ordinary professional way. This is because they lack the time to learn specific details, to wait for opportunities, and to devise strategic plans.

Another precept of this knowledge is to imitate nature, which does nothing in vain. Surely a man may do this if he well-interlaces his business and does not bend his mind too much upon that which he principally intends. For a man ought, in every particular action, to so direct the motions of his mind and to have one thing prioritized under another, that if he cannot have what he seeks in the best degree, he may yet have it in a second, or even a third. 

If he can have no part of what he purposed, he should yet turn the use of it to something else. If he cannot make anything of it for the present, he should yet make it as a seed for something in the time to come. And if he can derive no effect or substance from it, he should at least win some good reputation <note>original: "good opinion"</note> by it, or the like. In this way, he should demand an account of himself for every action, to reap something from it, and not to stand amazed and confused if he fails in what he chiefly meant to achieve. For nothing is more politically unwise <note>original: "impollitique"</note> than to focus on actions entirely one by one. For he who does so loses infinite opportunities which intervene, and which are many times more proper and favorable for something that he shall need afterwards than for that which he currently pursues.

<summary>Bacon argues that busy professionals often fail to advance their own fortunes due to a lack of time for strategy, and he advises that every action should be leveraged for multiple potential benefits.</summary>
<keywords>Advancement of Learning, Francis Bacon, career strategy, time management, professional development, versatility, opportunism</keywords>

# The Second Book

he urges for the present; and therefore men must be perfect in that rule: *These things you ought to have done, without leaving the others undone.* <note>original Latin: "Hæc oportet facere, & illa non omittere." A reference to Matthew 23:23.</note>

Another precept of this knowledge is not to engage oneself peremptorily <note>meaning: decisively or without room for change</note> in anything, even if it seems unlikely to be affected by chance. One should always have a window to fly out of, or a way to retreat, following the wisdom in the ancient fable of the two frogs. They consulted each other when their pond was dry about where they should go; one proposed going down into a deep pit because it was unlikely the water would dry up there, but the other answered, *"True, but if it does, how shall we get out again?"*

Another precept of this knowledge is that ancient rule of Bias <note>Bias of Priene, one of the Seven Sages of Greece</note>, interpreted not as an encouragement to treachery, but only as a call for caution and moderation: *Love as if you might one day hate, and hate as if you might one day love.* <note>original Latin: "Et ama tanquam inimicus futurus, & odi tanquam amaturus."</note> For it utterly destroys all usefulness when men embark too deeply into unfortunate friendships, troublesome grudges, and childish, moody envies or rivalries.

But I continue this discussion beyond the length of a mere example. I am led to do so because I would not have such areas of knowledge—which I note as *deficient* <note>meaning: lacking or incomplete in current scholarship</note>—to be thought of as imaginary things, or "castles in the air," or just a few minor observations. Rather, these are matters of substance and weight, where it is harder to reach an end than to find a beginning. It must likewise be understood that the points I mention and set down here are far from being complete treatises on these subjects; they are only small pieces intended as patterns. And lastly, no man, I suppose, will think...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Architecture of Fortune," or the practical knowledge required for an individual to advance their own estate and position in life.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning.

I do not want anyone to think that I mean fortunes are never obtained without all this effort. For I know they come tumbling into some men's laps, and many obtain good fortunes through simple diligence in a straightforward way: by meddling very little and keeping themselves from gross errors.

*But* just as *Cicero* <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, Roman statesman and orator</note>, when he sets down an *ideal* of a perfect Orator, does not mean that every lawyer should be exactly like that; and likewise, when a Prince or a Courtier has been described by those who have written on those subjects, the model is usually made according to the perfection of the <term>Art</term> <note>original: "Arte"; here meaning the highest theoretical standard of a profession</note>, and not according to common practice. So I understand that it ought to be done in the description of a *Politician*—I mean a man who is "politic" for the sake of his own fortune.

*But* it must be remembered all this while that the precepts which we have set down are of the kind which may be counted and called *Good Arts* <note>original: "Bonae Artes"</note>. As for evil arts, if a man would take for himself that principle of *Machiavelli*: *That a man should not seek to attain virtue itself, but only the appearance of it, because the reputation of virtue is a help, but the actual practice of it is a burden*; or that other principle of his: *That he should presuppose that men cannot be effectively managed except through fear, and therefore he should seek to have every man beholden to him, humbled, and in a tight spot*—which the Italians call "sowing thorns" <note>original: "seminar spine"</note>; or that other principle contained in the verse which *Cicero* cites: *Let friends fall, provided that enemies perish along with them* <note>original: "cadant amici, dummodo Inimici intercidant"</note>, just as the *Triumvirs* <note>The Second Triumvirate of Rome (Octavian, Antony, and Lepidus), who famously traded the lives of their own allies to secure the execution of their rivals</note> each sold the lives of their friends to one another in exchange for the deaths of their enemies; or...

<summary>Bacon distinguishes between the "Good Arts" of self-advancement and the "Evil Arts" associated with Machiavelli, arguing that while fortune can be found through simple diligence, a complete theory of personal politics must describe the ideal "politic man."</summary>
<keywords>Francis Bacon, Advancement of Learning, Machiavelli, Cicero, personal fortune, ethics, political theory, Triumvirate</keywords>

That other protestation of Lucius Catiline <note>original: *L. Catilina*; a Roman politician known for the Catilinarian conspiracy to overthrow the government</note> to set on fire and trouble states, to the end to fish in murky <note>original: "droumy"</note> waters and to disentangle <note>original: "unwrappe"</note> their own fortunes: *If any fire is kindled against my fortunes, I will extinguish it not with water, but with total destruction* <note>original: *Ego si quid in fortunis meis excitatum sit incendium, id non aqua sed ruina restinguam*</note>. Or that other principle of Lysander <note>A Spartan admiral and politician</note>: *That children are to be deceived with candies <note>original: "confittes"</note>, and men with oaths,* and similar evil and corrupt positions. Of these (as in all things) there are more in number than there are of the good. Certainly, with these exemptions from the laws of charity and integrity, the pushing forward of a man’s fortune may be more hasty and direct <note>original: "compendious"</note>. But it is in life as it is in travels: the shortest way is commonly the foulest, and surely the fairer way is not much longer.

But if men are in their own power and do support and sustain themselves—and are not carried away with a whirlwind or tempest of ambition—they ought, in the pursuit of their own fortune, to set before their eyes not only that general map of the world, *That all things are vanity and vexation of spirit* <note>A reference to Ecclesiastes 1:14</note>, but many other more particular charts and directions. Chiefly this: that existence <note>original: "Being"</note> without well-being is a curse; and the greater the existence, the greater the curse. And also that all virtue is most rewarded, and all wickedness most punished, in itself; according as the Poet <note>Virgil, in the *Aeneid*</note> says excellently:

> *What rewards, O men, can I think worthy to be paid to you for such praises? The gods and your own characters will first give you the most beautiful ones.* <note>original: *Quæ vobis que digna viri, pro laudibus istis / Premia posse rear solui? pulcherrima primum / Dij moresque dabunt vestri*</note>

And so it is of the contrary. And secondly, they ought to look up to the eternal providence and divine judgment, which often subverts the wisdom of evil

# Of the Advancement of Learning

plots and imaginations, according to that scripture: *He has conceived mischief and shall bring forth a vain thing* <note>Psalm 7:14</note>. And although men should refrain themselves from injury and evil arts, yet this incessant and "Sabbath-less" <note>original: "Sabbathlesse"; meaning a pursuit that never rests or observes a holy day</note> pursuit of a man's fortune does not leave the tribute which we owe to God of our time. We see that He demands a tenth of our substance, and a seventh—which is more strict—of our time. It is to small purpose to have a face directed toward heaven and a perpetual groveling spirit upon earth, eating dust like the serpent, *and fastening a spark of the divine spirit to the ground* <note>original: "Atque affigit humo Divinæ particulam auræ"; Horace, Satires 2.2.79</note>. 

If any man flatters himself that he will employ his fortune well, even though he should obtain it by ill means—as was said concerning Augustus Caesar, and later of Septimius Severus: *That either they should never have been born, or else they should never have died*—because they did so much mischief in the pursuit and ascent of their greatness, and so much good once they were established; yet these compensations and satisfactions are good to be used after the fact, but never good to be intended as a purpose. 

Lastly, it is not amiss for men, in the race toward their fortune, to cool themselves a little with that concept which was elegantly expressed by the Emperor Charles V in his instructions to the King his son: *That fortune has something of the nature of a woman; if she is too much wooed, she is the further off.* But this last is but a remedy for those whose tastes are corrupted. Let men rather build upon that foundation which is like a cornerstone of divinity and philosophy, where they join closely together: namely, that same "Seek first." For divinity says, *Seek first the kingdom of God, and all these things shall be added unto you* <note>original: "Primum quærite regnū Dei & ista omnia adiicientur Vobis"; Matthew 6:33</note>; and philosophy says, *Seek first the goods of the mind; the rest will either be supplied, or their absence will not be felt* <note>original: "Primū quærite bona animi, cætera aut aderunt, aut non oberunt."</note>.

<summary>Bacon argues against the relentless pursuit of worldly success at the expense of spiritual and mental well-being, asserting that one should prioritize divine and intellectual virtues over material fortune.</summary>

And although the human foundation of virtue has something of the same quality, as we see in Marcus Brutus when he broke forth into that speech:
> — *I worshipped you, Virtue, as a reality; but you are only an empty name;* <note>original: "Te colui (Virtus) ut rem: ast tu nomen inane es;"</note>

Yet the divine foundation is built upon the Rock. But this may serve for a taste of that knowledge which I noted as deficient.

Concerning government, it is a part of knowledge that is secret and retired in both respects in which things are deemed secret: for some things are secret because they are hard to know, and some because they are not fit to utter. We see all governments are obscure and invisible.
> — *A mind, infused through the limbs, moves the whole mass and mingles with the great body.* <note>original: "Totamque infusa per artus, Mens agitat molem, & magno corpore miscet." From Virgil's Aeneid, describing the world-soul.</note>

Such is the description of governments. We see the government of God over the world is hidden, insomuch as it seems to participate in much irregularity and confusion. The government of the soul in moving the body is inward and profound, and the passages of it can hardly be reduced to a clear demonstration. Again, the wisdom of Antiquity (the shadows of which are found in the Poets) in the description of torments and pains, next to the crime of Rebellion—which was the Giants' offense—does detest the offense of "facility" <note>term: "facility" here refers to the leaking of secrets or being too easy/open with private information</note>, as seen in the myths of Sisyphus and Tantalus. But this was meant for specific individuals. Nevertheless, even regarding the general rules and discourses of policy and government, a reverent and reserved handling is required.

<summary>Bacon contrasts the fragile human foundation of virtue with the divine and introduces the study of government as a naturally "secret" and complex subject that requires careful, respectful treatment.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Advancement of Learning," specifically transitioning from the private conduct of individuals to the public governance of states.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

But on the contrary, in the conduct of governors toward the governed, all things ought to be manifest and revealed as far as the frailty of man permits. For it is expressed in the Scriptures regarding the government of God, that this globe, which seems to us a dark and shaded body, is in the view of God like crystal: *And before the throne there was a sea of glass, like crystal.* <note>original Latin: "Et in conspectu sedis tanquam mare vitreum simile christallo" (Revelation 4:6)</note>

So, for princes and states—and especially for wise senates and councils—the natures and dispositions of the people, their conditions and necessities, their factions and combinations, and their animosities and discontents ought to be largely clear and transparent. This clarity is achieved through the variety of their intelligence-gathering, the wisdom of their observations, and the height of their station where they keep watch like sentinels. 

Therefore, considering that I write to a King <note>King James I of England</note> who is a master of this science and is so well assisted, I think it appropriate to pass over this part in silence. I am willing to obtain the same testimonial that one of the ancient philosophers aspired to; when others competed to demonstrate their abilities through speech, he remained silent and desired it to be recorded on his behalf *that there was one man who knew how to hold his peace.*

Notwithstanding, regarding the more public part of government, which concerns laws, I think it good to note only one deficiency: that all those who have written about laws have written either as philosophers or as lawyers, and none as statesmen. As for the philosophers, they make imaginary laws for imaginary commonwealths, and their discourses are like...

# *The Second Book.* 107

the stars, which give little light because they are so high. As for the lawyers, they write according to the states where they live, describing what is received law and not what ought to be law. For the wisdom of a lawmaker is one thing, and that of a lawyer is another. For there are in nature certain fountains of justice, from which all civil laws are derived but as streams; and just as waters take on colors and tastes from the soils through which they run, so do civil laws vary according to the regions and governments where they are planted, even though they proceed from the same fountains. 

Again, the wisdom of a lawmaker consists not only in a platform of justice, but in the application of it. This involves taking into consideration by what means laws may be made certain, and what are the causes and remedies of the doubtfulness and uncertainty of law; by what means laws may be made apt and easy to be executed, and what are the impediments and remedies in the execution of laws; what influence laws touching private rights of *mine and yours* <note>original: *Meum & Tuum*</note> have upon the public state, and how they may be made suitable and agreeable; how laws are to be drafted and delivered, whether in texts or in *acts*, brief or large, with preambles or without; how they are to be pruned and reformed from time to time, and what is the best means to keep them from being too vast in volume, or too full of multiplicity and contradiction; how they are to be interpreted: whether upon specific cases arising and being judicially discussed, or upon official legal opinions <note>original: *responses*</note> and conferences regarding general points or questions; how they are

Upon which I insist the less, because I purpose (if God give me leave) having begun a work of this nature in aphorisms <note>Aphorisms: concise, pithy statements of principle.</note>, to propose it hereafter, noting it in the meantime as deficient <note>Bacon marks subjects as "deficient" when he believes they have not been adequately studied or documented by previous scholars.</note>.

And for your Majesty’s laws of *England*, I could say much of their dignity, and something of their defects. But they cannot help but excel the Civil Laws <note>Civil Law: refers here to the Roman Law tradition used in much of continental Europe.</note> in fitness for this government; for the Civil Law was *a gift not sought for these uses* <note>original: "non hos quaesitum munus in usus" (Virgil, Aeneid). Bacon means Roman law was not originally designed for the specific needs of England.</note>. It was not made for the countries which it now governs. Of this I cease to speak, because I will not intermingle matters of legal *action* with matters of general *learning*.

Thus have I concluded this portion of learning touching *Civil knowledge*; and with *Civil knowledge* I have concluded HUMAN PHILOSOPHY; and with Human Philosophy, PHILOSOPHY in GENERAL. And being now at some pause, looking back into what I have passed through, this writing seems to me (*if the image does not deceive me* <note>original: "Si nunquam fallit imago" (Virgil, Eclogues).</note>), as far as a man can judge of his own work...
