So have I been content to tune the instruments of the Muses, so that those who have better hands may play them. 

And surely, when I consider the condition of these times, in which learning has made her third visitation or circuit in all its qualities: such as the excellence and liveliness of the wits of this age; the noble helps and lights which we have through the labors of ancient writers; the art of printing, which makes books available to men of all fortunes; the openness of the world through navigation, which has disclosed multitudes of experiments and a mass of natural history; the leisure with which these times abound—not employing men so generally in civil business as the states of Greece did (because of their popular governments) or the state of Rome (because of the greatness of their monarchy); the present disposition of these times at this instant toward peace; the exhaustion of all that can ever be said in controversies of religion, which have so much diverted men from other sciences; the perfection of your Majesty’s learning, which like a Phoenix <note>A mythical bird that is unique and reborn from its own ashes; here used to describe the King as a peerless leader of intellectuals.</note> may call whole flights of wits to follow you; and the inseparable property of time, which is ever more and more to disclose truth—I cannot but be raised to this persuasion: that this third period of time will far surpass those of the Grecian and Roman

<summary>Bacon compares his preliminary work to a musician tuning instruments and argues that the current age is uniquely positioned for a massive advancement in knowledge due to printing, navigation, and peace.</summary>

Only if men will know both their own strength and their own weakness, and take from one another the light of invention rather than the fire of contradiction; and if they value the investigation <note>original: "Inquisition"</note> of truth as a serious enterprise and not merely as a personal quality or ornament; and if they employ their wit and resources <note>original: "magnificence"</note> toward things of worth and excellence, rather than toward things that are common and popular. 

As for my own labors, if any man shall please himself or others by criticizing them, they shall make that ancient and patient request: *Strike me, but hear me* <note>original: "verbera, sed audi"; a phrase attributed to Themistocles</note>. Let men criticize them, provided they observe and weigh them. For it is a lawful appeal (though perhaps it shall not be necessary) to move from men’s first thoughts to their second, and from the present times to times further in the future. 

Now let us come to that learning which previous eras were not so blessed as to know: *Sacred and inspired Divinity*, the *Sabbath* <note>original: "Sabaoth," here used to mean a rest or destination</note> and goal of all men’s labors and journeys.

The prerogative of God extends as well to the reason as to the will of man. Therefore, just as we are to obey His law even though we find a resistance <note>original: "reluctation"</note> in our will, so we are to believe His word even though we find a resistance in our reason. For if we believe only that which is agreeable to our own senses, we are giving our consent to the subject matter and not to the Author—which is no more than we would do for a suspected and discredited witness. # The Second Book

This point was of such a nature that *Sarah* laughed at it; in this, she was an image of Natural Reason <note>Sarah laughed when told she would conceive a child in old age (Genesis 18:12), representing the skepticism of human logic toward divine miracles</note>.

However (if we will truly consider it), it is more worthy to believe than to know as we currently know. For in knowledge, the human mind receives impressions from the senses; but in belief, it receives impressions from the Spirit—an agent which the mind holds to be more authoritative than itself. Thus, the mind is acted upon by a worthier Agent. It is different in the state of man glorified, for then faith shall cease, and we shall know even as we are known <note>original: "faith shal cease, & we shall knowe as we are knowne" (referencing 1 Corinthians 13:12)</note>.

Therefore, we conclude that sacred Theology (which in our common language we call Divinity) is grounded only upon the word and oracle of God, and not upon the light of nature. For it is written, *The heavens declare the glory of God* <note>original: "Cæli enarrant gloriam Dei" (Psalm 19:1)</note>; but it is not written, *The heavens declare the will of God*. Regarding His will, it is said instead, *To the law and to the testimony: if they do not speak according to this word, it is because there is no light in them* <note>original: "Ad legem & testimonium si non fecerint secundū verbum istud" (Isaiah 8:20)</note>. 

This principle holds true not only in those points of faith concerning the great mysteries of the Deity, the Creation, and the Redemption, but also in those concerning the Moral Law truly interpreted: *Love your enemies, do good to them that hate you. Be like your heavenly father, who allows his rain to fall upon the just and the unjust.* To this, it ought to be applauded: *This does not sound like a human voice* <note>original: "Nec vox hominē sonat" (Virgil, Aeneid 1.328)</note>; it is a voice beyond the light of Nature. Thus, we see the heathen poets, when they indulge in a libertine passion, continually complain against laws and moralities as if they were opposite and malignant to Nature: *And what nature allows, [envious laws forbid]* <note>original: "Et quod natura remittit, invida jura negant" (Ovid, Metamorphoses 10.329)</note>.

<summary>The author argues that Theology is based solely on divine revelation rather than natural reason, asserting that faith is a higher intellectual state than sensory knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Theology, Divinity, Faith vs. Reason, Natural Law, Moral Law, Revelation, Sarah, Divine Will</keywords>

--- Page 312 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the limitations of human reason and the necessity of divine revelation in understanding the moral law.</meta>

*Envious laws forbid it* <note>original: "invida Iura negant"</note>: So said *Dendamis* the *Indian* <note>A gymnosophist or philosopher whom Alexander the Great encountered in India.</note> unto *Alexander's* messengers: That he had heard somewhat of *Pythagoras*, and some other of the wise men of *Greece*, and that he held them to be excellent men; but that they had one fault, which was that they held in too great reverence and veneration a thing they called Law and Custom. 

So it must be confessed that a great part of the Moral Law is of such perfection that the light of Nature cannot aspire to it. How then is it that man is said to have, by the light and law of Nature, some notions and concepts of virtue and vice, justice and wrong, good and evil? This is because the "light of Nature" is used in two distinct senses: 

The one is that which springs from reason, sense, induction, and argument, according to the laws of heaven and earth. The other is that which is imprinted upon the spirit of man by an inward instinct, according to the law of conscience, which is a spark of the purity of his original state <note>Referring to the state of humanity before the Fall in Eden.</note>. In this latter sense only, man participates in some light and discernment regarding the perfection of the Moral Law. But in what way? It is sufficient to check vice, but not to fully instruct one in their duty. Therefore, the doctrine of religion, both moral and mystical, cannot be attained except by inspiration and revelation from God.

The use of reason in spiritual things, however, and its scope, is very great and general. For it is not for nothing that the Apostle <note>St. Paul, in Romans 12:1.</note> calls religion *our reasonable service of God* <note>original: "rationabile obsequium"</note>, inso—

<summary>Bacon argues that while human conscience retains a "spark" of original purity to recognize evil, true moral and religious perfection can only be reached through divine revelation rather than human reason alone.</summary>
<keywords>Dendamis, Alexander the Great, Pythagoras, Light of Nature, Moral Law, Conscience, Reason, Divine Revelation, Original State</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between divine revelation and human reason, specifically contrasting the Christian approach with other religious systems.</meta>

# The Second Book

Inasmuch as the very ceremonies and figures of the Old Law <note>The Old Testament or Mosaic Law</note> were full of reason and meaning, they are much more significant than the ceremonies of idolatry and magic, which are full of *meaningless symbols* and *irrational characters* <note>original: "Non-significants and Surde Characters"</note>. But most especially the Christian faith—as in all things, so in this—deserves to be highly magnified for holding and preserving the golden mean <note>original: "Mediocritie," meaning a balanced middle ground</note> on this point, between the law of the *Heathen* and the law of *Muhammad* <note>original: "Mahumet"</note>, which have embraced the two extremes. For the religion of the *Heathen* had no constant belief or confession, but left everything to the liberty of argument; and the religion of *Muhammad* on the other side, forbids argument altogether. The one has the very face of error, and the other of imposture; whereas the Christian faith both admits and rejects disputation, depending on the context.

The use of human reason in religion is of two sorts: the first is in the conception and understanding of the mysteries of God revealed to us; the other is in the inferring and deriving of doctrine and direction from them. The former extends to the mysteries themselves, but how? By way of illustration, and not by way of argument. The latter consists indeed of proof and argument. In the former, we see God chooses to descend to our capacity by expressing His mysteries in a way that may be perceptible to us; He grafts His revelations and holy doctrine upon the notions of our reason, and applies His inspirations—

<summary>Bacon argues that Christianity maintains a perfect balance by using reason to illustrate divine mysteries without allowing it to override faith, unlike the irrationality of paganism or the rigid dogmatism of Islam.</summary>
<keywords>Human Reason, Theology, Comparative Religion, Golden Mean, Divine Revelation, Logic, Muhammad, Heathenism</keywords>

--- Page 314 ---
<meta>This page continues Bacon's discussion on the limits and uses of reason in matters of Divine Revelation compared to its use in Nature and human laws.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

Reason is to open our understanding, just as the shape of the key is to the internal structure <note>original: "ward"</note> of the lock. For the latter, we are allowed a use of reason and argument that is secondary and relative, though not original and absolute. For after the articles and principles of religion are established and exempted from the examination of reason, we are then permitted to derive inferences from them according to their analogy, for our better direction. 

In Nature, this does not hold true; for the principles themselves are examinable by induction <note>The process of drawing general conclusions from specific observations.</note>, though not by a middle term of syllogisms <note>original: "Medium of Syllogismes"; a form of logical argument where a conclusion is drawn from two premises.</note>. Furthermore, those principles or first positions in nature have no conflict with the same reason that derives and leads to the lower positions. 

However, this restriction does not apply to religion alone, but to many types of knowledge both great and small—namely, those wherein there are not only "things positioned" <note>original: *Posita*; facts or established truths.</note> but "things decided by will" <note>original: *Placita*; arbitrary rules or decrees.</note>. In such cases, there can be no use of absolute reason. We see this familiarly in games of wit, such as **chess** or the like. The moves and first laws of the game are positive, but how? Merely by choice <note>original: *ad placitum*; according to pleasure or will</note>, and they are not examinable by reason. But then, how to direct our play based on those rules to the best advantage to win the game is a matter of skill and rationality. 

So it is in human laws: there are many grounds and maxims which are "decrees of law" <note>original: *Placita Iuris*</note>, positive based upon authority and not upon reason, and therefore they are not to be disputed. But what is most just—not absolutely, but relatively and according to those maxims—affords a long field of disputation. Such there-

<sig>H h</sig>

<summary>Francis Bacon argues that while the fundamental principles of religion and certain human systems (like law or games) are accepted by authority rather than reason, reason is still essential for deriving logical conclusions from those established rules.</summary>

...gain. Therefore, there is that secondary reason which has a place in divinity, which is grounded upon the *Decrees of God* <note>original: "Placets of God"; refers to things that are true simply because God has willed them to be so</note>.

<margin>On the legitimate use of human reason in divine matters.</margin>

Here, therefore, I note this deficiency: that there has not been, to my understanding, a sufficient inquiry into and handling of *the true limits and rules of reason in spiritual things*. This would be a kind of divine Logic <note>original: "Dialectique"</note>. Because this has not been done, it seems to me a common thing for people—under the pretext of truly conceiving what is revealed—to search and mine into that which is not revealed. Furthermore, under the pretext of clarifying <note>original: "enucleating"</note> inferences and contradictions, they examine that which is established as absolute truth.

The first sort of people fall into the error of *Nicodemus*, demanding to have things made more sensible to the human mind than it pleases God to reveal them: *How can a man be born when he is old?* <note>original: "Quomodo possit homo nasci cum sit senex?" (John 3:4)</note>. The other sort fall into the error of the Disciples, who were offended by the appearance of a contradiction: *What is this that he says to us: "A little while, and you will not see me; and again a little while, and you will see me"?* <note>original: "Quid est hoc quod dicit nobis, modicum, & non videbitis me, & iterum modicum, & videbitis me?" (John 16:17)</note>.

I have insisted on this point all the more because of its great and blessed use. If this point were well-labored and defined, it would, in my judgment, be an opiate to stay and bridle not only the vanity of curious speculations which trouble the academic schools, but also the fury of controversies which trouble the Church. For it cannot help but open men's eyes to see that many controversies pertain merely to that which is either not revealed or is an absolute decree; and that many other controversies grow out of weak and obscure inferences or derivations, which latter sort...

Of the Advancement of Learning

sort of men would revive the blessed style of that great Doctor of the Gentiles <note>Referring to the Apostle Paul</note>, it would be carried thus: *I, not the Lord*, and again *according to my counsel* <note>original Latin: "Ego, non Dominus" and "secundum consilium meum"</note>, in opinions and counsels, and not in absolute positions and oppositions. But men are now over-ready to usurp the style: *Not I, but the Lord* <note>original Latin: "Non Ego, sed Dominus"</note>; and not only so, but to bind it with the thunder and denunciation of curses and *excommunications* <note>original: "Anathemaes"</note>, to the terror of those who have not sufficiently learned from *Solomon* that *the causeless curse shall not come*.

Divinity <note>Theology</note> has two principal parts: the matter informed or revealed, and the nature of the information or revelation. We will begin with the latter, because it has the most coherence with what we have just handled. The nature of the information consists of three branches:
1. The limits of the information.
2. The sufficiency of the information.
3. The acquiring or obtaining of the information.

To the **limits of the information** belong these considerations: how far particular persons continue to be inspired; how far the Church is inspired; and how far reason may be used—the last point of which I have noted as deficient. To the **sufficiency of the information** belong two considerations: what points of religion are fundamental, and what are "perfective" <note>matters of further development</note>, being matters of further building and perfection upon one and the same foundation; and again, how the gradations of light according to the

The dispensation of times is material to the sufficiency of belief.

<margin>Concerning the degrees of unity in the City of God. <note>original: "De gradibus unitatis in Civitate Dei"</note></margin>

Here again, I may rather offer advice than note a deficiency: that fundamental points and points of further perfection only <note>meaning: points that are secondary or for spiritual growth rather than essential for salvation</note> ought to be distinguished with piety and wisdom. This is a subject tending toward much the same end as what I noted before; for as that other suggestion was likely to reduce the number of controversies, so this is likely to abate the heat of many of them. 

We see that when **Moses** saw the **Israelite** and the **Egyptian** fighting, he did not say, *"Why do you strive?"* but drew his sword and slew the **Egyptian**. But when he saw the two **Israelites** fighting, he said, *"You are brothers; why do you strive?"* If a point of doctrine is an **Egyptian** <note>representing a completely alien or false belief</note>, it must be slain by the sword of the spirit and not reconciled. But if it is an **Israelite** <note>representing a fellow believer</note>, even if they are in the wrong, then we must ask, *"Why do you strive?"* 

We see regarding fundamental points, our Savior phrases the league thus: *"He that is not with us is against us"* <note>Matthew 12:30</note>. But regarding points that are not fundamental, he says: *"He that is not against us is with us"* <note>Mark 9:40</note>. So we see the coat of our Savior was entire, without a seam, and so is the **Doctrine of the Scriptures** in itself. But the **garment of the Church** was of diverse colors, and yet not divided. We see the chaff may and ought to be severed from the corn in the ear; but the **Tares** <note>term: weeds that look like wheat</note> may not be pulled up from the corn in the field. Thus, it is a thing of great use to define clearly what, and of what latitude, those points are which make men merely aliens and dis-

# Of the Advancement of Learning

incorporate from the Church of God. For obtaining this information, it rests upon the true and sound interpretation of the Scriptures, which are the fountains of the water of life. The interpretations of the Scriptures are of two sorts: Methodical, and Solute <note>original: "Solute"; meaning free, loose, or unrestricted</note>, or at large. For this divine water, which excels so much that of *Jacob’s* Well, is drawn forth much in the same way as natural water is usually drawn out of wells and fountains: either it is first forced up into a cistern and from there fetched and diverted for use, or else it is drawn and received in buckets and vessels immediately from where it springs. 

The former method, though it seems to be the more ready, yet in my judgment is more subject to corruption. This is that "Method" which has presented us with Scholastic Divinity <note>The system of theological and philosophical teaching prevalent in the medieval schools, often criticized for being overly technical</note>, whereby divinity has been reduced into an Art, as if into a cistern, and the streams of doctrine or positions are fetched and derived from there.

In this, men have sought three things: a summary brevity, a compacted strength, and a complete perfection. In the first two of these they fail to find what they seek, and the last they ought not to seek at all. For as to brevity, we see in all summary methods that while men intend to abridge, they actually give cause to expand. For the summary or abridgment becomes obscure through contraction; that obscurity requires exposition; and the exposition is deduced into large commentaries, or into commonplaces and titles, which grow to be more vast than the original writings.

<summary>Bacon discusses two methods of interpreting Scripture: the "Methodical" approach of Scholasticism, which organizes theology into a rigid system, and a more direct, "Solute" approach. He argues that systematic abridgments often lead to greater obscurity and longer commentaries than the original texts.</summary>

# The Second Book

headings, from which the sum was at first extracted. So we see the volumes of the schoolmen <note>original: "schoole-men"; medieval theologians who used Aristotelian logic to explain Christian doctrine</note> are much greater than the first writings of the Church Fathers, from which the Master of the Sentences <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160), whose "Four Books of Sentences" became the standard textbook of medieval theology</note> made his sum or collection. In the same way, the volumes of the modern doctors of the Civil Law exceed those of the ancient jurisconsults <note>legal experts or scholars</note> from which Tribonian <note>The Byzantine jurist who supervised the codification of Roman law under Emperor Justinian</note> compiled the Digest. Thus, this practice of creating sums and commentaries is what infallibly makes the body of sciences more immense in quantity, but more base in substance.

And as for strength, it is true that branches of knowledge reduced into exact methods have a show of strength, because each part seems to support and sustain the other. But this is more satisfying than substantial. It is like buildings that stand by architecture and compaction <note>the way parts are joined together</note>, which are more subject to ruin than those that are built stronger in their individual parts, even if they are less compacted. It is plain that the further you recede from your grounds <note>foundations or principles</note>, the weaker your conclusions become. Just as in nature, the more you remove yourself from particulars <note>specific observations</note>, the greater the peril of error you incur; so, much more in divinity <note>theology</note>, the more you recede from the Scriptures by inferences and consequences, the more weak and diluted are your positions.

And as for perfection or completeness in divinity, it is not to be sought, which makes this course of "artificial divinity" <note>theology organized into a rigid, man-made system or "art"</note> all the more suspect. For he who will reduce a branch of knowledge into an art will try to make it round and uniform; but in divinity

Many things must be left unfinished and concluded with this:
> "Oh, the depth of the wisdom and knowledge of God! How unsearchable are his judgments, and his paths beyond tracing out!" <note>original Latin: *O altitudo Sapientiæ & ſcientiæ Dei, quam incomprehenſibilia ſunt Iudicia eius, & non inueſtigabiles viæ eius* (Romans 11:33)</note>

So again the Apostle says,
> "We know in part," <note>original Latin: *Ex parte ſcimus* (1 Corinthians 13:9)</note>

and to attempt to create the form of a complete whole where there is only enough material for a part cannot be done without filling the gaps with suppositions and presumptions. Therefore, I conclude that the true use of these summaries <note>original: "Summes"</note> and systematic methods is appropriate for basic instructions or introductions preparatory to knowledge. However, to use them—or deductions drawn from them—to handle the main body and substance of a field of knowledge is harmful in all sciences and dangerous in divinity <note>term: theology</note>.

As for the interpretation of the Scriptures in a free and unrestricted manner, various methods have been introduced and devised; some of these are more overly-curious and unsafe than they are sober and authorized. Nevertheless, this much must be confessed: since the Scriptures were given by divine inspiration and not by human reason, they differ from all other books because of their Author. This, by consequence, requires a different approach to be used by the interpreter <note>original: "Expoſitor"</note>. For the Writer of them <note>referring to the Holy Spirit</note> knew four things which no man manages to know: the mysteries of the kingdom of glory; the perfection of the laws of nature; the secrets of the human heart; and the future succession of all ages. Regarding the first, it is said:
> "He who presses into the light shall be overwhelmed by the glory." <note>original Latin: *Qui scrutator est maiestatis, opprimetur a gloria* (Proverbs 25:27)</note>

And again:
> "No man shall see..."

# The Second Book

"No man shall see my face and live." To the second point: "When he prepared the heavens I was present, when by law and compass he enclosed the deep." To the third: "Neither was it necessary that any should bear witness to him of Man, for he knew well what was in Man." And to the last: "From the beginning all his works are known to the Lord."

From the first two of these passages, certain interpretations of the Scriptures have been drawn which need to be kept within the bounds of sobriety; the one is *Anagogical* <note>Anagogical: interpreting a text to reveal spiritual or mystical meanings regarding the future life or heaven.</note>, and the other is *Philosophical*. Regarding the former, man must not get ahead of his time: "We see now through a glass darkly, but then face to face" <note>original: *Videmus nunc per speculum in Aenigmate, tunc autem facie ad faciem*</note>. Nevertheless, there seems to be a liberty granted in this area, so far as it involves the polishing of this glass or some moderate explanation of this mystery <note>original: *Aenigma*</note>. But to press too far into it cannot but cause a breakdown and overthrow of the human spirit. 

In the body, there are three degrees of what we receive into it: *Nourishment* <note>original: *Aliment*</note>, *Medicine*, and *Poison*. *Nourishment* is that which the nature of man can perfectly transform and overcome; *Medicine* is that which is partly converted by nature and partly converts nature itself; and *Poison* is that which works entirely upon nature without nature being able to work upon it in any way. Similarly, in the mind, whatever knowledge reason cannot work upon and convert at all is a mere intoxication, and it endangers the dissolution of the mind and understanding.

But as for the latter <note>referring to the philosophical interpretation of scripture</note>, it has been extremely promoted in recent times by the school of *Paracelsus* <note>Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a Swiss physician and alchemist who argued that spiritual and physical truths were deeply intertwined.</note>, and

<summary>Bacon warns against the dangers of over-extending human reason into divine mysteries, comparing indigestible knowledge to poison that can dissolve the understanding. He specifically critiques the mystical and philosophical excesses of the school of Paracelsus.</summary>

<keywords>theology, limits of reason, Paracelsus, anagogical interpretation, philosophy of mind, divine mystery, nourishment vs poison</keywords>

Of the advancement of learning

There are some others who have pretended to find the truth of all natural philosophy in the Scriptures, scandalizing and slandering all other philosophy as heathenish and profane. But there is no such enmity between God’s word and his works. Neither do they give honor to the Scriptures, as they suppose, but rather greatly debase them. For to seek heaven and earth in the word of God—of which it is said, *Heaven and earth shall pass away, but my word shall not pass away*—is to seek temporary things among eternal ones. Just as seeking divinity in philosophy is to seek the living among the dead, so to seek philosophy in divinity is to seek the dead among the living. Neither are the pots or basins <note>original: "Lauers"; ritual washing vessels</note>, whose place was in the outward part of the Temple, to be sought in the holiest place of all, where the Ark of the Testimony was seated. 

Furthermore, the goal or purpose of the Spirit of God is not to explain matters of nature in the Scriptures, except in passing, and for application to human understanding or to moral and divine matters. And it is a true rule that *the authority of an author is small when he is handling a different subject* <note>original: "Authoris aliud agentis parua authoritas"</note>. For it would be a strange conclusion if a man should use a metaphor for the sake of ornament or illustration—borrowed from nature or history according to common beliefs, such as a *basilisk*, a *unicorn*, a *centaur*, a *Briareus* <note>A hundred-armed giant from Greek mythology</note>, an *hydra*, or the like—that he must therefore be thought to positively affirm the existence of those things as true. To conclude, therefore, these two interpretations,

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the interpretation of the Holy Scriptures, specifically addressing the limitations of human interpretation and the unique nature of divine revelation compared to human writings.</meta>

interpretations—the one by reduction or *enigmatic* <note>original: "Aenigmaticall"</note>, the other *philosophical* or *physical*—which have been received and pursued in imitation of the *Rabbis* and *Cabalists* <note>Jewish mystical and legal scholars</note>, are to be restricted by the warning: *Do not be wise in your own conceits, but fear* <note>original: "Noli altum sapere, sed time." A reference to Romans 11:20.</note>.

But the two later points known to God and unknown to Man—*concerning the secrets of the heart and the successions of time*—create a just and sound difference between the manner of interpreting the Scriptures and all other books. For it is an excellent observation that has been made regarding the answers of our Savior Christ to many of the questions which were proposed to him: they often seem irrelevant to the literal state of the question asked. The reason for this is because he was not like a man, who knows another man's thoughts only by his words; rather, knowing man's thoughts immediately, he never answered their words, but their thoughts. 

It is much the same with the Scriptures. Because they were written for the thoughts of men and for the succession of all ages—with a foresight of all heresies, contradictions, and the differing states of the Church, and even particularly for the individual elect—they are not to be interpreted only according to the narrow latitude of the literal sense of the passage. Nor should they be interpreted solely in respect to the specific occasion upon which the words were uttered, or in precise grammatical consistency with the words before or after, or even solely in contemplation of the principal aim of the passage; 

<summary>Bacon argues that because Scripture is divinely authored with foresight of all human history and thought, its interpretation must go beyond literal context to address the "secrets of the heart."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the interpretation of the Holy Scriptures and the various methods of theological writing.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

But [the Scriptures] have in themselves—not only totally or collectively, but distributively in every clause and word—infinite springs and streams of doctrine to water the Church in every part. Therefore, as the literal sense is, as it were, the main stream or river, so the moral sense chiefly, and sometimes the *allegorical* or *typical* <note>Typicall: relating to "types," or persons/events in the Old Testament believed to foreshadow the New Testament.</note> senses, are those which the Church uses most. I do not wish for men to be overbold in *allegories*, or indulgent and trivial in *allusions*; however, I strongly condemn that interpretation of the Scripture which is conducted only in the same manner as men interpret a secular <note>original: "prophane"</note> book.

In this section regarding the exposition of the Scriptures, I can report no deficiency. But by way of remembrance, I will add this: in perusing books of divinity, I find many books of controversies, and many of commonplaces <note>Commonplaces: collections of general points or themes for easy reference.</note> and treatises; a mass of positive divinity <note>Positive divinity: theology based on direct revelation or established authority rather than speculative reasoning.</note> as it is made into a formal art; a number of sermons and lectures; and many lengthy commentaries upon the Scriptures with harmonies and concordances. 

But that form of writing in divinity which, in my judgment, is of all others most rich and precious, is positive divinity collected from particular texts of Scripture in brief observations. These should not be expanded into general commonplaces, nor should they chase after controversies, nor be reduced into a rigid method of art. This is a style of writing that abounds in sermons, which will vanish, but is lacking in books, which would remain. It is a thing wherein this age excels; for I am persuaded—and I may say it, *let there be no ill will toward my words* <note>original: "Absit invidia verbo"</note>—

<meta>The text concludes with the catchword "verbo," leading into the next page's continuation of the Latin phrase.</meta>

<summary>Bacon argues that Scripture should be interpreted beyond its literal sense and advocates for a style of theological writing focused on brief, insightful observations of specific texts rather than dense, systematic treatises.</summary>
<keywords>Scripture, interpretation, allegory, divinity, theology, sermons, positive divinity, biblical commentary</keywords>

--- Page 325 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the interpretation of Scripture and the structure of Divinity, following the author's praise for the depth of British theological scholarship.</meta>

<margin>The flow of the Scriptures into positive doctrines.</margin>

By the *word* <note>original: "verbo"</note>, and in no way to the disparagement of antiquity, but rather as a healthy rivalry between the vine and the olive: I believe that if the choice and best observations upon the texts of Scripture—which have been made scattered throughout sermons within your Majesty’s islands of Britain for the space of these forty years and more—had been recorded in a continuous work (leaving out the lengthy exhortations and applications), it would have been the best work on Divinity written since the time of the Apostles.

The subject matter informed by Divinity is of two kinds: matters of belief and truth of opinion, and matters of service and adoration (which is also judged and directed by the former). The one is like the internal soul of religion, and the other is like its external body. Therefore, the heathen religion was not only a worship of idols, but the whole religion was an idol in itself; for it had no soul—that is, no certainty of belief or confession. One might well conclude this, considering the chief doctors of their church were the poets. The reason for this was because the heathen gods were not "jealous gods," but were glad to be admitted into a share of worship, as they had reason to be. Neither did they respect the purity of heart, so long as they might receive external honor and rites.

But out of these two result and issue four main branches of Divinity: *Faith, Manners, Liturgy, and Government.* Faith contains the doctrine of the nature of God and of the attributes of

<meta>God</meta>

<summary>The author argues that British sermons from the last forty years contain the finest theology since the Apostles and outlines the four branches of Divinity: Faith, Manners, Liturgy, and Government.</summary>
<keywords>Divinity, British theology, heathenism vs. Christianity, Faith, Manners, Liturgy, Government, Church history</keywords>

--- Page 326 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the classification of divine knowledge, specifically focusing on the nature of God and the works of Creation and Redemption.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

God, and of the works of God; the nature of God consists of three persons in the unity of the Godhead. The attributes of God are either common to the deity as a whole, or specific to the individual persons. The summary works of God are two: that of the *Creation*, and that of the *Redemption*. Both of these works, in their totality, appertain to the unity of the Godhead; yet, in their specific parts, they refer to the three persons. 

The work of Creation refers to the Father in the mass of the matter, to the Son in the disposition of the form, and to the Holy Spirit in the continuance and conservation of being. Likewise, the work of Redemption refers to the Father in the election and counsel, to the Son in the whole act and consummation, and to the Holy Spirit in the application. For by the Holy Ghost was Christ conceived in the flesh, and by the Holy Ghost are the Elect <note>original: "Elect" — those predestined by God for salvation</note> regenerate in spirit. This work is likewise considered either effectively in the Elect, or privately in the reprobate <note>original: "reprobate" — those rejected by God and destined for damnation</note>, or according to appearance in the visible Church.

Regarding manners <note>original: "manners" — here meaning ethics or moral conduct</note>, the doctrine of them is contained in the law, which discloses sin. The law itself is divided, according to its edition, into the law of Nature, the Moral law, and the Positive law <note>original: "lawe Positiue" — laws formally enacted by a deity or government, as opposed to natural law</note>; and according to its style, into Negative and Affirmative—Prohibitions and Commandments. Sin, in its matter and subject, is divided according to the Commandments; in its form, it refers to the three persons in the deity:

<summary>Francis Bacon outlines the theological framework for understanding God's nature and His dual works of Creation and Redemption, while also categorizing the different types of divine and moral law.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Creation, Redemption, Holy Spirit, The Elect, Moral Law, Law of Nature, Positive Law, Sin, Theology</keywords>

--- Page 327 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divisions of theology, specifically focusing on the classification of sins and the components of Christian worship.</meta>

Sins of infirmity <note>original: "Infirmity"; meaning human weakness or lack of strength</note> are committed against the Father, whose more special attribute is Power. Sins of ignorance are against the Son, whose attribute is Wisdom; and sins of malice are against the Holy Spirit, whose attribute is Grace or Love. In its movements, sin moves either to the right hand or to the left: either toward blind devotion or toward profane and libertine transgression; either by imposing restraint where God grants liberty, or by taking liberty where God imposes restraint. In its degrees and progress, it divides itself into thought, word, or act. In this section, I highly commend the application of the Law of God to "cases of conscience" <note>The practice of casuistry: applying general moral laws to specific, complex personal circumstances</note>, for I truly consider that to be a "breaking of the bread of life" into pieces for the people, rather than presenting it whole and uncut. But that which gives life to both these doctrines of faith and manners is the elevation and agreement of the heart, to which belong books of exhortation, holy meditation, Christian resolution, and the like.

As for the Liturgy or service, it consists of the reciprocal acts between God and man. On God's part, these are the preaching of the word and the Sacraments (which are seals to the covenant, or like the "visible word"). On man's part, these consist of the invocation of the name of God; and under the old Law, sacrifices, which were like visible prayers or confessions. But now, since adoration is performed *in spirit and in truth* <note>original: "spiritu & veritate"; referencing John 4:24</note>, there remains only the *offering of the lips* <note>original: "vituli labiorum"; literally "the calves of the lips," a biblical metaphor for praise and prayer from Hosea 14:2</note>.

<summary>Bacon categorizes sins according to the attributes of the Trinity and defines the Liturgy as a reciprocal exchange between God's word/sacraments and man's prayer/praise.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, sins of infirmity, cases of conscience, Liturgy, Sacraments, worship, spiritual devotion</keywords>

--- Page 328 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the divisions of Theology, specifically focusing on the administration of the Church and the nature of religious deviations.</meta>

# Of the Advancement of Learning

Although the use of holy vows of thankfulness and retribution <note>original: "retribution," here meaning a formal repayment or offering to God in exchange for a blessing</note> may also be accounted as sealed petitions.

And for the **Government of the Church**, it consists of the patrimony <note>The landed property or inherited assets</note> of the church, the franchises <note>Legal rights and privileges</note> of the Church, and the offices and jurisdictions of the Church, and the Laws of the Church directing the whole. All of these have two considerations: the one in themselves, and the other how they stand compatible and agreeable to the Civil Estate <note>The secular government or state</note>.

This matter of **Divinity** is handled either in the form of instruction of truth, or in the form of confutation <note>disproof</note> of falsehood. The declinations <note>deviations</note> from **Religion**, besides the primitive one which is **Atheism** and its branches, are three: **Heresies**, **Idolatry**, and **Witchcraft**.

*   **Heresies**: when we serve the true God with a false worship.
*   **Idolatry**: when we worship false gods, supposing them to be true.
*   **Witchcraft**: when we adore false gods, knowing them to be wicked and false.

For so your **Majesty** <note>Refers to King James I, to whom this work was dedicated</note> does excellently well observe, that **Witchcraft** is the height of **Idolatry**. And yet we see, though these are true degrees of difference, **Samuel** teaches us that they are all of one nature once there is a receding from the word of God; for so he says: *To rebel is as the sin of witchcraft, and to be stubborn is as the crime of idolatry.* <note>original Latin: "Quasi Peccatum ariolandi est repugnare, & quasi scelus Idolatriae nolle acquiescere." See 1 Samuel 15:23.</note>

These things I have passed over so briefly because I can report no deficiency concerning them.

<summary>Bacon outlines the administrative structure of the Church and categorizes the three main deviations from true religion: heresy, idolatry, and witchcraft.</summary>

--- Page 329 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's survey of the state of human knowledge, specifically addressing the field of theology and the methodology of his overall work.</meta>

them: For I can find no space or ground that lies vacant and unsown in the matter of Divinity <note>original: "Diuinitie"; the study of religion and theology</note>, so diligent have men been, either in sowing of good seed, or in sowing of tares <note>A reference to the biblical parable of the weeds (Matthew 13:24–30), suggesting that while much has been written on theology, much of it is also error or controversy.</note>.

Thus have I made, as it were, a small globe of the intellectual world, as truly and faithfully as I could discover, with a note and description of those parts which seem to me not constantly occupied, or not well cultivated by the labor of man. In which, if I have in any point receded from that which is commonly received, it has been with a purpose of proceeding toward the better <note>original: "in melius"</note>, and not toward the different <note>original: "in aliud"</note>: a mind of amendment and progress <note>original: "proficience"</note>, and not of change and discord. For I could not be true and constant to the argument I handle if I were not willing to go beyond others, but yet not more willing than to have others go beyond me again. This may better appear by the fact that I have propounded my opinions naked and unarmed, not seeking to take away <note>original: "preoccupate"</note> the liberty of men’s judgments by offering rebuttals <note>original: "confutations"</note>. For in anything which is well set down, I am in good hope that if the first reading moves an objection, the second reading will provide an answer. And in those things wherein I have erred, I am sure I have not prejudiced the truth by litigious arguments; which certainly have this contrary effect and operation: they add authority to error, and destroy the authority of that which is well discovered <note>original: "inuented"</note>.

<summary>The author concludes his survey of human knowledge, describing it as a "small globe of the intellectual world," and expresses his hope that his work will lead to progress rather than mere controversy.</summary>

For to call a matter into question is an honor and a promotion to falsehood, just as, on the other hand, it is a rejection of the truth. But as for the errors within this work, I claim and challenge them to myself as my own.
